Dedication . . .

We dedicate Beulah's Golden Jubilee Book to the early settlers of Beulah and
the surrounding community.

These stalwart people were blessed not only with a trust in their fellow man,
but also possessed an unwavering faith in their God. This innate trust and
faith girded them with a tremendous amount of courage and fortitude with which
to battle and conquer the almost unsurmountable obstacles that were set before
them.

They left behind a heritage of which we of the Beulah area can look upon with
pride--even unto the third and fourth generation.

Foreward . . .

In November, 1963 the present Jubilee Book committee was appointed by the
Chamber of Commerce and began to function actively. Those on the initial
committee were: Agnes Herman, Rags Thelander, Opal Unruh, Alice Abbey, Naomi
Unruh, Marie Stickney, Lawrence Joos, and Tillie Dettmann served as Chairman.
Due to the serious illness of her husband, Marie Stickney was excused from her
committee duties early in the year.

From the very beginning it seemed we were plagued with more than the usual
amount of setbacks--first of all--time was against us (no one should undertake
a project such as this, unless you allow yourself a year's time), then there
was the usual amount of illnesses among our members and their families, and
what seemed to handicap us the most, was the fact that many of the official
records of the various clubs and organizations were either not available or
non-existent. We got off to a rather slow start mainly because the people were
just not interested and some were even a bit belligerent; but gradually, like a
slow-moving ball, the compiling of the book began to gain momentum and gathered
more and more information. The more information it gathered the more the local
interest "picked up", so that toward the end of the ingathering of information
we were literally "snowed under". Information came from all parts of the
country and many states. I wish I could begin to list all of the wonderful and
cooperative people who helped us check data, collect histories, and arouse
local interest in the book, but the list would be too lengthy. We will be
eternally grateful to them.

I believe that I can safely speak for the committee when I say that the more
information we collected the more we realized we should have devoted at least a
year to the writing of this book. We are aware of the fact that many things
have been omitted, but some of these required a certain amount of research and
getting clearance to have them printed, therefore we were forced to lay them
aside. We did our best and we hope you will enjoy reading the following pages
as much as we have enjoyed doing the research, editing, and compiling of this
local history.

I do want to take this opportunity to thank the members of the committee who
worked so hard and put in long hours of tedious work: collecting information,
checking the data, and writing and editing the family histories. As the book
progressed more help was needed on the various phases of the project, so more
help was recruited--as typists Mary Lou Heihn and D. H. Dettmann; as
proofreaders, Catherine Scherbinske, Theresa Thompson, and Gert Buscher. Ernie
Ohlhauser took care of the "lion's share" of selling the advertising. Lynn
Aasen, as Chairman of the Jubilee Committee, and Herb Scherbinske, as President
of the Chamber, did a good deal of "leg work" doing the many diplomatic and not
so diplomatic errands that have to be done on an undertaking such as this was.

The Beulah Jubilee Book has been a project of the Beulah Chamber of Commerce
and those who worked on it have received nothing but the satisfaction that the
history of "our town" and its surrounding area has been compiled and thus has
been made available to all who wish to read it.

                Mrs. D. H. Dettmann
                Chairman of the Jubilee Book Committee

An Introduction By Way Of A Little Early Background

Early in 1911, settlers on either side of the Knife River Valley watched the
building of a large, commodious house and a two story business structure with a
mixture of bewilderment and amusement. It just didn't add up--why would Dr.
Vredenburg of Hebron build, what looked like the beginning of a new trading
store, here in the middle of the prairies? This particular spot was
inaccessible from the south, for there were no bridges spanning the Knife
River. Farrington was situated a mile to the east and was right on the bank of
the best river crossing--the south farmers would go no further to do their
trading. The north farmers had adequate trading facilities in Kasmer, Kronthal,
Expansion and Mannhaven, so they wouldn't be doing their trading here.

When the doctor was finished he named his new town, Troy. He never moved into
his house or started a business. No one seems to know why or what prompted him
to erect these buildings and what caused him to leave after they were finished.
Later on he sold them to the Tuttle Land Co. (These two buildings became known
in later years as the Moore house, which in turn became the Joachim Memorial
Home, and the Rose Hardware).

It would seem that the good doctor must have been speculating and had "jumped
the gun" a bit prematurely for when the N. P. R. R. laid it's railroad bed
through this area in 1913, it passed within two blocks of these two lonely
"watchdogs of the prairie". Now things began to happen and people became aware
of the possibilities of this location. Soon these two buildings were occupied
and new ones were being built. Troy was beginning to stir.

When the railroad was completed a R. R. official renamed this little hamlet,
Beulah. Thus 50 years ago the early settlers of Mercer County witnessed the
birth of a new town--

        BEULAH STINCHCOMBE BISHOP

Beulah was named after Mrs. Bishop--nee Beulah Stinchcombe--by her uncle L. C.
Pettibone of the Tuttle Land Company. Prior to this, this settlement had been
known as Troy.

Mrs. Bishop visited Beulah with her uncle in 1917 and again with her husband in
1928.

Her visit in 1917 coincided with the farewell ceremonies for some of the men
leaving for service in World War I.      The Bishops live in Calgary, Alberta,
Canada. They have several children.

        1963 CITY COUNCIL Left to right: Bill Keller, City Clerk, John Krausz,
        Reuben Krein, Wilmer Wiedrich, Aldermen, Lynn Aasen, Mayor, Jack Helm,
        August Neuberger, and Carl Menge, Aldermen. Other City employees are:
        Emil Wagner, Bill Schulz, maintenance, Fred Hauck, marshall and Joe
        Weind, water plant supervisor. Beulah's first town board was: W. D.
        Nieheus, Mayor; and Jacob Heihn and Frank Schaeffer served as the
        first aldermen.

THE CITY HALL

        The Beulah City Hall was built in 1959 at a cost of about $70,000.00. It
        measures 75' x 100' and bas the following facilities: two meeting rooms
        (that can be converted to three), kitchen, Woman's Club Library, the
        City office, and houses, two fire trucks and all the fire fighting
        equipment and city maintenance equipment.

THE WATER TREATMENT PLANT

        The above plant was built in 1962 at a cost of $180,000.00. This has
        now solved the Beulah City water problem that has plagued Beulah for so
        many years. Due to the high iron content in the city water, this plant
        was a necessary and welcome addition to the town.

BEULAH'S PARKS

Beulah has had a Park for almost its whole existence. The original park site
was purchased with money from the sale of the Fair Association property.

This is a particularly lovely natural woodland on the Knife River. The ash and
boxelder trees, with their spreading branches, create lovely moonlight
silhouettes and intricate patterns in the new fallen snow.

The Park is free. It is the pride of the town. It has excellent facilities for
picnicking, playground and sports. During part of the summer it still provides
the proverbial nostalgic swimming hole for Beulah youngsters.

It is in this same Park that we have our well lighted baseball park and
football field.

Adjoining it are the newly remodeled Rodeo grounds and one of the prettiest
nine hole golfcourses.

The town playground park is centrally located. It provides lighted tennis and
basketball courts, besides the children's well equipped playground.

Twenty miles north on the Garrison Reservoir, is the latest addition to
Beulah's Park System. Here, on Hafner Bay, is laid out a future swimming area.
Planting trees in this section was a local community project. The Bay is a
relatively sheltered one.

The excellent boating and fishing facilities are used by Beulah and surrounding
community all summer. When winter comes, fish houses dot the ice. Here one can
both fish and skate.

CHAMBER OF COMMERCE

        Beulah 1914

        Main Street around 1920

        First public appearance


The orderly development of a town is the goal of many community leaders.
Usually this leadership is centralized under some sort of civic organization
such as the civic club, commercial club or chamber of commerce. Beulah, too,
has had spirited leadership striving to advance the long-run orderly
development of the community. In the half century the road of the commercial
club and later the chamber of commerce has not been easy, has not been smooth,
but it has been interesting.

It all started back on August 6, 1914, when a group of civic leaders in the new
community of Beulah met in the Chris Henry Hotel. The purpose was to organize a
commercial club. Right from the start there were some dissenters, but by the
time the meeting adjourned, C. B. Heinemeyer was elected president of the new
group. Serving with him were R. G. Stuhr, vice president, W. C. Kees, secretary
and C. E. Ratramel, treasurer.

The first record of promotion for the new club was a bridge. There wasn't a
good bridge across the Knife River and in portions of the year trade from the
south of Beulah would be hampered. There were plenty of timbers, big ones,
around and labor was plentiful. A new steel bridge was in order. J. B.
Hagelberger met with the club representing the farmers from the south and said
that they would come in and help. The bridge was built, but just to keep
everyone happy, all laborers were paid 20¢ an hour.

Along in 1915 the big promotion was a market day. This was Mercer County's
first and the forerunner of many fairs and harvest festivals that were pretty
much a tradition in early history of the town.

Money to do things is always a problem. The commercial club solved this problem
back in 1915 by serving a banquet at the Chris Henry Hotel. All of the
surrounding public attended the affair and paid 50¢ a plate.

In the complex and fitful growth process, many problems arose. Usually these
problems were channeled into a committee. All too often the community response
and occasionally the club response towards a fruitful solution was lacking. One
of the first instances of total frustration came early in the club's history. A
hospital was needed, but action or community response to the need was slow.

On the fun side, the club decided in 1915 to help finance the community band.
Efforts were also made to roust out all the local "horn players" to make the
band a success. In the beginning the spirit was good and music rather poor, but
the progress of this new group far surpassed even the fondest expectations.

Should a street crossing be two planks wide?--or should it be three planks
wide? Two planks are cheaper. Three planks make possible for people to pass
without stepping off the plank walk. Motion for three planks--all votes aye.
So, Beulah will have three plank street crossings in 1916. Thus rounded out the
business during 1915.

One of the first accounts of true concern about other towns in respect to
competition came in 1916. It was suspected that Hazen, Ree, Zap and Glen Ullin
were overgrading the grain. After many thoughts otherwise, the club decided on
a "Kid glove" technique of writing to the commissioner of the railroad to make
an investigation. An investigation was made, but no action or findings were
ever made a matter of record.

In 1917 the club agreed to hire a band director. The salary would be $25.00 a
month and the club would pay $1.00 per week to one of the churches for the use
of their basement to hold practices. The band was going to be a success. By
this time even the rank beginners for 1915 were accomplished musicians and were
playing by written notes.

Along in 1921 the Mandan-Killdeer Mountain Trail was the talk of the day. The
MKM route would cost money. Beulah's share for "trail blazing" would be $85.00.
It was paid.

A corn growing contest was sponsored back in 1922. All of this was along the
countywide theme of "Corn and Clover". Winners of the contest were Ray
Sherwood, John Wetzel and Glenn Murray.

In the 20's many other organizations of a "community promotion" nature appeared
on the scene. While these new organizations were certainly welcome additions to
the town, it is quite evident that the true leadership or spearhead for the
community shifted from time to time. The role of the commerical club for many
years was a "lending hand" type of organization.

One of the big promotions was always the county picnic. Newspaper accounts
always mention a "talk by a prominent speaker", (although the accounts either
before or after never said who) he was always limited to "1/2 hour". A show was
held in the old theater and afterwards the seats were moved back and a big
dance followed.

Beulah had a world champion back in 1935. He was Art Unruh, the "Russian Peanut
Eating Champ" of the world. He shucked 435 sunflowers seeds in 16 minutes and
30 seconds. Close on his heels were Andy Zarodney and Willie Hiltz who finished
second and third. The whole "peanut shucking" affair was a brain storm of Tony
Benik, the proprietor of the local recreation hall. Challenges were sent out to
other communities to equal young Unruh's lusty record. No takers, the
championship remained. No one to this day can recall why the 435 seeds, anyhow
it's a lot of seed to wheel through ones mouth.

One of the strongest and most famous of all Beulah's "side" organizations in
history was the Athletic Club. Their baseball exploits are well known through
the semi-pro annuls in the Northwest. Back in 1934 the commercial club was
solicited to help promote the first night baseball game in North Dakota. A
newspaper account talked of a $5,000 lighting system, but those who worked on
the system think that may have been a bit exaggerated. Well, the first night
game was a terrific promotion. Jamestown was coming in to play and they had one
of the best semi-pro teams in the state. Beulah had Red Haroldson scheduled for
hurling chores and Bert Webber set as his battery mate. The prices were upped a
bit--adults 40¢, students 20¢ and kids would still go free. The game was a sell
out, but the lights didn't work. Next weeks paper carried one squib--"No
lights, no game. Fans sore, manager sorry."

The outgoing spirit of Main Street during the 30's is probably best exemplified
by the barbers in the town. Back in 1934 they (Buchmann, Tietz and Godwin)
offered free hair cuts to all the country kids. The offer was good for a few
weeks before school started. Needless to say, they were busy and country kids
were spic and span for the new school year.

Any progressive civic group has to be years ahead in their thinking. The task
of intelligent preparation for the future is a thankless job. Beulah's civic
club's minutes show many instances of businessmen dragging their heels and
trying, maybe unconsciously, to postpone progress. A progressive group must be
full of ideas and probably are going to make some mistakes. They have to make
some mistakes if progress is going to be made.

"Trying to attract" could well be the theme of the whole history of this civic
group. Attempts were made to attract individuals and industries that would
improve the economic climate of the town and at the same time would improve the
cultural and spiritual levels of the community.

Back in 1933 two industries were the major concern. How to get a modern
creamery and how to get a flour mill. The flour mill seemed to get the greatest
play and it was a matter for many hours of research and leg work. The
investigation revealed that the Spring Creek, Knife River watershed didn't
furnish adequate water for the mill. Some talk was made of a dam and reservoir,
but money was needed. The club was pretty low on funds at the time so the whole
idea was dropped.

The modern creamery fell into the area of postponement as many felt that it
would hurt many small cream buyers in town.

Oil had its day in Beulah, too. In 1937 to be exact, the club was sure that an
oil drilling company was coming in to set up and find oil. It caused lots of
talk, but for that time at least the oil company failed to show up.

Along about the same time a dish factory (clay products) was high on the agenda
for club promotion. The clay was right, the idea was good, but another
stumbling block showed--freight rates. The rates were just too high to make
it possible for a small plant to make a profit. The club kept the committee
active for a while, checking out many angles on sales and about the possibility
of other methods of shipment other than the railway, but eventually gave up
hopes.

During the dirty thirties, Beulah became the cowboy capitol of the Northwest.
Rodeos had started back in 1917 and were in full swing in the thirties. The
club's main function here was mainly one of vocal support and helping out the
day of the rodeo. The Reunion Rodeo was generally held on the 4th of July and
was always regarded as the best bet to "make rain" during the dry years.

There were many thoughts given to the farmers by the club all through the 50
years. Many civic-minded farmers in the trade area served in numerous
capacities through the years. One time 25 businessmen from Beulah visited farms
in the Zap area to check on a new farming practice called "basin listing". On
the way home they stopped at Zap and were served coffee and doughnuts by the
businessmen of Zap.

One of the first fund drives in a list of many promoted by the club was in the
interest of farming. A newly formed water conservation group wanted to send
John Moses to Washington to help in promoting water conservation practices. The
club raised $50.00 for the cause and one of the best attended meetings during
the 30's was recorded when Mr. Moses made his trip report to the group.

Entertainment for the community was always a problem. The club thought there
should be a new modern movie theater back during the early 30's and set out to
do something about it. A corporation was formed and shares were sold at $50.00
per share. The new Roxy Theater was built in the late 20's, opened in the early
30's but things were tough. There wasn't money for show tickets and the ugly
procedures of foreclosure were soon in action.

In the 40's a slow, tedious process of trying to get a hospital seemed to
occupy the lions share of discussion at every meeting. Time and time again the
club was thwarted in their efforts to get something started towards better
hospital service. It was quite evident the community was not ready for this
action. Once again the club was too far ahead of the community to forcefully
stimulate productive action.

Livestock sales rings have been one of the more interesting facets of Beulah's
history. Golden Valley had a sales ring in 1948. The club thought there was a
possibility that it could be purchased and brought to Beulah. A negotiation
committee was formed and before too long a livestock sales ring in Beulah
became a reality.

The old commercial club was years ahead of the United States government in
their concern about smoking. The concern wasn't about any disease; however, it
was about who was smoking and where. It seems that a rather large group of
teenage youngsters, grade school and high school students, liked to gather in a
certain down town establishment for a smoking session after school. The "hang
out" was up for "grabs" at one commercial meeting, but no one wanted to be
responsible for taking direct action. For them to go directly to the "hand out"
operator would cause hard feelings. To go to the parents would cause even
harder feelings. The law seemed helpless in the situation. Finally it was
decided that the proprietor would be cautioned, in a nice way of course. It was
never made a matter of record what happened. Undoubtedly, many of the teenage
smokers, who would now be in their thirties, never did know why the favorite
haunt got so law abiding in a hurry back in 1949.

The "darkest hour" in the club's long history was the fabled "road fight". This
lasted about three years and the intensity of feelings in this matter
practically precluded the possibility of anything else getting much attention
at the meetings. It seemed as though state highway No. 7 would bypass Beulah.
Of course this imposed a threat to the economic stability of the community. So
the civic organization had only one alternative, namely fight. So fight they
did, not always wisely, not always with tact and, as was to be the truth of the
matter in the end, never was the fight a success. The club lost members during
this time as many businessmen, while agreeing with the fight as a matter of
principle, realized before the club did that the fight was lost.

In 1954 there was talk of a Chamber of Commerce. This would organize the
thinking, the effort and the civic abilities of Beulah under one central
command. Every civic organization was counseled and on December 21, 1954, the
Chamber of Commerce was organized. The old Athletic Club and Rodeo Committees,
to name a couple, now debt ridden with unsuccessful promotions, came in. So did
many other lesser, short-term promotion committees that had cropped up under
the old commerical club.

The move to the new chamber title put some new wind in the old sock so to
speak. More farmers became members. The coal miners, who had always been one of
the most important factions in Beulah, but never civic club members, became
interested. Beulah had finally arrived, in a manner of speaking, with a system
that would consolidate their total civic effort. Nevertheless, even with the
new spirit, there would still be problems, heartaches and hard work. The new
chamber group was headed up by Emanuel Liebelt, Sr. Serving with him on the
board were Simon Link, Otto Kasper, Jack Padilla, Ed Schulz, Ralph Murray,
Francis Goodall, Carl Thelander, Tony Wolf, Ted Herman, Fred Galloway, Walter
Hafner and Dr. J. A. Power.

The first project of the new Chamber was the "nose blunting" affair of the
proposed chemical plant. The enthusiasm generated by the new organization
carried this project to a fast start. Lots of money was poured into an idea, a
seemingly wonderful idea, that to this date has the appearance of one Beulah's
biggest white elephants. The plan called for a plant that would produce carbon
and other by-products of coal. There was a need and ready market for these
by-products. Nevertheless, hoards of seemingly unsolvable problems of finances,
freight rates, and plant design staggered the imagination of even the wildest
thinkers in the group.

The first rodeo with the new group virtually froze out. In order to make ends
meet the members were asked to sign notes in the amounts of $100. This they
did. The money was raised and the chamber had weathered their first real battle.

While weathering a financial crisis, new committees were sent out into fertile
fields. Just to list a few, (1) check into a cheese plant (1957); (2) find out
about a TV booster (1957); (3) check out costs of swimming pools (1958); (4)
contact R.E.A. on power plant (1958); (5) contact national guard about an
armory (1958); (6) see about the possibility of a coin operated laundromat
(1958). All the ideas were years ahead of the town, but like the biblical
parable of the seeds some fell on fertile ground, not on stone and came forth
fruit for the betterment of Beulah.

        Taken during Crazy Days

        Sauerkraut cooks

In 1958 a new city hall gave the Chamber a new home. Once again the total scope
of influence of the Chamber expanded. The new building was spacious enough for
supper meetings and slowly, but surely, the attendance and the enthusiasm
picked up.

All clubs are at their best when they are extending a helping hand. One of the
Chambers fondest memories was being able to help the Golden Valley basketball
team make a trip to the regional tourney at Dickinson. Golden Valley didn't
win, but they represented the county very well. The chamber was proud of them
and felt a bit taller by making a small cash contribution in the team's behalf.

"Sauerkraut and wieners" should represent some of Beulah's national Heritage.
Back in 1959 the first annual "kraut and wiener" festival was held. The intent
of the festival was to gather the people in the tri-county area in the high
school gymnasium. The format would be fun, fellowship, free prizes and, of
course, all the kraut and wieners that one could stand. The first year was sort
of a fizzle. The second and third year it picked up a bit. Then in 1962 it
started to take hold. The idea of a "kraut and wiener" festival was novel
enough to rate mention in nation wide syndicated papers. Inquiries about the
promotion came in from many distant corners of the United States. In 1963--
WOW--the chamber of commerce cooks were set for 1200 people and thought they
would serve about 800. Something happened. People from all over showed
up--hundreds and hundreds more than had been anticipated. Chamber members
bedecked in white caps and aprons scurried around town buying more wieners,
more buns, more kraut and more paper plates. Everything, but everything was
gone. The "kraut and wiener" festival was at last a huge success.

Shortly before the 1961 Cowboys' Reunion the rodeo grandstands and much of the
operative arena area was dismantled during a terrific windstorm. The
grandstands were literally dumped into the middle of the rodeo arena. No rodeo
in 1961. "No rodeo ever" in Beulah said a few, quite a few, skeptics, but they
weren't acquainted with the tenacity of one Lynn Aasen who had just taken over
as chamber prexy. At the start, almost single handedly he inspired the
reconstruction of the rodeo grounds. The rebuilding of the rodeo grounds seemed
like such an impossible, hopeless task; but one by one, group by group, more
and more turned out each evening to tear down, clean up, salvage and burn, the
remains of the old rodeo arena.

THE BEULAH RODEO ARENA

        Note: the 2x's on the far left side of the picture show how much of the
        original bleachers were completely destroyed. Also note the lighted
        athletic field near the top of the picture and two of the Country
        Club's sand greens to the left of the arena.

        Some good "catches"

Bill Lindemann of Golden Valley was hired to make a new earth rolled
amphitheater. Time was running short in 1962. There were only a few short days
before the first horses were scheduled out of the chutes and the chutes were
scattered in what appeared to be a hopeless mess in one corner of the arena.
Fences weren't up and there were no concession stands and no electrical power.
Things looked tough.

The tempo picked up. Those who had chided the first efforts were caught in the
upsurge and they, too, showed up in one of Beulah's finest hours to see the
completion of the impossible task.

        Scene from the Carnival

        1963 All-around Champion Cowboy

        Herb Scherbinske, pres. of C. of C. presenting a hat to local cowboy
        Del Reich.

As the rodeo opened in 1962, Beulah's chamber of commerce stood tall, proud and
happy with a job well done.

The back water from the Garrison Dam has changed the habits of many people in
our community. People who had never been in a boat in 1950 found themselves
with motor boats in back yards, water skis in the garages, fishing gear in the
closet and less time for many of the old time hobbies. Water had taken over.

The Chamber had committees involved in checking into cabin sites, waterside
park areas, boat ramps, picnic areas and roads.

A big water carnival was sponsored in July of 1962. The committee picked the
windiest, coldest day ever recorded in July. But that didn't slow down the
crowd. Hundreds upon hundreds garbed in winter gear and carrying blankets and
hot coffee clamored around the hills at Hafner Bay to see the show. The big
deal was to be the ski jumping. The Chamber had recently constructed a
beautiful new ski ramp. Then right before the ski jumps were to start, the
"ramp" seemed to develop a slight list to the port side. Sure enough, something
was wrong. Within a few minutes the white capped waves chopped the ramp apart
like a tinker toy. The Chamber was a bit embarrassed, but the show went on and
was a success, ramp or no ramp. In 1963 the ramp was rebuilt, reinforced and
reinspected by all the marine engineers in town. Most all had the rather snide
remark "if you guys had asked me last year".

Recent work by the Chamber has centered around business attractions activities.
To mention a few, the new sales ring, a new veterinarian and TV repairman were
all Chamber initiated ideas as was the promotion of the P.C.A. office.

That brings us to the present time. The Chamber has expanded its efforts to
maintain a community of dignity and pride in all phases of human and civic
challenge. They will continue to operate in the area of calculated risk, and as
it was in the beginning, there will always be a few that will drag their heels,
but their ranks are getting thinner all the time.

        1949 Fire Chief Jim Stoppler and son Jimmy

        E. Grosz Fire Chief, R. Aanderud Secretary-Treasurer, Bob Easton

A DESTRUCTIVE FIRE

Six business houses totally destroyed by fire early Thursday morning.

Six of the best business houses in Beulah are in ruins as the result of a fire
which originated in some unknown manner in the Beulah Garage on Thursday
morning about 3:30 o'clock. The fire was first seen by Joe Evans, who sounded
the alarm.

The fire spread rapidly to the adjoining buildings and Mrs. D. M. Sovereign and
son, who were sleeping at the rear of the Palm Theatre barely escaped with
their lives. Mr. and Mrs. W. H. Sovereign who were sleeping in the
confectionary had a very narrow escape. Fortunately the wind subsided and by
the heroic work of the fire fighters, the W. A. Johnson Hardware Store was
saved and the fire prevented from crossing the street, where the roofs of the
buildings were caught on fire many times by flying embers.

West from the garage the fire crept slowly as wind was in that direction. The
Moen and Faust building was consumed without any apparent check to the fire, as
was the Kilber Pool Hall, but in the latter building most of the stock and
fixtures were saved. The next building, the Sailer Butcher Shop was nearly
saved but it finally burst into flames and was destroyed, and the fire was not
stopped in its westward course until it had reached the building occupied by
Dr. Neukamp's office.

The estimated net loss after deducting insurance is: W. H. Sovereign $2000, D.
M. Sovereign $3000, Beulah Garage $5000, Moen and Faust $2000, M. Kilber $1500,
and Jacob Sailer $4000, for a total loss of $17,500.

The above excerpt from the September 13, 1918 Beulah Independent, was no doubt
the beginning of the Beulah Volunteer Fire Department. Following a fire of the
magnitude of this, the local business men and insurance salesmen began thinking
in terms of fire protection. The business men's prime worry was to prevent and
offset losses, while the insurance men's problem was the sale of insurance to
cover these losses. The sale of insurance was near impossible because of the
prohibitive rates due to the lack of fire protection.

To solve this problem, L. F. Temme a local insurance representative along with:
Fritz Schnitker, Chris Moen, J. M. Faust, Jacob Heihn, Geo. Slowey, L. G. Rose,
P. C. Kenney, John Preston, Leo Crystal, Ted Muhlhauser, S. E. Colby, Ed Genz,
G. C. Poe, Jacob Bitterman, E. F. Munro, F. O. Gentz, A. D. Brown, Fred Luckow,
and J. C. Evans organized the first Beulah Volunteer Fire Department on August
13, 1920. Fritz Schnitker served as the department's first chief, with E. F.
Munro assistant chief, and L. F. Temme Secretary-treasurer. Their first
equipment consisted of two hand drawn two wheel chemical carts.

The department fought their first fire in the film room of the Princess Theatre
in February 1921. The damage from the fire was small, but it did point out the
earnestness of the organization. At a meeting following the fire, the members
responding to the call were paid $1.00 and the ones failing to answer the alarm
were fined $1.00. However it seems some of the members that missed the fire
also missed the meeting. To solve this problem the Marshall was sent out to
bring in the culprit, and since his excuse wasn't sufficient, he paid the fine.
This manner of assuring attendance at fires was continued until 1923.

The department holds its annual fire inspection during fire prevention week
early in the fall. During some of the early inspection reports we find the
mention of: prairie chimneys, uncovered haystacks, kerosene stoves, and coal
sheds. These terms tend to remind us just how long ago the department was
organized.

The first motorized equipment acquired by the department was a 1929 Ford truck.
This served them until 1949, when a new Ford unit complete with all the latest
equipment was purchased. The first electric siren was installed in 1933,
followed by the latest civil defense approved siren in 1963. 1952 saw the
acquisition of the latest in grappling equipment for lake rescue work. In 1953
the purchase of a Pnealator breathing device, and in 1960 Scott Air Pack rescue
equipment.

        One of the tipple Fires

Over a period of years the department has fought some spectacular fires, to
mention a few: Johnson home, Coupland Garage, two Knife River Coal Mining
Company tipples, Shilling Pool Hall, John Frisco home, Buschbacker Blacksmith
Shop, Princess Theatre, Arrow Creamery, and Klaudt Garage at Hazen.

The following have served as chiefs: Fritz Schnitker, Ted Muhlhauser, A. D.
Brown, Irv Niedermeyer, John Smith, C. L. Foster, Jim Stoppler, Fred Galloway,
and Erhardt Grosz.

The following are honorary members: L. F. Temme with 43 years of service, Ed
Moe 35 years, Mark Sexton 33 years, Manley Thompson 31 years, and Jim Stoppler
27 years.

The members serve without compensation. The department is financed by an
insurance tax refund from the state, and proceeds from an annual Fireman's Ball.

The custom of serving a dutch lunch at regular monthly meetings was begun, with
the repeal of prohibition, in 1933. Active members of the department and years
of service are: Bill Unruh 22 years, Erhardt Grosz 20 years, Fred Galloway 20
years, Sam Miller 18 years, Joe Muhlhauser 18 years, Robert Easton 14 years,
Adam Keller 14 years, Ed Morman 14 years, Albert Link 12 years, Bud Pulver 11
years, Lloyd Erickson 10 years, Herb Scherbinske 10 years, Glenn Wilmes 9
years, Joe Klein 8 years, Ted Voegele 6 years, Rueben Sackman 6 years, Helmuth
Hilz 6 years, Dennis Kirchmeier 4 years, Bill Krecklau 4 years, Wilmar Weidrich
4 years, Rob Jesswein 4 years, Mervyn Singer 3 years, Theophil Renk 3 years,
Richard Tesky 2 years, Evan Dschaak 2 years, Eldon Grishkowsky 2 years, Richard
Aanderud 2 years, Darold Benz 2 years, Harold Heine 2 years, and Ernie
Ohlhauser 1 year.

Cliff Foster, Jim Stoppler, Fred Galloway, Joe Muhlhauser, L. F. Temme, and Ed
Moe hold life memberships in the State Firemen's Association.

BEULAH RURAL FIRE DEPARTMENT

        Pictured above are the present officers, directors, and firemen of the
        Beulah Rural Fire Department. First row: Herb Scherbinske Vice
        President and Chief, Otto Reich, Edward Unruh, Carl Herman, Phillip
        Becker, Fred Boeshans, Joe Sebastian. Second row: Dan Gunsch, John
        Gunsch, Bill Heth President, Edwin Renner, Christ Zuern, Ervin
        Eisenbeis. Top row: Julius Orth Secretary-Treasurer, Delvin Reich, Otto
        Kessler, Andrew Sailer Assistant Chief, Lester Blohm, Christ Sailer. Not
        pictured: Jake Sailer, Ivan Keller, Eugene Keller, Myron Hafner, and
        Ferdinand Boehler. The Beulah Rural Fire Protection District, a tax
        supported organization, was formed in 1961 to furnish fire protection
        for a rural area consisting of an area from the Garrison Reservoir to
        12 miles south of Beulah and 6 miles east and west of Beulah. The
        original officers were: Ralph Smith President, Herb Scherbinske Vice
        President, and Julius Orth Secretary-Treasurer. Their first equipment,
        a 1962 Dodge Four Wheel Drive truck, complete with all the latest fire
        fighting equipment, was received on December 28, 1961. This equipment
        is housed in the City Hall at Beulah. It is delivered to fires by any
        of these 6 drivers who are members of the Beulah City Fire Department:
        Dennis Kirchmeier, Edward Morman, Ted Voegele, Fred Galloway, Richard
        Teske, and Herb Scherbinske. One director is elected from each Township
        and he appoints one fireman from his Township, these two men are
        responsible for recruiting the necessary help to man the truck and
        fight a fire when the truck arrives in their area.

        BEULAH COMMUNITY NURSING HOME

The Beulah Community Nursing Home became a reality because of an idea brought
up and studied by the board of Directors of the Joachim Memorial Home. The
original officers appointed to serve until the first annual meeting were,
President Ed Schulz; Secretary-Treasurer, Helmuth Hilz. This community
organization was incorporated February 25, 1960. A loan was received from the
Bank of North Dakota and Bonds were sold to raise money to purchase the
existing Beulah Community Hospital building. The building was bought in April,
1961. It was completely remodeled and furnished and opened as a twenty-two bed
Nursing Home June 26, 1962.

Eight residents were admitted the first day, of these eight five are still
living in the Home. Nursing Home has served thirty-six individuals since
opening. Ten of these have passed away and three have transferred to other
homes.

The first annual meeting was held February 6, 1962. At this meeting the
following board was elected; Ed Schulz, President; H. R. Engbrecht, Vice
President; Sam Miller, Fred Galloway and Fred Flemmer as Directors.

At present the Nursing Home is completely filled and has a list of people
waiting to make their home there.

The present board consists of the following; Ed Schulz, President; H. R.
Engbrecht, Vice President; Sam Miller, Fred Galloway and Carl Hilz as
Directors; Mrs. John Hyttinen, Sec-Treasurer; Ray Hagen, Legal advisor; Mrs.
John Schmierer, Marton and Rev. John Schmierer, Supt. and Chaplain.

        The Beulah Community Nursing Home is proud of it's bright and cheerful
        "dayroom", which is equipped with T.V., Radio and Phonograph. Here
        patients who are still able to be moved can get together and enjoy the
        fellowship of each other's company. Many people stop by to pass the
        time of day and welcome having a "homey" room such as this in which to
        visit.

THE REV. JOACHIM MEMORIAL HOME

The problem of taking care of our senior citizens, by providing a home for the
aged, had long been recognized by many of the people in the surrounding area;
but nothing was being done. The inspiration of taking immediate action was born
when Rev. August Hoeger was a guest at the Rudolph Schnaidt home. Before
anything definite could be done, Rudolph and Rev. Hoeger checked out the
possibility of finding a suitable dwelling place. They decided that the Albert
Heiser residence would be ideal. It was one of the larger homes in Beulah (in
fact it was the first house ever built in Beulah) and was within walking
distance of the churches and business district of town. Rev. Hoeger began
making the necessary arrangements for the purchase of this home.

At one of the first meetings (some of the local businessmen and
representatives from the various Lutheran Churches were in attendance) it was
decided that a suitable name had to be chosen. A. O. Totdahl made the
suggestion that it be named in memory of the late Rev. S. Joachim (he was the
local Lutheran pastor who had died earlier that same year and who had endeared
himself in the hearts of many of our local people). This name was agreeable to
everyone; thus the name of the Rev. Joachim Memorial Home was chosen.

At this meeting the first functioning board was selected. They were as follows;

Rev. J. Schmierer, President
Rev. H. J. Dirks, Vice President
Rev. Edward Goetz, Secretary
Rudolph Schnaidt, Treasurer

Fred Reinhardt, Director
Mr. Jacob Lang, Director
Attorney T. A. Sailer, Legal Advisor

A soliciting campaign was inaugurated. Through the fine cooperation of Beulah
business men and the Lutheran Churches in the tri-county area (many donations
came in from all parts of the state) the full purchasing price was paid and
enough money was made available to fully equip the home. Due to war-tume
shortages there were many difficulties encountered in purchasing some of the
necessary items; therefore the opening of the home was delayed until March 2,
1945. The first workers to serve the home and it's residents were; Miss Rose
Kiesz and Esther Lindemann of Golden Valley, North Dakota.

The home opened with six patients. Soon it was filled to overflowing (13
patients) and the space became more limited. As the waiting list grew longer,
the board realized that either the home had to be remodeled to accommodate more
people or a larger one had to be built. They decided to build, but to remain on
the same location. They rented the old Hillcrest Hospital building to serve as
a temporary home for their "charges" and proceeded in the removal of the frame
house. Now work on the new, fire-proof brick building began. The board at this
time was; Fred K. Flemmer, President; Rev. Theilen, V. Pres.; John Wolff,
Emanuel Heth, E. C. Isaack--Directors; Albert Wolf, sec. and Rudolph Schnaidt,
Treas. and Joe Muhlhauser, Building fund, treas.

It was a gratifying experience to watch the people from every denomination and
walk of life "pitch in" and give of themselves. They donated hours upon hours
of manual labor (such as; painting, varnishing, laying floor tile, sewing
drapes, scrubbing and many other menial tasks). The brick layers laid bricks at
regular wages for five days of the week and on the sixth day they donated a
days labor. Even some of the more able-bodied residents of the home helped
where-ever they could. All of this donated labor and the generous gifts from
friends of this entire area made this dream a reality. Credit should also be
given to the board who spent many hours planning--how to cut costs; how to
build; and the general supervision of the project so that nothing was wasted.
These combined efforts of the board and donated labor kept the cost down to a
minimum--$38,000.00. This amount added to the value of land, donated material
and labor, brought the value of the home up to $60,000.00.

On December 10, 1951 special Dedication Services were held at the Zion Lutheran
Church and at the Home--Speakers were; Rev. J. Schmierer of Delmont, S. Dak.
and Rev. August Hoeger of Fargo.

The Managers (or house parents) of the home at the time it was opened were Mr.
and Mrs. Albert Link (Rose Kiesz). They continued operating the home until Mrs.
Link's ill health forced their resignation. The managers of the home to the
present time have been: Mr. and Mrs. Andrew Froescher, Mr. and Mrs. Alex
Schulz; Mr. and Mrs. Erwin Mettler; and Rev. and Mrs. J. Schmierer (presently
employed).

The Rev. Joachim Memorial Home has a 33 bed capacity. Besides Rev. and Mrs. J.
Schmierer it employs six people. It has served as a "home for the aged" to
people of all denominations.

From the very beginning it has been an independently incorporated institution.
It is governed by a Board of Directors and the only church affiliation that it
has is: "that in order to serve as a member of the Board of Directors one has
to be a member in good standing of the Lutheran Church (any synod).

It provides a wholesome Christian atmosphere, where if anyone desires a
spiritual and religious uplifting they may attend the services and daily
devotions conducted at the home; or if they so desire they can have a pastor of
their own choice come in and administer their spiritual needs and desires.

With the opening of the doors to this Christian establishment, our older
citizens can look forward to their "sunset years" with a peace of mind in the
knowledge that here is a home--where they are always welcome; where they can
find the companionship of people their own age; and where their physical as
well as spiritual needs are well provided for. This has been the aim and
purpose of the home in the past and shall remain the aim and purpose of the
home in the future.

The board that serves the Joachim Memorial Home at the present is: Ed Schulz,
President; Herbert R. Engbrecht, V. president; Helmuth Hilz, Secretary; Rudolph
Schnaidt, Treas. Raymond Hagen, Legal Advisor; The directors are; Carl Merkel,
Calvin Hafner, Walter Hafner and Raymond Mohl; Mrs. J. Schmierer, Matron and
Rev. J. Schmierer, Supt. and Chaplain.

The people (other than those previously mentioned) who have served the Home in
various capacities on the Board of Directors over the past years are: Rev. G.
Busch, Rev. Heber, Rev. A. B. Solberg, Rev. Doege, Albert Hoffmann, Rev. John
Kammerer, Ed. Moe, Leo Hafner, Henry Singer, Paul Goetz, Gottfried Raszler,
Rev. Doyen, Ernest Herrmann, John Malke, Roland Unterseher, Robert Boeckel, Sam
Miller, Gottlieb Zeller, and Fred Galloway.

        The Joachim Memorial Home's Dining Room, here Daily Devotions are held
        for anyone who desires Spiritual-nurture.

        Coroner; Delivered Mercer County's only triplets: Grant Edmund
        McLaughlin, Gordon James and Gerald Keith. Ordinarily triplets occur

MONTANA-DAKOTA UTILITIES CO. HISTORY OF THE BEULAH ELECTRIC POWER PLANT

Montana-Dakota Utilities Co. has served Beulah with electric power since 1945.
It now serves approximately 78,600 electric and 104,150 gas customers in North
Dakota, South Dakota, Montana, Wyoming and Minnesota. In addition, the company
has substantial nonutility interests, including lignite coal and oil. Knife
River Coal Mining Company is owned by Montana-Dakota Utilities Co.

MDU operates more than 2,821 miles of electric transmission lines and 2,142
miles of gas transmission lines to serve 239 communities; employs approximately
1,575 people. The company's ten steam power plants (including the Beulah
plant), two diesel installations and one gas turbine installation are designed
to use locally produced fuels. Expenditures for fuel are presently more than
$1,900,000 annually.

The Beulah plant was built in 1925 at which time two reciprocating steam
engines were installed. One of these engines was a 150 kw "Ridgeway" and the
other a 250 kw "Lentz." Both "Ridgeway" and "Lentz" are famous old-time names
in the steam power business. These initially installed units were supplied
steam from three 100 horse-power horizontal return tubular boilers.

The reciprocating engines remained in service until 1927 when they were
replaced with a 2500 kw General Electric condensing steam turbine. At the same
time the turbine was installed, a Sterling type 750 horsepower Babcock & Wilcox
water tube boiler fired with a Harrington chain grate stoker was installed.

During 1928, the three horizontal return tubular boilers were replaced with
another Sterling 750 horsepower boiler, also built by Babcock & Wilcox.
Coincident with this installation, a 3500 kw General Electric turbine generator
set was placed in service.

In 1937, the plant was again expanded with the addition of a 750 horsepower
Riley water tube boiler fired with a Harrington chain grate stoker, giving the
plant a total generating capacity of 6000 kw and 2250 horsepower boiler
capacity. In 1937, this rated as a rather large lignite burning power plant.

In 1949, Stearns--Rogers Manufacturing Company, engineers and contractors of
Denver, Colorado, constructed a new addition to the plant which is called
Dakota Station. This section of the plant was built under an agreement between
Montana-Dakota Utilities Co. and the Dakota Electric Cooperative, Inc., a
generating and transmission organization made up of 14 REA Cooperatives in
North and South Dakota. The plant provides generating capacity for the
cooperatives as well as for MDU. The Dakota station features a 7500 kw General
Electric turbine and two 60,000 lb. per hr. B & W boilers equipped with Detroit
stokers. Plant efficiency was substantially raised by adapting to lignite
burning procedures pressures of 400 lbs. per sq. in. and steam temperatures of
750 F. This brought the total plant nameplate capacity to 13,500 kw. It should
be noted, however, that the Beulah plant is capable of "peaking operation"
substantially above this figure.

The water supply for the Beulah plant comes from deep wells and from the Knife
River, which is about 1/2 mile south of the plant. However, the Knife River
does not have a consistent and adequate flow capable of supplying the power
plant's cooling requirements. Consequently, one of the major and most
interesting features of the plant is a cooling pond and tower installation
located south of the plant building.

During the early years of operation the cooling pond served the plant
requirements without towers by spraying the discharge water into the air for
cooling after which it would fall back into the pond for recycling in the
system. This cooling pond with its multiple fountains was a beautiful sight
winter or summer but was wasteful of water because the strong North Dakota
winds frequently blew much of the spray water out of the cooling pond. To
correct this problem, "Marley" induced draft cooling towers, constructed of
redwood, were installed. In these towers, the water is pumped over the top
platform, falls through the tower in a finely divided spray while induced draft
fans produce a counter flow of air to provide the cooling effect. As in the
case of the cooling pond operation, the water is recycled. Evaporation tends to
concentrate the dissolved solids in the water, making it necessary to use
stabilizing chemicals for the prevention of corrosion and the discouraging of
scale formations in the plant heat exchangers (condensers).

The treatment of both boiler feedwater and cooling water in the power plant
cycle is a complex technical process so the plant is equipped with a small,
efficient laboratory which provides test results to guide treatment decisions.

Fuel for the Beulah plant is supplied by Knife River Coal Mining Company by
means of a conveyer belt extending from the North Beulah mine tipple direct to
the plant. This nearby fuel supply results in economies in the generation of
electricity which makes the Beulah plant economically competitive with some of
the newest, largest and most efficient MDU Co. stations at other major load
centers.

At the present time, the Beulah plant employs 23 men under the supervision of
chief engineer, Lester H. Bowers. Mr. Bowers has been an employee of
predecessor companies and MDU Co. since 1924. He was one of the first men to
work on the construction of the original plant. Mr. Bowers is assisted by J. H.
(Jack) Fiergola, an employee in this plant since 1929. Richard W. Aanderud is
station results engineer, responsible for plant efficiencies, control system
and technical assistance to plant supervisors. Lawrence Joos is station
statistician and clerk; the other employees are directly concerned with the
very interesting work of maintaining and actually operating the plant.

The Beulah town office has four employees, headed by Don Wetzel, district
supervisor. The others are Leonard (Bud) Pulver and Duane Stickney, linemen,
and Janice Welk, Cashier.

The Beulah plant employees are very proud of their record of 2,723 days without
a lost time accident, and the high degree of efficiency and reliability the
plant contributes to the MDU Co. Electric system operations.

MERCER COUNTY EXTENSION SERVICE

During the half century the agricultural extension service of the North Dakota
State University has had a big impact on our community. It has brought new
ideas into our homes, farms and community, it has offered new challenges to our
people both in town and in rural areas. Much of the change and progress in our
agriculture was motivated by the county extension agent and his associates.

Mercer County first became acquainted with extension work shortly after the
First World War through Miss Elsie Stark. Miss Stark was Home Demonstration
Agent in Mercer County from September 1, 1917, to July 1, 1919.

Grover C. Poe was the first Agricultural Agent elected in the county. The
Beulah Commercial Club sponsored petitions to secure an Agricultural Agent's
services. The voting public desired to obtain these benefits offered, and on
June 16, 1920, Mr. Poe was appointed. Mr. Poe's office was located in Beulah.

Mr. Poe's program emphasized diversified farming. Mr. Poe's motto was "Hogs and
Hominy, Cows and Clover" and as many early cooperators added, "It took Poe to
put it over." He was County Extension Agent from June, 1920, to December, 1924,
and from February, 1924, to September, 1926.

In 1933 at the start of the Federal Farm Programs Omar K. Cline was appointed
agent. The agent's office was then located in the court house at Stanton. He
worked mostly in organizing and administering the early federal farm programs.
Mr. Cline's tenure in office was from August 12, 1933, to August 31, 1935.
During the period of his service, he was assisted by William F. Cockburn during
the cattle purchasing program. Mr. Cockburn worked from September 1, 1934, to
October 17, 1934, and from December 10-20, 1934. "Bill" moved to Killdeer and
worked as the Dunn County Agent for the next 25 years.

Upon the resignation of Mr. Cline, Robert J. Montgomery was appointed to the
office. Mr. Montgomery did a large amount of work in organizing 4-H clubs and
homemakers clubs. Mr. Montgomery served the county from October 1, 1935, to
August 12, 1937.

Mr. Montgomery resigned his position to serve with the Soil Conservation
Service. Donald R. Groom was then appointed. During this period the work of the
county agent was tied quite closely to the AAA Program. Mr. Groom held office
until April 15, 1940, when Melvin J. Berdahl became agent.

Mr. Berdahl was active with 4-H clubs and homemakers clubs. Mr. Berdahl was
also charged with the responsibility of many of the War Emergency Committees. A
reserve officer, Mr. Berdahl was ordered to active duty in April, 1942, and was
replaced by Harold C. Schultz. It was during Berdahl's tenure that his office
and the AAA office was moved from Stanton to Beulah. Mr. Schultz was present in
the county only a short time before he also entered the service and was
relieved by Lawrence Restemeyer. Lawrence Restemeyer was agent from the spring
of 1942 until August 1, 1944, when he resigned to accept a position with the
Soil Conservation Service.

Due to the shortage of trained personnel the Mercer County Extension Agent's
position was open until December 1, 1946, when Wayne W. Owens assumed the
position.

Mr. Owens' work during his short stay centered around the reorganization of 4-H
and homemakers club work. He was also responsible for the initial work of Soil
Conservation Districts and Rural Electrification.

Thomas Martindale took over in 1949 and stayed until 1954. Tom was the first
agent who had stayed long enough to become acquainted in the county. He
completed the post war adjustment work dealing with REA and soil Conservation
districts and did much educational work for the rural telephone service.
Martindale also was responsible for the early leg work in the establishment of
the Fort Clark Irrigation District.

H. R. "Mac" McKenzie was appointed agent in May of 1954 and has served until
the present time. A strong advocate of diversified agriculture "Mac" pushed
livestock programs--especially livestock feeding. Feedlots springing up all
over the county are good evidence that the "push" has been successful.
Livestock quality programs, such as Dairy Herd Improvement Association,
artificial insemination program, beef performance testing and calfhood
vaccination programs, have all started in the past ten years.

Livestock programming is also evident in the county's 4-H program. Mercer
County livestock judging teams have consistently placed toward the top
statewide. Four-H teams have won the Valley City Winter Show contest twice and
have been in the state judging finals at Fargo five different years. In 1963
Mercer County's dairy judging team won the state championship and entered the
national event at Waterloo, Iowa.

        Mercer County's first wheat check. Henry C. Loy of Stanton holds the
        first government wheat check ever received in Mercer County. This
        picture was taken outside the county courthouse on February 2, 1934.
        This was a happy day in a mighty tough year.

MERCER COUNTY EXTENSION SERVICE

        Ben Janssen's Irrigation System. Back in 1936 R. J. Montgomery and Ben
        Janssen of Stanton worked out an irrigation system. The water was
        taken from the Knife River with a 12 inch pump powered by a 70 HP
        steam engine shown here. The water was run into laterals and spread on
        the fields.

        Grasshopper Control--1937 Style. Emil Rathjen of Stanton built the
        contraption shown here and in four days of operation caught 450
        gallons of grasshoppers.

        The Bait Station. Some 10 box cars of sawdust, 34 tons of bran, and 98
        barrels of sodium arsenite were mixed at this station. Some 20,000
        acres of cropland were sprayed under the direction of the county
        agent.

        Testing Work. Fertilizer test work conducted in the Beulah area in
        1956. This drill was used on small replicated plots on the Ernest
        Fandrich, Ed Schulz and Walter Hafner farms.
