        German Day--June 1st, 1916

MERCER COUNTY FAIR

First mention of a Fair by name was made at the July 21, 1915 meeting of the
first Beulah Commercial Club. It was approved as a project under the title
"First Annual Fair and Carnival." However, at the August 4 meeting the name of
the forthcoming Fair was changed to Mercer County Fair. Subsequently a Fair
Association was formed. The Commercial Club of Beulah, whereas it felt the Fair
Association should pay the paint bill for buildings, did authorize the
expenditure of $50.00 for street decorations for the second Fair in 1916.

The "Fair" again became a project of the Commercial Club in April of 1922, when
the reorganized Commercial Club looked into the Fair records and elected a four
man Fair Board, (L. G. Rose, Geo. Slowey, M. P. Morre and L. F. Temme) from its
members.

The Fairgrounds were located south of the N. P. railroad tracks and west of
First Avenue West.

These fall Fairs were of great interest to the surrounding community. Farm
families came in for the whole show. One county business man relates that there
was a high wooden fence surrounding the grounds and there was a "knot hole gang.

Under County Agent G. C. Poe's supervision, farm boys could take part in the
Farm Boys Encampment at the Fair. Besides seeing the fair, the boys had the
opportunity to attend lessons and lectures.

Adding much to the hustle and bustle at Fair time was the temporary encampment
of Indians from the Fort Berthold Indian Reservation. They were permitted to
come to the Fairs and rodeos by the Indian Agency, on the condition that the
town would give the Indians -a beef, one sack of sugar and two sacks of flour.

As has been told elsewhere in this book in the Rodeo Story, the roots of the
Cowboy's Reunion are in this Mercer County Fair.

The Fair Association sold its property and discontinued the Fair in 1925.

        Grover C. Poe First Mercer County Agent

        Grand Entry--1963

COWBOY'S REUNION

The roots of the Beulah Rodeo (since 1932 known as the "Cowboy's Reunion") go
way back to the time in 1914, when Grover Poe was Mercer County Extension
Agent, and helped organize the Mercer County Fair Association.

For the first Fair in 1915--located on land south of the railroad tracks--
they did have one "wild bull" ride. This fair on the fairgrounds, with a
grandstand just north of the present Billie Vincent house; the livestock
exhibit barns to the southeast; a race track south of the grandstand and the
fair buildings north to the railroad tracks, had among its exhibits a Shorthorn
bull owned by Johnny Maier. Frank Chase of the Fort Berthold Indian Reservation
offered to ride the animal, which he did. This feat thrilled the crowd. They
passed a hat and the $30.00 collected were given to Chase. This was the first
rodeo event in Beulah.

In the following years of 1916, 17 and 18, there were more rodeo events at Fair
time, although they were not sponsored by the Fair Association.

Quoting from an article written by Ed Boland: One day during the month of
August, I was mowing along the creek when A. D. Brown (former editor of the
Beulah Independent) and George Slowey came driving across the flat in a shiny
new contraption. They stated their mission flatly--"Could we stage a rodeo at
Beulah the following month?" We sat in the shade of the shiny monster and
endeavored to separate the possible from the impossible. When they left some
two hours later, the foundation had been laid for the now famous "Cowboy's
Reunion". At any rate, on the following Sunday, Roy Robinson, Oran Abbey, John
Maier and myself chartered a Model T and journeyed up to the old HS horse
ranch, operated by Gordon Schaffner and made arrangements for a string of
bucking horses. A deal was also made for corral lumber, with the Beulah lumber
yards, on a rental basis. Broken lumber was to be paid for, and a graduated
charge to be made for nail holes. Transporting stock by truck was yet to be
heard about in those days, so all stock was trailed in.

Among the top hands who took part in that first show were: George Defender,
Howard Wanna, Max Siegel, Ed and Pete Carmichael, Packineau boys, Billy Welton,
Chet Smith, Jack Nagel, Frank Two Bears, Ben Jones and Charlie Blake.

Among the famous bucking horses of that yesteryear were Rattlesnake, Blizzard,
Jail-Bird, Screw-Driver, White Sox, Whirligig, Tailspin, Warbonnet and Raggin'
Peggy. All had an undying hatred for cowboys, and could be relied upon to kick
up plenty dust when their turn came to swing into action.

The cattle for these early rodeos were from the local Maier, Boland, Murray and
Keogh ranches. The rodeo featured a quadrille--among members.

In 1925 the Fair Association discontinued the Fair--sold its property--paid
off its debts and with the balance of its money purchased the 80 acres
comprising the present Beulah Park. The rodeo, with Ed Boland arena director,
had already moved to this location. A good race track was one of its features.
The Cowboys and Indians who participated in the yearly rodeo (a 3 day affair
usually held about July 4) camped in the park area with their strings of
horses. Ticket sales opened at 10 A.M.

The races were a special attraction. Red Olson, Ben Bird and Schatz brought
their fine horses regularly. Glenn Murray and Paul Keogh were local
participants. There were 7/8, 1/4 and 1/2 mile races, pony races, saddle horse
races, pack-up-and-go races, women's races, relay races and wild horse races.

After Red Olson passed away, the following Rodeo had, in memory of him, one of
the most loyal rodeo race entrants, a riderless horse make the circuit of the
race track, while Ed Boland read a tribute to this colorful rancher and racing
enthusiast.

Bucking stock was secured from the Crowleys, Sam Rhodes and others, besides
those already mentioned.

At one time the local group purchased Killdeer Mt. area horses and pastured
them in the Medicine Butte country. This proved costly and was discontinued.
Sam Rhodes and the Kenyon boys each took a turn at putting on the rodeo.

There was usually some extra attraction, such as a clown act, (Billie Vincent
was a local clown), wild cow milking contests, the saddle and bareback riding
and calf roping events.

For many years, Tom Keogh, Johnny Maier and Ted Albers judged the riding, and
for more years, Bill Koester was the fence repair man.

        Smiley Burnett and Rodeo Assn. Wives

Among the best known early pick-up men were Pete and Ed Carmichael--later Mug
Murray and Walt Keogh were regular in that position.

A saddle was awarded the best all-around cowboy. The cowboys in these earlier
days wore chaps and put rosin on the seats of their jeans, so they'd be more
"adhesive."

The big parade preceding the rodeo was a regular feature.

In 1939 the Rodeo moved up on the hill. National and North Dakota names riding
here at that time included Jim and Tom Tescher, Dean Armstrong, Alvin Nelson,
Gene McCormick and Duane Howard.

In the original purchase of the park, there was a clause forbidding sale of
tickets to enter the park, and for this reason it became necessary to move the
rodeo, in order to charge admission. The land purchased at this time by the
Commercial Club, Park Board and Beulah Country Club, added to the original park
site the acres now used as Rodeo grounds and the Golf Course.

The 1940's saw the organization by farmers, ranchers and businessmen into a
Rodeo Association to carry on this tradition. The men in the group put in
$100.00 apiece to finance the rodeo. During the war years there was no rodeo.
In 1945 the Cowboy's Reunion was revived. Ralph Murray continued as arena
director and Ed Boland became the local announcer, to be followed by Nathan
Little Soldier, Al Buchli, Doug Dettmann and Mac McKenzie.

In 1947 the first Fettig horses were used. In the early 1950's the Cowboy's
Reunion became an RCA event. The clowns became professionals. One year Smiley
Burnette of Hollywood fame was a featured attraction.

In 1956 the Chamber of Commerce took charge of the Cowboy's Reunion. Since 1960
Fettig's have been in full charge of the rodeo--bring in all their own stock,
including the Brahma bulls, unknown to early rodeos.

The old grandstand was destroyed by a windstorm in 1960. This necessitated
rebuilding, and since the grounds had a decided slope, which was an extra
hazard in roping and racing, it was decided to revamp the whole grounds. With
the cooperation of ranchers, farmers and townspeople, donating their labor, the
present excellent grounds were ready for use in 1961.

THE NORTH DAKOTA HIGH SCHOOL CHAMPIONSHIP RODEO

One of Beulah's proudest achievements has been the establishment and
continuation of the High School Championship Rodeo. It didn't start out to be a
big affair back in 1950, but like "topsy" it just grew.

The first junior rodeo in 1950 was probably the wildest, most dangerous rodeo
ever held in Beulah's long rodeo history. Some members from V.F.W. Post hastily
drew up plans for the event, did a little advertising and secured permission
from the old cowboys' reunion rodeo committee to use the rodeo grounds. The
night before the rodeo a small band of "in town" cowboys met on the lower
segment of the Fort Berthold Indian Reservation to round up a few horses for
the rodeo. Along about morning, after a merry chase in the dark over the
uncharted areas of the reservation, enough horses were cornered for the rodeo.
Nathan Little Soldier, who was a member of both the V.F.W. Post and the
reservation, was the "ramrod" of this group of midnight rustlers. They weren't
really rustling though as most of the horses were unbroken stock carrying
Nathan's brand.

The next day the horses performed beautifully,except they had no realization of
the purposes of a fence. Time and time again the wild, surly broncs would
unleash their full force against fences and gates and literally smash
everything in sight. While this might seem to be a pretty dangerous business,
it was actually dangerous for the horses only, as the young cowboys had been
"unseated" in most cases with the first few bounds out of the chute.

During the next winter Nathan Little Soldier reported that a new group had
formed a National High School Rodeo Association. The V.F.W. Post joined and in
1951 had the first official State High School Championship Rodeo, The going
wasn't easy in the first years. The national association was a rather loose
knit affair and things were a bit on the "helter-skelter" side. The North
Dakota cowboys seemed to lack the contest experience as the North Dakota
champions who ventured into national competition were generally pretty badly
outclassed.

Then in 1953 things started to happen. The contest procedures of rodeo were
tightened up. The caliber of the young cowboys moved up and North Dakota rodeo
fans started to take note of the happenings.

A willowy young cowboy by the name of Pete Fredericks from Halliday started
things roDing. At the national event in Underwood, South Dakota, in 1953, he
literally put North Dakota on the map with a championship effort in bareback. A
couple of years later "Pete" again put North Dakota on the map this time with a
championship effort in the saddle and a good enough showing in bareback and
calf roping to be named national all-around champion. "Pete" cut his teeth in
the big old Beulah arena. Now he is nationally famous as a consistent winner in
the saddle and bareback events.

Along about the same time a stocky little fellow by the name of Don Rehm came
out of Hebron to capture the national high school championship in bull dogging.
This is a timed event, but no record times are ever established because of the
difference in arenas. It is noteworthy that no one, irrespective of conditions,
has ever equalled Don's time of 4.8 seconds in the dogging event in high school
competition.

While the young cowboys brought fame to the state, the V.F.W. Post suffered
financial losses on every rodeo. Many bingo games and fund raising drives were
held to pay for the event. Bigger towns in the west river rodeo country began
to express interest in wanting to buy out the project. The V.F.W. Post, while
tempted to give it up, felt that they owed it to the originators of the event
and to those who had worked so hard down through the years to continue the
event in Beulah.

In 1956 one of the consistent high school performers, Dale Little Soldier was
named as the typical young cowboy of America and won the Bob Crosby Award. The
award carried nation-wide publicity and Dale, too, helped put Beulah on the map.

In 1959 North Dakota produced a national roping champion. He was Sonny Ehr
hailing out of Minot. Sonny went on to be one of the best collegiate ropers in
the business.

North Dakota cowgirls have always done well in the national event. Highest
honors went to Sharon Burkhardt of Medora who won the national breakaway roping
crown in 1958.

In 1964 the Herman C. Schmidt Post 7759 will sponsor the 15th Annual North
Dakota High School Rodeo during the 50th anniversary of Beulah. The post's
contribution to establishing Beulah as the rodeo capital of North Dakota is
well known throughout the rodeo circuit. This will be the first two-day show in
the history of the North Dakota championships. Early indications are that some
150 young hopefuls from all parts of North Dakota will be in Beulah to vie for
state honors.

EARLY MINING DAYS

September 13, 1923--The year of the labor strike. The strike took on a more
violent nature when the windows of one of the local miner's house were broken
during the night. None of the family (which was sleeping) was hurt. Another
miner was "roughed up" a bit on main street. The United Mine Workers of America
took a dim view of such "goings on" and took immediate action to prevent any
more acts of violence.

THE FIRST MINING ACCIDENT

        David Hettich

This community was shocked Monday forenoon that David Hettich an employee of
Knife River Coal Mine had been killed while working in the mine as a skinner.
He had gone to work as usual that morning and about 9 o'clock his lifeless body
was found wedged in between a derailed car and the rib of the mine. The body
was discovered by Theron Davidson, a miner, who at once summoned help and
released him. His body was badly crushed and it is thought that death must have
resulted instantly.

He had been employed at the mine for the past 5 years and was one of their most
steady and conscientious employees. He was well liked by everyone, and his
passing has cast a gloom over the entire community.

His body was prepared for burial by undertaker Chilson and on Tuesday morning
his remains were shipped to his former home in Wishek for interment. Short
funeral services were conducted here before the train left, by Rev. Joachim,
Pastor of the Lutheran Church, of which the deceased was a member.

David Hettich was born in Russia and was 24 years, 1 month and 13 days old. He
came to the United States with his parents and located hear Wishek when he was
only a baby, and made his home in that community until coming to Beulah. He is
survived by his father, 4 sisters and 2 brothers. One sister, Miss Katie
Hettich, arrived in Beulah Monday evening to accompany the remains to their
home. The body was also accompanied by David Flemmer and Reinhold Kemmet, from
South of Beulah, who are cousins of the deceased, and by Fred Huber, of this
city who represented the Beulah miners. The deceased was also a cousin of Fred
K. Flemmer of this city.

This is the first fatal accident at the Beulah Mine and it seems almost
impossible that the life of such a splendid fellow as David Hettich had been
sacrificed. There never was a nobler, cleaner, better young fellow than Dave
and his friends were only numbered by the extent of his acquaintances.

Our hearts are indeed sad at the passing of this worthy neighbor and we wish to
join with all the citizens of this community in extending heartfelt sympathy to
his sorrowing relatives.

The Beulah Mine can be proud of their record of only 2 fatal accidents in all
the years of operation.

The following article was taken from the June 21, 1928 edition of the Beulah
Independent.

Sponsored in memory of David Hettich by Mr. and Mrs. F. K. Flemmer.

Unruh Garage had it's beginning in Zap in 1929. They began handling the J. I.
Case line of farm machinery in 1935. John Unruh moved his business to Beulah in
1949.

HISTORY OF THE AMERICAN LEGION

        The Members on the picture from left to right: Louis F. Temme, Post
        Adjutant; Albert C. Link, Sergeant-at-arms, holding American flag;
        Fred Welk, Post Chaplain, holding American flag and American Legion
        Emblem, standing behind Post Charter of 52 names; Paul Goehring,
        member, holding Legion Emblem; Nick Hertz, Vice Post Commander.

The American Legion in Beulah, was organized by Louis F. Temme, by petition on
October 8, 1919 with ten World War I veterans signing. They were L. F. Temme,
P. J. Janski, H. E. Benson, C. L. Foster, Fred Tesky, Edward Adolf, O. I.
Chadwick, John Klaudt, Clayton Malaise, and L. B. Williams. At this first
meeting F. O. Gentz, J. H. Bossen, John Giese, H. E. Snortland, Gust Long, J.
B. Crowe, D. I. Wolf, Emil Karlin, Theron I. Davidson, Steve Davidson, Theodor
J. Hafner, August Keller and Dan Huber also joined the organization. They
agreed that the Post should adopt the name Argonne in memory of our Comrades in
Arms who made the supreme sacrifice at the Battle of the Argonne Forest in
France. A temporary charter was issued to the Legion--The Argonne Post No. 85
on November 5, 1919. The permanent charter was issued August 20, 1920. There
were 52 names listed on the permanent charter.

The first years of the War, daisies were sold on Memorial Day in memory of
veterans. The Legion also places flags on the graves of our area cemeteries of
all the departed veterans.

The Beulah Legion Post, belonged to Oliver, Mercer and Dunn County Veterans
Organization, (existed from 1921-1931) Beulah Legion Post has in Card Book
Form, "The Stars and Stripes" printed in France, the first issue on February
8, 1918 and the last issue on June 13, 1919.

On January 11, 1934, the Legion bought their present hall from the Beulah
School District No. 27. The Legion was incorporated in 1941, as a non-profit
organization. During World War II the Argonne Post sold $14,000.00 in U. S.
Bonds series E and $3,000.00 in G Bonds. Both were in actual cash. The Beulah
Legion has always worked for the interest of all veterans and for the education
of our children. In order to familiarize them with our form of government the
Legion organized the North Dakota Boys State. The local Legion takes part in
this endeavor.

The following members have served as Commander of the Argonne Post: Louis F.
Temme, Sam Frank, E. M. Moe, Frank Uhler, Joe Runions, Rev. S. Joachim, Paul
Roberts, Nick Hertz, Eugene Crandall, C. L. Foster, Joe Lackwood, Fred Welk,
Anton Wolf, Albert C. Link, Andrew Sailer, Albert Zeller, George Peiffer, and
Anton S. Schall.

The 1963-1964 membership is: Louis F. Temme, Hilbert D. Kastrow, Fred Welk,
Anton J. Schaff, Anton Loning, Fred Hauff, N. J. Joyce, Adam Rueb, Albert
Zeller, John Hyttimen, Leonard R. Krausz, Otto Kasper, Monroe Miller, L. C.
Misslin, Gust Morlock, Christian Flemmer, E. A. Kanthack, C. L. Stickney,
Theodore L. Herrmann, Richard Feddersen, Wilmar R. Sayler, Cecil W. Smith,
Wilhelm T. Walz, Walter E. Haas, Nick Hertz, Paul C. Goehring, Emanuel Teske,
Ralph Smith, Paul Renner, August Keller, Nick Schumacher, R. F. Schwede, Albert
Froeschle, Ruben E. Sackman, Quentin Seifert, Otto H. Heihn, Albert C. Link,
Kenneth H. Schulz, Albert Pischel and Alvin Weil.

1963-1964 officers are Anton J. Schaff, Commander, Nick Hertz Vice Commander,
Louis F. Temme, Adjutant, Ruben E. Sackman, Finance Officer, Fred Welk,
Chaplain, Anton Loning, Historian, Louis F. Temme, Service Officer and Albert
Link, Sergeant at Arms.

THE AMERICAN LEGION AUXILIARY

The Beulah Unit of The American Legion Auxiliary was attached to Argonne Post
number Eighty-five Beulah, N. Dak. The Unit was organized and its permanent
Charter issued in June 1928. There are eighteen names typewritten on the
Charter as follows: Mrs. Anna Gentz, Mrs. Martha Scholz, Mrs. Eugene Foster,
Miss Erna Potratz, Mrs. Clifford Foster, Mrs. S. Joachim, Mrs. Joe Runions,
Mrs. Lydia Boettcher, Mrs. Bertha Crowe, Mrs. Theresa Moe, Mrs. Anna
Niedermeyer, Mrs. Orla Christiansen, Mrs. Theron Davidson, Mrs. Gerhard Helvik,
Miss Amber Davidson, Mrs. Geo. Davidson, Mrs. Christina Peterson, and Mrs.
Clayton L. Malaise.

When the charter was issued there were two Gold Star Mothers. They were Mrs.
Lucy Joos and Mrs. Wm. Rettke each of whom had lost a son among the poppies of
World War I.

Mrs. Anna Gentz was the Units first president and later became chairman of N.
Dak. Dept. of Community Service and Unit Activities.

Besides Mrs. Gentz the following women served as presidents during the Units
existence: Mrs. Martha Scholz, Mrs. Rose Hertz, Mrs. Ida Helvik, Mrs. Olga
Joachim, Mrs. Helga Temme, Mrs. Mary Buscher, Miss Amber Davidson, Mrs. Mary
Held, Mrs. Ann Foster, Mrs. Marie Stickney, Mrs. Anne Liebelt and Mrs. Anne
Niedermeyer. Mrs. Niedermeyer also served as Seventh District President and
later served as N. Dak. Dept. Poppy Chairman. She also served as Dept. Chairman
for Child Welfare Education, Community Service, Constitution Bylaws and
Legislation.

Being an organization of service, its outstanding activities were National
Defense, Americanism, Rehabilitation and Child Welfare. It assisted veteran's
children with medical, dental and clothing needs. It contacted Senators in
regard to war widows' and orphans' allowance and veterans' committee bills. It
brought cheer with gifts to veterans at hospitals, distributed food baskets and
gifts to their widows and children at Christmas as well as at other times.

The Unit sponsored pre-school clinics, contests in the schools such as the
following: Americanism Essays, Poppy Poster Contests, Flag Code Quiz' and Fidac
Essay Contests. It sponsored Red Cross, March of Dimes for Polio, Cancer, and
Heart Fund raising drives. It sold the little red poppy made by veterans in the
hospitals, the proceeds of which were used exclusively for relief and
rehabilitation of veterans and their families. The poppy is a patriotic symbol
which means honor for the dead, and service for the living.

The Unit organized and sponsored the local Girl Scout and Brownie Patrol. It
sent a boy or boys to Boys State at Wahpeton and later at the University of N.
Dak. for a weeks training in the conducting of city, county and state
government. Later the Dept. instituted Girls State and the Unit sponsored girls
for similar training.

The American Legion and its Auxiliary realize the imperative need to combat the
Red menace which threatens the over throw of our government and the destruction
of the principles upon which the republic is founded.

The moral influence which permeates every community having an Auxiliary is of
great value in instilling the duties of citzenship, love of country, and a
reverence for the flag into the hearts and minds of every boy and girl.

The Auxiliary is the largest member of the Fidac Auxiliary, and its chief
supporter in its program. Fidac is an international organization composed of
thirty-six associations of ex-service men of the world wars. These are grouped
in ten allied countries, endeavoring to develop better understanding and to
stimulate good will among all the people of the allied world.

The Unit received recognition for meritorious service from the Dept. with the
presentation of twenty-three citations, fifteen of which have the gold star,
rating the group as a superior Unit. The Unit also received eleven citations in
Americanism, besides eight National Citations for meritorious service in
carrying forward the National Program of the American Legion Auxiliary. The
Legion and Auxiliary received recognition from the National Treasury Dept. for
having sold $7000.00 worth of War Bonds in a period of two months. This money
to be used toward the purchase of four Super-Fortress Bombers. The Dept. had
pledged to raise the money for that purpose.

Financial responsibilities were met by presenting Home Talent Plays, food
sales, dinners, rummage sales, donations as well as by other means. The Charter
was turned into the State in the fall of 1956.

OLIVER-MERCER ELECTRIC COOPERATIVE. INC.

The origin of this cooperative stems back to February 23, 1945, at which time
an REA mass meeting was held. An REA Field Representative gave a short talk and
explained what REA is for, how distribution cooperatives are organized and
their function. It was moved that a rural electric cooperative should be formed
to serve the counties of Oliver and Mercer. Nine members were elected as
incorporators and they were: H. H. Henke, Wm. Van Oosting, Philip Maier, Nels
Porsborg, E. A. Bohrer, Rudolf Miller, Wm. Raab, Albert Hoffmann and Adolf
Raszler.

On June 20, 1948, the first farm was energized. The farm is in Oliver County
and is owned by Siguard Lovald. Through the years the Oliver-Mercer Electric
Cooperative has built many miles of distribution lines to serve their members.
At the present time The Oliver-Mercer Electric is serving approximately 1410
consumers and has some 1310 miles of line.

The directors and officers of the cooperative at the present time are as
follows: Albert Bauman, President of the Board; Dale Ford, Vice-President; H.
H. Henke, one of the original incorporators of the Oliver-Mercer Electric,
Secretary-Treasurer; Edward Orgaard, Bill Heth, Fred Kuch, Eldor Rasch, Ernest
Isaak, and Herbert Hafner.

Managers of the cooperative were Frank Belinskey, Richard Gabe, Miles Handy,
and Kenneth Ervin, who is presently serving as Manager.

APLIN

Before Beulah originated "Aplin" was known as a postoffice for the early
settlers in the vicinity south of Beulah. Mail coming through New Salem and
Hannover came to Nisbet, a place about nine miles northeast of the Aplin
school-house. The settlers took turns bringing the mail from there to the Aplin
homestead on Section 20, Township 143 North, Range 87 West in Springvale
district. This is located east of the Carl Helman farm.

Henry Clay Aplin and his wife (Lillian Kraus) came to N. Dak. in 1906 with the
Emigs and other relatives from Knobnoster, Mo. to homestead in this locality.
He was a mail carrier in Missouri prior to coming here. So he wrote to
Washington D. C. and explained to them the need of a postoffice here. They gave
him directions how and what to do to get it started. So one room of their house
was used as the postoffice until it was changed to Mrs. Stephens house located
where the Greenvale school is now. She also had a store at her home and a
church on her land. There was a school nearby.

As early as 1907 some of these pioneers were receiving mail addressed directly
to Aplin, N. Dak. Mail also arrived at its destination here in the Aplin
Community when it bore the address, Evans, N. Dak. though this was somewhat
later. The Evans postoffice was located on Section 7 Township 142 North, Range
87 West in Burt district where Mr. and Mrs. Ted Kusler now reside.

BOY SCOUTING IN BEULAH

        Top Row--Left to right: Jim Williams, Duane Seibert, Harvey Johnston,
        Joe Muhlhauser, Ervin Bates, Harold Bates, Howard Erbele. Middle
        Row--Left to right: Bill Schutz, Leo Erbele, Andy Karlin, Ray Mulhauser,
        LaVada Webber, Dale Rassmusson, Irvin Schmid, Norman Iverson. Front
        Row--Left to right: Neil Smith, Lloyd Iverson, Gus Younker, Francis
        Gilchrist, Paul Isaak.

The Boy Scout Program in Beulah has been organized and reorganized many times
in Beulah. The first being in April 1929. The committee selected to look into
the possibility of a local scout program was: L. G. Rose, A. D. Brown, and Rev.
S. Joachim. Rev. C. G. Elinger was the Scoutmaster and Johnnie Lofthouse the
assistant.

The Scouts who passed their tenderfoot test on June 6, 1929 and thus becoming
Beulah's first scouts were: Harvey Kees, Ivan Iverson, Harry Lautermilch,
Orville Chilson, Clair Kearns, Lyle Wagonveldt, Bertram Heinemeyer, James
Moore, Herbert Kittler, Lonnie Tysver, Irvin Bates, Lester Sigman, Elmer Bates,
Walter Teske, Ralph Sigman, Lester Roessler, Monroe Crystal, Osborne Herman,
Wm. Keller.

After scouting had begun to slacken in about 1933, it was reorganized with the
Beulah Lions Club being the sponsor. The scoutmaster was Bert Webber. At the
time there were 38 boys in the troop. The troop committee was Ted Herman, Ed
Moe, Fred Gentz, Carl Thelander, L. F. Temme and Jmm Stoppler. At this time the
Drum and Bugle corps was started. There were about 35 boys and one Drum Major
(Gale David) in the corps. Some of the members of this group that was known
throughout the state were: Lowell Kees, Irvin Becker, Alan Klaudt, Crawford
David, Art David, Ralph Shirley, Paul Klein, Bryce Tysver, Harvey Johnston,
Adam Sardotz, Joe Muhlhauser, Ray Muhlhauser, Harold Bates, Sherril Bates,
Norman Iverson, Lloyd Iverson, Howard Erbele, Leo Erbele, Jim Williams, Dale
Rassmusson, Delton Mounts, Frank Perkins, Celon Peterson, Elroy Herman, Irvin
Schmid, Irvin Renz, Hubert Renden, Jess Thompson, Garvin Murray, Wayne Murray
and Irvin Bates.

Local Scout Boosters in Beulah helped take care of the merit badge
advancements. Matt Mounts and the Knife River Coal Mine did many favors and
helped considerably in the advancement of Scouting in Beulah.

In 1935 with the help of the WPA the new Scout Hall was built on the hill west
of the mine offices. That land was donated by the Knife River Coal Co. The
building was 30x40 with a stone fireplace on one end a kitchen, store room plus
a large meeting room. The community was proud of this group of boys. Most of
these boys were in the Armed Services during World War II, and many of them
advanced to being officers and leaders of men.

BOY SCOUT TROOP 19

After Bert Webber resigned as Scoutmaster in 1938, B. Aandrud took over for a
while and then Nels Husom became the Scoutmaster for the next eight years. In
1917 Rev. Tyler Kriedeman took over the responsibility of the troop until he
moved in 1954. Edgar Goodman was the Scoutmaster for six years until Darold
Bentz, the present Scoutmaster, became the head of Troop 19.

One would like to make mention of all of the bovs who have belonged to the
local Scouting Program through the last twenty-five years, but the list would
run well over 200 names and space does not allow that. Even though their names
are not mentioned, we know that Scouting has meant much to the Youth of Beulah.

During the last ten years the Beulah Scout Troop 19 has actively participated
in the activities of the Missouri Valley Council. They have won a blue ribbon
award every year that they have participated in the District Scout-o-rama.
Every year they come home from the Heart Butte Scout Camp with the "Honor
Campers" award. They have five outings annually (which amounts to about 24 days
of outdoor activity). They also participate in several service projects. One
project such as: They picked up all the beer cans from the highways and side
roads. The net result--that two truckloads of litter were picked up.

The present Scout Committee is Ed Morman, Herman Hildebrand, Emil Wolf, Gus
Morlock. The Scoutmaster Darold Benz and Assistant Scoutmaster, Marvin
Schnaidt. There are 48 boys in Troop 19 at the present time.

THE JOE RUNION STORY

V. P. Wolf, 29, Mandan Fieldman for the Bank of North Dakota and Joseph Runion
47, Beulah, Mercer County Deputy Sheriff were shot by Fred Sinerius six miles
west of Beulah, Tuesday April 1, 1941.

The shooting took place at 2:30 P.M. at the Thompson farm, home of Bennie
Sinerius, son of Fred Sinerius, with whom he had been making his home since
being evicted from his farm now owned by the Bank of North Dakota.

The two men went to where Sinerius lived to look for some storm windows,
screendoors and lumber taken by Sinerius from his farm, which he had lost to
the Bank of North Dakota. The men argued some and began looking in an
out-building where they spied some of the sought for items. Meanwhile Sinerius
grabbed a 410 gauge shot gun and shot both men, hitting Runion in the face.
Both men began to run. Sinerius returned to the house returning with a 25-20
rifle. He shot Wolf-who dropped dead. Runion dropped to the ground and feigned
death until Sinerius went back into the house. Then he crawled to a coulee from
there going to the Ray Beich farm. Mrs. Beich took him by car to where her
brother Mr. Geise was working. Geise then brought Runion to the Beulah Hospital
where he was given first-aid and taken on to a Bismarck Hospital, where he died
at noon Wednesday.

Tuesday evening County Coroner, H. O. Chilson and Beulah Marshall Manley
Thompson fully armed went out to the home of Sinerius. He came out and when
they said they were looking for a dead man he pointed to the coulee. They found
Mr. Wolf first, where he had dropped. Without any resistance Sinerius came to
town with them. He signed a confession before Mercer County Sheriff Otto
Poschadel and Mercer County States Attorney, Floyd B. Sperry. He was taken to
Mandan before Judge H. L. Berry and F. M. McCurdy was secured as attorney for
the plaintiff. During a Special Term of Court Sinerius was charged with first
degree murder and sentenced to life imprisonment.

Joseph Runion was born in Wadena, Minnesota, October 2, 1893. In 1920 he
married Marie Arntz and moved to South Dakota. In 1924 he came to Beulah and
for many years operated the Runion Garage and more recently was manager of the
Hauser Garage. He served as County Deputy Sheriff for eleven years. Served in
World War I and was a member of the Beulah American Legion Post.

He is survived by his wife and a daughter Marion. One son preceded him in death
in 1938.

Funeral services were held in the Beulah Theatre, conducted by Rev. Father
Herman Schweitzer of St. Joseph's Catholic Church of Beulah. The Church Choir
sang. Services at the cemetery was conducted by the American Legion Post.

V. P. Wolf was survived by 4 children and a widowed mother. His father had died
about ten days before. Note: the above article was taken from the April 3
edition  of the Beulah Independent.

Mrs. Runion and daughter Marion still live in Beulah in the small house built
for her.

      BEULAH LIONS CLUB

The Beulah Lions Club was organized under the auspices of the Bismarck Lions
Club.

On May 12, 1930, the Beulah Lions Club received its charter from the
International Association of Lions Clubs. The Charter members are: Otto
Boeckel, service station; E. P. Boland, farmer and rancher; A. D. Brown,
publisher--Beulah Independent; T. A. Carignan, Knife River Coal Mine Co.; E. S.
Coupland, garage; J. B. Field, machine dealer; F. O. Gentz, banker; Dr. H. J.
Halbeisen, dentist; Theo. P. Herman, druggist; W. M. Lane, Service station;
Frank E. Marshall, lumber and car dealer; E. M. Moe, tailor and cleaner; L. A.
Peterson, meat market; Frank Pitzer, merchant; Dr. F. P. Rasmusson, Medical
doctor; Arnold Renden, merchant; L. G. Rose, hardware dealer; M. W. Sexton, N.
P. Agent; Louis F. Temme, Real Estate and Insurance and W. K. Wilson, attorney.

Dr. F. P. Rasmusson was first president and W. K. Wilson first secretary.

The Lions Club is a weekly luncheon club. Since its organization in May of
1930, it has met on Monday noon. The noon meeting does not interfere with other
civic organizations meetings.

A major contribution to the community has been its long sponsorship of the Boy
Scouts. It has also sent Beulah boys to Boy's State. The Lions Club has always
been road minded. There has always been a road committee working for better
roads for Beulah.

The International Association of Lions Clubs, is the largest civic organization
in the world.

The following members have served as president: Dr. F. P. Rasmusson, George
Kadlec, Waldo M. Lane, E. M. Moe, Theo. P. Herman, Louis F. Temme, R. T.
Hamilton, W. H. Esterly, C. A. Thelander, Joe Charvat, Rev. S. Joachim, M. P.
Moore, Bernard Mahowald, Dr. Ralph Vinje, Fred Galloway, J. E. Padilla, R. A.
Blumhardt, Herman Hildebrand, J. B. Field, Fred Riedel, H. N. Dally, Lester H.
Bowers, A. O. Totdahl, Douglas Dettmann, Emanuel Liebelt Jr., Anthony Wolf, Dr.
B. A. Girard, O. A. Carlson, Lynn Aasen, Donald Wetzel and Herbert Engbrecht.

THE BEULAH MINERS An era of semi-pro ball in North Dakota

        The Beulah Miners--about 1935
        Standing--Frank Pitzer, Joe Stewart, Schroeder, Lefty Kemp, Carl
        Wessels, Ole Thronson, F. O. Gentz. Front Row Anderson, Malloy, Sam F.
        Kerbs, Bert Webber.

BATTER UP! was a familiar cry in these parts during the early 1930's. Beulah
could almost have been called the Baseball Capital of Western North Dakota, for
it was at this time that the town supported a semi-pro baseball team. The
managers would begin organizing their teams and staging benefits to help
support these teams as early as February and March. These teams would play
quite a heavy schedule, sometimes three and four games a week.

1931

The Beulah Miners semi-pro team started in 1930-31. Bill Draffehn was the
manager and Frank Pitzer, Matt Mounts and John Erbele were the business
managers. The first team roster was as follows: Pitchers, Ken Olsen and Harold
Viestenz; Catcher, Ernie Folden, First base E. Carmichael; Second base, Dr.
Rassmusson and Charlie Geil, Third base, Bennie Knauss, Short-stop, Larry
Lawrence; Lee Perkins, R. Field; Bob Easton, C. Field; and Viestenz, L. Field
when not on the mound; Wally Hamilton, S. Kerbs, Ump Heihn were also used in
the out-field. This team played all the semi-pro teams around the midwest and
as far north as Winnipeg, Canada, and won the State Title.

1932

After having won the 1931 N. D. State Championship the managers were determined
to have a State Champion Team for a second time, so new players were hired by
the management. Two new pitchers that were imported were, Red Haroldson from
the Northern League and Lefty Martin from the eastern part of the state.
Webber, from Minnesota, was hired to do the catching. Midway in the season the
team got another shot in the arm, when the Miners hired Frank Stewart an
ex-major leaguer from Minneapolis as pitcher and his brother Joe Stewart came
to play 3rd base. The team was again one of the best in the state. They played
all the semi-pro teams from Dickinson, Bismarck, Jamestown, Minot and all other
major North Dakota semi-pro teams. The summary at the end of the year was; Out
of 45 games played they lost 9 for a percentage of .800. The batting average of
the entire team was .308. The three top batting averages were held by: Ole
Thronson--.485, Webber--.434, and Frank Stewart--.400. The 1932 line-up was;
Ole Thronson, Kerbs, Lefty Martin, Umpy Heihn, Viestenz, F. Stewart, Joe
Stewart, B. Webber, E. Carmichael, L. Rassmusson, Larry Lawrence, Charley Geil,
Lee Perkins, Hal Herman and Robert Easton.

In 1932 Beulah had a lighted Baseball diamond--one of the first in the State of
North Dakota. It was a "home engineered" contraption and many times not the
most satisfactory, but it was a step in the right direction. This is probably
as good a place as any to mention the many times forgotten "Heroes" of
baseball--the umpires--the local umpires who faithfully worked the games during
these years were Father Bauer (a local Catholic priest) and Christ Fuerst.

1933

In 1933 Roy Seibert, an "ex-three-eye" league player was the new manager (Roy
had managed the team once before). This team proved to be one of the strongest
in the state. Lefty Kemp, Frank Stewart, Harold Viestenz, and Joe Stewart doing
the pitching; Webber as catcher, Carmichael at 1st, Eddie Fogel at S. S.,
Thronson on 2nd, Joe Stewart at 3rd and on the outfield--Kerbs, Martin, Heihn,
and Weir.

Again the team played all semi-pro teams and won a greater share of the games.
Good baseball was in demand and crowds were in attendance at every game.
Traveling teams such as the House of David, and the Kansas City Monarchs were
great crowd pleasers. The teams from Dickinson, Bismarck, Williston and
Jamestown were getting stronger as the years summary showed. Of the games
played; Beulah won 25 and lost 15, tied 2 for a percentage of .625. The team's
batting average at the end of the season was .313. The top three batters were
Frank Stewart--.448, Thronson .360 and Joe Stewart--.347.

1934

Roy Seibert was manager, Frank Pitzer and Matt Mounts were the business
managers. New players this year were Malloy, Carl and Dutch Wessels, Larry
Lawrence, Thronson, Carmichael, Kerbs played their regular positions; Kemp,
Frank and Joe Stewart and Martin doing the pitching and Webber and Wessels the
catching.

Competition was becoming much keener as Bismarck's manager, Neil Churchill
imported more new blood for their team and the other N. D. towns were adding
strength to their lineups.

This was the year when only one player in the regular Beulah lineup was a local
boy--Ed Carmichael. All of the others came from Minnesota and Iowa.

1935

The new imported players this year were; Morty Schroeder and Anderson (catcher)
from Iowa, Lefty Schwenk came from the Western League. J. Stewart, Schwenk,
Martin and Kemp took care of the pitching duties, Webber, Anderson, and Wessels
did the catching. Carl Wessel at 1st, Ole Thronson at 2nd, Stewart at Short,
Webber at 3rd, Malloy, Kerbs, Martin in the outfield.

The team was winning most of their games and had won 3 from Bismarck when
Bismarck came up with Satchel Paige, Quincy Troupe and other players from the
American Association. The Miners lost 3 games to Paige and the Bismarck Team
won the North Dakota State Title. Paige was one of the greatest ball players
ever seen at Beulah. He beat some of the best traveling teams on the road
besides some of the best in the United States. Bismarck won the National
Semi-pro Championship in six straight games with Paige doing their pitching.

The Beulah Miners were noted all over the midwest for their fine ball teams and
the fine crowds from the Beulah area and surrounding towns were always at their
games. At Lemmon, S. Dak. 10,000 baseball fans watched the Kansas City Monarchs
beat the Miners in a 13 inning game as Joe Stewart pitched a five-hitter. The
fans will never forget such pitchers as Red Haroldson, Lefty Kemp, Frank and
Joe Stewart, Harold Viestenz and players such as Malloy, Kerbs, Thronson,
Carmichael, Lawrence, Wessels, and Webber.

In 1936, Baseball teams showed a decrease in games and shortly most of the big
teams started to play in the small leagues in the state. The Beulah Miners
started to play Amateur ball in the North Slope league. Bismarck and Dickinson
followed suit. Semi-pro baseball was slowing down and now the smaller new
amateur teams had a chance to play ball.

Several of the old players have passed away; Malloy, Thronson, Roy Seibert,
Carl Wessels, Lefty Kemp, and Lefty Martin to name a few. All were good ball
players and showed an excellent quality of sportsmanship.

Also to be remembered as promoters in starting and continuing the baseball
teams are Matt Mounts and Frank Pitzer. They spent much of their time and money
to give the fans in Beulah and surrounding areas such a fine brand of baseball.
We owe much to another avid baseball fan, A.D. Brown, whose colorful accounts
of the games added much to spark the enthusiasm of the local sport fans.
Through his accounts of the games and information compiled by Bert Webber of
Sidney, Montana, we hope we have been able to give an accurate account of
Beulah when BASEBALL WAS KING.

THE BEULAH COUNTRY CLUB

        Top Row--Left to right: Gordon Eider, Jim Kanthack, Sam Miller, Jack
        Fiergola, George Koppi. Middle Row--Left to right: Curtis Schulz,
        Dr. J. A. Power, Ed Morman, Ray Binder, Jake Renke. Bottom Row--Left
        to right: Erhardt Grosz, Bob Easton, Glen King, Jim Stoppler, Lyman
        Morley, and Clarence Schneider.

Early in the 1920's a group of Beulah citizens recognized the need for some
additional wholesome outdoor recreation. Some of the local gentry had played
golf, liked it, and started to scout around for an appropriate piece of land.
They did find the ideal spot just 1 1/2 miles south of town. It was state owned
land and was leased by Johnnie Maier. They entered into a verbal agreement with
Johnnie; that part of his leased land could be used for a golf course. They
would be able to use it in the summer and Johnnie would run his cattle on it
during the winter.

During the Summer of 1926, 5 holes were constructed on the West side of the
present Golf Course adjacent to State Highway #49, (at the present time these
are numbered 1, 2, 3, 4, and 9.) Approximately 15 men took part in this venture
in order to give the city of Beulah a golf course. This was School land and was
purchased from the State of North Dakota in 1944. At the time of purchase, the
City of Beulah, City Park Board and the Beulah Country Club went into a joint
partnership. The park board retains permanent jurisdiction of the land. Four
new holes have been added to the original 5. These holes were constructed on
the East side of the coulee and were numbered 5, 6, 7, and 8. With the
completion of the 9 holes in 1944, The Annual Beulah Invitational Tournament
was initiated. The greens were constructed in the following manner--the sod
was removed to a depth of 10 inches, approximately 40 feet in diameter. Clay
was filled in at a depth of 6 inches, the remaining 4 inches with sand. A high
grade of used oil was sprayed on the sand, and raked thoroughly. Due to the
construction of the greens and the natural topography of the land, the Beulah
Golf Course is considered by many, as one of the finest sand green courses in
the state of North Dakota.

In 1954 they purchased from Knife River Coal Mining Co. a building which at the
present time serves as our Club House. The following year, a screened porch was
added on the East, North and West sides of the club house. Foot bridges on
fairway No. 9 and fairway No. 5 spanning the coulee were constructed. An
extensive tree planting campaign has been started and plans are to plant more
in the future.

Our yearly membership ranges from 50 to 65 members or families. Some of the
events are; Ladies Day every Wednesday afternoon, social nite every other
Friday nite, and occasional local club tournament (mixed doubles and one ball),
and the "high lite" of the season is the Men's Annual Invitational Tournament
in either July or August. This tournament attracts golfers from various parts
of the State.

The following men and their respective families were members during the 1963
season--Curtis Schulz, John Hyttinen, Glen King, Bank of Beulah, Lyman Morley,
J. H. Fiergola, Clarence Schneider, Glen Wilmes, Reuben Krein, Raymond Hagen,
R. W. Easton, Sam Miller Jr., Carl Thelander, Forrest Stetson, William Unruh,
Otto Kasper, Dr. L. C. Misslin, Albert Zeller, Dr. J. A. Power, Mike Held,
Erhardt Grosz, Joe Stewart, George Koppi, Jake Renke, Leonard (Bud) Pulver, Jim
Kanthack, Ray Binder, Ed Morman, Helmuth Hilz, Lester Bauer, Al Hanson, Floyd
Richardson, Eddie Krieger, Albert Lorenz, Herbert J. Wolf, Lester Blohm, Lynn
Aasen, H. C. Sigman.

The following non-resident members maintained their membership during the 1963
season: Bernie Aarthun, Hazen, North Dakota; Randall Fretty, Stanton, North
Dakota; A. J. Wittmaier, Bismarck, No. Dak.; James Stoppler, Hazen, North
Dakota; Don Wilmes, Glen Ullin, North Dakota; Ted Blaski, Minot, North Dakota;
Harold Unterscher, Stanton, North Dakota and John Steffan, Halliday, North
Dakota.

LADY GOLFERS

        Back Row--Left to right: Mrs. C. Thelander, Mrs. J. Renke, Mrs. C.
        Schneider, Mrs. J. Power, Mrs. F. Stetson, Mrs. R. Easton, Front Row
        --Left to right: Mrs. L. Aasen, Mrs. G. Wilmes, Mrs. G. Koppi, Mrs. J.
        Fiergola, Mrs. J. Stewart, Mrs. A T. Lorenz.

Bill Unruh, eldest son of Mr. and the late Mrs. John Unruh, was born on a farm
in southwest Mercer County, October 12, 1912. He moved to Beulah with his
parents April 17, 1920 and has made it his home ever since. He married the
former Naomi David in 1937. They have one son, Milton. He graduated from Beulah
High School in 1957; spent four years in the Air Force, and now makes his home
in Minot, N. D. where he is employed at the J. C. Penney Co. Milton is married
to the former Rosalie Sailer, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Gotthold Sailer of
Beulah. They have three sons. Paul, age six, Tim, age four and Carter born Sept
27, 1963.

When Bill was a school boy and a young man, he worked as a clerk for Leo
Crystal, Morris Frank, L. G. Rose in the Rose Hardware and Implement and for
Frank Marshall in the Marshall-Malaise Hardware and Lumber. In 1938 when the
Marshall-Malaise hardware stock was sold to A. W. Klick, Bill went to work for
"Beulah Hardware." In 1942 he became a partner in the store and later the
owner. In 1957 the hardware stock was sold out. Mr. Unruh has operated since as
Beulah Plumbing & Heating. He became a licensed Master Plumber in 1941 and for
the past 23 years has served the community in that capacity.

JUNE 1916 ASCENSION DAY CELEBRATION

People came from 50 to 65 miles area. Most of them had to start at 5:30 or 6:00
to come to town. All went to their respective churches and then after church
the band played and led the entire group to the Fair Grounds Grandstand, which
held 1500 people and it was filled. Here political speeches were given. Pres.
of the German Club urged allegiance to U. S. and no longer to the Kaiser. State
Railroad Commission spoke on the growth of the area. H. L. Berry from Stanton
was main speaker. It was an all day affair.

ARROW CREAMERY

The Beulah Creamery was purchased in the fall of 1939 from the receiver. Edwin
Krause was the first operator. The following year we started pasteurizing and
bottling milk.

Our first equipment consisted of a 50 gallon vat and hand bottling. After a few
months of operation we found it necessary to increase the size of our equipment
and a 100 gallon vat was installed, followed by a 200 gallon vat. The plant
operation then consisted of buying cream and pasteurizing some 300 gallons of
milk a day.

In June of 1942, Krause left us to accept a position of operator of the Mott
Creamery. After Mr. Krause left, Erhardt Grosz then took over as manager. He
still holds the position.

On the 25th of Sept., 1948, the Creamery building burned to the ground all
except for about 20 feet of the front, which was patched up and used for a
buying station until 1960 when the present building was constructed.

BEULAH FUTURE FARMERS OF AMERICA

        Row 1: Vernon Christman (reporter), Levi Krein (sec.), Leo Schwarz
          (treas.) Mr. Lynn Aasen (advisor), Darrell Herman (pres.) Delmar
          Voegele (v-pres.) LeRoy Winkler (sentinel).
        Row 2: Rodney Sailer, Merlin Binder (officer at-large), Martin
          Schwarz, Ronald Raszler, Gaylen Sailer, Maynard Blohm, Timothy
          Davidson, Lon Schlecht.
        Row 3: Garner Sailer, Otto Raszler, Neal Sebastian (parliamentarian),
          David Hafner, Sidney Sailer, Clyde Frank, Rick Strom, Wayne Boeshans,
          Duane Boeshans.

The first FFA Charter was organized in Beulah High School in December 1950
after the addition of the Vocational Agricultural department.

There were thirty-four Charter Members and the first officers were:

President ____________________________ Lawrence Voegele
V-president __________________________ Gerald Kusler
Secretary ____________________________ Kenneth Meske
Treasurer ____________________________ Dale Strom
Reporter _____________________________ Ivan Zeller
Sentinel _____________________________ Bernard Keller
Parliamentarian ______________________ Eugene Schulz
Historian ____________________________ Clarence Jaeger
Librarian ____________________________ Harold Link
Officer-at-large _____________________ Duane Stickney
Advisor ______________________________ Mr. Clemens Brigl

Two advisors have served this Chapter since its beginning Mr. Clemens Brigl
from 1950 to 1955 and Mr. Aasen, the present advisor joined the Beulah Faculty
in 1955.

The primary aim of the Future Farmers of America is the development of
agricultural leadership, cooperation and citizenship. The FFA colors are
National blue and corn gold. Meetings are held the first and third Thursday of
each month.

The Chapter sponsors activities that will increase the size and quality of the
farming program for members. Beulah Chapter has sponsored a revolving Hog,
Barley and Dairy project.

Helping support worthwhile charities is a community service through which
members are able to do their part. Beulah Chapter contributes to Children's
Village, March of Dimes and N. Dak. Society for crippled children. Money was
raised by special projects to purchase a television set for the Beulah Memorial
Nursing Home.

Beulah has had the distinction of having one of its members receive the
American Farmer's Degree. Leland Erickson was chosen as American Farmer in
1962. Leland and Mr. Aasen attended the Awards Banquet held at Kansas City in
October, 1962.

Fred Boeshans Jr., Floyd Unruh and Francis Pulver have received their State
Farmer's Degrees.

SPRING CREEK 4-H CLUB

        Reinhold Raeszler. Ronald Raeszler, Virgil Voegele, Randy Voegele,
        Earl Schmid and Irvin Schmid

In 1957, a group of boys playing in the school yard, impressed their teacher,
Miss Mary L. Cox, as having a super abundance of energy and enthusiasm.
Thinking that their talents could be put to profitable and enjoyable use, Miss
Cox suggested a 4-H club be organized. With the assistance of tke Co. Agent,
Henry (Mac) McKenzie, a group of young people and their parents assembled in
the school house 3 1/2 miles west of Beulah--known as School #l of Spring Creek
School Dist.--on Oct. 1, 1957.

The first officers of the club and their respective offices were: Gordon Mohl,
president; Hilmer Mohl, V. pres.; Glenn Sailer, sec.; Ivan Schmid, treas.;
Dwight Mohl, news reporter. Other members joining the club during its first
year were Robert Keogh, Jr. and Norbert Helm. Irvin Schmid and Ervin Mohl were
selected as club leaders. Other past and present members are Brooks Keogh,
Claire Keogh, Faye Keogh, Lonnie Lorenz, Dale Morman, Patrick O'Neil, Larry
Pfenning, Ronald Raeszler, Randy Voegele and Virgil Voegele.

In 1958 Gordon Mohl won the district R.E.A. sponsored demonstration event and
attended 4-H Achievement Institute in Fargo, as a result of this victory. Glenn
Sailer and Ivan Schmid earned the livestock award trip to the Tri-State
Futurity at Miles City, Mont. in November.

Faye Keogh received the coveted belt buckle awarded for horsemanship in 1959.
Spring Creek 4-H club also won the county R.E.A. demonstration event and placed
second in the district with Ivan Schmid's demonstration on the use of soldering
equipment.

Brooks Keogh, a beginner in the club, pulled top honors in the horsemanship
contest on 4-H Achievement days in 1960. Ivan Schmid was a member of the Mercer
Co. 4-H livestock judging team which placed third in the district. He also
earned a medal for being top sheep judge of the day.

In 1961 Ivan Schmid was chosen to represent Mercer Co. in the district R.E.A.
demonstration event. The title of his demonstration was "Packing front wheel
bearings." He also attended 4-H achievement Institute and was named a member of
the county livestock judging team, which placed third in district competition.
First place in the 4-H achievement days junior livestock judging contest went
to Earl Schmid.

Robert Keogh, Jr. was a contestant in the R.E.A. demonstration contest and won
the horsemanship contest at 4-H achievement days in 1962. The county livestock
judging team placed second in district competition and included Ivan Schmid as
a team member. Ronald Raeszler took blue ribbon honors with a guernsey heifer,
in a large class of dairy anmmals.

1963 proved to be a successful year for Spring Creek 4-H club. Ronald Raeszler,
Earl and Ivan Schmid placed livestock exhibits in the blue ribbon class on
achievement days. It also culminated a profitable 4-H club career for Ivan, who
once again was a member of the county livestock judging team and was awarded
top honors plus a trophy for a top total in hogs in Dickinson. The team placed
second at 4-H achievement days. Ivan showed the top fat steer and won the beef
showmanship award. Recognition night he received the Key Club Award.

Sleigh riding and ice skating parties are favorite winter time recreations.
Summer recreation includes boating and picnics for members and their families.

Those having served in an official capacity (exclusive of organization year)
are: Glenn Sailer, president, 1958; Robert Keogh, Jr., V. pres., 1958;
president, 1959-60; Sec. 1961-62; Dwight Mohl, Sec. 1958; Ivan Schmid, Treas.
1958, V. pres. 1959, Sec. 1959-60, president 1961-62; Norbert Helm, news
reporter 1958; Faye Keogh, Treas. 1959, news reporter 1960; Earl Schmid, news
reporter 1959, V. pres. 1960, treas. 1961, 1962 and 1963, Sec. 1963; Ronald
Raeszler, treas. 1960, news reporter 1961-62, president 1963; Brooks Keogh, V.
pres., 1961; Randy Voegele, news reporter 1963; Virgil Voegele, V. pres. 1963.

Irvin Schmid and Reinhold Raeszler are serving as leaders of the club at the
present time.

KNIFE RIVER 4H CLUB

        Back Row--Left to right: Delmar Erickson, leader (2 yrs.), James
        Kusler, Glen Erickson, Darrell Herman, Jerry Welk, Maynard Blohm, Neal
        Sebastian. Second Row--Left to right: Leland Erickson, Asst. leader
        (3 yrs.), Charles Strom, Grace Erickson, Janis Zuern, Loretta Buchman,
        Doris Flemmer, Dorothy Buchman, Wanda Erickson, Jimo Menge, Mrs.
        Lester Blohm, Assistant leader (4 yrs.). Front Row--Left to right:
        Johnell Kusler, Myrna Murray, La Vonn Buchman, Joan Weil, Sharon
        Weil, Mona Murray, Carla Sailer, Mark Murray, Mrs. Ed Unruh, Leader
        (11 yrs.).

The Knife River 4-H Club was organized on March 24, 1947. The local Knife River
Farmers' Union lent a helping hand in the beginning in seeing that the club
program got under way. The first leaders elected in the new Knife River 4-H
Club were Emanuel Fischer and Mrs. Glen Murray. Heading up the group as
officers were: President--Gerald Kusler; Vice President--Gary Murray;
Secretary--Corrine Retterath; Treasurer--Phyllis Murray; and News
Reporter--DeLores Erickson. Charter members were Gale Abbey, Donna Abbey, Eunice
Strom, Peggy Murray, James Abbey, Carroll Erickson, Jerome Strom, Donald Cline,
Glen Cline, Esther Schlecht, Mary Margaret Herman and Dale Strom.

Knife River has always had a strong membership of around 20. The club has
always prided itself in its strong project work, but more than that, it has
developed a strong extra curricular activities program. These activities have
included such things as demonstrations, public speaking, livestock judging and
many other community services. The following is a thumbnail sketch of some of
the many honors that these activities have earned during the years.

DEMONSTRATIONS.
Dairy Foods (state event) 1952--Mary Margaret Herman
Wilma Binder
Dairy Foods (state event) 1956--Karen Herman
Patty Sebastian
Dairy Foods (state event) 1957--Patty Sebastian
REA (state event) 1959--Patty Sebastian
REA (state event) 1960--Delmar Erickson
REA (state event) 1962--Dorothy Buchman
Quality Dairy (state event) 1963--Neal Sebastian
Livestock Conservation (state event) 1963--James Kusler
Dairy Foods (state event) 1963--Janis Zuern

While this list shows only the participants in state events, it is noteworthy
that demonstration work has long been a part of the monthly club meeting.
Before the individual 4-H club member could gain the status of a state
demonstration participant, a great number of demonstrations were given on the
local level. The participant had to win an elimination contest within the Knife
River Club and had to win top honors in the county and district events before
going on to the state. In 1960 Delmar Erickson accomplished our club's greatest
individual effort in demonstration activities as he won the state demonstration
title on the "Care of Horses Hooves". Delmar used a wax model of a horse's hoof
and showed the proper techniques of horse shoeing. The demonstration was quite
a novelty and was made into a sound and color movie to use in teaching
demonstration techniques to other 4-H club members throughout the State of
North Dakota. He was awarded a $200 scholarship to the North Dakota State
University for his efforts.

STATE DRESS REVUE.
1953--Milda Kusler          1954--Karen Herman
1953--Mary Margaret Herman  1959--Karen Herman
1953--Karen Herman          1959--Patty Sebastian

The Mercer County dress revue has always seen some very stiff competition. In
the start of 4-H club work the girls would only have a dress revue twice during
a 6-year period. This made it impossible for many of the girls better in
clothing to participate in a local dress revue during their 4-H club tenure.

PUBLIC SPEAKING.
1953--Marleen Herman      1962--Neal Sebastian
1958--Karen Herman        1963--Darrell Herman

All of the Hermans--Marleen, Karen and Darrell--have had the high honor, but
also heart breaking experience, of finishing 2nd in the state competition in
public speaking. Public speaking activities have been one of the most popular
activities with the Knife River club.

FORMER KNIFE RIVER 4-H LEADERS

BEEF SHOWMANSHIP.
1958--Leland Erickson   1961--Delmar Erickson
1959--Floyd Unruh     1962--Neal Sebastian
1960--Delmar Erickson

The traveling trophy sponsored by the North Dakota Stockmen's Association
originated in 1958. It was held in the club for the first 5 years of
competition. Not until 1963 did the Knife River club relinquish this trophy to
another club in the county.

LIVESTOCK JUDGING.

Livestock judging has always been one of Knife River's strongest fields. Nearly
all of the members enrolled in livestock have tried their hand at livestock
judging and have participated in many club, county and district workouts. A
club team composed of Delmar Erickson, Floyd Unruh and Delton Herman took
second place at the J. H. Shepard Memorial Judging Contest at the North Dakota
Winter Show at Valley City in 1959. Club members who have been members of the
Mercer County 4-H judging team are as follows:

Gerald Kusler--1949             Delmar Erickson--1958
Glenn Cline--1949, 1950         Darrell Herman--1963
Delton Herman--1959             Neal Sebastian--1963
Leland Erickson--1958 thru 1962

HORSEMANSHIP.

Horses and the Knife River 4-H Club seem to go hand in hand. This project
started during the late 50's and saw Floyd Unruh gain a championship in 1957,
1958 and 1959. In 1963 the club helped with a county-wide 4-H trail ride in the
Medicine Butte area. Four-H members from all parts of the county camped over
night behind Medicine Butte and spent the daylight hours of two days studying
horsemanship techniques.

Other special honors gained by the club dunng the years include the "Rate of
Gain" trophy won by Maynard Blohm in 1963 and the grand champion Beef Heifer
Award won by Neal Sebastian in 1963. In 1960 Karen Hennan was awarded a $150
scholarship on the basis of all-around 4-H activities. Leland Elickson
represented the county in a Canadian Youth Exchange program in 1961. He spent
several days visiting with other 4-H families on the north side of the border.

For several years Ralph Murray, a long time leader of the Knife River 4-H Club,
served as president of the Mercer County 4-H Club Council.

KNIFE RIVER FARMERS UNION LOCAL

The Knife River Farmers Union Local was organized in 1937 with the following
Charter members: E. L. Montgomery, Paul Rettke, George Enderson, August Weil,
Emanuel Beick, Peter Beockel, William Walsh, Gottlieb Weil, Rudolph Schlect,
Albert Winkler, Christ Bitterman, William Thompson, Ferdinand Schulz, Harry
Houser, Joseph Reich and Gottlieb Schmidt. The first County Leader was Helen
Schmid and Marion Erickson was the first Junior Leader.

This Local now has a membership of over three humdred. It sponsored the Knife
River 4-H Club in 1947 and joined with other locals through the county in
helping promote the first Farmers Union Elevator.

Eight of our members: George Cline, Victor Strom, Mrs. George Cline, Carl
Herman, Julius Orth, Eunice Strom, Mrs. Victor Strom and Lester Blohm have gone
to Washington, D. C. on the Special Guided Tour.

Mrs. George Cline and Mrs. John Maier attended the 25th Anniversary of the
National Farmers Union at Dallas, Texas.

The ladies take an active part in promotional work and the following have
attended State Ladies Camp: Mrs. Adolph Anderson, Mrs. Floyd Erickson, Mrs.
Carl Herman, Mrs. Julius Orth and Mrs. Victor Strom. Mrs. John Kusler, Mrs.
Floyd Erickson, Mrs. George Cline and Mrs. Victor Strom have gone on the G.T.A.
Central Exchange Tour.

The Local promotes an extensive Youth Program consisting of Junior and Reserve
work. The Juniors and Reserves attend summer camps annually. Four Junior:
Donald Cline, Marleen and Karen Herman and Leland Erickson have attended
National Farmers Union All-State Camp at Red Rock Canyon in Colorado. Each year
the Juniors and Reserves present a Christmas Program. Meetings are held monthly
on the first Friday of the month.

FARMERS UNION OIL COMPANY
Beulah, North Dakota
34 years--1930 through 1963

The Beulah Farmers Union Oil Company obtained its Charter on May 12, 1930, as a
Cooperative Corporation. The original incorporators were: C. B. Heinemeyer, F.
P. Conrath, Peter Boeckel, Ernest R. Hafner, H. H. Houser, Jacob Fuhrman, John
Weiss, Glen Voeltz, Henry Conrath, John Eisenbeis, Theo. Hafner, John L.
Schmid, William Weiss, John Polriesz and Ira Miller.

The first appointed Board of Directors was: Rudolph Miller, Oscar Erickson,
Ernest R. Hafner, Albert Binder and Fred J. Miller. This Board served until a
regular Board was elected. The first elected Board of Directors was C. B.
Heinemeyer, Pres.; Chas. Herman, Vice-Pres.; Peter Boeckel, Sec.-Treas.; Felix
Conrath, Ernest R. Hafner, John E. Weiss and Ernest Bohrer. The first Manager
hired by this Board was Ira Miller.

The main purpose of this Company is to obtain petroleum products and farm
supplies for its members on a cooperative basis, returning all of the net
savings to its members. Until the Farmers Union Central Exchange, our wholesale
source, was organized, gasoline was purchased from Standard Oil Co. on a
contract basis. The Standard Oil dealer at that time was Mr. Vreeland, Hazen,
N. D.

The first Service Station facilities were located on the NP railroad
right-of-way, south of the present Beulah Drug Store. This site is still being
leased by the Company for bulk petroleum storage and other warehouse storage.

During July of 1931 the frame Service Station building on the NP right-of-way
burned, destroying most of the Co. records, and the building with merchandise.
This building at that time was owned by Theo. Herman and leased by this Co.
After settlement on the insurance loss, the building was purchased from Mr.
Herman. This same building was then remodeled and enlarged, with lumber
obtained from sawmills near Stanton and Manhaven, N. D.

        Left to Right: A. Binder, W. Weiss, A. Anderson, E. Hafner, P. Boeckel.

During the years from 1931 through 1947 this Company operated sub-stations at
the following towns: Golden Valley, Hazen, Stanton, Center and Zap. The Zap
sub-station is still in operation. Golden Valley and Center have been closed.
During 1947 the sub-stations of Hazen and Stanton were merged and separated
from the parent Company of Beulah. On vote of the owner stockholders, all the
stock held by members in this area was transferred to Hazen. These two
Companies are now being operated and incorporated as Farmers Union Oil Co.,
Hazen, North Dakota.

During 1939, on recommendation of the Board of Directors and approval by the
stockholders, the present building that houses this Company was constructed. It
served this Company until 1958, when an addition of 56x70 was constructed. The
original building was completely remodeled--modern facilities and equipment
installed.

In the 1931 fire, most of the early records of operation of this company were
destroyed. the following statistical information is therefore from available
records of the Co. beginning in 1933. Total gross sales for this period were
$22,649.83. Sales at the end of fiscal year ending Sept. 1942 had climbed to
$195,338.51. Net savings to stockholders for this year amounted to $21,527.91.

Gaylen Hollar, manager since March 9, 1933, resigned at this time. Mr. Hollar
then formed the Hollar Transport Co., Billings, Mont. He was succeeded as
manager by Raymond G. Retterath, who had been sub-station manager at Stanton.
(Sept. 30, 1942). Under Mr. Retterath's management, during the difficult war
years, sales at the end of the fiscal year ending Sept. 30, 1947 rose to
$201,276.32, with net savings to stockholders of $34,175.22. Sales during the
following seven years to Sept. 30, 1954 climbed to a then record high of
$286,969.86, with this year's net savings to stockholders and members of
$33,436.80. Mr. Retterath resigned at this time, and moved to his farm about 3
miles south of Stanton, which he still operates.

The Board of Directors then entered into a contract with Otto Kasper, as
manager of the Company. Mr. Kasper had been their bookkeeper since 1949. Mr.
Kasper has served as manager continuously since 1954.

Sales during the first year of Mr. Kasper's management rose to a record high of
$334,808.16; with net savings to stockholders and members of $35,099.99. Total
gross sales for fiscal 1963, exceeded the one-half million mark, or $543,599.85
with net savings to stockholders, also a record, of $73,728.63, or a percentage
of 14.27% on each dollar of purchase.

Total sales and savings for 34 years, since this Co. was incorporated, are as
follows: Sales volume of $7,862,238.65; total net savings to stockholders and
members of $1,016,203.07, or an average percentage in savings of 12.41% on each
dollar of purchase. Of the total net savings in the amount of $1,016,203.07,
$483,647.88 has been paid back to the owners in cash. Since the fiscal year
ending Sept. 30, 1963 to this time when this is being written, an additional
$33,000.00 has been paid back to the stockholders and members, representing all
of the 1953 stock earnings. This means there is no stock in this Company older
than that earned during 1954.

All of this has been possible from an original cash investment of $3057.17 by a
few farsighted, hard working and dedicated pioneer farmers of this general
area, and by the continued fine cooperation of all area farmers and business
people.

In going over the minutes of the early Board of Directors and Stockholders
meetings, one can't help but feel these people gave much time, effort and in
some cases money, to organize the area farmers and business men in a much
needed business enterprise, so vital to the survival of the family farm, small
towns and cities.

Of the original incorporators and original Board of Directors, we are still
privileged to have one in our midst, namely Ernest R. Hafner. Mr. Hafner still
lives on his farm 14 miles north of Beulah. He served on the Board of Directors
for 22 years. During his time on the Board and since, he has given much of his
time, effort and advice for the general welfare of this Company. Certainly many
of us owe much to him and others for the growth and economic impact this
Company has made on this community. The continued fine cooperation of many
farmers, ranchers and businessmen, with their patronage and support, will give
further growth to this Company and the community which it serves. We must all
continue to join hands as the pioneers did 34 years ago and continue to work
together for the best interest of all, regardless of race, creed, color or
political philosophy. Our thanks to Adolph (Ed) Anderson, Raymond Retterath and
others, for making the pictures available for this history.

It has been a pleasure and education to write the history of this great
Company. I should like to tbank all those who assisted me with some of the
early Company history. It is my hope and prayer that I may continue to serve all
of the people who are owners of this Company. Present Board of Directors: John
Kusler, Pres., August G. Miller, Vice Pres. Wilfred A. Herman, Sec-Treas., Em.
Stuhlmiller and Christ Bieber.

Managers of this Company in their order of service: Ira Miller, C. B.
Heinemeyer, Henry Conrath, Gaylen Hollar, Raymond G. Retterath and Otto Kasper.

                Submitted by, Otto Kasper, Manager

HISTORY OF ROYAL NEIGHBORS OF AMERICA BEULAH WEST CAMP NO. 8324

        Back Row--Left to right: Lillian Heihn, Gertrude Younker, Elsie
        Urness, Mildred Bowers, Marguerite Herman, Martha Heihn, Mary Welk,
        Lois Stewart, Ann Liebelt, Wilda Webber, Clara Elder, Joyce Herman,
        Mary Thomas, E. Esther Voegele. Front Row--Left to right: Katherine
        Niedermeyer, Jenny Wolf, Ann Niedermeyer, Fanny Koester, (charter
        member), Enga Larson--State Supervisor, Irene Lenhardt, Catherine
        Scherbinske. Taken at their Jubilee Rally lune 11, 1957.

Royal Neighbors of America, Beulah West Camp No. 8324, the oldest organization
in Beulah, was issued a charter June 22, 1917 by Deputy Lila L. West.

Twenty-four charter members were initiated into the camp and a full slate of
officers elected and installed that evening at Palm Theatre. A social and dance
followed. Members were: Ina Colby, Clotilda Evans, Fannie Evans, Bessie
Farrington, Minnie Farrington, Ella Johnson, Elizabeth Kenyon, Myrtle Korupp,
F. W. Koester, Mayme Luckow, Lucille Luthander, Myrtle Murray, Sam Murray,
Mabel Oulmonn, Ida Sovereign, Fannie Koester, Annie Stuhr, Rudolph Stuhr, Ellen
Trousdale, Katherine Vincent, Jessie Waldron, Isabel Wetzel, Paul Janski,
Jeannette F. Luetke. In the formative years, Mrs. Fannie Koester and Pauline
Birrenkott were active in retaining the organization's existence.

From twenty-four members in 1917, the camp has grown to a Beneficial membership
of 186 and Juvenile membership of 75, in 1964. Members are listed from N. Dak.
in twelve different states and in Sask., Canada.

Enga Nelson Larson, Bismarck, State Supervisor for Royal Neighbors of America,
Fraternal Benefit Society, since 1957, a Beulah native and former member of the
local camp.

Anna Niedermeyer, Bismarck, N. Dakota State Oracle since 1959, is also a former
member of Beulah Camp. She has held office as State Recorder and State
Recorder-Receiver, and was a committee member at the National R.N.A. convention
in Los Angeles in 1958.

Josephine Easton, Bismarck, was first Juvenile Director when the Juvenile
program was mitiated in 1933.

Ida Helvik, Bismarck, served as camp musician for nearly twenty-five years.

Mildred Bowers has the largest Royal Neighbor family. She is either mother or
maternal grandmother to twenty-five members. Since 1932, she has held nearly
every office in the camp.

Catherine Scherbinske, Recorder since 1953, a post she still retains, was
awarded a ten year service pin in 1963 at State Camp.

Lois Stewart, Receiver since 1957, has gained national recognition in the Royal
Neighbor Magazine by receiving honorable mention numerous times for her
original Yearbooks.

Every year Memorial Day flags fly from each R.N.A. grave, placed by members of
our camp. In 1963, nineteen flags were so placed. Each year the camp holds an
annual family picnic, Christmas party and Juvenile Party. The camp annually
donates to six charitable causes. The relief committee sends cards, plants or
gifts to sick members as well as farewell remembrances to those leaving.

June, 1938, a district convention was held in the Legion Hall. Ritualistic
ceremony was conducted by Beulah camp and assisted by the Hazen camp. A Jubilee
Rally was held Aug. 31, 1953. Forty Years of Progress was celebrated in the
school auditorium June 11, 1957, with 75 members and guests present.

Members who have served as Oracle are: Fannie Koester, Ida Helvik, Katherine
Niedermeyer, Clara Rasmusson, Anna Niedermeyer, Lena Peterson, Elwilda Webber,
Jennie Wolf, Joyce Herman, Mildred Bowers, Marguerite Herman, Martha Heihn,
Hildegard Froescher, Ann Liebelt, Esther Voegele, Emma Easton, Rose Gilchrist,
Josephine Easton, Orla Christenson and Mayme Swanson.

Present slate of officers include: Oracle, Mildred Bowers; Past Oracle,
Marguerite Herman; Vice Oracle, Katherine Niedermeyer; Recorder, Catherine
Scherbinske; Receiver, Lois Stewart; Chancellor, Lillian Heihn; Marshal, Ann
Liebelt; Managers, Mary Thomas, Katherine Niedermeyer.

THE KNIFE RIVER SQUARES

        Knife River Squares January 1955

The fun, fellowship and frolic of square dancing has been part of Beulah's
history since 1954. Carrying forth the national theme of square dancers that
"friendship is square dancing's greatest reward". The Knife River Squares have
long extended a welcome hand to all square dancers and wherever possible, they
have expanded the activity.

Beulah's square dancing started back in 1954. Francis Goodalls, then new in the
community, had been exposed to some square dancing in their home community of
Sanish and were instrumental in "stirring up" some enthusiasm on the local
level. Along in the middle of October, Mrs. Ted Herman and Mrs. Tyler Mason put
on a strong campaign to gather all the able bodied at the Dreamland Pavilion
one evening to listen to L. M. French of Dickinson talk about square dancing. A
good crowd was on hand and before the evening was over the seeds of square
dancing fellowship had found fertile ground.

"Frenchy", as he was known, turned his duties over to J. C. Mosbrucker of Glen
Ullin who finished the lessons in basic square dancing and helped organize the
Knife River Squares.

During the ten years of dancing, many dancers have come and gone. Some real
square dance "buffs" have been with the movement right from the start. Others
stayed only for a short time after the basic lessons.

In 1958 a group of Beulah square dancers helped start a new club in Hazen. Even
now during a period of keen competitive spirit between Beulah and Hazen, the
two square dancing clubs are harmoniously aligned and dance together all the
time.

Looking to expand square dancing to other communities, the Knife River Squares
helped the communities of Halliday and Taylor in their beginning in 1962 and
1963.

Recently a younger group of dancers called the "Teens and Tykes" has started.
This group includes dancers from the age of 6 to 16 in Beulah and Hazen.

The area Square Dancers were very fortunate that night in 1954 when Millie
McKenzie convinced Mac that they should take square dance lessons with the rest
of the crowd. Mac has since become one of North Dakota's most popular square
dance callers. Mac has been the backbone of square dancing in Western North
Dakota, he has taught and organized new clubs at Hazen, Halliday, Taylor, as
well as the Teens & Tykes of Hazen and Beulah. Mac has been a staff caller at
the State Conventions since their beginning, and he and his Canadian and
American caller friends planned and staffed the first International Square
Dance Convention at Estvan, Saskatchewan in 1959.

Squares is as follows:
