ST. JOHN'S CHURCH

St. John's Lutheran Church, 6 miles north of Zap, North Dakota was organized in
the year 1903. The date appears to have been Jan. 6, and the organizational
meeting was held in the home of Mr. Martin Weidner, located about two miles
southwest of the present church.

Two years previous Pastor J. Matthias of Hannover had conducted services for
the settlers who had come to take up a homestead or to buy land in this part of
the state.

In the meeting on January 6 it was decided to call this new settlement
Wuerttemberg, and to adopt for the congregation the name St. John's Lutheran
Church at Wuerttemberg. Charter members were: Messrs. Adolph Schlender, Adam
Herrmann, Adam Weigum, Hieronymus Laible, George Weigum, Jacob Krukenberg,
Gottlieb Krukenberg, Friedrich Brunmeier, George Michael Weigum, Adam Miller,
Martin Weidner. Only one of the charter members is still living, Mr. Friedrich
Brunmeier (Billings, Montana).

Since the congregation was at the time too small to have its own pastor, it
formed a parish together with Immanuel Lutheran Church near Hazen and was
served by the pastors of this parish:

The Rev. J. A. Duerr, Nov. 1902 to July 1905
The Rev. Wm. Schmook, August 1905 to May, 1909
The Rev. P. F. Siegel, Feb. 1910 to 1912
The Rev. E. Bliss, 1912 to 1913

During the first years of its existence the services of the congregation were
held in the home of Messrs. Martin Weidner and Martin Frasch. In the year 1907,
however, the congregation built its first church. It measured 22 by 24 feet,
excluding the chancel and the vestibule. This church was dedicated on Aug. 18,
1907 with festival services conducted by Pastors Wm. Schmook and J. H. C. Hinck
of the Underwood-Garrison parish. The land on which the church was erected was
donated by Mr. Friedrich Kasimir.

In the spring of 1914 the congregation decided to call its own pastor and to
build a parsonage and a barn. Messrs. Daniel Walz, Gustave Thomas, and
Friedrich Burnmeier served on the building committee. Mr. Adam Herrmann donated
ten acres of land to serve as a pasture, since horse and buggy was the way the
pastor and people got from place to place. For the protection of the teams,
used to bring the families to church, a barn was built by a number of members,
measuring 70 by 28 feet. Erected in 1917 this barn was destroyed by fire in
1949.

As the first resident pastor, Candidate Walter Feddersen was installed on Aug.
23, 1944 by Pastor F. W. Kaul of Dodge (Defiance). Pastor Feddersen served the
congregation until the year 1920 and was succeeded by Candidate George
Korlowske who was installed by Pastor A. Berner of Hazen. During the pastorate
of the Rev. Koslowske the present church, measuring 30 by 60 feet, was erected
at a cost of approximately $10,000. The new church was dedicated on Oct. 10,
1926, the Rev. F. W. Wohlfeil (Hazen), Ralph T. Schuricht (New Salem), and
Herbert Trinklein (Beach) serving as guest speakers.

Three years later, on June 9, 1929, the congregation observed the 25th
anniversary of its organization with Pastors Wm. Schmook (Santa Ana, Calif.)
and H. Gericke (Dodge) serving as guest speakers.

The congregation at an early date acquired a cemetery, the land having been
donated by Mr. Friedrich Kasimir. This cemetery, was dedicated on Ascension
Day, May 1, 1913, by Pastor E. Bliss. In 1918 the cemetery was laid out in
lots, and in 1919 a cemetery constitution was adopted.

Repeatedly the congregation endeavored to establish a Christian Day School and
had such a school in the years 1927 to 1930 and again from 1937 to 1938. The
following served as teachers: Messrs. Henry Schoenbeck, Erhardt Knittel, and
Martin Wegehaupt.

In the year 1936, from June 16 to 23, St. John's congregation was host to the
convention of the North Dakota District.

Pastor George Koslowske had started conducting services in the village of Zap,
but it was not until the year 1938 that definite steps were taken to provide
regular services for the members living in the village. In that year a building
committee was elected consisting of Messrs. Adam Herrmann, Fred Mittlelsteadt,
and Sam Miller, Sr. A basement was built on property donated by Mr. Adam
Herrmann and onto this a church was moved which had stood about seven miles
north of Beulah. The purchase price was $400 including furnishings. Messrs. Sam
Miller, Sr. and Adam Weigum were elected to serve as elders of the Zap members.
In a later meeting Mr. Geo. Miller was elected in place of Mr. Adam Weigum. On
Sept. 12, 1943 it was resolved to permit the Zap members to organize their own
congregation and to form a parish together with St. John's. Thus St. Luke's
Lutheran Church in Zap is a daughter congregation of St. John's. On April 29,
1945 the deed of St. Luke's church was transferred to the congregation by St.
John's.

To make it more convenient for the pastor, especially in sending his children
to school, the congregation in the year 1948 elected a parsonage moving
committee, consisting of the following: Messrs. Carl Morast, Theodore Herrmann,
Herman Walz, Fred Mittelsteadt, Reinhold Krukenberg, and John Wiedrich. The
parsonage was moved to its present location in Zap in June, 1948. Electric
lights were installed in the parsonage and also in the church in 1945.

Besides those already mentioned the following pastors served St. John's
congregation:

The Rev. Theo. Hilgendorf, 1930 to 1937;
The Rev. Walter Link, 1938 to 1940;
The Rev. Samuel Meske, 1940 to 1943;
The Rev. Hans Wiedenmann, 1944 to 1948;
The Rev. C. Baumung, 1948 to 1951;
The Rev. Herbert Burgdorf, 1952 to June, 1959

The St. John's Congregation celebrated its Golden Jubilee on June 23, 1953. The
congregation is still functioning with 75 souls consisting of; 54 communicant
members and 21 children. Rev. G. H. Fiechtner of Hazen is serving as their
pastor. The present officers of the congregation are: Pastor: the Rev. G. H.
Fiechtner; Chairman, Mr. Herman Walz, Elders; Messrs. Jacob Weigum and Walter
Mittelsteadt; Trustees: Messrs. Gerhardt Walz, Elmer Weiss and Carl Morast;
Treasurer: Erwin Eisenbeis; Secretary, Mr. Carl Morast.

Having for sixty-one years enjoyed the preaching of God's pure Word and the
administration of the unadulterated Sacraments, St. John's congregation praises
the Lord for this undeserved grace.

KNIFE RIVER COAL MINING COMPANY

The Knife River Coal Mining Company, one of the largest lignite producers in
the State of North Dakota, commenced operation as the Beulah Coal Company,
which was started in 1917 and was organized by M. M. Mounts, E. A. Hughes, L.
C. Pettibone and C. B. Little. This organization took over the operation which
had been started by Mr. Mounts earlier, and sunk a shaft which was located
approximately 200' northwest of the present Beulah Power Plant.

        Early Knife River Camp and Tipple

From 1917 to 1919 most of the mining was done on what was called the "Upper
Level". This vein was located about 33' underground and averaged approximately
11' in thickness, and by that time was nearly worked out. In 1919 the shaft was
deepened to a depth of approximately 170' and intersected a bed of lignite
called the "Lower Level". It was soon apparent that an excessive flow of water
would prevent continued operation and plans were formulated to start an
entirely new operation located approximately two miles to the north of the main
line of the Northern Pacific Railway Co.

        Drift Mine started in 1922

The company reorganized in 1922 under the name of the Knife River Coal Mining
Company and opened the new property which was a drift mine. A narrow gauge
railroad was constructed from the Beulah Power Plant site to the new operation,
and coal was transported from the new operation through the use of steam
locomotives.

Loading of the lignite was performed by hand labor and all haulage underground
was accomplished through the use of mules in the working area and four ton
direct current trolley type locomotives in the main haulage area. The seam
opened up by this operation averaged 14' to 21' in thickness and within the
next ten years the mine reached a peak employment of 220 men. The mine
encountered no water problem as had been encountered in the original shaft
mine, and by 1927 was producing approximately 230,000 tons per year.

A sales office was maintained in Bismarck, and shipments of lignite were made
by the company throughout North Dakota.

From 1923 to 1928 the mining company was owned by the United Public Utilities
with E. A. Hughes as Vice President and W. T. Kraft as Manager. In 1927 United
Public Service Company purchased the mining property and they sold to the North
Dakota Power and Light Company in 1929.

In 1930, as part of a modernization program a 20 ton trolley type locomotive
was purchased to replace the steam locomotives used on the narrow gauge track
from the mine to the tipple located on the Northern Pacific Railroad.

In 1931, Northwestern Public Service Company managed the North Dakota Power and
Light Company and also the mining company. In that year, the mine produced
293,000 tons and had an average employment of 206 men, and by this time a large
ventilation shaft had been sunk and a fan capable of delivering 100,000 cubic
feet of air per minute was installed in order to take care of what was rapidly
becoming a large underground operation. By 1935 this operation, under M. M.
Mount's supervision, had completed the modernization of the operation by
installing electric loading machines and the purchase of additional electric
locomotives for underground use. This modernization reduced the working crew to
165 men who produced 219,000 tons of lignite.

In 1936, a disastrous fire completely destroyed the tipple. However, within a
very short time temporary crushers were in operation which allowed the company
to take care of the Beulah Power Plant's needs. A new concrete and steel tipple
with modern equipment was completed late that year and was put into operation.

By the start of World War II in 1942, the company had added additional
locomotives to assist in the underground haulage and additional electric
loading machines to achieve a higher production, and produced 362,000 tons with
an employment of 108 men. During this period, a 400 ton capacity lignite
storage bin was erected between the tipple and the power plant in order to
provide a constant source of fuel to the plant.

        7 BU. Joy Loading Machine

In 1945 the Knife River Coal Mining Company was acquired by Montana-Dakota
Utilities Co. M. M. Mounts continued as superintendent, and Lee Perkins was
mine foreman. Additional loading machines and haulage locomotives were
installed, and by 1950 the mine was producing 418,000 tons with a work force of
125 men.

        The Underground rescue squad--

During 1950 a contractor was employed and stripping was started in an area
approximately three miles northwest of the tipple. The contractor, Helm
Brothers, employed a 2 1/2 cubic yard diesel dragline for stripping and a 3/4
cubic yard shovel for loading of lignite, and production from the strip mine
started in December of 1950. The coal from the strip mine was hauled by trucks
to a dump located nearby. The lignite was loaded into underground mine cars and
was hauled through the underground operation to the tipple. This stripping
operation enabled the company to continue to meet its tonnage requirements
which had been curtailed by an underground mine fire which seriously hampered
the underground operations.

In 1952, the Knife River Coal Mining Company purchased Helm Brothers, who had
been doing the stripping for the company on a contract basis. A large electric
walking dragline was purchased along with additional equipment and a new
stripping area was opened to the east of the underground property. A large
electric shovel was purchased to load the coal, and 20 ton diesel trucks were
employed to haul the coal from the stripping area to the tipple. The
underground operation was discontinued during this time, and by early 1953 all
lignite was produced from the new stripping operation, which is now known as
the North Beulah Mine.

In 1952 M. M. Mounts, who had been connected with the operation since its
origin in 1918, retired.

Through the next few years, the tonnage requirements of the company continued
to increase and in 1957 a large scale modernization of the tipple was completed
which included installation of large crushers and other equipment. The tipple
then had a capacity of producing approximately 400 tons of lignite per hour.
Plans were completed for installation of a large stripping unit, and in 1958
the "Beulah Belle", a large electric walking dragline utilizing a 196' boom and
a 12 cubic yard dragline bucket, was put into operation to replace the smaller
dragline previously used. Additional 20 ton diesel trucks were purchased along
with other equipment which enabled the mine to produce 703,000 tons in 1962.

In 1963 the Knife River Coal Mining Company put another mine into production,
which is located approximately three miles south of Beulah, North Dakota. This
mine is served by a spur track which connects to the Northern Pacific main line
at the west end of Beulah. The mine, which started production in August 1963,
employs large diesel trucktrailer combinations capable of hauling 35 tons of
lignite. The lignite is processed through a modern tipple which has a maximum
capacity of 800 tons per hour. The "Prairie Queen", a Bucyrus-Erie electric
walking dragline is used for stripping the over-burden. This large dragline has
a 175' boom and uses a 17 to 19 cubic yard bucket. A new Bucyrus-Erie electric
loading shovel of 8 cubic yard capacity is used for loading the lignite into
the haulage units. Well constructed haulage roads connect the stripping area
with the tipple and this operation, called the South Beulah Mine, together with
the North Beulah Mine, enabled the company to produce 711,000 tons of coal in
1963. It is estimated that the two operations conducted by the Knife River Coal
Mining Company in the Beulah area have a total production capacity of over
1,500,000 tons per year. This capacity will allow the company to amply fulfill
its tonnage requirements and will take care of the expansion of Montana-Dakota
Utilities Co. as well as other utilities which the mines serve.

At the present time, the company's operations in the Beulah area employ 58 men
and are under the direction of R. W. Easton, General Superintendent, H. H.
Scherbinske, Superintendent, and Jack Helm, General Foreman.

The company also operates other large strip mine operations at Gascoyne, North
Dakota, and at Savage, Montana.

THE HISTORY OF BANKING IN BEULAH

It might come as a surprise to many people of the area that the present Bank of
Beulah did not have its' beginning in Beulah, but had received the original
Charter to operate at Zap. How the Bank of Beulah happened to move to Beulah
who our predecessors were, and what became of them will be recounted in the
following histories:

THE FIRST STATE BANK

The first bank to open its doors in Beulah was the First State Bank. The First
State Bank received its Charter to operate a bank at Beulah, on August 25,
1913. The Charter was issued to the following directors: N. P. McGregor, E. A.
Kees, and W. C. Kees. It closed its doors on December 27, 1926. Information on
the years in between is rather limited. By reading old newspapers the following
information has been gathered.

It received its Charter on August 25, 1913. In July, 1919 this bank and the
State Bank of Zap (there also was more than one bank at Zap) mergered. At this
time Kenyon,  of Zap was added to the board of directors.

On January 1, 1924 the bank voluntarily closed its doors for a few days and was
placed under State Trusteeship with E. A. Kees as trustee. The bank operated
under this trustee-ship until October 1924, when it began to transact general
banking business. The old officers and management remained the same during this
period: N. P. McGregor, Pres., E. A. Kees, Vice Pres., and C. N. McGregor,
Cashier.

At the time the bank closed its door for the final time the officers were N. P.
McGregor, Pres., C. N. McGregor, Vice Pres., and Adolf Klein, Cashier.

GERMAN STATE BANK

The German State Bank was the second bank to open it's doors in the newly
formed village of Beulah. The State Banking Board granted a Charter to John
Young, J. G. Cowan, and P. S. Chaffee on March 18, 1915. There is no available
information on the activities of the bank for the next fifteen years, except
that Mr. Chaffee was always an enthusiastic promoter for Beulah. When Mr.
Chaffee died Beulah's loss was felt for many years.

Upon the death of Mr. Chaffee on July 9, 1930, Mr. Chaffee's stock was sold to
Robert Stroup of Hazen and F. O. Gentz. This transaction took place on July 10,
1930. On July 16, 1931 the bank changed hands again this time the stockholders
were the following: Tom Keogh, Jim Keogh, Frank Marshall, L. F. Temme, Geo.
Kadlec, Mrs. Alice Chaffee, John Young and Fred Gentz.

From what can be gathered from local residents the German State Bank seemed to
be plagued with many misfortunes: beginning with the untimely death of its
"sparkplug" Pearl Chaffee; then the general recession of the early thirties,
which had caused a slump in the general income of the community: All of this
combined with the years of drought and repeated crop failures coupled with the
crop destroying pestilence of grasshoppers constituted a weakening of the
over-all economy of the area and caused the consequent "breakdown" of the
financial structure of this banking institution. In 1937 it was in such dire
financial straits that additional funds had to be solicited and thus more
stockholders were added. Some of these were: Emil Slavick, Ted Herman; Dr. F.
P. Rassmusson and F. K. Flemmer. This still did not do the trick of
revitalizing a dying business and the following regrettable item appeared in the
June, 1938 issue of the Beulah Independent: "By consent of the directors the
German State Bank of this city was closed Wednesday Morning. All deposits are
insured by the F.D.I.C." Thus the death of a business was announced to the
public. As residents of Beulah read this announcement they couldn't help but
feel that part of the "early days of Beulah" had died too and that they had
lost an old friend. But as in the parting of a well-known friend, one has to
pick up the pieces and make new friends as on June 23, 1938 the following
article was published. "Beulah's new banking service started last Wednesday
when the Gov't commenced paying off the depositors of the defunct German State
Bank." There was no red tape to it, as the depositor just walked into the bank
got a Gov't check for the full amount due him. Right on hand was Mr. A. T.
Zimmerman to accept deposits for the First State Bank of Zap, which will
function until a new charter is secured." And so the Bank of Beulah was born.

BANK OF BEULAH

The present Bank of Beulah actually had it's beginning at Zap, North Dakota as
The First State Bank of Zap. The Zap Bank had purchased the German State Bank
building and fixtures in 1938. In the August 4th, 1938 issue of the Beulah
Independent the following article appeared: "On Monday August 1, 1938 the bank
here opened under the new name of Bank of Beulah. Permission having been
granted by the State Banking Dept. to make the change. The officers will remain
the same as under the former name, The First State Bank of Zap. It is reported
that the Bank of Beulah also has received permission to operate a paying and
receiving station at Zap. So service will be continued for it's customers. The
re-adjustment conforms with the gov'ts plan to have fewer and stronger banks."

The First State Bank of Zap was started in 1915, when the State Banking Board
granted them their Charter. The bank opened for business the following year.
The initial Board of Directors consisted of John Young, P. S. Chaffee and R. M.
Stroup. On August 1, 1922, A. T. Zimmerman joined the staff and three years
later purchased the interest of P. S. Chaffee and was elected a director. In
1929 R. M. Stroup moved to Hazen, retaining his interest until the following
year when Mr. Zimmerman and Ludwig Fuchs purchased his stock and Mr. Fuchs was
elected to the directorship. Also in 1931 Mr. T. F. Zimmerman, father of A. T.
Zimmerman, purchased the interest of John Young and was also elected a
director. Officers from this date on, until T. F. Zimmerman's death were T. F.
Zimmerman, Pres.; Ludwig Fuchs, Vice Pres.; and A. T. Zimmerman, Cashier and
Executive Officer.

On August 1, 1938 when the bank was renamed the Bank of Beulah, The First State
Bank of Zap became a paying and receiving Station for the Bank of Beulah.
Christian Fuchs was Assistant Cashier and was manager of the Station. When
Christ was inducted into the Armed Services on January 31, 1944, the Paying
Station was closed down due to the lack of availability of capable help.

After T. F. Zimmerman's death in 1954, Mr. Friedrich Fuchs was elected to fill
his position and these officers remained until A. T. Zimmerman and the Fuchs
Bros. decided to retire from the banking business. The Bank was then sold to
two Beulah business men; F. K. Flemmer and D. H. Dettmann and to local rancher
Robert Keogh who are the present stockholders. Mr. Flemmer is president; Mr.
Keogh is Vice President and Mr. Dettmann is Cashier.

When the bank changed hands Art Zimmerman stayed on for six months to acquaint
D. H. Dettmann with correct methods and practices of Banking. The bank
employees that remained with the bank were Esther Padilla, (resigned 1960);
Pauline Voegele (resigned 1961); and Viola Miller (resigned 1955). In July 1955
Edgar Goodman, who had worked for Mr. Flemmer and Mr. Dettmann in the Corner
Hardware for 8 years was hired. At approximately the same time LaVonne Renke
was added to the staff. She resigned in 1963. The present employees are: Edgar
Goodman, who has been with the bank since 1955; Jim Aanderud who was hired in
December 1960; Hazel Wagner employed as of January 1963; Clayton Beck employed
as of February 1963; and Dianne Wagner employed as of June 1963.

In 1956 the present owners decided to build a more adequate building. The old
building (which was one of the oldest in Beulah and almost a landmark in the
community) was torn down and a new structure was erected. The opening of the
new building was commemorated with a public open house on Sept. 15th, 1956. In
1963 the bank bought the adjoining lot from Lester Bauer and plans are in the
making for an extensive enlarging and remodeling program for the immediate
future.

Statistical HISTORY OF THE BANK

Capital Stock of the bank at the time it opened in 1916 was $10,000.00. In 1938
this was increased to $15,000.00. On December 22, 1943 the Capital Stock was
increased to $25,000.00, with the total Capital Stock, Surplus and Un-divided
Profits $39,688.92. On October 4, 1950 the Capital Stock was increased to
$50,000.00. This brought the total Capital structure including Surplus and
Undivided Profits to $132,331.76. Deposits grew from its beginning to
approximately $126,787.55 at the time of its move from Zap to Beulah. The
figures at the time the bank changed hands and the latest official figures are
as follows:

                                        May, 1955       Dec. 31, 1963

Capital Stock                           $   50,000.00   $   75,000.00
Surplus & Undivided Profit                 102,497.55      133,973.73
Total Deposits                           1,709,977.43    3,216,205.18
Loans                                      148,464.88    1,076,780.97

We are proud of the progress that Beulah has made in these last 50 years and of
the small part we have plaved in its business activities. We feel Beulah will
continue to make the same great strides ahead in the future. Beulah has been
good to us as the above records will show. For this we are humbly appreciative.

It shall be our endeavor to serve the people of the surrounding tri-county area
to the best of our ability. This has been our goal in the past and will remain
our goal in the future.

P. S. CHAFFEE

The Jubilee Book Committee felt that no history of Beulah would be complete
without some account of one of our most colorful early businessmen, Banker,
Pearl S. Chaffee.

Our information has been rather scanty; therefore we feel no one would be
better qualified to write a tribute to this illustrious, booster of Beulah,
than his dear friend, the late A. D. Brown.

The following is the tribute to Chaffee as "A. D." wrote it and published it in
the July 19, 1930, edition of the Beulah Independent.

"THE PASSING OF A REAL FRIEND"

Pearl S. Chaffee passed away at the Deaconess Hospital at Mandan, Wednesday
morning July 9th. After only a few days illness from complications of heart and
kidney trouble. He was taken to the hospital Monday afternoon. At his bedside at
the time of his death were his wife, his brother, C. C. Chaffee, Everett, Wash.
and a sister Mrs. E. A. Albee, Springfield, Mass. The latter two came to North
Dakota, arriving Tuesday morning to visit at their brother's home and found him
near death.

Pearl Stowe Chaffee the son of Mr. and Mrs. Lovell Chaffee was born at Troy,
Vt., October 24, 1878. He was married January 28, 1905 to Miss Alice McMahan of
Waseca, Minn. Two years later they came to Mercer County and located at
Expansion where Mr. Chaffee engaged in the banking business. In 1915 he
organized the German State Bank of Beulah and located in this city where he has
since resided.

Funeral services will be held in this city Friday afternoon and interment will
be made in the Beulah Cemetery.

For fifteen years Pearl held a prominent position among the broadminded
representative, and honorable citizens of our community. He was always eager to
help shoulder the burden of public responsibilities. He was broad in views
extra-ordinarily intelligent and was guided through his life by a keen sense of
duty and personal honor. He was kind hearted and thoughtful in the extreme and
there was no limit he would not go to, to help those who were deserving. He
proved his efficiency and judgment by the prominent part he assumed in the
affairs of the community in which be lived and labored untiringly to further
its advancement and development. He was modest and judicious, and unnumbered
are those who have gone to him when advice and consolation were needed in
guiding them along the troubled walks of life. Truly it can be said that he
gave more to his community than he could ever hope to take from it, and now
that he is gone, we who knew him best will think with pleasant memories of his
many acts of unselfish kindness and charitable deeds.

He will be greatly missed from this community and his place will be hard to
fill but although we are sad at the passing of such a worthy counsellor and
friend, we must carry on with the assurance that his memory will be the beacon
light to guide us to greater and nobler things.

Our most sincere sympathy goes to his bereaved wife and daughter and to other
sorrowing relatives and friends.

Sponsored by the Bank of Beulah.

SUNSET ON THE PRAIRIES
James W. Foley
N. D's Poet Laureate

Page 247--of Boys & Girls & other poems
They have tamed it with their harrows; they have broken it with plows;
Where the bison used to range it some one's built himself a house;
They have stuck it full of fence posts, they have girdled it with wire,
They have shamed it and profaned it with an automobile tire;
They have bridged its gullied rivers; they have peopled it with men;
They have churched it, they have schooled it, they have steepled it, Amen
They have furrowed it with ridges, they have seeded it with grain,
And the west that was worth knowing, I shall never see again.
They have smothered all its campfires, where the beaten plainsman slept;
They have driven up their cattle where the skulking coyote crept;
They have made themselves a pasture where the timid deer would browse,
Where the antelope were feeding they have dotted o'er with cows;
There's the yokel's tuneless whistling down the bison's winding trail,
Where the redman's arrow fluttered there's a woman with a pail
Driving up the cows for milking; they have cut its wild extent
Into forty-acre patches till its glory is all spent.

SUNSET ON THE PRAIRIES
I remember in the sixties, when as far as I could see,
It had never lord or ruler but the buffalo and me:
Ere the light of man was on it, and the endless acres lay
Just as God Almighty left them on the restful Seventh Day;
When no sound rose from its vastness but a murmurred hum and din
Like the echoed void of Silence in an unheard Prairie Hymn;
And I lay at night and rested in my bed of blankets curled
Much alone as if I was the only man in all the world.
But the prairie's passed or passing, with the passing of the years,
Till there is no West worth knowing and there are no Pioneers;
They have riddled it with railroads, throbbing on and on and on,
They have ridded it of dangers till the zest of it is gone;
And I've saddled up my poney, for I'm dull and lonesome here,
To go westward, westward, westward, till we find a new frontier;
To get back to God's own wildness and the skies we used to know--
But there is no West: it's conquered--and I don't know where to go.

                7th District N.D.F.W.C.
                March 1963

THE EARLIEST SETTLERS

The first inhabitants of Mercer County were the Indians. The three affiliated
tribes--the Mandans, Rees or Arikara and the Grosventre (also known as
Minatarees) were native to this particular area. The Mandans were an
agricultural people. Where they learned to till the soil is the subject of much
debate. Among the theories is the belief that those early Norsemen who wandered
inland across the prairie, account for the occasional blonde Mandan and for
their knowledge of living as an agricultural people. On the other hand, they
may have brought their agricultural habits with them from the southern U. S.
from where they are supposed to have migrated north. The Rees, closely related
to the Pawnees of Oklahoma, were known as "Corn Growers". The Grosventres
originally belonged to the Crow Nation. It was the segment that quarreled with
the Crows over buffalo land and left the alliance to later be taken in by the
Mandans and Rees, when these felt it to their advantage to swell their numbers,
that they might have greater strength against the Sioux, Cheyennes, Crows and
Assinaboines--which were roving and more war-like Indians.

Actually, Napoleon sold the native Indian into slavery when he sold the
Louisiana Territory to the U.S. We have made treaties with the Indian, to be
sure, but they've only held as long as it has suited us, not the Indian. In the
late 1800's the Indians, all through the middle west, were aroused because they
felt there was to be further infringement on their territories. The white man
wanted the gold in the Black Hills. When Sitting Bull was jailed for wandering
off the reservation, he protested at length that he was a free man--free to
roam--and wouldn't live on a reservation where there was no game. The latest
breach of treaty, moved the Indians off the Fort Berthold Indian Agency
bottomlands--their choicest land--to make way for the waters of the
Garrison Dam Reservoir.

The Fort Berthold Indian Agency was established in 1845. Fort Stevenson, across
the Missouri river a little to the east of Jacob Hafner's land, was built in
1874.

Well known Indian names in Mercer County in 1932 included: "THE MANDANS" -
Henry Sitting Crow, Cornstalk, Scatter Corn, Little Owl, Bullhead, Yellow
Breast, Hard Horn, Son of the Stars and James Holding Eagle. "THE REES" - Pat
Star, Running Wolf, Mrs. Skunk Head, Stribey Horn, Short Bear, Young Snake,
Enemy Heart, Boy Chief, Little Soldier, Alex Sage, Ed Lockwood, Frank Fox,
Albert Gillette, Reuben Ducket, Pat Malnorie and Miles Horn. "THE GROSVENTRE" -
Medicine Stone.

Many of our familiar Indian families' ancestors were numbered among Custer's
Scouts. They told their story of the war between the U. S. Army and the warring
tribes assembled under Gall at the Little Big Horn, to Judge A. McG. Beedle and
to the Secretary of the State Historical Society at the home of Bears Belly in
August 1912.

It must be remembered that the Indian Scouts liked Custer and that their duties
were to reconnoiter to find the enemy--to steal their horses if possible, and
then to return to the base camp. If they were caught stealing horses, of
course, they too fought for their lives, but fighting was not the primary part
they were asked to play in these wars.

Another interesting fact that accounts for the saving of Indians' lives in
fighting each other, was that they, like their white brothers, belonged to
lodges, and as brothers in a lodge they saved each others lives. Such was the
case of Sitting Bull once sparing the life of Bull Head when he was unseated
from his horse in battle.

Bloody Knife was chief of the scouts, and Little Soldier was second is command.
Bloody Knife's burial place is on Nathan Little Soldiers' land.

That Curly Head, a Crow Indian, was the sole surviving scout of the Battle of
the Little Big Horn is a misstatement. He was found in the battlefield after
the battle. He had crawled into a disembowled horse for refuge.

Descendants of the scouts and the scout who was the ancestor of each follow:
Reuben Ducket--step-father, Little Sioux; Geo. Gillette--step-father, Running
Wolf, who was the last of the scouts to die; Ed Lockwood--uncle, Boy Chief;
Nathan and August Little Soldier--grandfather, Soldier (Gerard who was in
charge at Fort Stevenson especially requested his aid). Soldier had two sons,
Lee Edwards Soldier and Clarence Little Soldier (father of Nathan and August);
Alfred Chase's father--Foolish; Rose Bell--grandfather, Bears Belly; and
Nathan and August Little Soldier's other grandfather--One Feather.

The Indian names were vivid and meaningful. Others among these were Red Tail--
Mrs. Carl Whitman's stepfather; Bear Ghost--Little Swallow's ancestor and
Straight Arrow, Mrs. Lockwood's grandfather.

Believed to be the oldest Indian on the reservation today is Jack Nagel's
mother, Mrs. Charlie Grinnell, who is a spry 96.

THE ADOLPH (ED) ANDERSON HISTORY My Father John

        John Anderson--as a young man   John--1931

About 1878 my father John Anderson, immigrated to America from Sweden, and
settled in the vicinity of Cambridge, Minn. Here he met Frieda Johnson whom he
married. She died in Minn. In the fall of 1905, together with neighbors, my
father came to Oliver Co. and purchased a quarter of railroad land, which was
located in Section 11-143-87. At that time there was a great boom in this
section of the country as the railroad was all staked out so that it would come
direct from New Salem northwest and cross Section 33-144 in Mercer County. A
townsite was planned near the junction of the Otter Creek and Knife River.

In the spring of 1906 my father returned to Oliver County with his three sons,
Albert, August, and Adolph (Ed.) bringing all his possessions on an immigrant
car. At New Salem he bought a team of horses, and took the first load of
necessary items the forty miles to the farm he had bought. He left us three
boys in a neighbor's shack and returned for the remaining possessions, which
had been left at New Salem. When he arrived at New Salem he found that a large
part of these items had been stolen. John Anderson's daughter, Jennie, did not
come out until 1913. She later married Robert Johnson. They had one daughter,
Inez, Mrs. Louis Dreveskracht of Longview, Washington.

One of the first things my father did was to erect a sod shack in which to live
and a wood barn for the livestock. We boys slept in the loft of this barn. The
first year, after using a walking plow to break the sod, potatoes, flax and
corn were seeded, and a good crop was harvested.

In 1906 my father filed on a homestead which is a part of the parcel of land I
am now living on. The winter of 1906-1907 was one of the most severe I've ever
seen, that winter one of the ranchers, Mr. Cook, lost 36 head of horses because
of the severe weather.

The following summer was dry and everything was burned out, and again in 1908
there was only a small grain crop.

There were only three ranches along the road, the 40 miles between our place
and New Salem. Our first post office was at Nesbit; then Aplin; and then
Farrington. The railroad did not come up northwest from New Salem, but took the
present routing. When the present North Branch railroad was built the Anderson
family furnished most all the potatoes used while it was being built from Fort
Clark to Killdeer.

A townsite was laid out and called Troy and the very first lumber hauled for
this new city was hauled by a four-horse team driven by my brother August.

In 1949 I married Ingrid Lennstrom of Winnipeg, Canada. We are members of the
Immanuel Baptist Church of Beulah. I am the only one of my father's children
still living.

THE ROBBIE JOHNSON STORY (from Inez Johnson Dreveskracht) (in memory of her
father)

Robbie Johnson was born in Cambridge, Minnesota in 1884--the son of Andrew and
Carrie Johnson.

His parents were both born in Sweden. His mother, Carrie, Norman came to
Cambridge in 1877 and married Andrew D. Johnson.

Robbie Johnson, as a young man in Minnesota, worked in a sawmill where he
learned to run steam engines.

In 1905 he came to Mercer County with his parents and a brother Leonard and
three sisters--Annie, Verna and Alice. He filed on a homestead 2 miles south of
Hazen in 1906.

In addition to homesteading, Robbie Johnson worked for Mr. Westlin, a
contractor. He helped build the first elevators and the first school house in
Beulah. He was one of the carpenters who built the Johnson Store (present Ben
Franklin Store).

At this time, H. C. Warrington had built the first hotel on Main Street. It was
here Robbie became acquainted with Jennie Anderson (sister of August and Adolf
Anderson) also from Cambridge, Minnesota. Jennie cooked in this hotel cafe.

In 1916 they went back to Cambridge to be married. The same year they returned
to North Dakota. They bought land in Oliver County, and moved there in 1919.
They had one daughter, Inez, Mrs. Louis Dreveskracht. Mrs. Johnson passed away
in 1935. Ten years later, in 1945, Mr. Johnson returned to Cambridge and
married a childhood acquaintance. The following year the Johnsons sold their
farm to Geo. Cline and moved to Beulah, where they lived until 1950.

At that time he purchased an apartment house in Longview, Wn. Here Robbie died
in 1956. His widow, Clare, lives in Ouincy, Mich. His daughter still lives in
Longview, Wn.

HENRY BECKER, SR.

Henry Becker was born in Alt Friedenstahl, So. Russia on December 20, 1876 to
Henry and Anna, nee Diek, Becker. In 1903 Henry immigrated to America with his
mother and brother, Philip. In 1904 his mother took up a homestead sauth of
Beulah. The Beckers were practically penniless and desperately needed money to
supply and stock their farm, so Henry and his mother would "hire out". Jobs
were scarce and wages were low, many times the two of them worked just for
their meals. Henry was known to be a very hard worker, even though he had been
physically handicapped since birth. Living under these trying times were too
much for Mrs. Becker and the Lord relieved her of her worldly burdens in 1905.
The Becker brothers decided that they should each take up a homestead of their
own, which they did in 1905, when they found some land side by side. Each of
them owned only one horse; in order to have a team they farmed together until
they could afford to buy additional stock.

Henry married Elizabeth Voegele on March 11, 1906. Elizabeth was born in
Gleuckstahl on July 20, 1882 to Michael and Elizabeth Voegele. To this union
were born 8 children; John, married to Mrs. Lydia Buechler, Katharina, Mrs.
Walter Keller; Anna, Mrs. Gotthilf Kruckenberg, and Henry married Alfa Mueller.
Four children died in infancy.

A homesteader's wife's lot was not an easy one; trying to make ends meet,
bearing children and raising them under these adverse conditions took its toll
among our settler women and Elizabeth was no exception. Elizabeth contracted
pneumonia, there was no doctor in Beulah so they had to get Dr. Schierbaum of
Hebron to attend her. It was so cold, they had to change horses 4 times and
drivers 2 times. There were no miracle drugs, and although everything was done
that could have been done, it all proved to be in vain. On February 13, 1916,
Elizabeth in the "prime of her life" passed away leaving behind a grieving
husband and 4 children. Henry Jr. was only a year old so he went to live with
his grandparents, The Michael Voegele's, until he was 8 years old.

At the urging of his wife's parents Henry married a second time, to Karolina
Voegele (widow of Gottlieb Voegele) who had three sons; Philip, John G., and
Edwin.

Karolina was the only mother the Becker children knew until her death on
January 23, 1923. Just before she died Karolina expressed the desire that her
two youngest sons should be raised by her family in McClusky; also that Henry
should keep and raise the eldest son Philip until he became 15. Henry saw to it
that this final request was carried out.

Henry Sr. farmed the homestead with his sons John and Henry Jr. until 1954 when
he and John moved to Beulah. Henry Becker Sr.'s life had been one constant
struggle of hard work and heartbreak. He died on November 13, 1956, all this is
now behind him and he rests in peace.

        Randy, Alfa, Connie, Henry, Sheryl

THE HENRY G. BECKER, JR. FAMILY

Henry G. Becker Jr. was born on February 1, 1915, to Henry and Elizabeth, nee
Voegele, Becker, on his father's farm, five miles north of Beulah. At the age
of one, his mother died and he lived with his maternal grandparents, the
Michael Voegeles, until the age of eight, when he went back to live with his
father and the rest of the family. Here he grew into manhood; farming with his
father and brother, John, until 1942, when he was inducted into the army. He
served in World War II from November 18, 1942 until February 11, 1946. He spent
7 months in active combat in France, Germany and Austria. After the war ended
and he received his discharge, he returned to the farm with his father and
brother John.

On June 12, 1954, he married Alfa Mueller of Golden Valley, daughter of Dan and
Elsie, nee Richau, Mueller. They were married in the Zion Lutheran Church, by
Rev. J. Kammerer. Alfa attended rural Golden Valley schools and the Golden
Valley High School. Prior to her marriage she was employed by the Golden Valley
Post Office and the State Merit System Office in Bismarck.

The Henry Beckers lived on the home farm, until 1959, when Henry sold his
interest in the Becker farm to his brother John, and bought a larger place 10
miles southeast of Beulah (the Art Heth farm). The first year on the new farm
was quite a struggle, as a drought hit the area and wheat averaged only 3 bu.
per acre and the hay harvest was almost nonexistent. The years following have
been easier and more rewarding.

The Henry Beckers have three children: Constance Elizabeth, born on March 6,
1955; Randall Henry, born on June 23, 1958; and Sheryl Elsie, born on November
9, 1960.

The Becker family has lived on their present farm for five years, they operate
it on a diversified basis, as they do some dairying, ranching and wheat farming.

Henry is a member of the V.F.W. and Alfa is a member of the Aplin Homemakers.
The Beckers also belong to the Zion Lutheran Church of Beulah, of which his
father was a Charter Member.

GEORGE AND EFFIE BELLE ENDRESON

I, George Endreson, came to North Dakota in 1903 to visit a friend, Sven
Swenson, who was ranching 5 miles south of Beulah on Brush Creek. While here I
decided to stay and file on a homestead. I had to return to my home in Brandon,
Minn. to settle my affairs, and on my way I stopped in Bismarck and had the
legal papers drawn for my claim, seven and one-half miles southwest of Beulah.
It was in the fall of the same year I came back to Mercer County, North Dakota
and built the "shack" which was my home for the next twelve years.

In the summer of 1911, Effie Belle Turner came from Eau Claire, Wisconsin to
visit her sister Mrs. Dale Sovereign and her brother, my neighbor, Bert Turner.
Effie Belle Turner also filed on a claim and stayed in North Dakota. Her
homestead was two miles east of Medicine Butte. In 1915 Effie proved up on her
homestead.

During this time grew the romance of George and Effie Belle. We were married at
Stanton, N. D. on June 26, 1916. My homestead is still the home place where we
live in retirement with George Jr.

Our children are: Cleo, Mrs. Gale David, Spokane, Wash.--2 children; Roger,
on his Uncle Bert Turner's place--married Minnie Binder--2 children; Lorraine,
Mrs. W. E. Elliot, Oregon City, Ore.--3 children, and George Jr. at home.

We have always been interested in school. I served as clerk for 35 years--Effie
was treasurer for 20 years. Today our son Roger and his wife hold the same
offices.

Our interest in church dates back to the pioneer Brush Creek Congregational
Church. Effie is still a member of the Congregational Ladies' Aid, Beulah.

GEORGE D. BOWERS

George D. Bowers was born March 25, 1869, and was raised in Hardin County,
Iowa. He grew up in Iowa. George married Nellie Cone, November 18, 1896. They
had nine children: Charley (deceased--1961), Iva (deceased--1962), Clifford,
Lester, Mary, Grace, Hazel, Robert and Dale (deceased--1918).

The George Bowers family moved to Kirkhoven, Minnesota in 1903, where they
lived until moving to Beulah in 1916. He farmed for two years before moving to
town, where he became employed in the power plant. His wife, Nellie, passed
away in 1918. Mr. Bowers retired in 1928 and passed away in 1953.

Lester H. Bowers, son of Mr. and Mrs. George Bowers, was born in Kirkhoven,
Minn. on Feb. 28, 1905. When he was 11 years old he came to North Dakota with
his parents. After finishing his schooling, he worked in the Beulah area, being
employed at the mine four years, and then at the Power Plant in 1924. He was
one of the first men to lay pipe in the present Beulah Power Plant.

On May 12, 1926, he married Mildred P. Pulver, daughter of Floyd and Vina Pearl
Pulver. To this union were born six children: Darhl, Pearl (Mrs. David Welk),
Wallace, Delbert, Yvonne (Mrs. Myron Bornemann) and Genevieve (Mrs. Wm.
Gutnecht). Lester and Mildred have 15 grandchildren.

On August 15, 1942, Lester became Chief Engineer at the Beulah Power Plant upon
the retirement of Mr. R. T. Hamilton.

Lester and Mildred are members of the Congregational Church in Beulah. Mrs.
Bowers is a member of the Ladies Aid and Royal Neighbors. Lester is a member of
the Chamber of Commerce, Lion's Club, and presently is the Lion's Zone
Chairman. His hobbies mclude hunting and fishing.

THE PHILIP BECKER FAMILY HISTORY

        Philip and Rosina

Phillip Becker Sr. was born on July 27, 1883, at Alt Freidensdal, S. Russia.
His parents were Henry and Anna, nee Diek, Becker. His father died when he was
but two years old. In 1903 he came to America with his mother and brother,
Henry. In 1905 Phillip took up a homestead 6 miles north of Beulah. He then
married Christine Lebs. To this marriage were born 3 children: Anna, Mrs. Ed.
Bauer of Lodi, California; Lydia, Mrs. Henry Bunk of Nashua, Montana, and
Reinholdt of Silverton, Idaho (married Emma Reinhardt). Phillip's first wife,
Christine died in 1913. This left Phillip with the responsibility of not only
running the farm but also the caring of his three small children. In 1914
Phillip married a young widow, Mrs. John (Rosina) Merkel.

Rosina Flemmer was born on August 28, 1892, in Glueckstahl, S. Russia to Jacob
and Fredericka, nee Jakober, Flemmer. She came to America with her parents in
1906. In 1910 she married John Merkel in the Peace Lutheran Church south of
Beulah. John and Rosina were blessed with two children; Carl of Beulah,
(married Martha Jaeger), and Louise, Mrs. Herbert Sailer of Hazen. John Merkel
passed away on August 20, 1913. Rosina was left a widow with two small children
to support; Carl was about two years old and Louise was born shortly after her
father's death.

After Rosina married Philip Becker they moved onto Phillips farm. Here on the
farm all of the Becker children were born. They were all brought up in the
Lutheran faith (Phillip Sr. was a charter member of the Zion Lutheran Church at
Beulah). Phillip Sr. passed away after a lingering illness on January 19, 1933.
Rose and her children farmed the Becker farm until 1939, when she and the three
youngest girls moved into Beulah. She made Beulah her home until 1946 when she
married Henry Zimmerman and moved to Glenn Ullin. The Zimmermans live in Glen
Ullin at the present time. Mrs. Zimmerman has retained her membership at the
Zion Lutheran Church at Beulah.

Phillip and Rosina Becker were the parents of nine children: Bertha, Mrs. Carl
Sailer, of Hazen; Helen, Mrs. Edwin Sailer, of Hazen; Phillip, married Erma
Buchfinck--they live on the Becker homestead; Rose, Mrs. Clarence Sailer, of
Hazen; Jacob (Jake) married Eleanor Reiner. They farm near Zap; Ludwig died in
infancy; Frieda, Mrs. Reuben Mittlesteadt of Hazen; Ida, Mrs. Leo Mittlesteadt,
of Hazen; and Esther, Mrs. Bob Shalkle, of Bismarck.

At the present time she has thirty-three grandchildren and six
great-grandchildren.

        BUCHFINCK FAMILY HISTORY

        Mr. and Mrs. Gust Buchfinck

Gust Buchfinck was born in August to Mr. and Mrs. George Buchfinck in South
Russia. The following year he came to the U.S. with his parents, and settled at
Stanton.

In 1909 he married Wilhelmina Loeffelbein. They farmed in Mercer County. Mr.
Buchfmck was also a horse and cattle buyer.

The Gust Buchfinck's farmed north of Beulah for awhile, on the Adam J. Keller
farm. They then moved to a farm north of Zap and here they farmed until they
retired and moved into Zap. They lived in Zap until their death's.

There were six children in the Buchfinck family.

Erna, Mrs. Jake Reinhardt of Hazen. They have four children: Agnes, (Mrs.
Harold Bentz), Dellma, (Mrs. Alvin Hildebrand), LeRoy (married to Connie
Mittlesteadt) and Jackie, all of Hazen.

Mollie, Mrs. Bill Strading, Edgeley, N. D. has one son, LeRoy. Diana their
other child was killed in a car accident in 1962.

George, who died in 1961 was married to Vivian Hafner. Their children are:
Carol, (Mrs. Nick Milosewich, who is a nurse in Newfoundland.) Jeanie, (Mrs.
Jim Sandquist of Longview, Wash.), Gary and Gloria at home in Minot.

Erhardt, married to Virgina Simpson, are owners of a motel in Baker, Oregon,
and have two daughters, Sherril and Kathy.

Ted, married to Alma hermann has a family of three: Merril, Sarah and Linette,
all of Zap.

Emma, Mrs. Philip Becker of Beulah, has two daughters, Shelia and Victoria.

Mrs. Buchfinck died on November 2, 1956 and a few weeks later on December 9th
of the same year Mr. Buchfinck died.
