BENZ FAMILY HISTORY

Mathias Benz and his wife Christina came to America from Krem, South Russia in
1893. They were accompanied by their sons Jacob and his wife Rosina, Mathias,
Phillip and John. They traveled to Freeman, South Dakota by covered wagon,
arriving in October. In December of that year Jacob Benz Jr. was born.

Mathias and Christine started north in their covered wagon with their children,
two horses, a cow and some chickens. They arrived at Cannonball, North Dakota
but had to hold up at that pomt for three days as one of the horses had a colt
and it was necessary to wait until it was strong enough to follow. They arrived
in the Beulah area and built their home, a sod house, and planted potatoes on
what is now the Helmuth Pfennig farm Sec 23, 146.88. One of the horses ran away
to the east and of course, Mr. Benz pursued it. When he finally found his horse
it was about ten miles away. While searching for his horse he noted that there
were more people residing in that area, so he returned home, dug out his potato
seed and moved his family east to what was soon known as Expansion. Here four
more children were born, Kathrina, Freda, Paulina and Minnie.

On December 26, 1907, son Mathias was married to Rosina Morast. They had ten
children, Herbert, Friedebert, Henry, Erhard, Julius, Lydia, Rose, Martha,
Pauline and Lea. Friedebert Benz and Emma Zeller were married in 1934 and made
their home at Kronthal. They had one son Darold, now living in Beulah and
engaged in the insurance business. His wife is the former Adella Hafner. They
have two children Mathew and Laura.

Mathias Benz passed away in 1946. His wife Christina is now living at Hazen.

There have been six generations of Benzs in Mercer County and the Beulah area.

        ERNST AND DOROTHY FEDDERSON HISTORY

Ernst Fedderson was born October 14, 1886, to Rev. and Mrs. Matthew H.
Fedderson of New Berlin, Illinois. He grew to adulthood in Homewood, Illinois.
He attended schools at Homewood and went to Business College in St. Louis, Mo.
He also attended College at Ft. Wayne, Ind.

He came to North Dakota in 1914. He lived with his brother Rev. Walter
Fedderson (a Minister of the St. John's Lutheran Church, north of Zap). Ernst
taught school in District #8 and later their sister Concordia came out to
Mercer County to teach school.

On January 12, 1916, he married Dorothy Herrmann, daughter of Adam and
Christine Herrmann of rural Zap.

Dorothy was born in Butte, Nebraska. She came to Mercer County, while she was
still a young girl (1902). Her parents farmed north of Zap.

After Dorothy and Ernst were married they moved on a farm north of Zap, until
1947. Due to Ernst's poor health they moved to Beulah. He passed away March 17,
1951.

The Fedderson's had four children: Richard of Beulah, served for four years in
the U.S. Army. He is married to the former Hertha Wegner. They have three boys;
Stewart, Wayne, and Leslie.

Agnes, Mrs. Wm. Pfeifer, lives in Molalla, Oregon, where Wm. is employed in the
logging business. They have two sons and a daughter; Gloria, Timothy, and
Thomas.

Melvin is married to the former Irene Schulz. He is employed at the Zap Post
Office. They have a son and daughter; Dennis and Mavis.

Marva Lou, Mrs. Gerald Kugth lives in Ashland, Kentucky. Gerald is employed as
a teacher at the St. Paul's Lutheran School. They have two sons, namely;
Jonathon and Nathaniel .

Since her husband's death Mrs. Fedderson has kept busy. She is the cook for the
Beulah School Hot Lunch Program and in the summer time she keeps busy with her
favorite hobby--her flower garden. She is a member of the Concordia Lutheran
Church of Beulah.

BENJAMIN AND MARY BUSCHER

        Bud, Daralene, Dale Betty, Ben and Mary, Lloyd

In 1914 Benjamin Buscher, son of John and Mary Buscher left his home in
Millerville, Minn. to go to work for his brother at Fairmount, N. Dak. It was
here that he met me, Mary Hubbard, daughter of Homer and Theodosia Hubbard. We
were married in 1915 at Breckenridge, Minn. We lived in the Rosholt, S. Dakota
and Wheaton, Minn. areas.

Ben began working for Occident Elevator Co. and in 1929 they sent him to
operate the elevator at Golden Valley. Our children attended Golden Valley
Schools; Dale and Daralene graduated from the Golden Valley High School in
1934. While we were living here our youngest daughter, Betty Lou was born.

The grain dust affected Ben's health so he had to give up the elevator
business. In 1935 we moved on a farm north of Beulah. The next two years were
hot and dusty years and we soon discovered that farming wasn't the best for
Ben's health either. In 1937 we moved back to Beulah and Ben went to work in
the Tipple at the mine.

Wherever we lived Ben played baseball every summer and while living at Beulah
he was manager of the baseball team. He also served as Justice of Peace until
his heart-condition forced him to give that up. He continued to work whenever
he could until 1940 when his health compelled him to retire completely. Dale
and his wife Lucille moved to Beulah. Dale took over the family
responsibilities so that Bud could finish high school. After graduating in
1941, Bud worked at the Beulah Mine. Ben passed away on July 9, 1942 and was
laid to rest in the Breckenridge Cemetery, Breckenridge, Minn.

In the fall of 1943 I moved to Bismarck where I worked as a receptionist for
the Bismarck Hospital. Betty and Lloyd graduated from Bismarck High School.
During the past 12 years I have been working as a House Mother in Fraternity
Homes at Fargo, North Dakota and Delaware, Ohio.

In 1959 my life was again greatly saddened by the untimely death of our son
DuWayne (Bud) who suffered a fatal heart attack on February 5. My other
children have scattered from coast to coast. Dale, his wife Lucille and
daughter, Marlene, live in Portland, Oregon. Daralene (Mrs. Daniel Finnegan)
her husband and two daughters; Maureen and Kathleen live in Greenwhich, New
York. Lloyd, his wife Lynn and their daughter, Lezlie live in Palo Alto,
California. Betty, (Mrs. Marvin Carlson), her husband and three children, Kirk,
Kari and Kaye live in Delaware, Ohio. Bud's wife, Gertie and their two
daughters Mary Kay and Linda Rae live in Bismarck.

        HENRY SINGER, SR.

        Hilda, Herbert, Ervin, John, Esther Henry Sr., Henry Jr., Emma Singer

Henry Singer, Sr. was born at Hoffnungstahl, S. Russia on August 22, 1892 to
Christian and Rosina, nee Flemmer, Singer. There were 8 children in the family,
6 are still living. Henry, Lena, (Mrs. Henry Lang), Wm. Singer, Pauline, (Mrs.
Adolf Weigum), Andrew, Mathilda, (Mrs. Herman Datlevson) and Rosa, (Mrs. Ed.
Giesinger). Henry immigrated to America with his parents in 1902 and for the
first 2 years the family lived at Freeman, S. Dak. On May 4, 1904 the Christian
Singers took up a homestead 14 miles southeast of Beulah, now known as the
Adolph Sailer farm. Henry was confirmed in the Lutheran Faith in 1907; at the
Churchtown Lutheran Church by Pastor Wm. Klein. Henry farmed with his parents
until he married in 1913.

Emma Blum was born on February 25, 1895 at Freeman, S. Dak. to Emanuel and
Wilhelmina, nee Quast, Blum. There were 7 children in the Blum family.

Henry and Emma were married on November 23, 1913 at Rhine, by Rev. John Mau.
They took up a homestead at Otter Creek and lived there for 10 years when they
moved back to his fathers homestead. Here they made their home until they moved
into Beulah in 1944. Mrs. Singer, a woman known for her quiet manner and
gentleness, died of cancer on Oct. 29, 1949.

The Henry Singers had six children; Hilda, Mrs. Lester Blohm, lives in Beulah.
They have 2 children Gloria, Mrs. Arlo Heine and Maynard; Ervin married Ruth
Kusler, they have one son Mark, and make their home in Dickinson; Herbert
married Alice Voeltz. They live in Williston and have 4 children; Glenn,
Stacey, Niles, and Cindy. John lives at Reno, Nevada. Esther, Mrs. Adolph
Sailer, lives on the Singer homestead. They have one daughter, Karla. Henry Jr.
married Mabel Boeshans. They have one child, Sherry and live in Moorhead,
Minnesota.

Henry married Mrs. Rose Bauer, of Hazen on March 12, 1950. Rose's grandaughter,
Ardella (Mrs. Marvin Meissel), made her home with the Singers until she
graduated from high school in 1961. Mrs. Singer has two sons by her first
marriage: Edwin of New Salem and Eugene of Billings, Montana.

The Singers still live in Beulah where they are members of the Zion Lutheran
Church. Mr. Singer served on the town board for 6 years and on the Board of
Directors for the Joachim Memorial Home.

THE JACOB W. FLEMMER FAMILY

        Ludwig, Carl, Irene, Lydia, Conrad and Mike Esther, Jacob and
        Christiana, George

Jacob W. Flemmer, son of Jacob and Eva Flemmer was born at Gleuckstahl, South
Russia on June 13, 1888. At the age of 19 he immigrated to the U. S. and took
up a homestead south of Zap.

Christiana Voegele was born on October 12, 1891, at Gleuckstahl, S. Russia, a
daughter of Michael and Fredericka Voegele. She came to America in 1906 with
her parents and family.

On December 14, 1911 Christiana and Jacob were united in Holy Matrimony at the
Peace Lutheran Church south of Beulah. During the early years of their marriage
they rented various farms south of Beulah. Farming has never been easy and the
Flemmers seemed to have more than their share of setbacks. The hardships the
family endured are almost indescribable. Many times when fuel was low,
Christiana and her small children would have to snuggle in their beds in order
to keep warm. Often the family menu consisted of coffee and bread. The first
real tragedy to strike the Flemmer home was the death of their two oldests
sons, Daniel and David (identical twins). They died within a week of each other
when they were but 11 months old.

In 1932 the family moved into Hebron and things began to "look up". Jacob found
work and the family had a nice home to live in and enough food to eat. At last
it seemed they were getting on their feet. Then another tragedy befell the
Flemmers. On May 21, 1934 the car in which they were riding was hit by the fast
train going through Hebron. One woman was killed and for many days following,
Jacob and Christiana's lives hung in a balance. The Doctors had given up hope
that they would survive. After many months of convalescing in the Hospital they
were able to return home. But the accident had left it's mark. Christiana had
lost the use of an eye and the partial use of her right arm. Jacob who had a
severe head injury recovered more slowly. He never quite got over the effects
of the accident.

The Flemmers moved to Zap in 1938. Jacob worked in the Strip Mine and did some
carpentry work. These later years were their best. He built a new house in Zap
and they lived here until Jacob's death on April 5, 19S6. Christiana made her
home in Beulah for the most part with her daughter Lydia, until her death on
August 1, 1960.

At one time during World War II the Flemmers had five sons in the Armed
Services. Carl, Ludwig, Conrad and George were in the U. S. Army and Mike in
the U. S. Navy.

Jacob and Christiana were the parents of 13 children; five died in infancy and
eight are still living. Lydia, of Beulah, married Ted Buechler  deceased), they
had one son, Freddie. Lydia is now married to John Becker. Carl of Minot,
married Mrs. Lydia Keene. They have one son Duane. Ludwig of Beulah, married
Bernice Luder. They have two children Carol and DeLon. Conrad, of Pick City,
married Jewel Buechler. Their three children are: Floyd, Connie and Jack. Mike,
of Portland, Oregon, married Shirley Grauman. They have two sons Michael and
Roy. Irene of Portland, Oregon, married Benny Wiedrich. They have five
children; DuWayne, Geraldine, LaVonne, Eileen, and Janet. George, of Portland,
Ore. married Gertie Fischer. Their four children are: Esther, Donna, Judy and
Georgette. Esther, of Portland, Ore. married Ralph Hanway. They have two
children--Kathleen and Kevin.

JOSEPH BOESHANS KATHERINA (MEYER) BOESHANS

The Philip Boeshans family lived in Neusatsz, South Russia. Their son, Joseph
and his wife, Katherina, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Ludwig Meyer, with relatives
and friends left South Russia (the Crimea) in the year 1893 because the rulers
sought to force them into the army, away from their land.

The ship was small and the voyage stormy. The beating waves caused the ship to
spring a leak. There was fear among the immigrants. One man, one of the older
people on board, really too old to be starting on this voyage, was the only one
who proved to be a good sailor. He also drank all their stock of alcohol. This
was the senior Mr. Wittmeier.

Like so many of the mid-European settlers they came West by immigrant train.
This group came to Freeman, South Dakota. They worked here in the rural area
for two years. They then came north by wagon and team to the original Boeshans
homestead; South half of section 4-146-87; one mile long and 80 acres wide.

The original deed to this homestead is now the property of Fred Boeshans. To
the original holding has been added additional railroad land.

Joseph and Katherina Boeshans had four sons; Ludwig (died in infancy);
Heinrich, Johanes and Friedrich grew to manhood.

The land, prairie sod, was broken by hand plow. The main crop was wheat.
Marketing was a three day trip to New Salem with an overnight stop at Hannover
each way.

There were probably two market trips a winter. These were neighborhood
projects, when the families took turns making the trip and shopping for each
other.

Once in a great while, perhaps once a winter, the grain was hauled across the
ice to Elbowoods, where there was a mill and wheat could be processed into
flour. Fred can remember the loading chutes on the river and the elevators at
Expansion, seven miles away, and at Ree, 5 miles. The grain was hauled down the
river by steamboat.

Flour was the main stay of the early family's diet. This was typical of their
German background and its famous dough baking.

When cows became part of the farm economy, it was butter making and marketing
that became important. The butter making was so important that when curious
Henry upset the churn, it was a serious matter.

The first home was all sod. The plow was used to cut the sod in square blocks
which were piled on top of each other to form the walls of the house. The house
then had to be plastered inside and out with a mud and straw coating. This
coating had to be renewed yearly. The first house had two rooms. This house was
replaced by a larger sod house with a kitchen, living room and two bedrooms.

They also built a large root cellar and a sod barn. Mrs. Boeshans' relatives
came and settled in the Krem area. The Wiedrichs lived east of the Boeshans
place; Morasts and Millers were other neighbors.

The home was the center of entertainment. Mr. Boeshans played the accordian--
everyone sang.

The Links lived with the Boeshans family the first year before they
homesteaded, as did the Hafners and Wiedenmeyers.

The winters being quite severe became very boring for the two wives (Link &
Boeshans). On one occasion they pestered the men to visit the Gottlieb Fisher
family. The snow was so deep they spent all morning trying to get one mile to
the Fishers and never did get there. They had to turn around and come back home.

Wood was readily obtainable at the river. There were three good springs on the
homestead. When they settled there they didn't care to get too close to the
Indians. Mrs. Boeshans was especially afraid of them. Their homestead was the
farthest west at one time. The Indians made a yearly trek by wagon to Mandan
for the 4th of July. They went by wagon and buckboard with blanketed squaws
sitting upright in the wagon box. They wanted only to trade with the settlers.
They were glad for what was discarded of a butchered cow's carcass. They liked
the cow's stomach.

Church was held in the different homes, when a traveling minister could come,
usually in August. Otherwise, George Miller read from the Bible to the
assembled group of people in the various homes.

They had no regular schools. A Mr. Fisher taught in the houses. The Kronthal
school was built in 1918. It was 1926 before anyone graduated to go on to
school. Then John Boeshans and John Reinhardt went away to Concordia in St.
Paul, Minn.

Mr. Boeshans came to this country with a broad speaking ability. He could
converse in German, Russian and Turkish. German was the language of these
people who came to the Crimea from Germany. He grew to manhood in the Crimea
and learned the Russian language there as well as that of neighboring Turkey.

The strictly horse and buggy era ended for the Boeshans family in 1912 when
Joseph Boeshans bought a 1912 Ford. Mother Katharina was frightened to think of
riding in the new vehicle. When finally persuaded to get in and go for a ride,
she told her husband "better look out--you'll hit a rock." Said Joseph, "Don't
you think I know my land?"--whereupon he hit a rock and tore a tire all to
shreds.

Prairie fires were a terrible menace. Of the fires Fred remembers, is one that
burned at the original homestead taking the haystacks and the machinery stored
between them. Another one in 1915 which came from the west, roaring across 110
acres of flax and killing the cattle in the pastures, burning everything as far
east as Krem.

In 1916 Joseph and Katherina Boeshans built the buildings which now comprise
the Fred Boeshans farmsite, and moved there. At this time they rented the
original site--first to Paul Geiding and later to Fred Kuster.

Katherina Boeshans died September 6, 1935. In the spring of 1936 Fred took over
the original home and put in his own first crop.

Joseph Boeshans died in July 1940. Due to the appropriation of land for the
Garrison Reservoir, Henry felt he had too little land left and sold what he
still owned to Fred in 1948. He then moved to Cedar, Minnesota where he had a
dairy farm. Now he is in the raising and marketing of chicken broilers. Henry
married Frieda Horning. They formerly lived on the old homestead. The following
are the children of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Boeshans: Ernest, Irene, Bennie, Louie,
Arthur, Hilda, Rose, Hubert, Donald, Frieda, Herbert, Marilvn and Mark.

John, who had studied at Concordia in St. Paul, Minn. worked in Beulah at the
Farmer's Elevator for a while before going back to finish school. Afterwards he
managed a seed elevator in Fairbault, Minn. and a feed mill in Frederick, Wis.
John married Elizabeth Avery, prior to moving to Eugene, Ore. in 1940 where he
went mto the lumber business. He bought his own lumberyard in 1942. John died
in 1957. They have one son, James, who lives in Springfield, Oregon.

Fred Boeshans married Lydia Weidner in the fall of 1936. They have seven
children:

Fred Jr. born Nov. 8, 1937 -- graduate NDSU.

Vernon born May 25, 1939 -- attending NDSU.

Sylvia born Feb. 6, 1941 -- Nurse -- Jamestown.

Mabel (Mrs. Henry Singer) born Apr. 7, 1942 -- Moorhead, Minn.

Patty (Mrs. Clyde Fandrich) born May 14, 1944 -- Towner, N. D.

Wayne born Oct. 13, 1945 -- at home.

Jerome born Oct. 28, 1949 -- at home.

They operate a first class milking shorthorn dairy farm. This herd was started
as a purebred milking shorthorn herd in 1940. In 1947 they entered their first
dairy show. They have won National and State Honors repeatedly. Among the
outstanding awards are: National leaders; Junior yearling class leader in both
milk and fat in 1957; State class leaders; grand champion cow in 1953-54-55-59
and 60; Grand Reserve and Grand Champion overall breeds, 1954; Premier breeders
award in 1954, 57 and 59--plus many class winners.

PAUL GOETZ

Paul Goetz was born in South Russia in the year 1878. As a young man of 20 he
immigrated to the United States. He travelled west to Mercer county in North
Dakota. There he was employed by the Mannhaven Mercantile Transportation Co.
While thus employed he invested his earnings in land which he bought from the
United States government.

In 1905 Paul Goetz married Anna Goetz. They lived at Krem where he owned a home
and operated a store and poolroom. He was also the postmaster. This position he
held for about 6 years when he quit and travelled to Oregon to seek his
fortune. He stayed but a short time there; then he returned to Krem. Sometime
in 1912 with visions of making a fortune raising wheat in Canada, he traded his
Mercer County land "sight unseen" for some Canadian acres. The venture was
doomed from the start when the Canadian land was not as productive as he had
hoped.

So in 1915-16 it was back to Mercer County for Mr. Goetz and his family. This
time he located in the young town of Beulah. There he built the present
Monroe's Bar building. It was built to house a butcher shop on the ground floor
with rooms to rent for lodging upstairs. The Goetz family lived in the present
Clarence Schneider house.

His Canadian dreams again took him and his family north--this time to
Forrest, Saskatchewan. But again his dreams did not materialize, so back to Zap
in Mercer County he brought his family. This time he operated a lumber and
hardware store as well as a garage, which dealt in Ford cars and Fordson
tractors.

In 1920 Mr. Goetz disposed of his property in Zap and moved to Hazen where he
built a garage (present Hazen Motor Co. building) and had the Ford and Fordson
agencies. This he operated for 16 years. During this time he had numerous other
business interests. He bought two houses and a farm--farmed some and was a
cattle buyer.

His public service included many years as mayor of Hazen during the late
twenties and early thirties; several terms as Mercer County Commissioner;
numerous church offices in the Peace Lutheran Church of Hazen and land
appraisal. He appraised land for the Federal Land Bank from 1944-46 for the
farmers affected by the Garrison Dam; for the Bureau of Reclamation; for the
Fort Clark Irrigation Project; for the Pipe Line Company of Tulsa, Oklahoma and
for the Northern Pacific Railroad at Hazen.

Mr. Paul Goetz passed away January 30, 1960 at Hazen. His wife Anna had
preceded him in death on August 30, 1936. In 1936 he was married to Mrs. Salome
Hildebrandt, who survives him.

The children of Paul and Anna Goetz are: Albina (Mrs. Forrest Stetson, Beulah;
Erwin Goetz, Hazen; Emil Goetz (deceased, Jan. 15, 1961) Hazen and Ruben Goetz,
Carson, N. D.

MICHAEL H. BOLAND

As with most of our pioneers, it was the desire to become land owners rather
than tenants that brought the Michael H. Bolands to settle in this area.

Born near Toronto, Ontario in 1837, Michael Boland moved, with his parents, to
Mower County, Minnesota in 1848. He married Susan Crowe, a native of Maryland,
at Johnstown, Iowa on August 5, 1876. To this union were born eleven children:
Mary, Nellie, John, Tom, Susan, Ray, Elizabeth, Kate, Ed, Maude and Mildred.

The family resided in south-eastern Minnesota until November, 1902 when they
boarded immigrant cars and set out for their newly acquired homestead on the
Dakota prairies.

The first winter was spent on a farm south of Glen Ullin and the following
April they moved to their homestead on section 18, township 143, range 87 in
Oliver County. Michael, with a son and two daughters, John, Mary and Nellie,
each proved up on a quarter section. Shacks were built on their respective
quarters near the center of the section so they would be settled together.

They had brought very little with them; however, they did have four horses,
three cows, two wagons, a breaking plow, a harrow and a meager supply of
household goods.

Breaking ten acres of ground and seeding flax composed the first spring's
farming. With succeeding years the farming became more diversified. A band of
brood mares was built up to supplement the family income.

Water was a definite problem. Numerous wells were dug but to no avail. All the
water used for household purposes and for the livestock was hauled a distance
of five miles daily.

The family lived mostly on home grown foods, buying very little except staples
such as flour and sugar. Very little clothing was purchased--most of it being
made by Mary, a seamstress by profession. What trading there was, was done in
Glen Ullin. They would go down one day, stay over-night and return the next
day. As can be readily imagined, this trip was not a frequent undertaking.

Of the boys, Ed was the rancher while John and Ray were the farmers. It was
John who bought the first tractor shipped into Beulah--an iron-wheeled
International. Tom was the hunter and trapper. To stimulate the sport of
hunting he imported the first pheasants to this area. These he acquired from
the State Game and Fish Department.

Michael lived to the age of 91 years, passing away on October 16, 1928. His
wife, Susan, passed away on April 6, 1935.

Of the eleven Boland children, four survive.

E. P. (ED) BOLAND

Nestled in the hills ten miles south-east of Beulah, on a bend in Otter Creek,
lies a ranch. It is not a big ranch as ranches go but it is stocked with
quality Hereford cattle, the result of decades of hard labor, planning,
economizing and perseverance.

To a few of the old-timers this ranch may be remembered as "the old Brander
place." To some it is the Open A Cross ranch or possibly the Quarter Circle
Lazy Four ranch. To the majority it would be recognized as the Boland ranch.
But to one man it had a different name. To him it was the "Hiding place from
the winds of Adversity."

This man was E. P. (Ed) Boland.

During his earthly sojourn the ultimate of this man's life, next to providing
for his family, was working in the outdoors. Whether it was caring for the cows
during calving season, putting up hay for winter feeding, building fences or
developing springs or wells, he was in his glory when working close to nature.
In his later years, when the fire of youth had begun to ebb, he was heard to
say of his ranch, "I like it here. It is quiet and peaceful and good for the
soul."

For him, dawn broke in a little Minnesota town called Blue Earth on May 8,
1893. Leaving his birth place he came, with his family, the Michael H.
Boland's, to this area shortly before the turn of the century.

The family homesteaded on a tract of land about seven miles south of the
present site of Beulah.

Educational facilities being what they were, his formal education was
meager--but to compensate for it he read incessantly in his spare time.
Throughout his hfe, books and magazines were constant companions and
consequently he acquired the distinction of being a self-educated man.

Early youth was spent on his father's homestead where the chief livelihood was
dry land farming and a band of brood mares. To him riding and breaking horses
was much more interesting than walking behind a plow or operating a threshing
rig. And besides, land was meant for grazing--not for cultivation. He left one
summer and worked on a cattle ranch near Chance, South Dakota. It was for only
one summer but from then on the die was cast. He returned to North Dakota and
began raising cattle on what had been a sheep ranch owned by John and Dan
Brander.

Rodeos were of prime interest. One of the founders of the Beulah Cowboy's
Reunion, he was the first arena director and in later years was an announcer.

Came sundown. Good Friday, April 15, 1960. The corral gate opened and Ed passed
through. "Gone Home'-the priest had said.

For his wife, Joan, and his children, Wayne and Joyce, remained the product of
his life, his ranch. But gone was the man.

THE COX CLAN

"Oh little voice ye call me back
To my far, far countrie
And nobody can hear ye spake,
Oh nobody but me.

        Mary L. McGuire

Nestled near the Irish Sea lies the County of Cork. In a small hamlet of this
soft climate lived Christopher Cox and his wife Catherine Reynolds on their
wisp of a farm. To them were born three sons, Michael, Patrick and some years
later Christopher. Christy abided with his aging parents, assisting them with
the farm duties until he completed his secondary education. As was the wont in
that time the young people drifted across the sea to Scotland where industrial
development was making great strides. He chose Glasgow as his destination. It
was there that he met and married Mary McGuire; fondly called "Molly" by those
dear to her, an Irish miss from the County Mayo, the daughter of Michael
McGuire and Bridget Lyons; a family of three boys and eight girls. Although the
Emerald Isle was dear to the heart of the McGuires the entire family left their
Celtic land behind them as well as fond memories of Mayo, Donegal and Galway to
find a new home in Glasgow, Scotland.

Because of political unrest Molly and Christy faced the tragedy of leaving
their homeland never to see it again. It was a quandary whether to go to
America or Australia. Feeling the strife of English rule the decision was
America. Many warm friends and relatives as well as cherished belongings and
memories were left behind. Amid tears and blessings the young family set sail,
a memorable 11 day journey on the S. S. Tuscania. An old sturdy wicker hamper,
a farewell gift from friends remains in the family, a symbol of that parting.
The Cox family traveled the N. P. Line to N. Dak. where they were welcomed by
Molly's sister and her family, the R. E. O'Neils.

From Dunn Center they came to Beulah and settled on a farm east of town. Mr.
Cox began working in the coal mine to bolster his farm income to aid in the
support of his family, now grown to eight children; four sons, Michael Redmond,
Hugh, Thomas Patrick, and Christy; four daughters: Bridget, Teresa, Catherine
and Mary. The family enjoyed their home life and the years of "just growing
up". Christy often remarked "Indeed ye are a clannish lot" God was kind to all,
there was an abundance of health, happy family life and well being, although
not great monetary wealth. All were graduated by the Beulah High School. All
furthered their education to their various occupations. The war years left
heartache as Mary and Christy added service stars to their window. Mary felt
deep grief as she saw her sons depart for combat, little realizing she would
not be here to welcome them home. Pat's wife, Ann Miachel Cox, and baby, Mary
Ann, were also lost to the family by death.

        Christopher Cox

Molly and Christy had kept alive the pride in the family heritage by imparting
to their children the appreciation of Erin with misty tales of banshees,
leprechauns and Shillelaghs. Many an Easter dawn saw a sleepy child blinking
hopefully at the horizon. For some, the sun does dance on The Joyous Easter
Morn, according to the Irish tale and Christy Cox.

Until World War II eliminated the supply, Shamrocks grown on Ireland's turf
proudly decorated each member of the family on St. Pat's day. They endowed to
their children a sensitivness to their Catholic religion, lasting family
friendships and many wonderful memories.

The descendants of the late Christopher and Mary Cox, are happily looking
foreward to a gathering of the clan in Beulah June 1964.

The family of Jess and Teresa Thompson; Susan, Gregory, Russell, and David. The
family of Herb and Catherine Scherbinske; Bruce, Wynne, and Kittra. Also Mary
Cox, all residing in Beulah. The family of Pat and Nanette Cox, Thomas, Joseph,
Kellie Ann and Michael. The family of Redmond and Monette Cox; John,
Christopher and Dennis, all residing in Billings, Montana. The family of Donald
and Bridget Ording; William Christopher, and Carolyn. The family of Hugh and
Rosemary Cox; James and Richard all residing in Seattle, Washington. Christy
and Mildred Cox of San Diego, California.

        Dad     Mr. & Mrs. Christy Cox

JOHN AND KAROLINA DAFFEE

        Standing--Left to Right: Albert, Philip, Esther, Otto, Samuel.
        Sitting--Left to Right: Bertha, Eva, Mrs. Daffee, Mr. Daffee,
        Martha, Pauline.

John Daffee was born in Peterstahl, S. Russia on November 17, 1884 to Karl and
Margareta, nee Fuchs, Daffee. John is the only surviving member of a family of
3 brothers and 5 sisters. In 1905 he married Karolina Dietrich, who passed away
in 1911. To this union were born 3 children: one died in infancy, John (now
deceased) and Rose (deceased) who married Albert Boeckel, they had three
daughters Violet, Loretta, and Hilma.

Like many of our Mercer County Homesteaders, John decided to come to America to
file for a 160 acre homestead. In 1906 John took up a homestead 8 miles north
of Beulah which he farmed until his retirement in 1945.

Karolina Flemmer Daffee was born in Gleuckstahl, South Russia on January 14,
1890, to Philip and Katharina, nee Kessler, Flemmer. The Philip Flemmers had 6
sons and 2 daughters; of these, 2 brothers and 2 sisters are still living. As a
young girl Karolina immigrated to America with her parents in June 1901. They
lived at Lehr for two years. In 1903 her family homesteaded 15 miles south of
Beulah.

On November 16, 1911 Karolina Flemmer was united in marriage with John Daffee
at the Neu Gleuckstahl Congregational Church by Pastor Fred Anhorn. This union
was blessed with ten children; Pauline, Mrs. Emil Morast, Terry, Mont.--has 3
daughters, Adeline, Beverly, and Karen; August, whom our Lord called Home at
the age of sixteen; Eva, Mrs. John Wegner, of El Nido, Calif.; Philip married
Alma Weil and they live in Bismarck, N. D., their children are; Darlene,
Vernon, Brenda, Kennedy and Thomas; Bertha, Mrs. Paul Neubauer, Portland,
Oregon has four children, Shirley, Jane, Darlene and Roger; Martha, Mrs. Pere
Pearson, Philip, S. Dak., has 3 sons; Philip, Alvin, and Lester; Esther, Mrs.
Irvin Morris, Merced, Calif., has one son, John (Buddy); Otto, Merced, Calif.,
one son, Dan. Samuel, married Edna Mohl and lives in N. Charleston, S. Caro.
they have 5 children, Larry, Betty Jean, De Lilah, Carol and Candice; Albert
married Sharon Schmidt. They live in Bismarck, N. Dak., and have one son, Allan.

The Daffees had the same trials and tribulations that were common to most of
our Mercer County Homesteaders. Farming during the early years of their married
life was no easy task. Horses and a man's back were the only power that ran the
farm machinery. As there was no town of Beulah at that time, they did their
trading at Kasmer. They knew the heartbreak of: the death of one of their
youngsters, the hot dry years of the drought, and the low prices of the 30's,
(they can remember when they sold their hogs for 2 cents a lb.), and like so
many of our German-Russian immigrants, the learning to speak the English
language has not been easy. With all of these trials, one must truly say that
the Lord has been good to them. They have had good health, there was always
food for their table and above all, they have had their faith in that "The Lord
Will Provide". Church has always been an important factor in their family life.
They are members of The Assembly of God Church at Golden Valley.

        Mr. and Mrs. John Daffee on their 50th Anniversary

The Daffees will always remember their Golden Wedding Anniversary, which they
celebrated in 1961. It was on this happy occasion that the whole family had
gathered together for the first time in 25 years. For this wonderful experience
they are very thankful. Truly their "cup runneth over".

ELI GUNDERSON

The year following the coming of the Eids, another family of young people
arrived from Bryant, S. D. and settled on claims, north of Beulah. They were
Eli and David Gunderson and two sisters whom we knew in later years as Mrs.
Dave Kuster and Mrs. Trousdale. Of these, we know Eli Gunderson best. He went
into the General Merchandise business in 1904-5 in the community known as
Kronthal today.

In January, 1909, Eli Gunderson and Bertha Anna Eid became man and wife at
Krem, and the following year, 1910, the Gundersons moved their store from
Kronthal to Kasmer, which was then a growing community with a bank and several
other business establishments. This was to be rather shortlived business
venture because by 1912-13, the Northern Pacific Railroad was completing its
rail service through the towns, of Stanton, Hazen, Zap, Golden Valley. Zap is
where the business places of Kasmer decided to move, and so with the bank being
established there, Mr. Gunderson decided to move his store too. In 1914, they
built a beautiful home which stands uninhabited today.

After a few years, Mr. Gunderson sold the store to Abe Kamins, who stayed with
the business about 20 years. In the meantime, the Lucky Strike mine operators
were enticing anyone with a few dollars to spare, to invest them in the mining
business. With the advent of the Dakota Collieries Mine, Lucky Strike closed
its pits. This was a terrific blow, not only to Mr. Gunderson, but nearly every
place of business, because the miners left many unpaid debts and many left town
for good.

Nothing daunted Mr. Gunderson however. He turned his time and thoughts to
opening a small mine west of Zap which he later sold to Kamins & Co., and
opened another one south of Zap, which later became the Bob Hogarth Mine.

At one time between mining interests, Mr. Gunderson became restless and
purchased a knitting machine. Thinking perhaps that was an easier way to make a
living, but being wholly unused to being confined to the house, this work
became boresome, and he gave it up after several unsuccessful tries at knitting
socks and mittens.

For a few years Mr. Gunderson was partially retired, attending only to the
various interests he had in and around Zap. In 1946, he became ill and passed
away that same year. His widow, Bertha, (the couple had no children) stayed on
in her home for a number of years, but is now a resident of St. Luke's Nursing
Home in Dickinson. She has reached the age of 84 years.

        Agnes Eid Hipfner

EID FAMILY HISTORY

In the fall of 1903, Mr. & Mrs. Christofer Eid left Hawley, Minn. to live on
their homestead 12 miles north of Beulah. With them were their children, Ida,
Bertha and Carl; also Dorthia Halvorson, a close friend of the family. She had
made her home with the Eid family off and on when they lived in Minn. The
Christofer Eid's oldest son, Sivert, who was married and also lived near
Hawley, came to N. D. at this same time. The Sivert Eids had three children
born in Minn., Chester, Oscar and Isaac. Ingelow, Thea (died in infancy) and
Agnes (Mrs. Joe Hipfner) were born in N. D.

The Eids--Sivert, Bertha (Mrs. Eli Gunderson), Ida and Carl all homesteaded
north of Beulah, as did Dorthia Halvorson. Christofer's homestead centered the
"Eid settlement."

After braving the hardships of three long winters, the entire family moved to
the West Coast in the fall of 1907. They settled at Burns, Ore. but this was
not to be a long stay. During the winter, Mrs. Eid passed away, leaving two
tiny children, Ingelow 4 years and Agnes, 6 months old, besides her husband and
the three boys. Mr. Eid decided to come back to his homestead in the spring of
1908. The task of caring for his family fell to his mother and Dorthia
Halvorson. Isaac and Oscar went to Minnesota to live with maternal grandparents.

At that time there were no railroads and their nearest postoffice was Krem.
Mail and supplies were brought by steamboat from Bismarck to Mannhaven from
where they were distributed to various points. Expansion and Ree were also
points at which the boats unloaded their goods and took boat loads of grain to
market.

Several years after returning from Oregon, Christofer Eid passed away and was
returned to Minnesota for interment, but Mrs. Eid remained with the family
until her death ten years later.

Sivert Eid took over the farming interests of his parents and with the help of
Chester and Ingelow, managed the entire farming operations.

In 1940, Sivert Eid became ill and after a short time passed away. This left
Chester and Ingelow to take over their father's farming interests. Since then,
Ingelow is deceased and Chester makes his home in rural Beulah. Agnes and her
family, the Joe Hipfners, live in Beulah, and the home place is occupied by
Ingelow's widow and son, Leroy.

        John and Elizabeth Henderson
        Isabell (Mrs. Manley Thompson) Florence (Mrs. A. Roedell)

A LITTLE BIT OF EARLY HISTORY OF MERCER COUNTY AS IT CONCERNS
THE JOHN HENDERSON FAMILY

The earliest settlers that established themselves in the Dakotas west of the
Missouri River were the cattle and sheep ranchers. To protect them against the
raids of hostile Indians, who resented the encroachments; military posts were
established, one of which was Ft. Rice, Dec. 15, 1885. Young John Henderson,
Canadian origin, and Elizabeth Zimmerman, whose parents came via covered wagon
from Nebraska, were married and engaged in the stock and sheep ranching
business.

During 10 years of residency at Ft. Rice 5 children were born, the first son
passed away at four months. Next in order of birth were: Florence May (Mrs.
Andrew Roedell); Isabell Frances (Mrs. Manly Thompson); Myrtle Mathilda (Mrs.
Earl Ferguson); and Albert Jay.

John had not taken advantage of homestead rights, and new land adjacent to
Beulah attracted him. He squatted on land he soon learned was school land and
not open to entry. Several years later they homesteaded on the north side of
the Knife River where they built a more commodious home for the growing family.

The family arrived at their new location in a covered wagon bringing with them
a few cows, horses and machinery that was a must with a stock rancher: a mowing
machine and rake. These days men started ranches with long days and short bank
accounts. Mrs. Henderson: who died on Nov. 25, 1942 at Nampa, Idaho at age 70,
left a detailed description of their first home which was a combination of sod
and dugout structure. In this dwelling their next two children, Roy James, and
Wesley James were born. The caved in remains of their first home are still
visible near the side of a steep bank on the south side of the river across
from Beulah. John William and Walter Lewis were born after they moved to the
new home on the homestead on the north side of Knife River. This Knife River
Ranch at one time fed as many as 160 head of cattle and 1200 sheep.

To this family nine children were born, eight of them still living and active.
In the month of September 1961 they assembled in a happy family reunion near
Nampa, Idaho. John Henderson died at age 89 in Oct. 1948 at Pendleton, Oregon.

Where the Hendersons spent their early life it was many miles to a medical
doctor. Mrs. Henderson educated herself to become a practical nurse and
mid-wife. Many a youngster, still living owes his life and that of his mother
to her expert care at the time of his birth.

JACOB AND MARIA EISENBEIS

Jacob Eisenbeis was born on January 30, 1881, in Gleuckstahl, South Russia, a
son of Jacob and Magdlina, nee Walz, Eisenbeis. His father died while he was
still living in Russia. In 1893 his grandparents decided to immigrate to the
United States and brought the three brothers; Jacob, John, and Christ, with
them. They settled in Bowdle, South Dakota until 1903, when Jacob went to
Freeman, South Dakota to find work. In 1906 his younger brother John had taken
up a homestead in Mercer County, North Dakota. This sounded like a wonderful
opportunity, so Jacob moved once more. In 1910 he filed his homestead claim on
160 acres of land northwest of Beulah, which was near his brothers farm.

On February 12, 1911, he married Maria Koerner of Freeman, South Dakota and
brought her back with him to his homestead. Here they not only farmed, but Jake
also ran a small implement business. He sold Hart-Parr Tractors, battery
operated Crosley radios and gas refrigerators. He loaned money to many of his
friends and neighbors who were in dire financial straits. In the 30's he had
the opportunity to help many. He was an exceptionally good-hearted man. His
faith in his fellow-man will never be forgotten, by those he helped in those
desperate and troubled times.

He and his wife were active and faithful members of the Peace Lutheran Church,
northwest of Beulah. They lived on their farm in Mercer County until 1943, when
they retired and moved to Freeman, South Dakota. Jake died in 1946. His wife,
Maria, still makes her home at Freeman. Jake's other brother Christ also lived
at Freeman.

The Eisenbeis's mother, Magdlina, had stayed in Russia and later married Christ
Opp. They came to America in 1901, therefore the Eisenbeis brothers had one
half-brother Simon Opp of Nampa, Idaho; and two half-sisters; Mrs. John Fischer
of Beulah and Mrs. Emil Diede of Eureka, South Dakota.

Although the Jake Eisenbeis's had no children of their own, they were always
more than generous and good to all of their nieces and nephews. The John
Eisenbeis children will never forget the many acts of kindness and generosity
afforded them by this thoughtful and unselfish couple; Jacob and Maria
Eisenbeis.

THE JOHN EISENBEIS FAMILY HISTORY

        Standing--Hilds, Ervin, Walter, Art and Clara.
        Sitting--Reuben, Mr. and Mrs. John Eisenbeis, Raymond.

John Eisenbeis was born in Gleuckstahl, South Russia on February 8, 1885, the
son of Jacob and Magdlina, nee Walz, Eisenbeis. His father died while he was
still a young lad in Russia. He came over to America in 1893. In 1903 John came
to North Dakota and worked in the Mannhaven area for a number of years. In 1906
he took up a homestead seventeen miles northwest of Beulah.

Before the railroad came to Mercer County, John had to haul his wheat to
Garrison and Mannhaven. It usually took a few days to make the trip with a team
of horses pulling a wagon or sled. If a snow storm came up during one of these
trips they would have to stay in town or with the nearest farmer. These were
the days when law and order were not of the best. Everyone had to look out for
himself. One time when John had left the place for a few days, he came back to
find that his bachelor shack on his homestead was stolen. For awhile John
served as a Constable and it was his duty to help maintain law and order. When
some clothing had been stolen from the merchant at Kasmer, he was called upon
to search a cabin in the vicinity. When he arrived at the suspects home he was
told that the suspect was sick in bed. He took one look at the patient and
personally helped him get out of bed and make a speedy recovery. There under
the bedding was the stolen goods.

On January 13, 1910, John married Lena Ries, daughter of Jacob and Frena, nee
Schrag, Ries. Lena had been born on Dec. 31, 1886 at Freeman, South Dakota.
Here she grew to womanhood.

John brought his young bride home to his little house on the homestead. A few
years later they built a new, and larger home. Little did this young woman
dream that she would live on this very farm for the next fifty-three years.
Over the years they bought additional farm land and this 160 acre homestead
grew to a 560 acre farm. It was on this farm that all the Eisenbeis children
were born and raised. They all worked on the farm. Although not all of them
became "tillers of the soil", they were all taught how to farm and to farm
well. Taking pride in your work was instilled in them at an early age. The
Eisenbeis children attended the nearby rural Slope School.

John was a charter member of the Peace Lutheran Church. which is located 16
miles northwest of Beulah. It was built in 1910. It was in this little country
church that all their children were baptized and confirmed. In this church John
and Lena renewed their marriage vows on July 10, 1960, when they celebrated
their Golden Wedding Anniversary.

John passed away on May 21, 1963, and thus life on the farm came to an end for
Mrs. Eisenbeis. She now makes her home with her eldest daughter, Clara. She
remembers the past with mixed feelings of remorse and satisfaction.

There are seven children in the Eisenbeis family, eighteen grandchildren and
one great-grandchild.

Walter of Tulelake, California, is a veteran of World War II. He served in the
Army from 1942-1946, much of this time was spent in Germany. After his
discharge he won a homestead in Tulelake, California. He is married to the
former Elizabeth Aulman. He was born on July 22, 1914.

Art was born on January 25, 1917. He lives on and operates a farm fourteen
miles northwest of Beulah. He is single.

Erwin was born on July 1, 1921. During World War II he served in the U.S.
Infantry in the Asiatic Pacific Theatre with the 63rd Artillery Battalion,
stationed at Kumma, Japan. He married Evelyn Morast and farms on the Jacob
Eisenbeis farm 15 miles northwest of Beulah. They have six children; Betty Lou,
Carol, Myron, Mary Ann, Julie Ann, and Erwin Wayne.

Raymond born on February 21, 1923, is in business for himself, he is an
Electrician at Beulah. He is married to the former Esther Ricker. They have
three children; Clyde, Allen, and Jo Ann.

Reuben born October 15, 1924 lives on the John Eisenbeis family farm. He is
married to Mabel Walz and they have a son, Lyle.

Clara, Mrs. Albert Koemer lives on a farm at Marion, South Dakota. Their son
Elroy is married to the former Donna Miller. Clara was born on November 26,
1912.

Hilda born April 10, 1920. She married Reuben Bauer and they live at Zap, N.
Dak., where Reuben is employed by North American Coal Co. They have seven
children and one grandchild. Janice, (Mrs. Ronald Gunsch has a daughter Lynell)
Judy, Leroy, Sandra, Ruby, Marvin and Shelly are all still living at home.

LIFE IN A NORTH DAKOTA SODDIE

        This picture of Mag and Ezra by their Soddie was taken May 7, 1909

The ride was a long and bumpy one and as Mag sat on the hard, uncomfortable
wagon seat, she couldn't help thinking: That she and Ezra must have completely
lost their minds to have left their beloved State of Missouri. Ezra, her
husband had always dreamed of having a piece of land of his own. The 160 acres
of land that had enticed them to make the move just wasn't worth it. How could
they possibly live in this "god-forsaken" country? Why they had driven miles
and miles and hadn't even seen a tree or passed a fellow human-being. Mag's
sister Ora and her husband Will Stephens had met the emigrant train at Glen
Ullin. They had even helped them load their belongings onto the wagon, but they
hadn't prepared them for the wide expanse of the rolling N. D. prairies. As Mag
looked at Ora she was filled with the determination that if her sister could
take it so could she.

The Emigs filed their homestead claim on Sec. 6-142-87. They soon built a 10 x
16 wood shack to live in until the sodbustin and seeding was done. These were
trying days for both the Emigs, for they had never been away from home and they
were desperately homesick. Mag wrote home to her mother, "I think our horses
are smarter than we are for last nite they ran away, I think they're headed for
Missouri. I wish I was running with them." But she didn't run, she faced the
loneliness head-on: as Ezra walked behmd his plow and with each acre of virgin
soil he turned over, he became prouder and more determined to make a success of
this venture. Slowly the prairies lost the look of complete desolation and took
on the warm friendliness of a new-born pup.

When Ezra built Mag her "New Soddie" (pictured above), they both began to
suspect that their transplanted roots were beginning to take hold. Then with
the birth of their first child on March 17, 1911, they were sure of it. They're
called this little St. Patricks bundle of joy, Mary Ellen. Now Mag had no more
time for loneliness. What with the new baby to care for; chores to be done;
meals to be cooked (on her Majestic coal-range) mail to be gotten (this was a
23 mile trip to Nesbit and back); and of course one had entertaining to do.
(Nothing fancy just wholesome) new friends (of many nationalities) were made.
Sometimes language was a bit of a barrier, but this was soon overcome, for they
all had one thing in common--they were settlers seeking a way to make a good
honest living. If a neighbor was sick you dropped your work and went over and
helped, if their barn was destroyed you had a barn-raising, if there was a
death, you comforted. Thus life in the Aplin Community became one big
adventure. In 1915 Ezra and Mag built a new 2-story frame house and altho Mag
was "pleased as punch" over the new home, she knew she could never forget the
early days in the "soddie." Here she had become a mother and she had added to
her title of wife the well-earned words--helpmate and partner. All their
regrets were a thing of the past for they knew this was where the Lord had
meant them to be. They were home!

THE EZRA EMIG FAMILY HISTORY

        Mary Ellen      Margie       Joan                Jacqueline
                                Grand-daughter      Great Grand-daughter

Ezra Emig was born January 10, 1881 at Astoria, Illinois, the son of Jesse and
Mary Emig. The Emigs moved to Knob Noster Missouri in 1892. Here Ezra met
Margaret Knaus, the daughter of Geo. Washington Knaus and Martha Ellen
Landingham. Ezra and Margaret were married on July 9, 1903. Margaret's
birth-date was March 20, 1882.

On March 31, 1906 the Emigs came to Oliver County where they filed their
homestead claim. Both of the Emig children were born on this homestead: Mary
Ellen on March 17, 1911 and Margie on July 16, 1921. The girls enjoyed life on
the farm. They both spent most of their childhood days and most of their adult
lives on farms. They attended rural schools; and graduated from the Beulah High
School--Mary Ellen in 1929 and Margie in 1938. Both of the girls joined the
teaching profession: Mary Ellen taught for approx. 20 years; Margie taught for
8 years.

Mary Ellen married Joe Schulz in 1937, they had one daughter Joan, (Mrs.
Kemmeth E. Stephens). Joan, like her mother., attended D. S. T. C., graduating
in 1960. She is teaching at Stevensville, Montana. She has a baby daughter,
Jacqueline Joan.

The Emigs never moved off the homestead and they were fortunate enough to be
able to celebrate their Golden Wedding Anniversary (July, 1953) on their
original claim. They celebrated this joyous occasion with an "open house" to
which many friends and neighbors came by to extend their congratulations and
best wishes. Ezra and Mag were active in the Aplin Community affairs, they were
members of the farmers Union Local and the Congregational Church. Mag has been
a member of the Aplin Homemakers since 1935. The Emig's were blessed with
reasonably good health until 1953 when Ezra had major surgery--after which he
was never able to farm actively again. So Margie began to shoulder the
responsibility of running the farm. Ezra's condition became progressively worse
and he passed away in June 21, 1957. A year and four months later, the Emigs
lost another member of the family when Mary Ellen succumbed on Oct. 27, 1958.

Life goes on--Mag and Margie "busy as beavers." They have no hired help.
Margie is in charge of the farming operations; while Mag, her mother, takes
care of the household duties. Together they are continuing what Ezra and Mag
started 58 years ago.

FREDERICK AND FANNIE KOESTER

Frederick Wm. and Fannie Koester were both born in the state of Iowa. They
attended grade school and grew to adulthood in the same community. On June 7,
1899 they were united in marriage. Three children were born while they were
still living in Iowa; Earl (died as an infant), Roy and Bessie. In 1904 or 1905
the family moved to N. Dak.

After they arrived they made their home with the Arthur Smith's. Later Koester
decided to work for Barney Evans and moved in with them. They stayed here until
they had built a sod house on their 80 acre homestead. This was the birthplace
of two more of their children, Gladys and Freddie. Later on a frame house was
built on this homestead.

The Koesters were active in Community affairs. Fannie was clerk for Greenvale
School Dist. for 40 years. She was also a telephone operator for the first
telephone lines and an officer of the Grange and Royal Neighbors (Charter
member), and Homemakers. Mr. Koester was a member of the school board for many
years and an officer of the Grange and Woodman Lodge.

They were janitors for the Grange Hall, where dances were held every week (the
hall was 1/2 mile north of the Koester farm). Later they held dances in their
home as a recreational center.

Mr. Koester was a harness maker by trade and also worked with Robert Johnson's
steamer, threshing machine for many years.

The Koesters celebrated their 50th Wedding Anniversary in 1949.

Mr. Koester passed away on Sept. 9, 1950. Mrs. Koester passed away on Sept. 16,
1958.

GEORGE AND GLADYS CLINE

Gladys Koester married George D. Cline on April 4, 1926. She has lived in the
home community all of her life. George and Gladys were blessed with six
children; Kenneth, Leora, Lucille (died at the age of five), Margaret, Glenn,
and Donald.

The Clines farmed most of their married life with the exception of the years
1943 to 1946. (They ran a grocery store in Beulah). In 1946 they bought the
Robert Johnson farm. This they farmed until the fall of 1963. They were
planning to retire and move into their new home in Beulah, when George suffered
a fatal heart attack in 1963. The house is now completed. Gladys moved into her
new home in 1964.

DR. ARTUR EWERT

Dr. Artur Ewert was born Jan. 1, 1911 in Poland. He attended school in the town
of Kalisch from 1921 to 1930. He then continued his education at the University
of Poznan, Poland, from 1930-1936. From 1936 to 1937 he was in the Polish army
for his military service. At the end of this period he interned and received
his license to practice medicine in Lodz, Poland, in March of 1939.

On November 29, 1941 Dr. Ewert was married to Louisa Weber. The following
February Dr. Ewert was drafted into the German army. He served with the army on
the East Front from Oct. 1942 until he was captured by the Russians May 8,
1945. He was held captive in Breslau, East Germany until July 1945. After
trying for one whole week to cross East to West Germany, across a 4 mile
stretch of meadow and each day being turned back--finally on Aug. 11 he found
the guards asleep and was able to join his family in West Germany. His family
had evacuated to West Germany in 1945. There he practiced medicine at
Moenchroeden near Coburg until July of 1956 when he and his family emigrated to
America.

He established a residence at Goodrich, N. D. in August of 1956. In order to
obtain his license to practice in the United States, he interned in the
Bismarck Hospital under the auspices of the Quain & Ramstad Clinic. He became
licensed in 1959.

In August of 1960, Dr. & Mrs. Ewert and their children Ingrid, Heide-Marie,
Karen, Gerd and Angelika moved to Beulah. Here he is the official Northern
Pacific Railroad doctor and also the official government doctor for the Twin
Butte Indian Agency. He is resident doctor for the Beulah Nursing Home and the
Joachim Memorial Home as well as taking care of his regular practice.

Dr. and Mrs. Ewert and their family are members of the Baptist Church.

Dr. Ewert's mother and two sisters reside in Calgary, Alberta, Canada. His
brother practices medicine in Vancouver, British Columbia.

GEORGE SLOWEY

George Slowey was born in Phillips, Wisconsin on Dec. 8, 1873. He came to
Mercer County in 1893. At that time he farmed in the Kasmer vicmity. In 1896 he
married Josephine Lapenski in Minneapolis. During these years he was an ardent
amateur photographer.

When the railroad came through Zapin 1913 the Kasmer businessmen moved south to
Zap. Among them was George Slowey. He erected a combination pool hall and
barber shop there. From 1915 to 1919 George Slowey and J. B. Field were
partners in a farm implement business.

After this venture Mr. Slowey moved to Beulah, where he opened an International
Harvester Agency, which he later sold to his former partner, J. B. Field. Here
he was also agent for the State Farm Mutual Insurance Co. George Slowey was
County Commissioner for many years. He had Beulah's interest at heart, and we
have him to thank for some of our early road improvement. He was one of the
early group of Beulah boosters, along with A. D. Brown and P. S. Chaffee.

The Slowey home was foster home for many children. It was also a boarding and
rooming house for teachers. The food was of the finest and "cleanliness was
next to Godliness" as a virtue.

        Eldon, Reine, Delmar, Mardla, Henry
                        Glenn

THE REINHART GRISHKOWSKY FAMILY

Reinhart Grishkowsky, son of Mr. and Mrs. John Grishkowsky, nee Margaret
Schneider, was born on October 18, 1902, at Otter Creek, North Dakota. In 1907
he moved with his parents to Fruitland, Idaho, where he grew to manhood.

Martha, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Gramm, nee Anna Scheidt, was born Dec.
20, 1902 at Tendayl, South Dakota. Her parents moved to Oliver County and it
was here that she spent most of her girlhood days. In 1920 she moved with her
parents to Fruitland, Idaho, where she met her future husband, Reinhart
Grishkowsky.

While in Boise, Idaho Martha took up Home Economics and sewing. She also took
up 2 years of music in Beulah. Her teacher being Marta Kaasik, and Edith
Lovestrom.

Reinhart went to Ed Cox Engineering School at Portland, Oregon. They also both
took a course in bookkeeping from Mrs. Ed Vasicheck.

Martha and Reinhart Grishkowsky were united in marriage on March 2, 1924, by
Pastor H. L. Westendorf at St. Lukes Lutheran Church of New Plymouth, Idaho.
This union was blessed with four sons; Delmar, Eldon, Henry and Glenn.

The Grishkowskys moved back to Mercer County on September 12, 1924 and farmed
south of Beulah for nine years. It was during the time that they lived on the
farm that their three oldest sons were born. In 1932, the Grishkowskys, their 3
boys and one truck, moved mto Beulah. This was at the height of the depression
when both jobs and dollars were very scarce. Reine worked at the Dakota
Colleries for the next two years. He bought the Beulah Truck Line from Ed.
Carmichael in 1935. He is still actively engaged in the operation of this
trucking business. It was during these years (1942) that their fourth and
youngest son, Glenn, was born.

In 1942 they leased the Carmichael Coal Mine and later in 1944 they purchased
it from L. F. Temme. They operated the underground mine, with a small mule
transporting the coal from the underground, until 1945, when they started
stripping operations and sold the coal locally until April, 1963. At this time
the Knife River Coal Co. leased their Coal rights and the Grishkowsky Coal Mine
became a "Business of the past".

The Grishkowsky boys are all married and on "their own." Delmar attended the
Beulah School and served in the U.S. Navy Seabees from 1944-1946, spending most
of the time in Okinawa. After his discharge he attended Metropolitan business
College at Chicago. He married the former, Charlotte Dietrich in 1947. They
have two sons; Steven and Scott. They made their home in Beulah until 1953 when
they moved back to Chicago where Delmar is an Assistant Engineer for the Lanz
Construction Co.

Eldon enlisted in the U.S. Navy in 1944. He was stationed at Dallas, Texas,
where he served in the Navy Electronic Program. After his discharge, he went
back to complete his high school education and graduated from the Beulah High
School in 1948. He then took a one year Electrician Course at the Wahpeton
State School of Science. In 1950 he married Eleanor Bitterman of Zap. They have
twin sons; Vance and Blair. Eldon is in business for himself at Beulah. (he is
a master electrician.)

Henry graduated from the Beulah High School in 1944. He attended Concordia
College at Moorhead, Minnesota for 2 years and later attended Wartburg College,
Waverly, Iowa, for 2 years. After his graduation from Wartburg College he
attended Wartburg Seminary, at Dubuque, Iowa for three years. He interned in
Ministry for one year at Aberdeen, S. Dak. He graduated from the Seminary on
May, 1955. He was ordained into the ministry on June 12th, 1955, at his home
church, The Zion Lutheran Church of Beulah. His first Pastorate was Trinity
Evangelical Lutheran Church of Derinda, and St. Pauls Lutheran Church of
Hannover, Illinois, where he and his wife; the former Rosebud Hyink of Palas
Park, Illinois; are faithfully serving to the present time.

Glenn attended, Beulah School and graduated from the Beulah High School in
1961. He attended the N.D.S.U. and Interstate Business College at Fargo. In
1963 he became employed in the Bookkeeping Dep't at the Hebron Brick Co. He is
married to the former Gretchen Pulles of Hazen. They have one daughter, Lori
Ann.

For the past thirty two years Reine and Martha have lived in Beulah, with the
exception of one year (1961) when they lived in Hazen. They have always been
active and faithful members of the Zion Lutheran Church. Martha has been a
member of the Zion Mission Circle for 21 years. She was a member of the German
Ladies Aid for 9 years. Reine has served as deacon and at the present time is
on the Church Building Committee. He has always taken a great deal of interest
in the local Boy Scouts Program, of which his sons were past members and 2 of
his grandsons are present members. His greatest contribution being; that
anytime the group needs to be hauled anywhere he is always willing and ready to
take them with his truck. Over the years he has made about 62 trips for the
scouts. These trips are something that he looks forward to every year, and does
gladly.

The Grishkowskys feel grateful for the way the Lord has blessed them through
the years. They survived the "lean years" of the depression, they have four
healthy sons of which they are truly proud, and though they have had to work
hard, life has been interesting and worthwhile. In 1962 they built a new home
at Beulah. Here the Grishkowsky boys, their wives, and their five grand
children are always welcome.

Martha and Reine always look forward with eager anticipation to their family
gatherings. Some of their other interests and hobbies are fishing, gardening,
traveling and photography.

MR. AND MRS FRED GALLOWAY

        Fred, Lillian & Charles    Jerry

Fred was born Oct. 26, 1914 on a farm in Rolette County, son of Charles Arthur
and Cora Mae (Gailfus) Galloway. He attended country school and graduated from
Rolla High School in 1933. He farmed for sometime, also worked as a trucker and
in a grocery store.

On May 10, 1939 he married Lillian Baerwald, daughter of Samuel and Amanda
(Krueger) Baerwald at Cando, N. Dak. The Galloways have two sons--Jerry a law
student at the U.N.D., Grand Forks, N. Dak.; and Charles Arthur at home, a
Junior in High School.

Fred has been in the grocery business for many years. He worked at Rolla, N.
D., was transferred to Cando, N. D. as an Ass't. Manager, then transferred to
Beach, N. D. where he was the manager. In 1942 he bought the former  I. G.A.
store from Mrs. Sovereign and has been Beulah's Super Valu man since then.

Lillian's parents came to N. D. in 1908 and settled near Upham, N. D. (McHenry
Co.) where Lillian was born. She has three sisters: Mrs. Ella Baszler and Mrs.
Fred Weigum of Hazen and Mrs. Ernest J. Sailer of Bismarck. Her father was an
old Country school teacher. He did some teaching in N. D. teaching German and
Religion and was very interested in music, directing a choir all his life. He
lived in Mercer County for about four years (near Mannhaven). He died at
Egeland, N. D. Oct. 1930. Lillian's mother passed away at Hazen, Dec. 25, 1960.

Fred has taken an active interest in the growth and progress of his town. He
has been: a member of the School Board the past twenty years, a member of the
Chamber of Commerce, a member of the Board of Directors Nursing Home and a
member of The Beulah Fire Department.

They are members of Zion Lutheran Church where Fred served in several offices
of the Church and Lillian teaches Sunday School and is a member of the A.L.C.W.

Fred's parents were born in Northern N. D. where his mother still resides, as
does a sister Mrs. Abner Desrud. His other sister Mrs. Don MacKinnon lives in
Seattle, Wash. His father passed away in 1931. He has a great Uncle who
celebrated his 101st. birthday March 24, 1964. This uncle came to N. D. as a
young boy.

        Standing--Left to Right: Philip, Theodor, Martha, Leo, Ernest.
        Seated--Left to Right: Otto, Walter, Jacob Hafner, Mrs. Jacob Hafner,
        Herbert, Harry.

MR. AND MRS. JACOB HAFNER

When Catherine the Great ruled Russia, she sent agents to Germany to induce the
German people to move to South Russia and till the soil. Her inducements were:
religious freedom, free land and military exemptions for 100 years. At that
time--some time after 1784, the grandparents of Mr. & Mrs. Jacob Hafner
migrated from Germany to South Russia. They settled on the Crimean peninsula on
the Black Sea.

When the 100 years were up, many of these German people, wanting their freedoms
to continue, did not stay in Russia or return to Germany but immigrated to
America. Among these immigrants to America in 1874 were Mr. and Mrs. Henry
Hafner, (the parents of Jacob Hafner) and their family, and about the same time
came Mr. & Mrs. Philip Schnabel (parents of Mrs. Jacob Hafner). Both families
settled in Dakota Territory, 30 miles north of Yankton in Hutchinson County.
There were many families that came to Yankton from South Russia during the
years 1873-74 & 75. Among them besides the Hafners and Schnabels, were these
pioneer Mercer County residents: Hubers, Isaaks, Kellers, Mettlers, Teskes,
Wiedenmeyers and Bohrers.

The Henry Hafner homestead was 4 miles west of what is now Freeman, S. D.--this
was known as the Heilbron area. They were Lutherans.

This is the Henry Hafner family record:

Heinrich Hafner                 Born: Aug. 13, 1826;
Died: July 22, 1890 at Freeman, S. D.
Jacobina Hafner, nee Luese      Born Dec. 27, 1830;
 Died: Feb. 1, 1897 at Freeman, S. D.

The children are the following:

Martin                          Born: Jan. 23, 1850
 Died: Feb. 23, 1906, Freeman, S. D.
Matheus                         Born: Mar. 6, 1852
 Died: Sept. 1, 1925, Freeman, S. D.
Katharina (Dubs)                Born: Jan. 27, 1856
 Died: Oct. 11, 1945, Freeman, S. D.
Johannes (John)                 Born: May 18, 1858
 Died: Jan. 10, 1936, Freeman, S. D.
Friedrich (Fred)                Born: Apr. 30, 1860
 Died: Nov. or Dec. in Montana
Heinrich (Henry)                Born: May 18, 1862
 Died: Nov. 28, 1937, Freeman, S. D.
Christian (Christ)              Born: May 18, 1864
 Died: July 28, 1904, Zeeland, N. D.
Sophia (Bauer)                  Born: Jan. 17, 1867
 Died: June 29, 1939, Zeeland, N. D.

 Died: Feb. 13, 1959, Dickinson, N. D.
