 Died May 15, 1960, Zeeland, N. D.

The Philip Schnabel family settled north of Menno, S. D. They belonged to the
Reformed Church. This is their family record:

Philip Schnabel Deceased
Elisabetha Schnabel (nee Aipperspach)           Deceased

Their children:

Christina (Mrs. Geo. Stoebner) Tripp, S. D.     Deceased
Elisabetha.(Mrs. Jacob Hafner) Dickinson, N. D.
Ludwig Schnabel, Woolsey, S. D.                 Deceased
Katharina (Mrs. Carl Serr) Upham, N. D.         Deceased
Fred Upham, N. D.                               Deceased
Dorathea (Mrs. Fred Theurer) Upham, N. D.       Deceased
Magdalina (Mrs. Ludwig Bertsch) Upham, N. D.    Deceased
Rosina, (Mrs. Andrew Mettler) Upham, N. D.      Deceased
Henry Upham, N. D.                              Deceased

The Henry Hafners began their farm operations with oxen. They were afraid of
the Indians.

Jacob Hafner first received his education in the country school. There he was
taught both English and German. Later a Missouri Synod Lutheran Parochial
school and church were built near the Hafner homestead. Mrs. Hafner's education
was very limited. Her religious instruction, however, was not.

When Henry Hafner died in 1890, Jacob took over the family homestead. Two years
later he married Elisabetha Schnabel. Jacob's mother lived with them until her
death in 1897. Because he had made a home for her, he became owner of the
homestead. At this time land in that area was selling for $15 to $20 per acre.
People were moving north where land was cheaper and more plentiful, and one
could still homestead. After his mother's death in 1897 Jacob sold their
homestead to his brother Martin. ln June of that year he and Fred Mettler Sr.,
Jacob Huber, Jacob Schramm and Peter Bohrer went to North Dakota looking for
land.

The Hafners had a family of 3 boys and 1 girl at this time. They had
experienced prairie fires and the great drought of 1894. At this time people
had complete crop failures. There was no work and there were tramps begging for
food everywhere.

Mr. Hafner wanted plenty of land for larger farm operations and grassland. This
he and the others found in Mercer County. All the men bought land. In choosing
their land, each man looked for water and fuel. The Hafner choice was very
fortunate for there was coal on their land and the Missouri bottoms provided
wood for fires and fence posts, and later for railroad ties. In later years the
younger boys, Otto, Walter and Herbert cut and sawed thousands of railroad ties
for the Beulah and Zap mines. They had their own sawmill.

In 1898 Hafner filed on a claim on a quarter section of Sec. 8. He bought the S
1/2 of Sec. 9 for $760--and the N 1/2 of Sec. 17 for $880. This is Range
146-Twp. 87 which is 15 miles north of Beulah.

The Joseph Boeshans family was already settled on adjoining land. Hafner lived
with them on this first trip and he brought his family here when they joined him
in March of 1899. He had intended to bring his family the preceding fall and
did begin construction of a sod house, but when that fall there was a big
prairie fire, which burned all the grass and most of the hay--he waited to
bring his family and goods until spring. The children born in S.D. were Philip,
Theodor, Leo and Martha, (Mrs. Wiedenmeyer). The ones born in N. D. were
Ernest, Harry, Otto, Emma, Walter and Herbert.

The Hafner, Mettler, Schramm, Bohrer, Huber, Mastel and Wiederich families all
came by passenger train from Tripp, S. D. to New Salem late in March.

Mr. Hafner shipped his 9 head of horses, 10 head of cattle, some hogs,
chickens, furniture, clothing and a 6' binder and other machinery by freight
train to New Salem. It was a four day trip to their new home.

In 1899, the first year of farming in North Dakota he broke 90 acres; put in 45
acres each of oats and flax. With five of his neighbors, he owned a horse
powered threshing "rig" that had a hand feeder and straw carrier.

While horses were his main source of power, he had at one time 66 head of
horses. In the band were about 25 young horses. At one time he used to run
about 100 head of cattle. He was among the first to raise much corn and hogs in
this area.

In his farming operations, much hay was put up for feed. The horses, because
they were the power producers, were fed the choice hays and grains. The cattle
were fed the poorer hay and straw.

He sold his interest in the horse-powered threshing machine in 1905 and had
Link & Hoepfner thresh for him with their steam rig in 1906. Christ Tuft's
steam rig threshed his 1907 crop. Then in 1908 he bought his own threshing
outfit--the first 20 H.P. International Harvester portable gas engine in the
community--and a 26" Peerless Geiser sieveless threshing machine. In 1914 he
bought his first 20-40 Case 2 cylinder gas tractor for $1200.00, complete with
a 500 gallon gas tank and a drive belt. Later he bought a 6 bottom plow. In
1919 he bought a 30-60 Aultman-Taylor 4 cylinder tractor and 8 bottom plow for
$7,100. Inflationary prices were particularly noticed on the price of farm
machinery. This tractor and plow was used for breaking and plowing land. He
could now break about 20 acres or plow 25 to 30 acres a day. This was quite an
improvement over plowing with horse power--as a 1 bottom walking plow plowed
2 to 3 acres per day; a gang plow using 4 or 5 horses plowed 5 to 6 acres per
day. He never owned a combine. His first truck was purchased in 1922. In 1935
he bought a Twin City 21-32 tractor, complete with 4 bottom plow, packer and
press drill for $1700 from L. G. Rose. This was the first rubber tired tractor
sold in Beulah. The Aultman-Taylor tractor was used until 1943 or 44. It stands
in retirement on the Ernest Hafner farm today.

Mr. Hafner and his neighbors hauled their grain to Expansion and Mannhaven,
which were river ports. These elevators were small and were soon filled, so
they were forced to market their grain elsewhere. They had a choice of either
New Salem, which was a 3 day trip, or Garrison. If they took it to Garrison
they would have to cross on the ferry, except in winter, when they would make
the trek on the ice, with horses and bobsled. All the neighbors who had grain
yet to be hauled would arrange to make the journey at the same time. They would
take turns enroute, breaking the trail, as many times there was much snow
covering the ice. The one would stay in the lead for about a half-mile or so;
then would drop back to the end of the line--thus giving their horses some
rest. This was a 2 day trip. At that time, there were many elevators in
Garrison, as much grain was shipped from there. Later when the railroad came as
far as Stanton, that became their shipping point; when it was extended further
west, it was Hazen and Beulah.

They were their own doctors--particularly in illnesses with elevated
temperatures--such as grippe, severe colds or flu. Ears of corn were heated
to the boiling point in water. With four or five to a "pack" were wrapped in
towels, and these packs placed close to the patient, who was covered with
additional bedding so much perspiring was produced, and patient would
(unwillingly, many times) remain in the pack for 45 min. after which the body
was rubbed with a mixture of alcohol and lard. It proved to be a very
successful treatment, when medical aid was not available.

Like all other early settlers, the Hafners provided for most of the year's meat
supply in the winter time. Hogs and beef were butchered. The hams, shoulders
and bacons were smoke cured. This provided most of the summer meat. The beef
was cut up, wrapped and packed in a meat box; first with a layer of snow--then
meat--until all was put away. If a shortage of meat should occur in the summer
time, they would butcher a beef--share it with neighbors--and each would be
able to keep the meat fresh for quite a while in the spring cooler boxes. Milk,
cream and butter was kept sweet and fresh in this same way. Before cream
separators were on the market--the milk was put in the spring and allowed to
cool. After 10 to 12 hours in the spring, the cream would rise to the top, so
it could be skimmed--thus separating the cream from the milk.

In addition to Mr. Hafner's farming operations, he served as County
Commissioner in 1912; was treasurer of the school board for 20 or more years;
was a member of the first Board of Directors when the Farmer's Elevator was
built and held various offices in the Church.

Andreas Weidner stayed with the Jacob Hafners when he came from S. D. in the
early 1900's, to homestead in Mercer County. Mr. Hafner helped him erect his
sod house. In turn, he helped Mr. Hafner with his farm work.

Before Mr. Hafner's sons were old enough to help with the farming and other
work, he had hired help. Louis Boeckel was his first hired man; then Michael
Koehler; Henry Reinhardt and Edward Mautz of Garrison. All his sons stayed at
home and worked until they were of an age to start out for themselves. As a
reward for their work and loyalty, each was given a parcel of land, 5 horses, 3
cows and a wagon--plus the privilege to use his machinery until they were able
to get their own.

Jacob Hafner retired from active operation of his farm in 1937. Ted Hafner quit
farming and moved to Montana in 1948. At that time the Ted Hafner farm house
was moved to the homesite (now Herb Hafner farm) and Jacob Hafners lived in it
until 1953 when they became residents of the Joachim Memorial Home. Later they
moved to St. Luke's Nursing Home in Dickinson, where Jacob Hafner passed away
Feb. 13, 1959 at the age of 89. Mrs. Hafner, who is 93 years old, still makes
her home at St. Luke's.

The Hafners lost 600 acres of good bottom land to the Garrison Reservoir, for
which they were paid $20.00 per acre by the Federal Government.

This is the Jacob Hafner family record:

Philipp Heinrich (Henry)--Born Dec. 20, 1892 at Freeman, S. D.

Theodor Johannes (John)--Born June 9, 1894 at Freeman, S. D.

Martin Jacob--Born Nov. 21, 1895-Died March 13, 1896 at Freeman, S. D.

Leo Friedrich (Fred)--Born Feb. 4, 1897 at Freeman, S. D.-Died Feb. 28, 1958.

Heronymus Gottlieb--Born Sept 12, 1902 in Mercer Co. N. D.

Emma--Born Nov. 22, 1907; Died Mar. 23, 1919 in Mercer Co.

Walter--Born Feb. 6, 1911 in Mercer Co. N. D.

Herbert George--Born May 15, 1913 in Mercer Co. N. D.

THEODOR J. HAFNER FAMILY HISTORY

Theodor J. Hafner, son of Jacob Hafner and his wife Elisabetha Hafner (nee
Schnabel) was born June 9, 1894 near Freeman, S. D. When he was 5 years old his
parents and family moved to Mercer County, North Dakota.

His limited education was received in the rural schools.

He lived with his parents on their farm for 25 years, except for the year or
more he spent in the service. During World War I, he served in the Army in
1918-19. He saw active service in France and was in the front lines when the
Armistice was signed. His experiences overseas, included that of being an
interpreter in Germany. He received his discharge in May of 1919.

Theodor married Minnie Wacker, July 2, 1919. She was the daughter of the late
Mr. & Mrs. Christ Wacker of Garrison. The Hafners farmed 15 miles north and one
mile west of Beulah. They owned 640 acres of land.

In 1948 he lost about half his acreage to the Government for the Garrison
Reservoir. His farming operations were greatly reduced by this loss of land,
and he decided to quit farming. He sold his personal property, and moved his
family to Billings, Montana. There he built a modern home and worked as a
carpenter, laying wood floors, until his retirement. Mr. & Mrs. Hafner still
reside in Billings.

Mr. Hafner can recall, when he was a small boy, his father was working in the
field with a four horse team and noticed a thunder storm approaching. He
unhitched the team and started driving them home. En route a bolt of lightning
struck, killing all four horses, but did not harm him. He also has a vivid
memory of the 30's when drought and grasshoppers took the crops; wheat dropped
to 23¢ per bushel and the Government bought the cattle.

Mr. Hafner farmed with horses until 1927, when he bought an IHC 22-36 tractor.
He never owned a combine, but hired neighbors to do this for him.

Mr. Hafner served on the school board for 20 or more years; held various
offices on the church council and was a member of the Mercer County draft board
during World War II. The following is the family record of the Theodore J.
Hafners:

Theodor J. Hafner--born June 9, 1894--Son of Jacob Hafner.

Wife: Minnie A. Wacker--born Aug. 17, 1898--Daughter of Christ Wacker--Married
July 2, 1919.

Verona E., born April 4, 1921--Married Lewis M. Halter April 23, 1943.
Children: James L., Jack, Deborah J., Jerilyn, Billings, Montana.

Inez L., born March 21, 1923--Married LeRoy Klaus, June 2, 1946. Children:
Candace M., Curtis R., Bismarck, N. D.

Paul T., born Sept. 9, 1926--Married Phyllis Fitzpatrick, October 6, 1950.
Children: Gregg A., Janet I., Julie K., David P., Joan C., Billings, Montana.

LeRoy L., born March 11, 1928--Married Diana Steiger, May 18, 1952. Children:
Lorelei R., Denise N., Nickolaus D., Wendy M., Billings, Montana.

Glenn F., born Feb. 3, 1933--Married Margaret Harris, Dec. 5, 1953. Children:
Rene M., Kevin G., San Bernardino, California.

Naomi R., born Oct. 14, 1934--Married Clarence Foos, July 25, 1952. Children:
Cynthia R., Daniel T., Constance R., Laurel, Montana.

        Standing--Left to Right: Marjorie, Hubert, Sylvia, Hilmer. Front
        Row--Left to Right: Marvin, Delores, Ernest Hafner, Arlene, Mrs. E.
        Hafner, Eunice.

ERNEST R. HAFNER HISTORY

Ernest R. Hafner, son of the late Jacob Hafner and his wife Elisabetha Hafner
(nee Schnabel) was born Sept. 16, 1900 in Mercer County, 15 miles north of
present Beulah.

Mr. Hafner received his education in the rural schools.

On January 18, 1925 he married Ottilia Herrmann, daughter of the late Adam
Herrmann and his wife, the late Christina Herrmann (nee Walz). Mrs. Hafner was
born Jan. 3, 1904 in Mercer County, 7 miles north of what is now Zap, N. D.

The Hafners are members of the Missouri Synod Lutheran Church.

Mr. Hafner worked for his parents until 1925. In that year he started his own
farming operations. That same year he built his present barn and a small house
16' x 26'. His intentions were to build a larger and modern home in a few
years, but then came the depression in the 30's, and shortly thereafter, World
War II. With the Government priority on many items and other restrictions, he
was not able to accomplish this until 1944, when their present modern 5 bedroom
home was built.

Mr. Hafner farmed with horses until 1929, at which time he bought his first
tractor; a 22-36 McCormick-Deering. They own 1120 acres of land, and a good set
of farm buildings; miLk about 16 cows and run, on an average, 50 range cows.

He bought his first combine, a J.I. Case 12' pull type in 1942 for $1870.00.
This was used until 1962 when it was replaced with a 12 ft. Oliver
self-propelled combine.

Mr. Hafner uses modern machinery in his farming operations. Two 4 bottom diesel
tractors with plows and drills, are used by him and his two sons, Hilmer and
Marvin, to put in their crops. His sons own and farm on their section of land
which is located near their father's.

Mr. Hafner will never forget the "dirty thirties". Wheat sold for as little as
23¢ per bu. He sold 46 head of cattle for $608.00, and this money was used to
purchase feed for wintering the few he kept. Due to the drought and shortage of
feed, farmers were forced to sell their cattle. The Government appraised and
bought these cattle at a set price. The maximum price per head was around
$18.00.

The Hafners were very fortunate to live so close to school and church during
the years their children attended grade school. The school has not been
operating for several years, and the church has been moved to Stanton.

Mr. Hafner served on various boards. He served 23 years on the Farmer's Union
Oil Co. board, (elected as a member of the first Board of Directors); 25 years
as board member on the Beulah Farmer's Elevator Co.; 3 years on Kronthal school
board; served on AAA corn-hog program board during the entire program; served
in the State Legislature 1948-56; 11 years as secretary of the Church and 21
years as Chairman of the church board.

He remembers well when his father bought his first car in 1915, which was a
Saxon Six.

The following is the family record of the Ernest Hafner family:

Ernest R. Hafner, born Sept. 16, 1900 in Mercer County. Mrs. Hafner (nee
Ottilia Herrmann) born 1904 in Mercer County.

Delores (Mrs. Chester Joos) born Oct. 16, 1925 in Beulah lives in Minneapolis,
Minn.--no children.

Marjorie (Mrs. Eddie Wiedrich) born Dec. 14, 1927 in Beulah, lives in rural
Hazen--2 girls and 3 boys.

Hubert (married Lenora Ost) born Feb. 3, 1929 in Beulah--their family--2 girls
and 3 boys.

Eunice (Mrs. Les Hjelm) born Apr. 27, 1931-Beulah--2 sons, Plentywood, Mont.

Sylvia, born Sept. 28, 1932, Beulah.

Hilmer, born Nov. 25, 1935-Beulah; farms with his father.

Arlene (Mrs. Gregg Fiergola), 1 girl, 2 boys, St. Cloud, Minn.

Marvin, born Dec. 23, 1938-Beulah (married Jo Elaine Misslin) (farms with his
father).

HARRY HAFNER FAMILY

Heronymus G. Hafner, son of the Jacob Hafner's, was born on September 12, 1902,
on his father's farm 15 miles north of Beulah. Harry stayed home and helped his
father on the family farm, until he married and started farming on his own.

Harry married Frieda E. Laible of Zap, on November 7, 1926 at Kronthal Lutheran
Church, Pastor F. Geske, officiating. Frieda was born on November 27, 1902 to
Hieronimus and Magdelena, nee Herman, Laible. The Laible's lived on a farm five
miles north of Zap. There were six children in the Laible family: three boys;
George (deceased), Henry of Helena, Montana, and Gust of Leucadia, California;
three girls; Mrs. Louis (Martha) Kehn of Butte, Nebraska; Mrs. Leo (Katharine)
Hafner (deceased); Mrs. Harry (Frieda) Hafner.

Harry received a 1/2 section of land from his father and started farming in
1927. At this time he still farmed with horses, although some of the area
farmers had begun using tractors. Until Harry was on his feet financially he
used horses. The Hafners still live on their original farm although, since the
construction of the Garrison Dam, there have been many changes in the
surrounding area. What was once an expanse of rolling N. Dak. prairies and farm
land, is now a gigantic body of water. Where they used to drive many miles
cross-country to visit friends and neighbors, now they go only a mile or two to
the shore of the reservoir, where they can always find a friend or old
acquaintance to visit, while they fish from the lake's shore (this is now a
favorite pastime for most of the people in the area).

The Hafner's have two sons; Edwin, who graduated from Beulah High School in
1945. Edwin served in the Armed Services from 1946-1949, spending most of this
time in Germany. He came back and attended Interstate Business College. When he
was recalled into Service, he was sent to Korea where he served from 1950 -
1951. Following his discharge he again entered Interstate business College
where he completed his education. He is employed in the Bookkeeping Dep't. of
The Fargo Linoleum Co. He married Marjorie Seigert on June 16, 1956. They have
two children Paula Rae and Scott Edwin. They make their home in Fargo, North
Dakota.

Gilbert graduated from Beulah High School in 1948. He attended N.D.S.U. from
which he graduated in 1953. He lives in Denver, Colorado, where he is employed
by Sunstrand (a Missile Plant) as a Draftsman.

THE WALTER HAFNER FAMILY HISTORY

Walter Hafner, son of Jacob Hafner, was born on February 6, 1911, on his
father's homestead fifteen miles north of Beulah. He received his education at
the Kronthal School. In 1937 he started farming on the half section of land he
had received from his parents. On June 5, 1938 he married Leona Kirchmeier,
daughter of the late Gottfried and Agneta (now Mrs. Frank Kasper) Kirchmeier.
Leona was born twelve miles south of Hazen in Oliver County.

In the spring of 1939 Walter leased 3/4 Sec. of farmland from the Bank of North
Dakota, later in the fall of the same year he bought it. A few years later they
purchased the Frank Manchester Homestead from George Sagehorn. This was mostly
river-bottom land which they used for pasture.

In March of 1948 the U.S. Gov't. took Option for Purchase on 478 acres of this
land for the Garrison Dam. The farm buildings are right on the banks of what is
now known as Hafner's Bay. Here is where the Beulah Chamber of Commerce has
held their Water Carnival for the past two summers. Most of this land was
pasture and when the Lake water began to cover it, they had to sell their
cattle. In 1948 they moved to Beulah and are now doing only grain farming. A
shelter belt was planted west of the farm yard in 1956.

The Hafner's are members of the Concordia Lutheran Church at Beulah. They have
five children:

Demaris, graduated from Beulah High School and Nurses Training at Jamestown
College. She is employed as instructor at the St. Joseph Hospital at Dickinson,
North Dakota. Her husband, Harvey Unruh, operates the Stockman's Bar at
Dickinson and attends Dickinson State Teachers College.

Betty Lou graduated from the Beulah High School in 1960. She is married to
George Strecker. They have one daughter, Tanja Kay. They live at Fargo, North
Dakota, where George attends N.D.S.U. and Betty works at Northwest Savings
and Loan Co.

Duane is a Junior at the Beulah High School. Robert is in the seventh grade.
Kevin, their youngest son, will be starting school in the fall of 1964.

HERBERT HAFNER

For a half century, Herb Hafner has lived on his father's homestead. He was
born in 1913 in the house he lives in today, it was built in 1904. Herb is the
youngest of the Jacob Hafner children.

Herb managed his father's farming operations for some time prior to his
retirement, and later became owner of the farm.

Herb received his grade school education in the Kronthal school, as did his
children. He started school the same year the new school was completed and
consolidated, which was 1918. The Hafners attended Kronthal's Trinity Lutheran
Church. This is where Herb received his religious instruction.

In 1938 Herb married Katherine Oberlander, who was born in 1914 in Dunn County.
When she was 5 years old, her family moved to Mercer County. She received her
education in the Center School District.

The Herb Hafners have four children: Myron, born in 1939, married Caroline
Wolf--they have two children and live on a farm north of Beulah; Adella, Mrs.
Darold Benz, they have two children and live in Beulah; Herman, born in 1944,
attends college in Wahpeton; David, born in 1946, attends Beulah High School.

In addition to farming, Herb served as Director of Kronthal School District,
continuously from 1938 until its dissolution in 1963; Director for 11 years on
the Oliver-Mercer Electrical Co-op; helped organize the Riverside 4H Club, and
was its leader for 10 years; Director of Lakeside Telephone Co. for 5 years;
Member of Kronthal Trinity Lutheran Church's Board of Education for 12 years,
and served on the building and incorporation committees of the Beulah Livestock
Auction Market, built in 1963.

A. D. BROWN

A. D. Brown was born in Clay County, Iowa, September 14, 1882, the son of
William Brown and Lydia Youmans Brown, who came to Iowa from New York State.

After graduating from Humboldt College he worked in newspaper offices in
Ayrshire and Ruthven, Iowa.

In 1906 he married N. Leone Anthony and in 1907 the young couple moved to
Mercer County, North Dakota. They made their home for a short while, with Mr.
Brown's parents, who had at that time settled on a farm south of Stanton.
Shortly thereafter they took up a homestead south of Stanton. It was then that
Mrs. Brown's mother, Mrs. Norman D. Anthony came from Iowa to make her home
with them.

Mr. Brown was associated with C. F. Schweigert and served as editor of the
weekly newspaper "The Mercer County Republican" published at Stanton.

With the coming of the railroad in 1914 he and Mr. Schweigert started the
Beulah Independent. Ed Wiedenmeyer was editor and manager. In 1915 "A. D." took
over the Independent and in 1916 moved his family to Beulah.

The Independent was hand set and--printed on an old George Washington hand
press. In the late 1920's a power driven press replaced the hand press. This
George Washington press was bought by John Young in 1899 from a printer in
Lidgerwood, N. D. and shipped by rail to Bismarck and then by boat to
Mannhaven. On it were printed the Mannhaven Journal and the Stanton Pilot. It
was sold to Eastman in 1903; sold to Otto Schriber and later moved to Stanton
to print the Mercer County Republican, and then moved to Beulah. In 1935 this
press was put on display at the University of North Dakota Museum as the last
old hand press used in North Dakota.

As independent as the name of his paper, "A. D." dedicated his life to the
betterment of his community and bowed to no man or political machine. He
pridefully called the Independent "this old rag of freedom."

He was interested in baseball and played 1st base on early teams. He was a
great supporter of the town band and played bass drum and sousaphone for many
years.

For 24 years the Independent was a "family newspaper" with Mr. Brown as editor
and publisher. Mrs. Brown served as unofficial business manager and reporter.
Four daughters, Virginia, Maxine, Thelma and Alberta each in turn graduated
from floor sweeper and printer's devil to type setter. The Independent was hand
set until 1940 when a used linotype was acquired from the Bismarck Tribune.

In 1938 Mr. Brown was stricken with a heart ailment which took his life
December 26, 1939.

Grandma Anthony, who made her home with the Browns for 33 years, and who held
the undisputed title of "best cook in town" passed away in 1941.

Mrs. Brown continued to publish and edit the Independent until October 1943
when the paper was sold to Harold Dally. She then moved to Seattle, Washington
where she lived with a daughter. She died at Auburn, Washington in June, 1958.

M. M. MOUNTS

I, Madison M. Mounts, was born at Bozeman, Mont. April 15, 1887, where my
father had property and owned a hotel. My mother and father both died in the
same year, when I was only 11 years old. Following the death of my mother on
Jan. 1, 1899 at Townsend, Mont., I lived with five different families while
attending grade school at Helena, Mont. until I went to my married sister in
New York City in Feb. 1901, where I graduated from high school #116 and later
attended Mulls College on 66th Street. While attending school in New York City
I was employed for five years by the wholesale jewelry firm of Chas. L. Trout
Co., located at Maiden Lane and Broadway. I returned to the west in 1907 and
was employed by the Cook Const. Co. of St. Paul, Minn. until 1913 attaining the
duties of Supt. of Construction in Mont., N. D., Minn. and Canada. My last
important railroad work was in the building of extensions for the NP between
Stanton and Killdeer, N. D. and had charge of that extension for the Cook
Const. at the time of its opening, until finished and also plotted and had
charge of the lot sales in all the townsites along this line for the Tuttle
Land Co. of Bismarck, N. D.

I was married Aug. 2, 1913 at Great Falls, Mont., to Ella A. Jereb. She was
born in Livingston, Mont. We had three children: Edwin B., who died at the age
of 5 1/2 years; Della M., married and living in Minnesota; Dalton C., married
and living in Orangevale, Calif. We lived in Bismarck four years, while there
we joined the Presbyterian Church. I am a member of Meridian Lodge #116 AF and
AM at Hazen and 32nd degree member of Bismarck-Mandan, Consistory-Scottish Rite.

The Tuttle Land Co., owners of the Townsites between Stanton and Killdeer had
sales representatives in each of the towns to sell lots, consequently, many
lots were being sold from day to day and many buildings moved in without lots;
their buildings temporarily parked in streets, etc. This was the case when we
advertised in the Bismarck, Mandan and Stanton papers to hold opening lots
sales on three consecutive days, as follows: Hazen, Oct. 28, 1913; Beulah, Oct.
29 and Golden Valley, Oct. 30. The weather was perfect all three days. At
Hazen, many buildings were moved down from Krem and occupied many lots. At
Beulah the only thing I can recall for certain is there was a lumber yard in
the center of Main Street at the intersection of 1st Ave. W., with the German
State Bank on one corner and a drug store opposite. The town "Beulah" was named
by L. C. Pettibone, Pres. of Tuttle Land Co., in honor of his niece "Beulah
Stinchcombe."

The Tuttle Land Co. a S. D. corporation was dissolved and its Corporate charter
cancelled on Feb. 8, 1921, leaving M. M. Mounts as the sole survivor of the
Co., as all Directors are dead.

We moved to Beulah in 1917, where I had previously acquired the property
adjoining Beulah on the east, hiring my friend A. B. Falconer, a graduate
Engineer and my brother Chas. L. to assist me in surveying the property and
adjoining lands preparatory to drilling test holes in order to determine the
extent of the coal fields. This drilling proved the existence of enormous veins
of coal from 18 to 22 feet in thickness underlying lands in the N 1/2 of Twp.
144, R. 87. Many options were taken and much land was bought.

In 1917 M. Mounts, L. C. Pettibone, E. Hughes and C. B. Little, organized the
Beulah Coal Mining Co. They bought my property. Work of producing coal began
from the original shaft which I located at the tipple site, dug down 34 feet to
the top of an 11 ft. vein of coal and it became the largest producing hand
loading underground mine in the state.

In 1922 the Co. reorganized under the name of Knife River Coal Co. and retained
me as manager. Later we decided to open a new mine into the 18 to 22 ft. coal
vein in Sec. 18-144-87 and built a new modern tipple and railway car loading
equipment. A new power plant was built by Hughes Elec. Co. in 1925. We built a
railway from our tipple north to the new double slope entrance, and eventually
installed 4 electric mobile underground coal loading machines and an outside
haulage locomotive, capable of hauling from 40 to 50 car trips of coal to the
tipple, and became the largest producing mine in the state.

In 1928 the mine was sold to the United Public Service Co., Chicago; a year
later to the vast "Insull" interests and then to the NW Public Service Co.,
Huron, S. D. and in 1947 to Montana Dakota Utilities Co. of Minneapolis, Minn.
I was retained by all these companies as manager until my retirement in April,
1952.

        Gunderson Coal Mine
