THE CARL AND ROSINA HEIHN FAMILY

        Standing--Left to right: Hilda, Otto, Fred, Emma, Herbert, Pauline,
        and Ted. Sitting--Left to right: Frieda, Carl, Rosina, and Alvina.

Carl Christian Heihn Jr. was born the son of Carl Christian Heihn and
Elizabeth, nee Herrmann, Heihn. In 1876 his parents and family decided to
immigrate from Caucaus So. Russia to America. Carl Jr. was born enroute on
board the ship "The Kaiser Wilhelm". Upon arriving at New York they traveled to
Dakota territory, settling at Tyndall, S. Dakota. They lived here for
twenty-four years.

In about 1898 the elder Mrs. Heihn died, leaving Carl Sr. with eight motherless
children. In the spring of 1899 he married a widow Christina, nee Glasser,
Geiszler. She had arrived from Friedenstahl, South Russia the fall before. Her
first husband, Heinrich passed away in 1898 leaving her and one daughter,
Rosina, alone. (Four of her children had died in Russia). They had sold all of
their property in Russia and ventured to America.  Their destination had been
Eureka, but due to a mix-up they came to Tyndall. Thus Rosina and Carl Jr. met.

In the spring of 1900, thinking that they could make a better living in a newer
area, the Carl Heihn Sr. family with many other settlers loaded their worldly
possessions (stock, machinery, household goods, personal belongings) on a
railroad car and started for Mercer County, North Dakota. The stopping off
place was New Salem, N. Dak., as here was where the railroad came to an end.
They packed all of the possessions unto wagons and the long 50 mile trek to
Mercer County began. The trip took four days. Upon arriving in Mercer County,
Carl's parents homesteaded a farm six miles north of what is now Beulah. This
farm is now operated by Jake Sailer.

Carl Jr. lived with the family until he had staked his own homestead claim 3
1/2 miles northeast of Beulah. In the summer of 1900 a granary was built and
later that fall he and his bride (his step-sister, Rosina) moved in and called
it "home". Carl Jr. and Rosina Geiszler were married on November 8, 1900, in
the Missouri Lutheran Church northeast of Beulah, by Rev. H. Arndt.

The Heihns lived here on the homestead for forty-five years. Here the ten Heihn
children were born and raised. They all attended the near-by rural schools and
grew to adulthood in the Beulah area. Mr. Heihn farmed until his death on
February 21, 1945. In the fall of 1945 Rosina disposed of all of her farming
equipment and some of her household goods and moved to town (Beulah). After
Mrs. Heihn moved off of the farm, her son Herbert worked the farm for two more
years when he sold out. The Knife River Coal Mine Co., who owned the land sold
off all the buildings and has rented out the land.

Although she suffered from chronic arthritis for many years Mrs. Heihn lived
alone in her little house in Beulah until 1962, when her health failed her
almost completely and she has since made her home at the Beulah Community
Nursing Home.

The ten Heihn children and their families are as follows: Frederick Carl (born
1901-died 1952) married Bertha Bauer, they had one daughter Shirley Kathleen
Heihn Pynn. Theodore (born 1903) married Martha Pfenning. They had three
daughters; Janice Rose (died 1955), Carolina Fern, Mrs. Sharold Oster; and Mary
Lou at home. They live in Beulah. Hilda (born 1906) married John Lippert. They
live in Beulah and are the parents of ten children: Esther, Mrs, Warren Jenkins
Sr.; Alice, Mrs. Elmer Steige; Agnes, Mrs. Peter Zeigler; Theodore, married Ore
Lee Adams; Gladyce, Mrs. Arnold Krein; Helen, Mrs. Daryl Zimmerman; Clarence
married Ardith Engel; Bernice, Mrs. James Helm; Lynda, Mrs. Virgil Benz; and
one son died in infancy. Otto Heinrich (born 1908) married Lena Richau. They
have one daughter, Judith Ann, and live in Beulah. Emma Heihn was born in 1911.
She lives in Chicago, Illinois. Herbert (born 1913) married Frieda Sailer. They
live in Fargo, N. Dak. They have one son Delton who is married to Carol Lowe.
Pauline (born 1915) married Jake Rueb and they live on a farm east of Beulah.
They have one daughter Carol Rose, Mrs. Leonard Mosbrucker. Alvina (born 1917)
married Joe McLaughlin and lives on a farm west of Hazen. They have six
children; Kathleen Rose, Mrs. Harold Beck; Joseph Patrick married Marceal
Elder; James Carl married Patricia Feist; Geraldine Sarah, Mrs. Don Pavlicek;
Shirley Ann died as an infant; and Thomas Michael, at home. Tillie (born 1919)
married Robert Schwede and lives in Beulah. They have two daughters, Rosemary
Elizabeth and Mabel Marguarite at home. She has three step-children; Robert
Schwede Jr. married Sally Huck; George, and June, Mrs. Mahlon Moore. Frieda,
(born 1921) married Walter Andres and lives in Bismarck.

Tillie Heihn, Mrs. Bob Schwede recalls many experiences that her father would
tell about the early days of Mercer County. Carl was one of the first to own a
threshing machine and for many years did all the threshing for many of the
people of Mercer and Oliver Counties. She remembers many times when they were
youngsters, their father was asked by the neighbors to look after sick animals.
Another recollection is of all the times their dad would go out butchering and
would make sausage, headcheese, etc. In those days meat had to be soaked in a
salt brine for days and then hung up and smoked in what they called a "smoke
house".

The hardships that were endured were also shared by others which made them a
lot easier to take. The dust storm years were especially bad; for day in and
day out dust filled the skies and blotted out the sun. Nothing grew but
thistles which were gathered for hay for the livestock. We certainly have a
wonderful Heavenly Father to have blessed us so richly and brought us thus far.

MR. AND MRS. J. JACOB WOLF

Christ Wolf and Katherine (nee Giese) immigrated from Germany to Lechlental,
South Russia, where J. Jacob Wolf was born, July 7, 1866. They came to America
Nov. 2, 1886 and lived in Huron, S. D. until May 8, 1896 when they traveled by
covered wagon to North Dakota.

J. Jacob Wolf married Katherine Morast Nov. 7, 1897 and they homesteaded 8
miles north of Hazen and lived there until June 11, 1942. They then moved into
Hazen. J. Jacob and Katherine Wolf raised nine boys and three girls.

        Caroline, Audrey, Jerry and Geneis, Herb and Lydia Wolf

Herb J. Wolf, son of J. Jacob Wolf, was born Nov. 24, 1906. He married Lydia
Miller on Jan. 3, 1929. To this union were born five children, Geneis (Mrs.
Ruben Brunmeier); Garvin, who died in July 1955; Jerry, Audrey (Mrs. Herb
Sasse) and Caroline (Mrs. Myron Hafner). Herb and Lydia have 10 grandchildren.

Herb farmed 8 miles north of Beulah, then moved to Mont. in 1934, where he was
employed at the Ft. Peck Dam. In Jan. of 1937, they moved back to Beulah, where
Herb worked for M. P. Moore, hauling ashes from the power plant.

On April 9, 1940, he started to work for M.D.U. Co., as coal and ash handler.
In 1949 he became an operator. The Wolfs are members of the Zion Lutheran
Church.

Ruben Brunmeier is employed by M.D.U. Co. and is living in Mandan. Myron
Hafners live 17 miles north of Beulah on a farm. The Herb Sasses live in
Mandan, where he is employed by M.D.U. Co. Jerry Wolf, M.D., is married and
practices medicine at Bottineau, N. D.

PATRICK KEOGH

Pat Keogh, a short wiry man with beard and clay pipe, came from Ireland. He
came to Illinois where he obtained employment on a Mississippi riverboat. He
dealt with many rough characters and settled many a bloody fight, before he
decided to try farming instead. His farming was in Swift County, Minn.
Minnesota farming was not the success he had looked for, so Mr. Keogh obtained
employment with the railroad building westward across Dakota Territory. On May
3, 1882, he brought his family, Mrs. Keogh--Catherine Caliher and children,
Tom, Jim, Mary, Sadie, Frank, John and Margaret to the Knife River Station
section house to live. Pat Keogh was section foreman and Mrs. Keogh cooked for
the section crew as well as her own family. Her recipes for plum pudding and
mince meat are still used by her grandchildren.

At that time there were no other settlements between Mandan and Dickinson.
Dickinson was the end of the division and there were but a few building at
either place.

The section house at Knife River Station was a flimsy building sodded over on
the outside to make it more weatherproof. It was large enough to provide
sleeping quarters for the family and ten section hands, plus a kitchen and
dining room. The house was on the south side of the tracks just below a little
hill 1 1/2 mi. west of Hebron. On the north side of the track there was a
bricked up cistern. This cistern received water from a dammed up creek through
a pipeline run into the bottom of the cistern. A windmill pumped the water up
to a wooden tank above it. Into the creek bank nearby was dug a large root
cellar. Since the year 1882 was a wet year the potato crop was good and there
were plenty of potatoes in the root cellar that first year.

There was no church, no school. Mrs. Keogh's only contact with the outside was
with the trainmen passing and a once a month trip by rail to Mandan to shop for
supplies at the McGillie & O'Rourke store.

Knife River Station had become the campsite for the gang that built the RR.
There were a goodly number of whiskey bottle corks gathered, which Mrs. Keogh
sewed into cloth cases for the children to wear when they learned to swim in
the creek.

Buffalo hunters stopped in at the section house for meals too. Among them Jack
Backhus and Swen Swenson. Backhus was a drinking man and Swenson was not. This
caused them to part company. Swenson stayed on with the Keoghs, and he became
the official hunter for the railroad company. There were not too many buffalo
in this area in the 80's tho they were numerous at one time, as evidenced by
the trails and bones. Many loads of buffalo bones were gathered, and sold for
$14.00 per wagonload. In the Bad Lands area many were seen and hunted. Jim told
of the buffalo hunters who came in and killed them by the hundreds, saving only
the hind quarters and hides. The hind quarters would be shipped out--the
hides used for robes, coats and mittens.

When a band of about 100 Indians appeared over the hill south of the section
house, frightening Mrs. Keogh that first fall, Swenson was there to ease the
fears, for he knew the Indian ways. Pat Keogh, Swenson and some of the other
men smoked the peace pipe evenings with this band before the Indians trekked on
north to the Fort Berthold Reservation.

Prairie fires were a real menace. The railroad sent men out to plow fire breaks
as early as 1883. The man who plowed those in the vicinity of the section house
was Owen Duffey, a former neighbor of the Keoghs on their Minnesota farm. He
too left farming for railroading.

Eldest son Tom helped on the RR from the age of 14. When he was 19 be became
section foreman when his father quit railroading to homestead just east of the
W. H. Haasebrook place. Son Jim helped his mother.

All that is left of Knife River Station is the bricked up cistern and the
outline of the foundation of the buildings. The buildings were lost in a
prairie fire. The name has been changed to commemorate the Irishman and his
family that made the place home to the early railroad men and buffalo hunters.
It is marked "Keogh" on the railroad right-of-way.

Pat Keogh was born in Mayo County, Ireland in 1837--died in 1910. Mrs. Keogh,
born in 1846, died in 1899; Sadie, born 1875 and died in 1910. Interment:
Haymarsh cemetery.

        Mr. and Mrs. Tom Keogh  Mr. and Mrs. Jim Keogh

KEOGH BROS. TOM AND JIM

In 1889 Tom and Jim, with two Koesel boys, decided to go ranching. The first
ranch camp was located on Beaver Creek, a half mile from where Tom later built
his permanent homesite. It was a one room "shack" built of used railroad ties.
They chose this location, as it had all the qualifications for a ranch, i.e.,
good range, good grass, plenty water and shelter for livestock in winter, along
with keeping well within shipping limits. These four started out with 300 head
of cattle, shipped in from Iowa. It was open country (no fences) so they had to
ride herd often. They said all they had to do was ride, eat and sleep. They
attempted to keep cattle grazing within a 10 mile radius. Generally this was
possible except during storms, when cattle would drift with the storm. They
told of one storm that was particularly severe and of long duration. Some of
their cattle were found within 16 miles of Mandan; many were not found until
spring, after the snow had gone. These were bunched up in the bottom of a deep
embankment. Jim froze his eyes on a long, cold ride in search of cattle. During
another storm in late spring, they suffered a loss of 50 head. Weather had been
very mild, melting much of the snow. In the Haymarsh country the large marsh
area filled with water. A sudden and severe storm came up and the cattle
drifted onto the pond which was then frozen, and all perished there. Blackleg
contributed to cattle losses too. Until the Govt. made the vaccine available to
ranchers their home remedy was salt, potash and sulphur. The blackleg appeared
in fall after the first hard frost. Wolves were also a menace. It was not
uncommon to see large packs of wolves which they assumed followed the cattle as
they were trailed in from Texas and Mexico. Many times when they rode, they
carried guns and hunted them; too they used to run them down on horseback,
which was about a 10 mile chase before they could corner them and club them to
death. They were considered one of the hardest of the wild animals to trap.

The Keoghs and Koesels batched and ranched together for 6 years, then they went
broke. The partnership was then dissolved. At that time they were running 700
head of cattle. Despite the number, they couldn't make a go of it, as cattle
prices were so low. They shipped them to the St. Paul market, where they
received $2.65 per cwt. However, later, they shipped to Chicago, because of the
better market.

The Keogh Bros. were determined to have a successful ranching career, if
possible, so they ventured forth again, backed by an easterner with whom they
ranched on shares. They borrowed money at different times, on which they paid
12% interest, with the first year's interest being retained by the lendor out
of the principal. In 1896 they began building where Tom lived for many years
(present Ralph Murray ranch). They built a 16' x 24' house; a barn large enough
to house 10 head of saddle horses, and a shed 60' x 80'. The walls were built
of railroad ties standing on end. The roof of the house was shingled; the barn
and shed roofs were first covered with poles, then tar paper and sod. The first
winter after they built at this site, they went up to the Missouri river north
and east of Dunn Center and cut about 500 poles, which later were used for
corrals and sheds. That same winter they hauled these poles to the head of the
brakes and piled them there. In spring they went back and barked them. The
following winter they hauled them to their home camp. A friend, Swen Swenson,
looked after the camp during their absence. This was a long, hard jaunt for
both humans and horses. They used a four horse team, and took 3 days to come
from the river, a distance of 45 miles. Some of these trips were made in 40-45
below zero weather. They stopped at line camps along the trail. At one of these
camps (present W. Herman ranch) lived an elderly bachelor, Mr. Buschcart, who
had pancakes on the "menu" often. For shortening in the cakes he used the clot
of blood from around the gun shot wound of an animal--but they agreed, they
were "darned" good, and when one was hungry enough, anything tasted good.

Ranching operation costs in the early years was very little. There was a State
tax which had to be paid on each head of cattle owned, but it was a small
amount. Too, it was open country--few claims had been filed--and as they used
to say, "You'd climb the highest peak--and as far as you could see--it was
all yours."

The Indians were often seen traveling through the country on horseback, with
more than one rider to a horse, carrying all their camping equipment, tents,
etc. The horse wore a leather collar into which the drag poles were anchored,
and on these poles were tied their supplies, cooking utensils and papoose. When
they set up camp these poles were used in erecting their tepees. There was only
one time when the people became alarmed about the Indians. Word had gotten
around that the Sioux from Standing Rock Reservation were on the warpath. The
settlers around Hebron erected a sod fort, into which many moved for
protection, should they invade, but the invasion never developed.

In the early years, stock trains coming from the west would run day and night,
carrying stock to the St. Paul and Chicago markets. In those years, the
brakemen had to handbrake each car. Between present Richardton and Knife River
Station, due to the steep grade of the railroad bed, many brakemen lost their
lives as they jumped from car to car braking them while the train was moving
full speed ahead.

When Keogh Bros. were financially able to go into business for themselves, they
bought "Minnesota dogies" a term used by ranchers for cattle from dairy stock,
which was all they could buy. As time went on, their dream of running Herefords
became a reality. They continued to raise Herefords as long as they ranched.

Frank and Jack were younger than Tom and Jmm. They too went ranching north of
the Missouri river in McKenzie Co. Jack later went to Canada, but Frank
remained at his first location.

In 1898 Keogh Bros. bought the section Tom lived on for $1.50 per acre from the
railroad co. About this same time Jim filed on a claim just south and east of
where Tom later built his home. They built a combination tie and sod house on
the claim, where the Jim Keoghs lived the first two summers they were married,
as it was more convenient for summer haying, but in winter they lived in their
home located 3 1/2 miles SW of present Beulah, on the Knife River.

It wasn't "all work and no play" for the Keogh Bros. Good saddle horses would
get them to Hebron in less than 2 hours, where dances were regularly held, and
which they often attended. "After the ball was over" they'd ride back to the
camp. In the late 90's however, they went by team and buggy, as there were
extra passengers waiting for them in Haymarsh--namely the Koller girls--Theresa
and Bertha.

The T up and T down brand was registered in Montana Territory by Pat Keogh for
his horse brand. Jmm and Tom used this brand for some time, but later Frank had
it recorded in N. D. It is still in use on the Frank Keogh ranch at Keene. The
Key-O cattle brand was recorded in Montana (presumed used at Fort Keogh) before
Jim and Tom had it recorded in N. D. This brand is in use today by three of
their children.

Tom and Jim married sisters--Theresa and Bertha Koller--who came to Dakota from
Wisconsin. Three of their brothers, Martin, Lawrence and Mike Koller came in
the early 90's. Martin and Lawrence filed on claims in the Haymarsh country.
Mike went ranching in the Bad Lands. Theresa was the first to come to care for
Martin's three small children after his wife passed away. Bertha came a few
years later and taught school in Haymarsh. Bertha filed on a claim in Haymarsh,
which she later sold. When Theresa and Tom were married in 1901, Bertha lived
with her brother, caring for the children and teaching school. Bertha and Jim
were married in 1903. Tom and Jmm continued to operate as Keogh Bros. for most
of 50 years, before dissolving partnership.

Mr. and Mrs. Tom Keogh had three sons: Frank--Dickinson; Paul and Clarence--
Whitehall, Mont. In 1948 they sold their ranch and moved to Montana, where Tom
passed away in 1955. Mrs. Tom is presently a resident at St. Benedict's Home in
Dickinson.

Mr. and Mrs. Jim Keogh had a family of three sons and four daughters: Edwin,
Tucson, Ariz.; Agnes, Mrs. Ted Herman, Beulah; Robert, Beulah; Walter, Nye,
Mont.; Helen, Mrs. Al Buchli, Wibaux, Mont.; Katherine, Mrs. R. H. O'Neil,
Larson, N. D. and Lucille, deceased. Mrs. Keogh passed away in 1948 and Jim in
1961.

It is with pride we record this, their history, for posterity. In recalling
events of the past they were always referred to as those of the "good old days!"

        Wedding picture of Charles George Herman and Frances Elizabeth Tavis.

THE HISTORY OF CHARLES G. HERMAN

Charles G. Herman was born in Saarbrucken, Germany, on Sept. 29, 1876, to Peter
and Rose, nee Klier, Herman. The Peter Herman family came to the United States
in 1883. They first settled in the Pennsylvania coal mining area, where the
family; Minnie, Charles, August, and Rose lived for a time. Later they moved to
Michigan, but did not stay long; for like so many of our early settlers, they
followed the "Westward Movement."

The Herman Family arrived in Glen Ullin in 1890 and took up a homestead about
four miles north of what is now Glen Ullin. It was rough going for the Hermans
in those early years, what with sod houses without floors, and only one room.
The whole family had to work hard to make a living. They plowed the land with a
hand plow and Oxen.

At the age of fourteen Charlie decided to make some extra money for the family
by working for the railroad for $15.00 a month. When he was about eighteen
Charlie realized that "hiring out" was not for him, and made the decision to
start a sheep ranch. He took up a homestead about twelve miles south of Beulah,
built a small house and bought some sheep. He was now a full-fledged rancher.
But things did not go as smoothly as he had hoped for; trips to Glen Ullin for
groceries took two days, though later on, when the Evans and Farrington Post
Offices were built, it was handier for their mail, at least.

In 1900 Charlie was married to Frances Tavis of Glen Ullin. As a girl Frances
had come with her parents, John and Theresa Rolfe Tavis, from West Virginia. It
had been necessary for Frances to miss some schooling in order for her to take
her turn, with her seven sisters and two brothers, at sheep herding. Thanks to
the size of the family, her turn did not come too often.

To this union of Frances and Charles five children were born: Alice, Mrs. Oran
Abbey; Theodore, Carl, Wilfred, and Cleo, Mrs. Thure Orefalk. For two years the
Herman family lived in Glen Ullin. In 1908 they moved back to the homestead,
where the number of sheep Charlie owned sometimes reached 2,000. He was the
first man in the area to own a Ford Car. During this time the railroad was
being built, and the crews would winter their horses at Charlie's. He then
bought land in the Aplin area where he established and operated the Charlie
Herman Cattle Ranch until 1943, when the youngest son, Wilfred, took over.

In 1912 he was elected sheriff of Oliver County and the family moved to Center
where he served in this capacity for two terms. While in Center he also
operated the Ford Garage. On Armistice Day, 1918, the family moved back to the
ranch. At that time the Hermans got their mail at the Aplin Post Office. They
built a new home in 1922 and it was at this time that Charlie started as a
cattle buyer. In the early 1930's he served in the North Dakota House of
Representatives for two terms. In 1943 he moved to Beulah, where he continued
in the cattle buying business.

In 1947 Mrs. Herman passed away after a lingering illness of four years.
Charlie continued to live in Beulah until his death on Christmas Eve, 1956.

History submitted by Alice, Mrs. Oran Abbey.

        Oran Abbey          Alice Abbey

THE HISTORY OF ALICE HERMAN ABBEY

Alice Herman was born at Glen Ullin, N. Dak. in 1901. She attended her first
two years of school in Glen Ullin, and later went to country school in the
Aplin area, where her family was then residing. She was graduated from Glen
Ullin High School in 1920, and then began teaching. For six years she taught
the lower grades in Beulah, while getting her College education in Valley City
during the summer terms. In 1926 she married Oran Abbey, son of C. W. and Cora
Armstrong Abbey, who homesteaded south of Beulah.

In the late 1920's they started farming three miles south of Beulah on what was
then known as the Sam Murray homestead. In addition to farming, Oran Abbey was
also employed as a mule skinner in the Knife River Coal Mine. Oran also
operated a small coal mine on his own farm, and did veterinary work for the
surrounding area for many years.

Oran Abbey was killed in a car accident about one half mile from his farm, in
the year 1943. His wife, Alice, was left to raise nine children who were now
fatherless.

Alice Abbey continued living and working on the farm for four years.
Eventually, however, she decided it was too difficult to get her children
through school, continue as organist at St. Joseph's Church--a job which she
held from the early 1920's through 1956, and which she holds once more--and
still manage to keep up the farm work. So in 1947 Alice rented the farm out on
shares, bought the house in Beulah that is still her home, and moved her family
into town. And it was here that her nine children went through High School.

Her daughter Lois graduated from St. Alexius School of Nursing and later
married Loren Lang, son of Mr. and Mrs. Jim Lang. The Loren Lang's now reside
in Sidney, Montana, where he is employed by Montana-Dakota Utilities. They are
the parents of five children.

June attended college at Fargo, North Dakota and then became a stenographer.
While working in Denver, Colorado she met and married Michael Dolce, son of Mr.
and Mrs. Jerry Dolce. The Mike Dolce's are presently living in Billings,
Montana, and have a family of four children.

Lenore married Donald Wetzel, son of Mr. and Mrs. Harry Wetzel. Don and Lenore
live in Beulah, where Donald is the District Manager of Montana-Dakota
Utilities Office. They have four children.

Marilyn was employed as a Nurse's Aide in the Hazen Hospital until her marriage
to Arvid Gustafson, son of Mr. and Mrs. Joe Gustafson. Marilyn and Arvid, with
their three children, are presently living on a farm near Stanton, North Dakota.

The eldest son, Gale, married Kathy Gillon, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Jack
Gillon of Red Lodge, Montana. They are now living in Sidney, where Gale is
employed by Montana-Dakota Utilities.

Donna Abbey married Stanley Stasel, son of the late A. G. and Mrs. Florence
Stasel. Stanley Stasel is in the Law and Accounting business in Minneapolis,
Minnesota. Stanley and Donna are the parents of one daughter.

James Abbey is currently doing construction work in Renton, Washington.

Colleen, the youngest daughter, married Delmer Flemmer, son of the late Edward
and Mrs. Hella Flemmer. Delmer is now employed by the company of Lange
Electric. Mr. and Mrs. Delmer Flemmer have four children.

Alan, youngest of the nine children, is presently living with his mother, and
is employed by Knife River Coal Mining Company. He is engaged to be married in
the Spring of 1964.

        Left to right: Lois, June, Lenore, Marilyn, Donna, Colleen.

        Left to right: Gail, James, Alan

Beulah Drug Store

Theo  P. Herman, Ph.G. purchased the Drug Store from the Geo. W. Thamert estate
in December 1928. His first store was a small frame structure, which was
replaced by the present building in 1930.

        Beulah Drug Store 1930

The Drug Store has always been #37 West Main, except for the period when Mr.
Kuhl, the first druggist in Beulah was located on the north side of Main
Street. Druggists succeeding Mr. Kuhl were L. G. Stutsman, Dan Hall and George
Thamert.

Ted is a native North Dakotan, son of the late Mr. and Mrs. Charles Herman; and
was born in Glen Ullin in 1902. He graduated from NDSU School of Pharmacy in
1926. In 1929 Ted married Anna Keogh, daughter of the late Mr. and Mrs. James
Keogh of Beulah. They have one son Jerome, born June 27, 1935. He too is a
graduate of the NDSU School of Pharmacy with a B. S. degree. Since his
graduation in 1957, he has been associated with his father in business.

In 1957 Jerome married Betty Huber, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Dave Huber,
Beulah. They have three daughters; Renee Ann, born Apr. 3, 1958, Laura Jean,
Jan 21, 1961 and June Marie, Jan 21, 1961.

The Beulah Drug Store history would not be complete without including Clarence
Schneider, who has been with us since Feb. 1946.

Naomi (Mrs. Wm.) Unruh completes the force.

        Ted             Jerome

        Clarence        Naomi

CARL HERMAN

Carl Herman, son of Charles Herman and Frances Tavis Herman was born July 19,
1904. He lived with his parents on a farm south of Beulah from 1904 until 1911
when the family moved to Center. He attended the Center Public School until
1916 when he transferred to the Sacred Heart Parochial School in Glen Ullin. He
also attended Glen Ullin High School and Capital Commercial College at
Bismarck. He farmed with his father until the time of his marriage.

On October 2, 1933 he and Marguerite McLaughlin were married by Father Charles
Bauer at Saint Martin's Church at Hazen. Marguerite, the daughter of Jack
McLaughlin and Maggie Maloney McLaughlin, was a graduate of Hazen High School
and attended Dickinson State Normal School for two years and taught school at
Hazen, Stanton and Beulah.

They moved to a farm south of Beulah, known as the Carl Stephens farm, where
they have resided until the present time with the exception of two years
1937-1939 when Carl was employed at the Beulah Drug Store.

They have four children and one granddaughter.

Marleen was born May 12, 1939. She graduated from Beulah High School and
Dickinson State College and has taught at Mandan and Dickinson. On August 5,
1961 she and Lloyd Schnaidt, son of Mr. and Mrs. George Schnaidt, were married
at Saint Joseph's church by Father R. Landoll. Lloyd is a graduate of Beulah
High School and NDSU School of Pharmacy and is employed at the Service Drug at
Dickinson.

Mary Margaret, born March 12, 1937 graduated from Beulah High School and
Dickinson State College. She has taught school at Killdeer, Grand Forks, Sauk
City, Wisconsin and Duluth, Minnesota. On June 14, 1958 she and Dennis Wolf,
son of Mrs. Elsie Urness and the late Albert Wolf were married by Father R.
Landoll at Saint Joseph's church at Beulah. Dennis a graduate of Beulah High
School and the University of Wisconsin School of Medicine is a practicing
physican at Fort Yates. They have one daughter Karla Kay born September 15,
1963.

Karen was born July 5, 1943. She was graduated from Beulah High School and is a
senior at Dickinson State.

Darell was born September 16, 1948 and is a sophomore at Beulah High School.

LORANZ AND LUCY JOOS FAMILY HISTORY

Loranz Joos was born in New Lisbon, Wis., on March 13, 1859. He spent his
childhood there and then later moved to Jamestown, N. Dak. He was married to
Lucy Bouch also of New Lisbon. She was born on February 26, 1862.

They lived in the Jamestown area for years, where their two sons, Philip and
Edwin attended school. In the early 1900, they came to Beulah, where they
purchased land from Norman E. Vredenberg and his wife Elizabeth. This land they
farmed and built their home.

Grandpa Joos went into the hardware business. His store was one of the first
buildings to be built in the new town. This store in later years became known
as the Super Valu Store. One of the many items that he sold in the store were
windmills, which were quite common in those days. His store was also used for
church services and any entertainment. These being held in the upstairs. The
store was originally built by Carl Henke in 1908. Later Grandpa Joos sold the
store to Mr. L. G. Rose, who in turn sold it to Mr. W. H. Esterly in 1941.

When the First World War broke out, their son Philip was called to service; as
many of the young men were in those troubled times. Philip was killed in action
in France in 1918. This was a sad blow to his family.

In about 1919, their son, Edwin married Hulda England, daughter of Mr. and Mrs.
John England. To this union were born three children, Philip, Ellen and
Lawrence.

It was tough going in the early 20's and the 30's, due to bad crops, high
prices and grasshoppers. But the old pioneer back-ground made it easier in many
ways. On Feb. 9, 1931, Loranz Joos passed away at the age of 72.

        Edwin P. Joos
        Lawrence, Ellen & Philip

For years later, until her death in 1941, Grandmother Joos, spent her time
raising a garden, farming and was active in her church until ill health forced
her to stop. She was a Gold Star Mother.

Her son, Edwin continued to do the farming, until he retired and illness kept
him confined to home until his death in September of 1962. After his
retirement, his daughter, Ellen took over the job of running the farm.

        Linda & Paul
        Alan, Vera, Philip & Marilee

Edwin's oldest son, Philip spent four years in the army during World War II.
Most of this time was spent in Puerto Rico. While still in service and
stationed in Utah, he met and married Vera Mower, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. R.
A. Mower of Ogden, Utah. They were married in June 1945. To this union were
born four children, Paul, Linda, Marilee Sue and Alan Edwin. Philip is employed
with the General MiDs Inc. Sperry Div. He has worked for them the past 18
years. His present job is Operator Utility man in the F. A. Processing Plant.
He grew up in the Beulah area and went to the local schools. Philip's wife was
raised in Ogden, attended schools there and became employed at the Army Defense
Depot in Ogden, where she has worked for 15 years. She is an IBM operator and
is presently in charge of the Tape Library. She is also a student at Weber
State College, majoring in Education.

Ellen, daughter of Edwin, has spent her entire life here on the farm which was
her grandparents. She attended the local schools. During World War II, she
joined the Women's Army Corps. She spent three years in the service of her
country. After returning home from the service, she resumed her job of farming
and she also started to work in the local post office. She has not found farm
life dull, far from it, she enjoys it. In spite of being busy, she still finds
time to help others, and also time for her hobbies and sports. She is a member
of the V. F. W. Auxiliary, Schmidt Post, and a member of St. Joseph's Church,
Beulah.

Lawrence, the youngest of the three children, also was raised on the family
farm and educated in the local schools. During World War II, he spent three
years in the army, serving part of the time in Italy. Upon returning home, he
began working in the local theatre as projectionist, a job he held until 1963.
In 1961 he began to write mystery novels. He is the author of a mystery novel
entitled "THE GREEN MANSION MYSTERY" which was published in 1963. A second
novel will soon be published and it is called "THE MYSTERY OF THE KNEELING
DOLLS". He has won an honorable mention for a story he did for the
Montana-Dakota Utilities magazine. He is a member of the Mystery Writers of
America Club.

        Mr. and Mrs. John England

JOHN AND MARY ENGLAND FAMILY HISTORY

John W. England was born March 8, 1862 in the state of Missouri. Mary Gibson
was born Sept. 18, 1860. They were married on August 18, 1884, when John was 22
years old. Their home was in Borndard, Mo. They had eight children. Still
living are: Mary, Harley, Harry, Hulda, Viola and Bertha. The deceased are as
follows: Rosy B. (June 21, 1885--Jan. 15, 1889); James W. (May 31, 1887--Sept.
21, 1887).

On May 30, 1904 Mary England passed away. Two years later, in 1906, John
England moved, with his children, to North Dakota. They settled on a farm south
of present Beulah in 1907.

It was the following spring that John England planted his first crop on his new
farm. He planted flax. The crop was fairly good. He had to haul his flax far to
market.

John Anderson, a local farmer, raised a large potato crop and supplied the N.
P. railroad construction crew with potatoes. In the fall of 1914, John England,
who was then employed by the N.P.R.R. Co.in Killdeer, was hired to haul the
potatoes to the consumer after they reached Killdeer.

In 1915 Mr. England was taken ill. He was hospitalized at Brainerd, Minn., then
the nearest R. R. hospital. There he passed away on July 20, 1915.

Hulda England married Ed Joos. Their children John England's grandchildren) are
Philip, Lawrence and Ellen Joos.

FOUR GENERATIONS OF THE ADAM J. KELLER FAMILY

Adam Johann Keller

        Adam S. Keller and wife, Katherina
        Gottlieb and David

Adam Johann Keller, son of Johann Adam Keller and Anna Marie, nee Bock, Keller,
was born on December 3, 1859, at Waterloo, South Russia. At the age of fourteen
he came to this country with his parents and settled at Menno, South Dakota. On
March 23, 1879, he was married to Katharina Teske at Yankton, South Dakota.
Katharina was born at Neudorf, South Russia on February 28, 1861, the daughter
of Gottlieb and Katharina, Wiederich, Teske. She was nine years old when her
family immigrated to the United States, and settled at Hosmer, South Dakota.

After their marriage Adam and Katharina lived at Menno, South Dakota until they
moved to Tripp, South Dakota in 1882. To this union were born twelve children:
Gottlieb, David, August, Emilie, Mrs. Philip Hafner; Edward, Wilhelm. and
Jacob. Four children died during a diptheria epidemic in South Dakota and
another died in infancy.

The Kellers had heard of the wonderful homesteading opportunities in the sister
state of North Dakota. They had sons who would soon be eligible to homestead so
they decided to migrate there in search of land. Adam made the first trip alone
in a buggy, which he converted into a covered wagon. He came to Mercer County
and scouted some land; when he saw the spring, which is on the present Keller
farm, he knew he had found the land he was looking for. In 1899 he bought Sec.
13 Twp. 145 R. 88 from the railroad (they owned this property). In the spring
of 1900 the family joined an emigrant train, which took them as far as New
Salem. On this same train were the Zuerns, Heihns, Kerners, and Liebelts, they
were also going to N. Dak. When the train arrived at New Salem on April 1, 1900
there was three feet of snow on the ground. The Kellers had brought their
horses, cows, mower, wagon, plows, oats, wheat, flax, and of course they didn't
forget to bring their greyhound dogs along. (Through the years the Kellers
derived much pleasure hunting coyotes with these specially bred and trained
hunting dogs). The family unloaded their supplies at New Salem and started out
for their future home. When they got to Hazen the Knife River was very high and
they just managed to cross it. The family spent the first night at the August
Isaak home, and arrived at their homesite the next day. They lived in a tent
until the barn was finished. This barn was their only living quarters until the
next year when Katharina's father, Gottlieb Teske, built a new frame house for
them. Gottlieb was a carpenter and built several of the homes in the
surrounding area. It was in this house that the Keller children grew to
adulthood. The Adam Keller homestead was located on N.E. 1/4 Sec., 14 twp. 145
R. 88, and it was on here that the first log house was built. This little log
house became the first school in the community in 1904. In 1905 Eli Gunderson,
a merchant from Kasmer, built the school across the road from the present-day
Keller School.

        Jake, Wm. and Ed Keller, David and wife Olga, Mrs. and Mr. Adam
        Keller, Emeilia (Mrs. P. Hafner) and August

The first year the only crop planted was flax. The first two years were very
dry and no crops were harvested. Before the coming of the Railroad to Beulah,
the grain had to be hauled to New Salem and Hebron, and in the winter time they
traveled across the frozen Missouri River to Garrison. The family flour was
rolled at the Roller Mill in Krem.

The Kellers were very active in church affairs. The First Reformed Church was
organized at Hazen on June 22, 1900. Church services were held at the various
homes until the opening of a school, and then they held services at the Keller
school. In 1919 the first church was moved from four miles north of Beulah to a
mile east of the Keller homestead. This church was sold to the Zap Lutheran
Church, Missouri Synod in 1938 and is still being used.

During these early years farm machinery was very high priced, so Adam and his
two sons, Gottlieb and David, formed a partnership to buy their first tractor
(a Rumley) and a threshing machine (a Perrless), which they purchased in 1912.
These replaced some of the horsepowered machinery. This tractor could pull 8
breaking bottom plows or 12 regular bottom plows.

On March 15, 1914, the country north of Beulah experienced a terrible prairie
fire. One of the Keller boys lost his claim shack and a neighbor, Rev. and Mrs.
Blumhagen lost two of their childen. The two youngsters were home alone at the
time of the fire, the little girl perished and the boy was severely burned. He
had to have medical aid, so Adam and a neighbor, Cottlieb Klaudt, took him to
Bismarck. They took turns driving the horse and buggy. This long journey of
mercy proved of no avail, for the youngsters passed away two days later.

And so life on the Keller farm went on until in December of 1921, when they had
an auction sale and the Kellers moved to Lodi, California. Most of their
children were living in the Beulah area so in 1922 they came back to Beulah,
and they lived in town for a few years, until they moved back again to the
family homestead. Here they farmed until 1935 when they rented the farm to Gus
Buchfink. They lived in Beulah until their deaths. They were members of the E.
U. B. Church of Beulah. Mrs. Keller passed away on July 21, 1938 and Adam
stayed in Beulah until his death on October 6, 1942. Five of the Keller
children are still living: Gottlieb, David, August, and Emilie of Beulah, and
Jacob of Wapato, Washington. Edward passed away at Bismarck in 1958 and Wilhelm
died (also in Bismarck) in 1963.

At the time of his death, Adam Keller had 39 grandchildren and 8
great-grandchildren.

        Three Keller Brothers--August, Gottlieb and David (1963)

GOTTLIEB AND EMMA KELLER

Gottlieb was born on June 6, 1881, at Menno, South Dakota. He attended school
in South Dakota, and came to North Dakota with his parents in 1900. He
homesteaded S.E. Sec. 10 Twp. 145 N. R. 88 W. in 1902.

In 1904 he married Emma Fischer at Hazen. Emma was born on September 12, 1885,
in Krem, South Russia, a daughter of William and Carolina, nee Nuss, Fischer.
She immigrated to America with her parents in 1894. The family settled in
Freeman, South Dakota for awhile and then they came to North Dakota where they
took up a homestead northeast of Kronthal. Emma's father had been a School
Master in the Old Country, (as had been his father and grandfather before him).
He continued to teach here in America during these early homesteading years,
having taught in the Kronthal, Expansion and Mannhaven areas.

After their marriage Gottlieb and Emma moved to Gottlieb's homestead. Their
first house was built by Gottlieb's grandfather, Gottlieb Teske. During these
first years on the farm Gottlieb was called upon many times to serve as a
dentist (there was no dentist anywhere near). If someone had a toothache he
would have to find Gottlieb (many times he'd be out in the field) and have the
tooth extracted. In 1915, the Kellers remember, they bought their first car, an
Overland, and that same year they bought their first tractor, a Hart Parr. In
1947 the first barn to be built on the homestead was destroyed by fire, two
saddle horses perished in the flaming barn. The same fall they replaced it with
the present structure.

The Kellers farmed, raising grain, cattle, horses and sheep, until 1948 when
they built a new home and moved into Beulah in December of that year. One of
Gottlieb's hobbies has been woodworking and fishing. He spends many enjoyable
hours at the Garrison Dam fishing with his old friends and acquaintances.

The greatest ordeal of his life occurred in January 1963, when his right leg
had to be amputated below the knee. He is by no means an invalid, for after he
returned from the hospital he made his own peg leg in his work shop. He leads
an active life once more. The Kellers are members of the Zion Lutheran Church
of Beulah.

The Gottlieb Kellers had three children: Walter--born on February 27, 1906,
farms the home place; Hilda--born June 2, 1907 now Mrs. Melvin Leraas, lives
in Loveland, Colorado; Ruby, Mrs. Harry Dieterich of Irvington, California was
born on August 24, 1919. the Kellers have 7 grandchildren, and 5
great-grandchildren.

THE WALTER KELLER FAMILY

        Violet, Larry, Judy and Ivan
        Walter and Katie Keller

Walter was born on February 27, 1906 on the family homestead, where he grew to manhood--attending the Keller school and later farming with his father.

Walter remembers that as a young lad on the farm he did a lot of rabbit and
coyote hunting. He would have to reload his own shells. But the days on the
farm were not all this carefree, for his father was a hardworker and he
expected the same from his son. As a young man he started helping and later
operated a threshing rig. This he did for thirty consecutive years, including
the year that his leg was in a cast. On December 2, 1928 he married Katherina
Becker on the family homestead, north of Beulah, Rev. S. Joachim officiated.

Katherina was born on March 12, 1908, to Henry and Elizabeth, nee Voegele,
Becker. As a youing girl she attended the nearby Klundt school. Katie's mother
died when she was only eight years old. Her father married a second time. Her
stepmother, Karolina Voegele, died when Katie was fifteen, and she assumed the
responsibility of managing the Becker home (with the help of her brothers and
sister, Ann). This she did until she married.

The Walter Kellers went to live on the Gottlieb Keller home place. They did not
live with the Senior Kellers though, for they each had their own home. Walter
farmed with his dad until 1948 when his father retired and Walter took over the
management and operation of the farm, which he is now doing with his son Ivan.
They raise grain crops and Hereford cattle. Walters pride and joy is his farm
workshop, where he spends the winter months repairing his own and neighbors
machinery, cars, trucks,. tractors or whatever is in the need of repair. Walter
is presently serving his 26th year on the school board. The Kellers have four
children; Ivan, Violet, (Mrs. Marvin Schnaidt), Judy and Larry still at home.
They have 5 grandchildren. They are members of the Zion Lutheran Church of
Beulah.

IVAN KELLER FAMILY

        Ivan and Irma Keller with their children Danny, Debby, Lori and
        Jacqueline

Ivan A. Keller was born on September 5, 1931. He attended rural schools from
which he graduated in 1946. He spent his entire boyhood on his father's farm.
Here he was taught how to farm by both his dad and grandfather.

On June 8, 1953 he married Irma Boeshans at the Zion Lutheran Church, Pastor J.
Kammerer officiated. Irma was born on November 18, 1933 at Golden Valley. Her
parents are Joe and Marie, nee Krug, Boeshans. In 1951 she graduated from Leith
High School and attended D.S.T.C. She taught in the local rural schools for 2
1/2 years.

Ivan still farms with his dad; but in later years he has been building up his
own herd of Guernsey dairy cattle, from which he obtains some income by selling
milk.

They have four children; Jacqueline, Danny, Debby, and Lori. They are members
of the Zion Lutheran Church of Beulah

VIOLET AND MARVIN SCHNAIDT

        Marvin, Susan and Violet Schnaidt

Violet D. Keller was born on May 30, 1935, on her parents farm. She attended
rural schools and graduated from the Beulah High School in 1954.

Violet was united in marriage to Marvin W. Schnaidt on August 27, 1954, by
Pastor J. Kammerer at the Zion Lutheran Church of Beulah. Marvin was born on
September 14, 1930, son of Rudolph and Anna Heth Schnaidt. He attended Beulah
Public School and graduated from the Beulah High School in 1949. In July, 1951
he was inducted into the Army where he served for two years.

Since their marriage Violet and Marvin have made their home in Beulah. For a
number of years Marvin worked for the Occident Elevator and then in May 1958 he
began working for M.D.U. at the power plant and has worked there ever since.
Violet has been the local Avon representative since May of 1955. They have one
child, Susan Lynn, born on May 3, 1958.

They are members of the Zion Lutheran Church, where Violet is a member of the
Ladies of the Church organization and Marvin belongs to the brotherhood.

THE DAVID KELLER FAMILY

David and Olga Fischer

David Keller, son of Adam and Katbarina Keller was born at Tripp, South Dakota
on November 15, 1883. He attended school near Tripp. He came to North Dakota
with his parents in 1900. In 1904 he homesteaded S.E. 1/4 Sec. 14 Twp. 145 N.
R. 88 W. His house was built by his grandfather Teske. (This house has since
been moved into Beulah, next to the Texaco Service Station--the Rose Gleich
residence).

He was married to Olga Fischer at Hazen on November 28, 1907. Olga, was born on
May 27, 1889 in S. Russia, to William and Carolina, nee Nuss, Fischer. At the
age of six she came to America with her parents. Olga and Mrs. Gottlieb Keller
were sisters. The Dave Keller family lived on the homestead for several years
until they traded it to Adam Keller, for S.E. 1/4 Sec. 12 Twp. 145 N.R. 88 W.
on which they built their farm. This farm is presently owned and farmed by
their son, Eugene.

The David Keller family had nine children: Arthur and Pauline (twins, both
deceased); Albin a carpenter at Billings, Montana; Viola (deceased); Ernest, a
contractor at Snohomish, Washington; Hedwig, Mrs. Dean Conrad, lives in
Germany; Irene, Mrs. Duke Bodine, Everett, Washington; Eugene and William both
of Beulah, North Dakota.

The Kellers farmed their farm until 1952. Although in the later years they
spent many of the winters in warmer climates. In 1934 and 1937 they spent the
winter in Florida, but most of the time they went out west. In 1952 they bought
a home at Albany, Oregon, here they lived until Mrs. Keller passed away on
March 23, 1955. Aher her death Dave sold the house at Albany and spent his
summers in North Dakota and winters in warmer climates. In 1960 he sold his
farm to Eugene.

On June 26, 1960 he was married a second time to Mrs. Ida Wetzel Richter. They
made their home in Beulah until Ida's death on October 31, 1962.

Although he is 80 years old, Mr. Keller is still very active and takes a great
deal of pleasure and pride driving his own car, a 1950 Pontiac. He drove to the
west coast last fall, making the trip by himself. He is spending the winter
there and plans to return to Beulah in the spring. He hopes to be back in time
for Beulah's Jubilee Celebration.

David Keller is a member of the Beulah Church of God.

EUGENE E. KELLER FAMILY

        Karen, Dwight, Eugene Jr.
        Helen, Gerald, Eugene Sr.
                Joan

Eugene Emil Keller was born on June 2, 1925 at Beulah, N. Dak. He grew up on
his fathers farm, which he now owns. He attended rural school at Kellers School
#2. For a short time he worked for the N.P.R.R. until January 1945, when he
enlisted in the U.S. Navy. He served aboard the U.S.S.S. Harmon, a destroyer
escort, in the South Pacific theatre of Operations.

On June 3, 1946 he was married to Helen Boeshans at Carson, North Dakota. Helen
was born at Optima, Okla. on March 29, 1927, to Joe and Marie, nee Krug,
Boeshans. At the age of three she moved to North Dakota with her parents. Here
she attended schools and graduated from Hazen High School in 1945. She attended
Wartburg College and taught at the Keller school for 3 1/2 years. They have
five children: Eugene Jr., Karen, Dwight, Gerald, and Joann.

After their marriage, Eugene farmed with his dad for two years, during the
winter months of 1946 he worked in the underground Coal Mine at Beulah. In the
fall of 1947 they moved to Absorokee, Montana where Eugene joined a crew of
custom combiners and followed the harvest fields from Texas to Montana. In the
fall of 1948 they returned to Beulah and Eugene worked as a mechanic at
Liebelts Garage until 1950.

They returned to the farm and farmed with his dad until 1952, when they leased
the farm. This same year they bought their first Holstein Cows from Joe
Gustafson. This formed the foundation of their present 40 cow dairy herd. The
crops were fair from 1952 to 1958. During this time two new wells were dug on
the farmsite and running water and sewer put in. 1959 was an extremely dry year
and the pastures and crops dried up. Hay was almost non-existent, so they were
forced to sell their Hereford range cattle. In 1960 they bought the farm from
Dad Keller. Finally in 1962 they had a bumper crop, with wheat going up to 50
bushels an acre.

The Kellers have been active in the community. Eugene has served on the school
board for twelve years, and is a past commander of the V.F.W. Post 7759 of
Beulah. He also helped organize the first Fire Dept. of Beulah. At the present
time both Eugene and Helen are leaders of the Riverside 4-H Club. They are
members of the Congregational Church of Beulah.

WILLAM D.KELLER

        Standing--Charlotte Ann, William Jr. Kevin Sitting--Judith, Stacey and
        William Sr.

William D. Keller was born at Beulah on October 10, 1929. He spent his youth on
his father's farm north of Beulah. He attended rural schools and later on
Beulah High School.

He enlisted in the U.S. Army in 1946 and served in Korea from January 1947
until April 1948. After his discharge he farmed with his father for about one
year. On July 31, 1949, he was united in marriage with Judith L. Richter,
daughter of the late John and Ida (Wetzel) Richter. Judy was born at the Dakota
Colleries Mine Camp at Zap, on February 23, 1934. After their marriage they
farmed for two more years and then moved into Beulah in 1951.

Since then Bill has been doing construction work. For the past eleven years he
has been employed with the J. L. Cox and Son Pipeline Co., as a truck driver
hauling heavy equipment. This work has taken them throughout the entire United
States. When they are not out on a job (usually in the winter months) they make
their home in Beulah, where the Keller children attend the Beulah Public School.

To this family were born five children: Charlotte Ann, William Jr., Kevin
Kelly, Curtis Albin (deceased) and Stacey Annette. The Kellers are members of
the Beulah Congregational Church.

AUGUST KELLER FAMILY

August Keller, a son of Adam and Katharina Teske Keller, was born at Tripp,
South Dakota. In 1900 he came to North Dakota with his parents. In 1911 August
took up a homestead claim 10 miles north of Beulah. This quarter of land is
still part of his present farm. He is a veteran of World War I. He was called
into the U.S. Armed Services in the year of 1918. He spent six months overseas,
mostly in France. He was wounded on Armistice Day in 1918. In the spring of
1919, he came out of service, received his discharge, and continued to work on
the farm with his father.

In the year of 1920 he was united in marriage to Louise Klundt, daughter of
Wilhelm and Elizabeth Klundt. They made their home on August's farm north of
Beulah, which they still actively farm. They are members of the Peace Lutheran
Church which is about 17 miles north of Beulah. August is also a member of
Beulah American Legion.

This couple has two daughters; Elsie Marlyn and Irma Mardella. Elsie graduated
from the Beulah High School in 1952. She attended N.D.S.U. at Fargo, from which
she graduated in 1956, with a B.S. Degree in Home Economics Education. She
taught Home Ec. in New Salem for three years, in Beulah for two years, and also
taught at Cordova, Alaska for one year. At the present time she is Home
Extension Agent in Williston. She married Leland Gilbertson April 5, 1964.

Irma graduated from the Beulah High School in 1954. She went into Nurses
Training at the Bismarck Hospital, from which she graduated as a Registered
Nurse in 1957. She married Robert Bitner in 1957. They live in Devils Lake and
have four children.

The Antelope Community Band

        Left to right: John Tesky, August Keller, Henry Basen, Ed Keller, Pete
        Boeckel, Andrew Tesky, Ed Erickson, Small boy Walter Keller.

PETER AND BARBARA KEMMET

        Ernest, David, Helen and Ray
        Mr. and Mrs. Kemmet

Peter Kemmet was born on December 5, 1881 in Gleuckstahl, So. Russia, to
Christian and Rozina, nee Schnaidt, Kemmet. There were ten children 5 boys and
5 girls, four of whom are still living.

Peter was inducted into the Russian Army in October, 1903. He served for three
years during the Russian-Japanese War. He was a member of the Russian Army
Band, playing an instrument similar (though somewhat larger) to the bassoon. He
was discharged with the rank of sargeant at Christmas time in 1906. In 1907
Peter sailed on the ship, "The Rhine", for the United States. He arrived at
Glen Ullin on December 6th where he was met by his sister and her husband, Mr.
and Mrs. John Jakober, Sr. He lived with the Jakobers until he took up his
homestead in 1908.

Barbara Jakober was born in Gleuckstahl, So. Russia on February 22, 1884 to
Karl and Louisa, nee Stok, Jakober. There were 8 children in the Jakober
family, 3 sons and 5 daughters, 3 are still living. When her brother John and
his family decided to come to America, Barbara wanted to go with them, but she
had no money. Her brother-in-law Matt Hettick lent her the money for her fare.
She packed her few personal belongings into her knapsack, bade her family a sad
farewell and set sail on the "Kaiser Wilhelm II". In the year of 1905 she
arrived at Ashley where she was met by her Uncle, Friedrich Nies and he helped
her find work. The first year she worked for the John H. Wishek family for 50¢
a day. With the money she earned she paid back her debt to Matt. Hettick.

In 1906 she left Ashley and took up a homestead 16 miles south of Beulah on
what is now the Ernest Kemmet farm. Her brother John lived only a few miles
from her "place" so he would loan her some of the neccessary equipment with
which she could begin to "break the land". The equipment consisted of a hand
plow and three horses, with these Barbara personally broke 9 acres of sod and
planted some flax. The first crop yielded 40 bushels which she sold for 70¢ a
bushel; thus Barbara had made the first $28.00 from her farm. Her only mode of
travel was a stone boat. Her only help was her eight-year old nephew, Fred
Flemmer. Barbara will always remember one evening when she and Fred were just
getting ready to eat, she happened to glance out of the door and saw a huge
bull trying to break through the protection fence that had been built around
her one-room sod house. She tried to chase him away but only succeeded in
drawing his attention to them. She barely got the door closed and bolted, when
the bull began trying to break into the cabin. The bull was pushing the door on
the outside while Barbara and Fred were pushing on the inside. After what
seemed to be an eternity, the bull got tired of trying to dig his way into the
house, turned around and went on his way. Later on they heard that this very
bull had almost gored a man to death. Needless to say the supper was left
uneaten as they hurried to the nearest neighbors to spend the night. These were
not ideal conditions under which a single woman should live.

Peter Kemmet's homestead was adjacent to Barbara's and when they were married
they combined the two into one farm which they farmed until their retirement in
1959.

Barbara and Peter were married on March 23, 1908, by Pastor Wm. Klein. They
were the first couple to be married in the Friedens Gemeinde (Now known as the
Peace Lutheran Church). The congregation had not built the church as yet so
they were married at the home of Barbara's sister, The Jacob Flemmers. To this
union were born 4 children: Reinhold (Ray) married Edna Jung (now deceased),
they had two children Alvin and Shirley. Ray lives in Everett, Wash. Helen, who
lives in Bismarck, married Ed. Voegele and later on Reuben Hummel (now
deceased). She has 5 children: Clemens, Delmar, Arlene, Joan Voegele and
Barbara Hummel. David married Anna Schmidt, they live in Bismarck and have 2
sons, Du Wayne and Clyde (Buddy). Ernest lives on the Kemmet farm, married Mary
Entzel and they have 2 children, Audrey and Lennie, The Kemmets have 15
great-grandchildren.

On March 23, 1958 the Peter Kemmets celebrated their Golden Wedding
Anniversary. All of their children were present at this happy occasion. Life
has not been easy, they had to work hard (many times for little or nothing).
They had their share of disappointments, but they did not give up, they kept
right on working as best they could. Peter has always enjoyed good health,
although Barbara has been "sickly" in later years. All in all the Kemmets feel
that they have no complaints to make and that the Lord has been good to them.
The Kemmets have belonged to the Lutheran Church all of their lives and at the
present time are members of the Zion Lutheran Church of Beulah.

        The Kemmets two youngest grandchildren Audrey and Lana

MR. AND MRS. JOHN JAKOBER, SR.

        Standing--Left to right: Christ, John, Rose, Anna, Katie, Louisa,
        Pauline. Seated--Mr. and Mrs. John Jakober.

John Jakober Sr. was born July 9, 1878 in Gleuckstahl, South Russia to Carl and
Louise (Stock) Jakober. He married Katherina Kemmet, daughter of Christian and
Rosina (Schnaidt) Kemmet on Nov. 21, 1902. Katherina was born in Gleuckstahl,
S. Russia on Oct. 29, 1877.

It took them 14 days to come to America by boat in Oct. 1905. They then went by
train to Ashley, N. Dak., where they stayed with their Uncle Frederick Nies.
The Nies's helped them get started in farming. They bought horses, cows, a
wagon, and plow and rented a piece of farm-land. They lived in their Uncle's
summer kitchen for one year, while they looked for some homesteading property.
When they found none near Ashley they realized that they would have to go
further west where there was still some land available. In May 1906 John Sr.
and his brother-in-law Jacob Flemmer (who had just come from Russia) started
looking for land. They went to Turtle Lake where they met Jacob's brother Karl
Flemmer. Together they headed west, crossing the Missouri River with the ferry
at Washburn. They investigated prospects near Stanton and in the Krem vicinity,
but there was no available land. They decided to return to Turtle Lake. By this
time the Missouri was experiencing it's "spring rise" and it was impossible for
the ferry to run. But this handful of immigrants didn't realize how deep the
back water in coulees could be, but they soon found out when they thought they
could drive across and their buggy began to float and the horses had to swim.
The men were forced to take position at the rear of the vehicle in order to try
to guide it and keep it from tipping. Needless to say they were water-soaked
and when they got to dry land the first thing they had to do was build a fire
and dry their clothes. By now their impression of this "New America" was not the
greatest. They went back to Mandan; Carl went back home to Turtle Lake and John
Sr. and Jacob went by train to Glen Ullin. From here they walked north to the
Frederick Voegele farm. They continued looking for land, finally finding some
about 21 miles northwest of Glen Ullin, in an area known as the South Beaver
Creek Community. They both filed their claims.

John Jakober went back to Ashley to take care of his farming. In July he came
back and broke land and built a sod house roofed with timbers, which were
covered with sod. He went back to Ashley again and harvested his crop. In Oct.
1906 the Jakobers loaded their belongings into a box car, this included
household items, two cows, 4 horses, 1 plow and some chickens. They went by
train to Glen Ullin. From there they loaded their belongings into a wagon, and
tied the cows behind the wagon and set out for their homestead.

The next spring they broke up 20 acres of land with a walking plow that had
four horses hitched to it. They seeded flax. In the summer of 1909 they built a
wooden house which still stands as a part of the remodeled home on the original
homestead. The wooden house was 18' x 24' and was built for $300.

In reminiscing on early days the Jakobers had many things in common with the
other homesteaders such as: their only transportation was the horse, buggy or
wagon. When they hauled their wheat to the elevator in Hebron by horse and
wagon (this was usually a 4-hour drive) they would have to wait in line for
their turn; many times the fast train would come tearing by, scaring the
horses. They would rear and snort and one would really have to hang on to keep
them from bolting the line. They remember the terrible rain and hail storm of
1915 which completely wiped out the crop. In the fall of 1918, the flu hit the
area and entire families would be sick in bed at the same time. So many of the
people were sick that when someone died it was hard to find someone strong
enough to dig the grave, and there were many graves that had to be dug at this
time.

Not all the times were bad, there were some bright things happening too. In
June 1921 the Jakobers bought their first car, a Model T. Ford for which they
paid $700.00. This was the "pride and joy" of the whole family. Never again was
there ever a car so "elegant".

John and Katherina Jakober lived on the family farm until October, 1958, when
they sold it to the son, John Jr. They retired and moved to Hebron, where they
bought a home and live at the present time.

The Jakobers had ten children, six of whom are still living. Rose, Mrs. August
Jaeger, Beulah, N. Dak.; Pauline, Mrs. Hugo Jaeger, Bismarck, N. D.; Louisa,
Mrs. Casper Olson, St. Paul, Minn.; Anna, Mrs. Toney Worek, Denver, Colo.; John
who lives on the family farm and Christ who married Martha Sasse and lives in
Beulah. Katie passed away January 10, 1933 and Katharina, Marie and Carl died
as infants. The Jakobers have 9 grandchildren and 5 great-grandchildren.

THREE GENERATIONS: FREDERICK JAEGER, AUGUST JAEGER, AND CLARENCE JAEGER.

THE FREDERICK JAEGER, SR. FAMILY

        Back Row--John, Hugo, August, Paul, George. Second
        Row--Richard, Anna, Fred. Seated--Martha, Mr. and Mrs.
        Fred Jaeger, Frieda, Rose.

Frederick Jaeger Sr. was born Jan. 31, 1877 in Stein Baden, Germany to Karl and
Elizabeth (Merz) Jaeger. He came to America in 1895 at the age of 18. He found
work on the Ferdinand Leutz ranch in the Farmers Valley area.

Anna Birkmaier, daughter of Christoph and Margaretha, nee Schaich, Birkmaier
was born in Neuffen Wurtemberg, Germany on August 15, 1882. She came to America
with her parents at the age of three and they settled on a farm 6 miles south
of Hebron.

Frederick and Anna were married on April 6, 1901 at Hebron. After they were
married they moved on a ranch 18 miles south of Zap (on Frederick's homestead).

This union was blessed with eleven children, ten of whom are still living:
Paul, New Sharon, Iowa; Frieda, Mrs. Christ Teske, Zap, N. D.; Hugo,
(deceased); John, Mandan, N. D.; August, Beulah, N. D.; Rose, Mrs. Albert
Schiermeister, Zap, N. D.; George, Olympia, Wash.; Martha, Mrs. Carl Merkel,
Beulah, N. D.; Richard, Bismarck, N. Dak.; Anna Mathies, Olympia, Wash.; and
Fred Jr., Bismarck, N. D.

The Jaegers farmed their original homestead (some additional land had been
added through the years; it's now known as the Johnny Gunsch Farm) until 1942,
when they retired and moved to Hebron. Frederick passed away in 1951, and Anna
stayed in Hebron until 1953 when she moved to Beulah. She is now a resident at
the Joachim Memorial Home in Beulah.

        The Jaegers on a Family Outing

        August and Rose Jaeger

August Jaeger is the son of Mr. and Mrs. Fred Jaeger. Rose, Jakober, Jaeger is
a daughter of Mr. and Mrs. John Jakober. Both August and Rose spent their
childhood and grew to adulthood in the South Beaver Creek Area. The Jaegers
remember many incidents of the early days of herding cattle, the first school
and church, using wagons, buggy and horse-back transportation. Rose remembers
her first ride in an automobile. Dr. Schierbaum of Hebron had a little red
one-seater which he used when he went on sick calls in the area, and one day he
gave Rose and her sister their first ride. Such a thrill!

August and Rose were married on July 12, 1932 at the Peace Lutheran Church, by
Pastor Em. Fuchs. They went to live on the Adam Fuchs farm, but these were the
depression days and farming wasn't the most profitable so on Sept., 1936 they
moved to Hebron. They lived here for one year before moving to Zap in October,
1937. This was the time that August started working for the Dakota Colleries.
From October 1939 to April 1950, they lived at the Strip Mine Camp (this was a
little settlement of about 30 miner's families). On April 20, 1950 they moved
to their present home in Beulah. The Jaegers have two sons; Clarence and Alvin.

August is still working for the Strip Mine. He and his son, Clarence own and
operate a construction firm, the Tri-County Corporation.

The Jaegers are members of the Zion Lutheran Church. Mrs. Jaeger is a member of
the Women of the Church Society and of the Mercer County Gray Ladies.

ALVIN JAEGER

Alvin was born Dec. 10, 1943. He is a 1961 Beulah High School Graduate. He
attended the Bismarck Junior College for two years and at the present time is a
Junior at the Dickinson State Teachers College.

MR. AND MRS. CLARENCE JAEGER

Clarence graduated from the Beulah High School in 1952. He then attended
Wahpeton State School of Science. He served for two years in the Armed
Services. After his discharge he worked on construction jobs in Florida and New
York.

Clarence married Leah Lackman on Dec. 22, 1962. Leah is the daughter of Mr. and
Mrs. Alex Lackman of Edgeley, N. Dak. She is the Home Economics instructor at
the Beulah High School. Clarence manages the Tri County Corp which he and his
father own.

HISTORY OF GAMBLES STORE

Mr. L. G. Rose opened the first Gamble store in Beulah in September of 1933.
William J. Church purchased it in 1937. Edwin E. Jose bought the store in 1940.
On May 22, 1942 Mr. Jose sold the store to Flemmer Sales, Inc. they operated it
until June 1945. When the Gamble Franchise was dropped Beulah was then without
a Gamble store until John Brazzell set up a store in January 1946. Mr. and Mrs.
Herman Hauff of Golden Valley bought the store on November 30, 1950, and are
the present owners. In 1956 the Hauff's purchased the Esterley building next to
their store; this additional space made it possible for them to stock furniture
and more appliances.

Herman is a son of Gottlieb and Mary Hauff (deceased) who had homesteaded north
of Golden Valley in 1907. Herman's wife Vivian is a daughter of John and Gladys
Erickson Scheidt of the south Beulah area. They came to Mercer County in 1907
and 1910 respectively. Mr. and Mrs. Hauff have four children Joanne, Jerry,
Mary Ann, and Janice.

THE YEAR OF 1927

July 4--the celebration was attended by 5,000. 100 miners employed by the local
mine.

September--Beulah began installation of their first electric street lights. It
was the first town along the North Branch to be able to boast of this. They
were turned on November 10.

FRED HAUCK FAMILY HISTORY

Fred Hauck Jr., son of Fred Hauck Sr. and Mary DeWald Hauck, was born December
28, 1903, in South Russia. He came to this country while still a boy with his
parents. He grew up and attended schools in the Richardton area.

On April 14, 1925, Fred married Rose Mary Brawn, daughter of Joseph and Eugenia
Brawn of Glen Ullin, North Dakota. To this union were born six children; Anna
(Mrs. Steve Goldade), Lorraine (Mrs. Harold Pischel), Josephine (Mrs. Leo N.
Ziegler), Cleo (Mrs. Richard Mettler), Wilfred and Kathleen.

In the fall of 1925 Fred and Rose Hauck moved to Oliver County on Sec.
32-142-83. There they farmed until the fall of 1946 when they moved to Beulah.

During the years 1946 to 1948, Fred continued to farm and do other work around
Beulah. Then in 1948 he took over the duties of Chief of Police in Beulah, a
position which he still holds.

The Haucks have fifteen grandchildren. They are members of the St. Joseph
Catholic Church of Beulah.

The Steve Goldade family in the fall of 1956 moved to Silverton, Idaho. He is
employed at the Burke Lead and Zinc mine in Burke, Idaho. They have four
children.

In 1953 the Harold Pischel family moved to Osburn, Ida., where he was employed
at the Burke Mine. Then in 1961 they moved to Grants, New Mexico where he is
employed in an uranium mine. Pischels have three children.

The Leo N. Zeiglers are living in Columbus, Mont., where he is employed as the
manager of the G.T.A. Elevator. Before going to Montana, they lived at
Gladstone, N. Dak.They have three children.

Richard Mettler and family are living in South International Falls, Minn.,
where he is employed at the Paper Mill. they also own and operate the Dicks
Liquor Store. They have two children. They formerly lived in Tioga, N. D.

Wilfred served in the Armed Services, part of this three year hitch was spent
in Germany. Following his discharge he enrolled in The Wahpeton School of
Science. He is married to the former Cecilia Pfliger of Hazen. They are living
in Willmar, Minnesota where he is employed as a plumber. They have three
children.

Kathleen is the youngest of the Hauck children. She is a Senior at the Beulah
High School.

The Elite Beauty and the Modern Barber Shop are owned and operated by Mr. and
Mrs. Wilmer Wiedrich. Their son Timothy, age 8, is in the barber chair. Mr.
Wiedrich is a graduate of Moler Barber College of Fargo. Mrs. Wiedrich is a
graduate of Christie Beauty School of Bismarck. The building was established in
1953.

MR. AND MRS. FREDERICK HUBER

Frederick Huber was born on June 10, 1884,in Gleuckstahl, South Russia to Jacob
and Christina, nee Voegele, Huber. There were ten children in the Huber family:
Jacob, John, Michael, Christina, (Mrs. John Bieber), Rosina, Fred, Christian,
Daniel, Gottlieb, and David. Fred, Christ, and David are still living.

Fred decided to leave Russia, as he too had heard of the wonderful
opportunities offered in that far off land of America. On August of 1905, Fred,
a young man of 2l, arrived at Glen Ullin. From 1905 to 1907 Fred worked on a
sheep ranch in Oliver County, in the Otter Creek vicinity, for $300. a year.
After two years of hard work and saving all the money he could, Fred took up a
homestead about 18 miles south of Beulah.

Rosina Retzer was born on October 17, 1889 to John and Christina, Henger,
Retzer. She came to the U. S. with her parents in December 1905. The Retzer
family farmed for two years at Hosmer, S. D. and then moved to N. D. and took
up a homestead 20 miles north of Glen Ullin, in Mercer County, now the Dave
Retzer farm. Rosina is the only survivor of the Retzer family as Christina,
Mrs. Fred Wald, Jacob, and Fredericka, Mrs. John Wolff have all passed away.

Fred and Rosina Huber were married on February 15, 1908, at the Neu Gleuckstahl
Congregational Church, Pastor Herman Seil, officiated. They went to live on
Fred's homestead and lived in the little sod house until 1913, when they built
their first frame house. Here they raised their eight children. On this farm
they weathered the dust storms of the 30's, the disappointments of low prices
for the farm products, and the endless, backbreaking work of farming your
homestead without the help of the modern machinery of today. The worries were
many: whether or not you'd have enough money to feed and clothe your family
through the long N. D. winters; then with the coming of spring, whether you'd
have enough money to buy seed; and after seeding came the longest wait and
worry of all, would it rain, and if you had a good stand, you'd wonder if you
could get it off before you "hailed out", (as crop and hail insurance was
unheard of in those days). The Hubers were a frugal and strong willed couple;
so they withstood all these hardships that plagued them during the greater
share of their farming days, and they made a success out of their farming
operations.

When Fred Jr. took over the management of the Huber farm, in 1937, Fred and
Rosina moved into Beulah. They built a new home in 1951, where they are living
at the present time.

The Hubers had eight children: Lydia, Mrs. Christ Ahl of Dickinson, N. D.;
Anna, Mrs. Christ Aipperspach, of Vallejo, Calif.; Bertha, Mrs. Andrew Seiwert
(died in 1944); Fred Jr. lives on the Huber farm; David farms near Beulah;
Hilda, Mrs. Emanuel Seiwert of Vallejo, Calif.; Gustav of Denver, Colorado; and
Walter who teaches school at Hebron, North Dakota.

THE YEAR OF 1916

January--the town of Beulah was incorporated.

February--Knife River Tele. Co. agreed to erect and maintain a telephone system
in Beulah, completed in May. Operators, Mr.and Mrs. Evan Munro. Dale Sovereign
built Beulah's first theatre, "The Palm", which boasted that it had electric
lights, roomy chairs and perfumed air.

May 1916--Beulah goes on daylight Savings time--clocks set ahead.

June--Beulah votes to build a city hall.

Summer--Max Martell started building our first concrete sidewalks.

August--the first attorney moves in--Sammuel P. Rigler.

September--Beulah gets its first creamery--built by Fred Krause.

November--Business men hauled 100 loads of gravel on Main Street.

EMANUEL LIEBELT

Emanuel Liebelt was born July 10, 1902, near Rheim, Oliver County, North
Dakota, where his parents, Joseph Liebelt and Louisa Schulz Liebelt homesteaded
in the late 1890's. From there the family moved to Colorado for two years. Then
they returned by covered wagon to the Harvey area. Soon they returned to the
original homestead. Gradually acquiring more land, the family farmed there
until 1919.

Emanuel worked nights while he attended business college in Bismarck. He then
managed the bank in Coleharbor, N. D. for Frank Vogel. This led to a two year
job in the Bank of North Dakota.

During a change of bank administrations, the selling field began to look more
promising, so the Fuller Brush Co. gained a new salesman, who worked his way up
to become the assistant branch manager at Fergus Falls, Minnesota. In 1924 Mr.
Liebelt married Anne Maye VanVleet. She was born in Galesville, Wisconsin, in
1905 and came with her family to Driscoll, N. D. in 1909. She graduated from
Bismarck High School, then worked in the N. D. Hail Insurance Department and
the Bank of North Dakota.

After a period during which Mr. Liebelt worked for the Valley City Granite and
Marble Works, the family moved to Hazen. There he began selling Chevrolets. He
then became general manager of a Chevrolet organization in New Salem and the
operator of his own automobile agency in Glen Ullin. After thirteen years in
Glen Ullin, Mr. Liebelt started Service Chevrolet in Beulah. Here the family
has remained and the three Liebelt sons: Emanuel Jr., Beuhl and Jonathan
received their diplomas from Beulah High School.

For over 23 years the Liebelts have been active in both business and civic
work. Mrs. Liebelt has been the head of such varied activities as Royal
Neighbors, American Legion Auxiliary, Beulah Homemakers' Club, Congregational
Ladies' Aid and Sunday School. Mr. Liebelt served for six years on the
