Chevrolet Motor Division Dealership Planning Committee. He helped reorganize
the Commercial Club into the Chamber of Commerce and served as president of
this organization for three years. During this time he also helped to have
Beulah's status changed from village to city government, and for one and
one-half terms, served as mayor of the city.

W. H. ESTERLY

On New Year's Day of 1933 the W. H. Esterlys moved to Beulah from Hazen, where
Wesley had been working in the law office of John Moses and where Grace had
taught in the high school. Both were originally from Minnesota. They had met at
North Central College, in Illinois. After graduation Grace taught in Minnesota
and Wesley studied Law at the University of Minnesota.

The Law office which the Esterlys purchased from S. M. Frank was the building
now used as a jewelry shop by John Hyttinen. After renting the Irv. Neidermeyer
house for several months, the Esterlys enlarged the apartment at the rear of
the office and lived there for several years before buying and remodeling the
present J. W. Maier home.

As he built up his law practice in Beulah, Mr. Esterly was active in various
community affairs--Lions Club, Community Club, Park Board. He was a member of
the Board of Education for 17 years.

Four Esterly children, Mary, James, Margaret and Charles completed the family.
Mary was graduated from high school in 1951. She then attended North Central
College, where she majored in Home Economics.

Although he had cut down on few activities and kept his law office open to the
end, Mr. Esterly's last four or five years were spent combating chronic
leukemia. He died October 1, 1953, while on a business trip to Minneapolis. His
body was brought back for burial in the Beulah cemetery.

The family remained in Beulah for several more years. Jim graduated from high
school in 1955. That fall the family moved to Leaf River, Illinois, where Grace
became a teacher of English and speech. Mary, now graduated from college,
taught Home Economics in Forreston nearby. Jim attended college and the younger
children were in school in Leaf River.

Mary became Mrs. George Martens, and is now the mother of twins, James and
Judith, and of a younger son, Michael. Her address is R. 1, Box 69, Forreston,
Illinois.

Jim graduated from North Central College and then studied medicine at the
University of Chicago. He is now finishing his interne year there and plans for
residency, specializing in Pathology. He and his wife Barbara have a one year
old son, Tommy.

Margaret, a 1960 graduate of Leaf River High School, attended the University of
Illinois. In August 1963, she became Mrs. Gerald Schelling. She is graduating
in Biology, while her husband continues working at the University on his
Master's degree.

Chuck, a 1964 high school semor, has been active in sports and music, serves as
editor of the school annual, and is an honor student. His plans are to attend
the University of Illinois at Urbana.

The family regards Beulah as "home" during the years they like to remember.

        Charles         Margaret        Mrs. Esterly    James   Mary

REV. AND MRS. S. JOACHIM

Solomon Joachim, son of Gottlieb and Dorethea Joachim was born on April 30,
1895, near Eureka, South Dakota. He received Holy Baptism and at the age of 14
was confirmed in the Lutheran faith. Upon completing the eighth grade he
entered the Eureka Lutheran College and then continued his studies at the
Wartburg Seminary at Dubuque, Iowa. During his first year at the Seminary he
was called to serve in the United States Army. After the Armistice was signed
he reentered the Seminary and completed his Theological training on June 16th,
1921. Later, during his pastorate he pursued further studies and earned for
himself the Bachelor of Laws degree in 1927.

Shortly after his ordination at Enderlin, N. Dak. he was united in marriage to
Olga Uttke and then served the Lutheran Parish at Bentley, N. Dak., for five
years. In 1926 he was called to the Zion Lutheran Church at Beulah. They
arrived on Sept. 30, 1926. He served this parish untiringly for 17 years until
his death.

Pastor Joachim not only served this local congregation but also the Dakota
District of the Church on the Mission Committee and was it's treasurer for
three years.

He was an active member of the American Legion, the local P.T.A. and the Beulah
Lion's Club.

He joined the Beulah American Legion (the Argonne Post No. 85) early in 1927.
He held the office of Chaplain, Historian, Commander, and Adjutant, which post
he held at the time of his death. In the years 1939-1940 he was appointed
District Deputy of the Seventh District.

He was a member of the Beulah Lions Club continuously from Sept. 4, 1935 until
his death. He was secretary of the Club for three years and president during
the year of 1942-1943.

Rev. Joachim always took a fine interest in forming future citizens and worked,
therefore, with untiring efforts with the Boy Scouts, serving as Scout Leader
in his later years. His work was always well done. In his friendly way, he made
lasting friends who knew him as a friend indeed.

The Joachims had three foster children; Betty Ann, Lynn, and Donald.

Rev. Joachim conducted his last service at the Zion Lutheran Church on December
19, 1943. He passed away on January 1, 1944 at the Beulah Hospital. He had
reached the age of 48 years and 9 mos. Funeral Services were held at the Zion
Lutheran Church, and he was laid to rest at the Enderlin Cemetery.

Olga Joachim was born at Enderlin, where she grew to womanhood. She attended
the Concordia Conservatory of Music at Fargo. This was a great asset to her as
a pastor's wife as she served for many years as the church organist. She also
gave piano lessons to the local youngsters. There are many Beulah residents and
former residents (now in their 30's and 40's) who remember their feeble
attempts at refinement at the parsonage piano. Mrs. Joachim counting and Rev.
humming along at whatever rythm or rate of speed the pupil set.

The Joachims served Zion Lutheran as a team. Every summer they conducted German
School for a whole month. The enrollment would be from 60 to 80 pupils and only
two teachers, Rev. and Mrs. Joachim. Here they would be taught how to read,
write (script) and spell in German; Luther's Catechism, Bible History, Church
Doctrine and umpteen familiar German Hymns. On top of all this there would be
practice for the annual Children's Day Program, which included pantomines,
marching drills and many musical selections. If one attended every summer, by
the time you reached confirmation age you would not only be an accomplished
German reader, writer and speller; you would also know the Catechism from cover
to cover, and be able to sing along at most of the important church functions
without the aid of a Hymnal. The amazing thing of all of this is, that there
was no discipline problem (even tho the age ranges were from 5 thru 14, and
many times the classes were conducted in one room.) German school wasn't all
hard work for they played too. There isn't a girl who had ever attended, that
doesn't remember playing Mrs. Joachims favorite game "Last Couple Out". Or the
boys who will remember the competitive softball games with the Catholic boys
who were attending Sister School down the hall. (During these years the
churches could rent space at School to have their vacation Church School.)
Later on the Joachims also had English Bible school here.

Olga Joachim was also active in civic affairs. She was a member of the Beulah
American Legion Auxiliary, Beulah Womens Club, and the P.T.A. She also took an
active part in German Ladies Aid and Mission Circle.

After Rev's death Mrs. Joachim and children left Beulah and lived for awhile at
Enderlin. In later years she remarried and changed addresses. She died of
cancer on January 20, 1958, at Fitzimmonds Hospital, Denver Colorado. She was
laid to rest beside the grave of her husband Solomon at the Enderlin Cemetery.

With the passing away of Mrs. Joachim Urness another chapter in the book of
"Fond Memories of Beulah" was closed, but not forgotten.

THE YEAR OF 1918

January--the local light system was connected with the Beulah Mine and Plant.

In March 1918 Atty. Sander J. Cotlow bought out Rigler.

April--Beulah was the first town in the state to have a woman serve on the
Village Board, Mrs. H. H. Kenyon.

June--Mrs. Irv. Vincent enlisted as a Red Cross Nurse. She joined her husband
who was already in service.

September 11--the first Mercer County Boy was killed in action in
France--Lawrence E. Higbee of Beulah.

This page sponsored by the F. K. Flemmers.

        Edith, Norman, Elmer, Esther, Ed and Lillian

ED AND LILLIAN FISCHER

Ed Fischer was born at Ashley, North Dakota, on August 31, 1904, to Frederick
and Magdelena, nee Jenner, Fischer. There were eight children in the Fischer
family. The six that are still living are: Adolph, Philip, Ed, Katie, Mrs.
Victor Stephens, Esther, Mrs. Dan Kirchmeier, and Hattie, Mrs. Don Weldon. Ed
grew to adulthood in the Hazen area. In 1923 he went to Canada, where he met
his future wife, Lillian.

Lillian Walters was born at Wenstrup, South Dakota on Sept. 15, 1908, the
daughter of John and Sarah, nee Wallman, Walters. In 1910 the Walters family
immigrated to Canada, where Mr. Walters was engaged in farming.

Ed and Lillian were married on October 20, 1927, at Camrose, Alberta, Canada.
They lived in Canada working on and renting farms. In 1943 they moved back to
the United States. They farmed for a few years and then operated a cafe in Zap,
until 1950, when they moved to Beulah. In 1950 Ed started working for M.D.U. at
the Beulah Power Plant, and has worked there ever since. The Fischers have four
children:

Esther is married to George Keller. They have two sons; Edward and Duane. They
lived in Beulah and the surrounding area until 1962, when they moved onto the
Nordin Ranch at Sentinel Butte, North Dakota.

Norman graduated from the Zap High School in 1950. He was inducted into the
U.S. Army in April of 1951. He served to nine months in the Korean War Theatre
of Operations as a Lab. Technician. After his discharge in 1953 he enrolled in
the Medical Technician School at Minneapolis. He is married to the former
Florence Borner. They have three children; Debbie, Tammy and Marlen. They make
their home at Forsyth, Montana, where Norman is a Lab. Technician at the
Forsyth County Hospital.

Elmer was inducted into the U.S. Army in September 1953. Here he served as a
cook and paratrooper. He was stationed for 9 months with the Occupation Forces
in Southern Japan. He received his discharge in 1955. Elmer is presently
employed as a chef in The Green Brae at Sparks, Nevada. He and his wife, Jean,
have one daughter, Donna and one son, Larry.

Edith is married to James Lagge, formerly of Beulah. They and their two sons;
Rick and Bruce; live in Chelan, Washington. Jim owns and operates Jim's Barber
Shop.

The Ed Fischers are members of the Zion Lutheran Church of Beulah, where
Lillian is a member of the Women of the Church Society.

        Ervin, Mr. and Mrs. Goetz, Clifford

MR. AND MRS. JOHN GOETZ

John Goetz was born on December 15, 1903 in Gleuckstahl, South Russia, to
Christ and Rosa, nee Lapp, Goetz. There were 7 children in the Christ Goetz
family, all of whom are still living; Rose, Mrs. Fred Berg; Katie, Mrs. Jake
Flemmer; both of Mobridge, S. Dak.; John of Beulah; Christ, Jake, Philip, and
Edward, all of Akaska, South Dakota. He immigrated to America with his parents
in 1906, where his father took up a homestead 4 miles south of Akaska, South
Dakota.

Rosa Lapp was born on October 8, 1903 in Gleuckstahl, S. Russia, to Martin and
Fredericka, nee Huber, Lapp. There were 8 children in the Martin Lapp family;
Margaret, Mrs. Fred Schutz of Beulah; Rose, Mrs. John Goetz of Beulah; Frieda,
Mrs. Ben Fischer of Pick City; Mary, Mrs. John Reinhardt of Beulah; Anna, Mrs.
John Kessler of Bismarck; Louisa, Mrs. Christ Kessler of Beach; Lydia, Mrs.
Albert Heiser of Worland, Wyoming and Emil of Beulah.

John and Rosa Goetz were married on November 30, 1926, at the Zion Lutheran
Church of Beulah, by Pastor S. Joachim. They lived for a short while in Akaska,
S. D. In the fall of 1927 they moved to a farm 19 miles southwest of Beulah,
which they still farm with their youngest son, Clifford.

The John Goetz's were blessed with 2 sons, Ervin and Clifford. Ervin married
Elaine Neuberger in 1951, their children are, Delden and Charlene. They live on
an 80 acre dairy farm at Stanchfield, Minnesota. During the summer months Ervin
works with the boilermakers and welders. Clifford, who lives on the family
farm, married Marlene Tabert in 1957. They have 4 children; Peggy, Steve,
Bonnie, and Linda.

The Goetz's lived on their farm until 1957 when they built a new home in
Beulah, where they are living at the present time. They are members of the Zion
Lutheran Church of Beulah.

        Goetz Threshing Crew

JOHN MAIER, SR.

        Josephine Maier         John Maier Sr.

John Maier Sr. was born in Germany on June 24, 1854. He immigrated to America
in 1882, and settled in Cleveland, Ohio. Here he met Josephine Marie
Klingenstein (she was born on January 10, 1857 at Uberlingen, Germany). John
and Josephine were married on July 4, 1882. The first two years of their
married life they lived in Cleveland where John Sr. was employed in the wire
mill. Here their oldest daughter, Tillie, was born. But life in the big city
did not appeal to John. When he heard of all the homesteading land available in
the Dakotas, he felt that he just had to come out west to try his luck. He came
as far as Jamestown by train--here he bought a homesteader's outfitter rig--a
covered wagon pulled by a team of oxen. He arrived in New Salem on March 19,
1884. He took up a homestead claim eight miles north of New Salem. He hurried
and built his "claim shack" and sent for his wife and daughter. Their traintrip
out to North Dakota was quite a harrowing experience for this young pioneer
mother. For one of their fellow travelers was an Indian Chief. She had heard
many stories about these so called wild savages and when the chief picked up
her little girl and carried her around in the train, she knew a moment of
panic. But then she realized that he was only trying to be friendly and
helpful. She also found out that this was no ordinary Indian, for he was well
known and his fame was widespread. His name? Sitting Bull!

The John Maiers lived on their homestead for about five years. Three of their
children; Anne, Bill and Carl, were born here. In order to supplement the
family income John Sr. worked part-time at the Sims brickyard and also dug
wells in the surrounding area.

Five years later the Maiers' moved to a new farm site. Here they built a larger
sod building with living quarters on one side and the barn for the stock on the
other side. The partition separating the two was made of sod. Moving to this
new farm was a wise and fortunate choice, because nearby was a flowing spring,
which gave them an ample supply of water. It was on this farm that the last
four Maier children were born. This farm is now the Philip Maier farm.

The road ahead was rough, but John was on enterprising and hardworking young
man. He and the older Maier children would gather buffalo bones that lay on
their land and sell them by the pound. He did some grain farming but his main
interest was livestock--cattle and sheep. At times he ran as many as 1500
sheep. Here on the North Dakota prairie John and Josephine raised their
children in the knowledge that in order to get ahead, one had to work hard and
make the most of what you had. They learned how to handle stock, and most of
them have spent a greater share of their lives either in ranching or dairy
farming. They all loved to work with animals. The Maier children received their
formal education in the rural schools in Oliver County. At that time the school
year was only three months--December, January, and February. The last day of
school was always a memorable event, for this was the day when the teacher
would give all of her pupils a little bit of candy. One last day, in
particular, Johnny and his brothers and sisters will never forget. This was the
last day of school and it had started to snow early in the morning and
Josephine was apprehensive about letting the children go to school that
morning, but they just insisted that they should go, and they went. By noon a
raging North Dakota Blizzard had begun. A nearby neighbor by the name of Julius
Kuether came to take all of the children over to his farm for safety. So that
none of the children would become lost in this howling snow storm, he made a
human chain by tying all eleven of the children to a rope and herding them to
his farm house. Here they lived on a diet of corn-bread, while the blizzard
raged on for three days and nights. During this time Mrs. Maier was almost
beside herself with worry. She was home alone with two younger children--her
husband had gone to town and he too couldn't return. One can almost imagine her
feeling of relief and thankfulness, when the storm subsided and the farmer
brought home her children and her husband came safely home from his trip to
town.

As the Maier children grew to adulthood they left the family farm--some
married and had families of their own and others decided to farm for
themselves. Thus it was that in 1913 that John Jr. came to Mercer County. His
father had bought some land here and Johnny came out to farm and ranch on the
land.

John Sr. passed away on July 28, 1914. After his death his wife Josephine
farmed the Maier farm with her son Philip. In 1918 Philip took over the farm
and Josephine moved to Beulah. Here she bought a home, and lived until the time
of her death, on February 25, 1932. The John Maiers were blessed with eight
children: Tillie, Mrs. Matthias, deceased; Anna, Mrs. J. F. Albers of Hannover;
William of New Salem; Philip of New Salem; Carl, deceased; John of Beulah;
Josephine, Mrs. Wm. Toepke, deceased; and George of New Salem.

John and Josephine Maier were active and faithful members of the Lutheran
Church, Missouri Synod. They were both laid to rest at the Lutheran Cemetery at
Hannover, N. Dak.

JOHN W. MAIER, JR.

        Standing: Josie, Betty and Polly
        John and Ella
        Grandchildren: Jerome, Sara Jo, Wayne and Johnnie

John W. Maier was born on his father's farm on May 10, 1892 in Oliver County,
near Hannover, North Dakota. Here he spent his boyhood days. Life on the farm
was an exciting and rewarding experience for Johnny for he loved horses and
enjoyed working and caring for animals. This inborn love for all kinds of
animals prompted him to decide to start a ranch of his own. His father owned
some land in Mercer County and on March 26, 1913 he and his brother Carl came
out to start their ranch. After the death of his brother, Johnny continued
running the place. He ran mostly sheep and cattle, for his ranch was ideally
located on the bottom land of the Knife River, so there never was a water
problem. Here he spent many hours breaking and training horses, out of which he
still gets a great deal of satisfaction. He did some grain farming, but
compared to today's mechanized farming methods their farming methods seem quite
primitive. They had to break the sod with a hand plow which was pulled with a
team of three to four horses. One didn't accomplish much, sometimes only 2-3
acres were broken in one day.

At the time that Johnny came to Mercer County, there were two buildings on the
present town-site of Beulah. This was called Troy. The railroad had not come
this far as yet, but the railroad grade had been finished, they didn't start
laying track until November 6, 1913. From then on Troy--later named Beulah--
started to grow. Johnny remembers the growth of Beulah very well, for his
ranchland started only about a mile south of town, on the other side of the
Knife River. In fact some of the Beulah Park was at one time part of his ranch.

On November 25, 1937, he married Ella Klundt, daughter of Wilhelm and Elizabeth
Klundt. This union was blessed with three daughters, Josephine, Betty and
Arlene (Polly). Johnny and Ella lived with their daughters on the Maier Ranch
until 1951. Here Johnny taught the girls how to "sit a horse" almost before
they knew how to walk. He and his family were great Rodeo enthusiasts. He
helped them train their own horses and took great pride in watching them
participate in the High School Rodeo at Beulah. Josie did go to the National
High School Rodeo in 1954 at Hallettsville, Texas.

When Johnny sold his ranch to Ernest Fandrich in 1951, he and his family moved
into Beulah. Johnny bought another ranch, which is located about ten miles
south of Beulah. He manages this ranch from town; here he runs a good herd of
Black Angus cattle and keeps a few head of saddle horses and a couple of
shetland ponies for his grandchildren. The three Maier daughters are all on
their own.

Josephine (Josie) graduated from the Beulah High School in 1955. She attended
the University of N. D. and D.S.T.C. from which she graduated with a B.A.
degree in 1959. She is married to Lincoln Reinhiller of Elgin and they have one
daughter, Sara Jo. They live in Garrison where Lincoln is employed by the
Garrison Public School as a coach and teacher.

Betty graduated from the Beulah High School in 1958. She is married to Noel
Helm of Beulah. They have three children: Jerome, Wayne and Johnnie. They make
their home in Beulah where Noel is employed by the Knife River Coal Mine as a
machinist.

Arlene (Polly) graduated from the Beulah High School in 1959. After high school
she attended Christie Beauty School of which she is a 1961 graduate. She now
owns and operates her own Beauty Shop in Killdeer, North Dakota. She is married
to George Tebor of Grassy Butte, North Dakota.

The John Maiers are members of the Concordia Lutheran Church of Beulah, of
which they are charter members.

In 1957 Ella started on a business venture of her own. She bought the Flowers
Drive In which was about a half mile out of the city limits of Beulah. This
proved to be quite a successful undertaking and before long she felt the need
for a larger building. In 1958 a new modern Drive-In was constructed. It is
known as the Round-Up Drive Inn. It operates from April 1st until December 1st.

        John Maier Jr.--A day's catch of coyotes

SAMUEL A. MURRAY PATIENCE BRIDGES MURRAY

Samuel Andrew Murray, son of Isaac Samuel and Patience Bridges Murray was born
Aug. 27, 1873 at Denver, Missouri. He married Myrtle Elwilda Wyatt, who was
born Mar. 5, 1874 in Rosendale, Missouri, daughter of Francis Marion and Marion
May Wyatt, on March 26, 1901 at Albany, Mo.

He came to N. D. in the fall of 1905 by train to New Salem. He rented a team
and buggy from Ben Bird to locate a homestead site. The first two nights he
camped at the Mike Boland farm. The third day he returned to New Salem and took
a train to Bismarck and filed a claim to Sec. 2--Twp. 143--R.--88, in Mercer
County, which is three miles south of the present site of Beulah.

He returned to Missouri and made preparation to move to N. D. In the early
spring of 1906, Sam, his father-in-law F. M. Wyatt, two brothers-in-law, Sam
and Rush Wyatt, his brother Charley, who had all homesteaded near by, arrived
in New Salem. They shipped their livestock, some farming equipment and
household goods to New Salem.

Sam Murray bought lumber at New Salem for a house and hauled it across country
to his homestead site. While building the house, he stayed with Charley Wright
and his mother, who lived 2 miles east of his homestead. This started a
friendship which lasted until the death of Charley Wright in 1938. Prior to his
death he made his home with the Murray family for 14 years.

In April 1906, Mrs. Murray, two daughters, a sister Cynthia Wyatt and
sister-in-law Mrs. Charley Murray arrived in Glen Ullin on an immigrant train.
Later Cynthia homesteaded on the same section as her brother and brother-in-law.

Mr. Murray, as did his neighbors, hauled wheat to Glen Ullin and brought back
groceries and supplies.

The Murrays endured many hardships and heartaches, the same as all pioneer
families, but they all seemed to be endowed with the strength and fortitude to
cope with them.

Sam Murray, having taken a Veterinarian apprenticeship in Missouri, was called
upon night and day to doctor livestock. He was frequently called on to care for
the sick, which included delivering babies, pulling teeth, setting broken
bones, etc. He also prepared the dead for burial.

He was very active in promoting community improvements such as schools and
organizations. He helped organize the Brush Creek School District (later called
Knife River Dist. #10). He served as clerk of this district; the Knife River
Telephone Co.; the Mercer Co. Fair Association; was a member of the Woodman
Lodge, Odd Fellows, Charter member of Royal Neighbors, Assessor of Mercer Co's.
3rd District, Acting Postmaster at Beulah, and in his late years a member of
St. Joseph's Catholic Church.

Mr. Murray helped haul lumber for the first school houses built south of
Beulah; also lumber and supplies from Hebron for the Farrington bridge
southeast of Beulah. After his crop was harvested and threshed, he would take
team and hay rack and work across the river with threshing crews in McLean Co.,
crossing the river by ferry. He hauled his wheat to Washburn, where he had his
years supply of flour milled.

Mrs. Murray was a member of the Beulah Congregational Church, Charter member of
the Congregational Ladies Aid; Charter member of Royal Neighbor's Lodge and
member of Clay Butte Homemakers (present Aplin Homemakers). She was an
excellent gardener. She was a good wife, mother and neighbor.

The children of Mr. and Mrs. Murray are: Opal, (Mrs. Ed. Unruh) of rural
Beulah; Elwilda, (Mrs. Bert Webber), Sidney, Montana. These two children were
born in Missouri. Joyce, (Mrs. Wilfred Herman) rural Golden Valley; Ralph,
rural Beulah, (married Ella Voegele) and Forrest "Mug", rural Beulah (married
Lida Greseth). These three children were born at the homestead.

The grandchildren are: James Williams, son; and Floyd Unruh, step-son of Opal
Unruh. La Vada, (Mrs. George Kuffer), Judith, George, Janet, Jane, Larry and
Robert, children of Elwilda Webber. Diana (Mrs. Richard Grosz) and Delton,
children of Joyce Herman. Patience, daughter of Ralph. Lonn, Myrna, Mark and
Mona, children of Forrest.

On March 26, 1951 Mr. and Mrs. Murray celebrated their Golden Wedding
Anniversary. On Aug. 20, 1951 Mrs. Murray passed away and six years later on
March 27, 1957 Mr. Murray passed away. They are buried in the Beulah Cemetery.

        C. R. Wright

THE CHARLIE MURRAY FAMILY HISTORY

On a bleak March day in 1906 a freight train pulled into New Salem, North
Dakota. Two young brothers from Missouri unloaded their basic farming equipment
and took their horses to Ben Bird's livery stable before finding a place to
spend the night. They had been to N. Dak. the fall before, located homesteads
and filed their claims in Bismarck for the sites they had chosen. New Salem was
the nearest railroad point to their homestead sites at that time One of the
young men, Charles Murray, awoke the next morning to find one of his four
horses had died during the night. Since four horses were essential to farming
and he hadn't the money to replace it, the future looked very bleak. Not
knowing anyone he went to the banker at New Salem, told him what happened and
asked him what to do. After a few questions--no security required, the banker
loaned him money to replace the horse. This basic faith in a man and his
honesty helped many men settle the land in North Dakota. Charles Murray
traveled by wagon and team to his chosen homestead site, five miles south of
present day Beulah. In the next six weeks he, his brother, Sam, and Sam Wyatt
built each a two-room shack to live in, a sod barn, and started farming. The
latter part of April he went to New Salem to meet his wife, Annie, and his two
children, Paul and Verdie. They were now North Dakotans!

Mr. Murray was Mercer County Commissioner from 1919-1922, he also shipped the
first load of livestock from the City of Beulah in 1913. There were no stock
yards, so the livestock was jumped from the horse drawn wagons into the stock
car. He continued to buy livestock for the next thirty-five years.

During the ensuing years they had many hardships--crop failures, hail, loss of
vital livestock, hauling grain to Glen Ullin until the railroad reached
Beulah--but they also had neighborhood parties, quilting bees, barn raisings
and helped build school and churches, organize school districts, and township
governments. Mrs. Murray was much in demand for nursing and midwife duties. She
enjoyed competing in "Spelling Bees" at local schools in those early days, too.

Mary Ann (Annie) Murray was born in Worth County Missouri, on November 25, 1877
to Ellen and Jasper Miller.

Mrs. Murray was an exceptional woman; her unselfish efforts in visiting the
sick and comforting the discouraged were an inspiration to all who knew her as
a friend and neighbor. She was an active and faithful member of the Baptist
Church. Annie and Charlie Murray were married in Albany, Missouri on March 11,
1897. This union lasted for sixty-two years and was broken by the death of Mrs.
Murray on March 17, 1959.

Born in Denver, Missouri, on June 14, 1876, Mr. Murray still resides in Beulah.
Although he is approaching his 88th birthday he is still active and alert. He
spends his winters visiting his children in the various parts of the country;
many times traveling by jet--a far cry from that first trip to North Dakota via
freight train.

Charlie and Annie Murray were blessed with seven children, Verdie and Earl have
passed away; but five children remain of this pioneer family.

Paul Murray, born March 1, 1898, attended Valley City Teachers College, farmed
in N. D. a few years and then moved to Montana. In recent years he has been
doing carpenter work. He is now partially retired and makes Livingston, Montana
his home. He married the former Polly Mitchell and they have two sons, Garvin
and Wayne. The Paul Murray's have 5 grandchildren and 1 great-grandchild.

Glenn Murray, born September 22, 1908, graduated from the Beulah High School in
1927. He attended Valley City Teachers College and taught rural school one year
before starting to farm. He worked for the Beulah Coal Mine 8 years, and has
farmed in the Beulah Vicinity 35 years. For the last 20 years he has also been
a cattle buyer. He is married to the former Bea Erickson and they have three
children Mrs. Norman, Phyllis, O'Neil, Gary, and Mrs. Arman, Peggy, Wolff. The
Glenn Murrays have 14 grandchildren. Both Glenn and Bea have participated
countless times in the various racing events of the Beulah Cowboys Reunion.

Deane Murray, born January 29, 1916, graduated from Beulah High School in 1933.
She attended the NDSU in Fargo for two years. She is married to Dr. John F.
Sherman who is an Associate Director with the National Institutes of Health in
Washington, D. C. They live in Kensington, Maryland and have two daughters,
Betsy and Mary Ann.

Ferne Murray, born June 11, 1920, graduated from Beulah High School in 1936.
She attended NDAC for one year. She married Rueben Nordin in 1941. Rueben
passed away in 1959. Ferne and the Nordin children lived on their ranch at
Sentinal Butte until 1961. She is now married to John C. Combs, an Engineer
Tech. with the Soil Conservation Service. They live in Killdeer and have five
children; James, Doreen, Candace, Kenton Nordin, and Carolyn Combs.

Herman Murray, born February 3, 1924, graduated from Beulah in 1942. He spent
1943-45 in the NROTC of the Armed Services. In 1950 he graduated from Colorado
A & M, now lives in Laurel, Montana, and is employed by the Midland Feed Co. of
Billings. He is married to the former Cornelia Lennick and has four children;
Warrine, Karen, Brad, and Don.

MONROE'S BAR

        Monroe amd Lorraine Miller

Monroe Miller was born Aug 26, 1921 on a farm 5 miles north of Beulah, He
stayed on the farm until 1941 when he went out west and worked on construction.

On Aug 13, 1942 he married the former Lorraine Forrest of Pocatello, Idaho.

He served in the Marine Corps during World War II. Then came back to Beulah and
has worked for the Knife River Coal Mine since 1946.

They have one son Tommy age 20 who has served three years with the Armed
Forces, and two daughters Judy age 17 and Barbara age 11.

He purchased the bar on Jan. 1, 1959.

MR. AND MRS. DAN MILLER

        Dan and Barbara Miller

Dan Miller was born on December 21, 1893 at Krem, South Russia. His parents
Andrew and Magdalena Miller immigrated to the United States in 1894. They filed
the homestead claim on a parcel of land 14 miles north of Beulah (in what is
now known as the Kronthal area). It was on this homestead that the Andrew
Miller children grew to adulthood. When Dan was but eleven years old, he
suffered a great loss with the death of his father. He was digging the family
well (in those days they had to be dug by hand) when he was overcome by gas
fumes and by the time he was found he was dead. Dan's mother continued farming,
even though her children were quite young. They are: George (deceased), Dan,
Emanuel (deceased), Sam, Margaret, Mrs. Geo. Schmidt, Kathrina (drowned at the
age of 16), Louisa, Mrs. Phil Schmidt, and Elizabeth, Mrs. Mike Durengo.

Barbara Dschaak was also born in South Russia. She was born on February 27,
1895 and came to the United States with her parents, Jacob and Louisa Dschaak.
They settled fourteen miles north of Beulah and took up a homestead. There were
twelve children in the Dschaak family: August, Christ (deceased) Emil
(deceased), Henry (deceased), William, Jacob, Fred, Walter, Barbara, Jacobina,
Mrs. Wm. Reedy (deceased), Martha, Mrs. B. LaViolet, and Elvina, Mrs. Alvin
Keller.

Barbara and Dan were married on November 15, 1914 at the Kronthal Lutheran
Church with Rev. Paul Buck officiating. After their marriage they settled on
Dan's farm north of Beulah. It was here that all of the Miller children were
born, raised and educated in the near-by farm schools. The marriage of Dan and
Barbara Miller was blessed with four sons; Monroe of Beulah, Norman of Savage,
Montana, Melvin of Eureka, Mont., and Walter who passed away at the age of 7
months; and two daughters: Clara McWilliams of Beulah and Alta, Mrs. Paul
Harmon of Minneapolis, Minnesota.

The Millers now make their home in Beulah. They have lived here since 1944 when
they moved off of the farm. They are members of the Zion Lutheran Church of
Beulah.

THE KARL HILZ SR. FAMILY HISTORY

        Standing--Emanuel, Gottlieb, August, Carl Jr., Bill.
        Sitting--Martha, Karl and Elizabeth, Bertha.

Karl Hilz Sr. was born in South Russia on October 22, 1881. His wife Elizabeth
Heinle was also born in Russia. Her birthdate was October 2, 1878.

The Hilz's were married in 1901. Four of their children were born while they
were still living in Russia; August, William, Carl, and Katherina.

On July 22, 1919 Karl and his family came to North Dakota. Shortly after their
arrival in this new land they were saddened by the loss of their youngest
child, Katherina. In 1911 they took up a homestead three miles east of Beulah.
It was on this homestead that the rest of the Hilz children were born. The
Hilz's farmed until 1930 when Karl and his family moved to Beulah. He began
working for the Knife River Coal Mine. He worked in the coal mine until 1944
when he retired.

The Karl Hilz Sr.'s lived in their home until the death of Mrs. Hilz on June
13, 1959. She had been ill for about seven years and the last years was
confined to her bed.

Karl Sr. is a member of the Zion Lutheran Church of Beulah. At the present time
he is making his home at the Joachim Memorial Home at Beulah.

Karl and Elizabeth Hilz had eleven children, four of whom are deceased.

August, of Beulah, married the former Elsie Renke. They have one son Lloyd.

William (Bill) of Beulah, married the former Christine Schnaidt. The Bill
Hilz's have two daughters, Ilene and Alta.

Carl Jr. of Beulah, married the former Ida Schutz. They have one son, Helmuth.

Martha, Mrs. Emil Wetzel, lives in Bismarck.

Emanuel, of Beulah, married the former Lorraine Benz. They have two daughters,
Bernita and DeLilah.

Gottlieb, of Beulah, married the former Lydia Benz. Their two children are,
Gordon and Marlinda.

Bertha, Mrs. Walter Beck of Underwood had two children, Myron and Duane.

ALEXANDER MOHL

        Standing--Left to right: Lorraine, Edna, Orland, Erhardt,
        Raymond, Howard, Almond, Florence and Alice.
        Sitting--Left to right: Arnold, Ervin, Mr. and Mrs. Mohl,
        Navoline, Selma and Inez.

Alexander Mohl, was born in South Russia on January 1, 1890, a son of Anton and
Fredericka, nee Bentz. He came to the United States with his parents and family
in 1906. His father took up a homestead near Mannhaven, north of Hazen. Here
Alex grew to manhood. There were six children in the Mohl family: Jacob
(deceased), Alex, Peter (deceased), Helena, Mrs. Geo. Zeiszler (deceased)
Eliza, Mrs. Jake Zeiszler (deceased) and Mrs. Sophia Weidner.

Alex and Eliza Mohl were married on March 30, 1913. Eliza was born on November
27, 1894, the daughter of George and Mary, nee Krause, Buchfinck. The
Buchfinck's had homesteaded in the Stanton or Krem Area, and it was here that
Eliza was raised and grew to young womanhood. There were 11 children in the
Buchfinck family, two half-brothers and one half-sister are still living.

The Alex Mohl's farmed in the Mannhaven area until 1928, when they moved to
Halliday. In 1930 they rented a farm about 3 miles north of Beulah. It was here
that they witnessed their hardest and most trying times. This period of time;
that went down in the annals of history as "the dirty thirties," was a reality
to the Mohls. The almost unbearable times of drought, dust storms,
grasshoppers, and low marked prices will never be forgotten. They survived this
trying period of time, and gradually things got better. The Mohls farmed until
1945 when they retired and moved into Beulah. Mrs. Mohl passed away on May 6,
1962. Alex still makes his home in Beulah where he is a member of the Zion
Lutheran Church.

There are fourteen children in the Mohl family. Ervin and Ray of Beulah;
Erhardt of Zap; Arnold and Howard of Glendive, Montana; Orland and Almond of
Calif.; Mrs. Fred (Selma) Ost; and Mrs. Carl (Florence) Schnaidt, of Beulah;
Alice of Grafton; Edna Schulz of Lodi, Calif.; Mrs. Norman (Loraine) Lundgren
of Irvington, Calif.; Mrs. Esley (Navoline) Ford of Stockton, Calif.; Mrs.
Clarence (Inez) Miller of San Diego, Calif. There are 36 grandchildren and 5
great-grandchildren.

        THE ERVIN MOHL FAMILY

        Linda, Myron, Hilmer, Dwight and Bruce Wade, Mr. and Mrs. Mohl,
        Curtis. Not pictured: Rodney and Gordon.

Ervin Mohl is the son of Alexander and Eliza Mohl. He was born in the Mannhaven
area and attended the rural school there.

Hilda Boeckel Mohl is the daughter of Peter and Louise Boeckel. She was born on
a farm in the Beulah vicinity and has lived in this area all of her life.

Ervin and Hilda were married on Nov. 20, 1938, by Rev. P. F. Schilling at
Beulah. They rented a farm. They farmed in the summer and Ervin worked in the
Dakota Colleries Mine in the winter. They did this for three winters.

In 1940, when there were many vacant farms available, (due to drought and crop
failure) they bought the farm where the family now resides. The land was
originally homesteaded by Carl Johnson who built the house in 1913. The country
store and post office site of Bowdish is on one part of the farm.

The Mohl farm consists of six quarters of land, which used in a combination
grain and dairy farm.

Ervin served on the Spring Creek School Board for a number of years and is now
a board member. The Mohl's are also members of the Zion Lutheran Church of
Beulah.

There are nine children in the family. Rodney, who lives in Williston, and
works in the Core Laboratories; Myron is a truck driver at Hazen; Gordon, lives
in Casper, Wyoming and works for Core Labs; Hilmer works with his father on the
farm; Dwight, a senior at the Beulah High School; Linda their only daughter is
a sophomore at the Beulah High School; Bruce, Curtis, and Wade attend grade
school at Beulah.

The Mohl boys have always been interested in athletics; Myron, Gordon, Hilmer,
and Dwight have all been members of the Beulah High School basketball,
baseball, and football teams. Rodney served two years in the U.S. Army,
spending 18 months in Germany.

The Ervin Mohl's celebrated their Silver Anniversary on November 29, 1963. This
was an especially memorable occasion as all of their children were present.

EDWARD MORMAN

Edward S. Morman was born on a farm June 7, 1913 in the Haymarsh Community, 11
miles northeast of Hebron. The son of Charles and Amelia Wehri Morman, the
sixth child of a family of twelve children.

Haymarsh got its name in the early days, as it was a low marshy region covered
by tall dense growth of grass. Here everyone of the region made hay as they
still do today.

Edward received his elementary education at St. Clemens parochial and public
school at St. Clemens parish in Haymarsh community.

After completing his elementary education Ed. helped his father with farming
and raising sheep. In the summers Ed helped a group of men, managed by Henry
Holler shear sheep in and around the community. When Ed was 18 he hired out as
a ranch hand on the Urban ranch, near Hebron. This ranch was managed by his
brother Henry Morman.

On August 17, 1935 Edward married Helen Ehlers a teacher who was then teaching
in the Hebron community. In the fall of 1935 Ed and Helen moved to Glen Ullin,
where Ed helped his brother John with Farm work and Coal mme work. Helen taught
a rural school west of Glen Ullin.

His wife Helen attended Minot State Teacher's College, taking a course in
primary education.

In the fall of 1936 Ed and his wife moved to Beulah where Ed was employed by M.
P. Moore Dray and Transfer Co. Here Ed worked for two years.

In the fall of 1938 Ed gained employment at North Dakota Power and light plant.
(Now MDU) Here he began as an Ash man. He worked at many jobs in the plant
working himself up to head mechanic which job Ed took over in 1945. This job he
still holds today.

Ed has always been active in the civic life of the Community. He has served 13
years on the Beulah Park Board, he is now Chairman. He is a member of the
Chamber of Commerce and is now one of the directors. He is a Troop Chairman for
Troop 19 of Beulah Boy Scouts and represents Beulah at the Missouri Valley
Council Meetings. Ed has been a member of the Beulah Fire Department for 14
years.

Ed is a member of St. Joseph Catholic Church o$ Beulah. He belongs to the
Knights of Columbus and is their treasurer.

Helen has also taken an active part in the school, church and civic activities
of the town. She is a member of the Zion Lutheran Church and an active member
of the Women of the Church Society. She has been a member of the Woman's Club
for twenty-six years and a member of the Beulah Mother's Club for twenty-six
years.

        Donald          David

There are four children in the family, 3 sons and one daughter. Donald
graduated from B.H.S. Attended Brainerd Jr. College, Brainerd, Minn., UND,
Grand Forks, No. Dak., and Wisconsin University, Madison, Wisc. He has degrees
in Business Administration and Law. He married Dorothy Outka of Hettinger, No.
Dak. She is a graduate of UND with a degree in nursing. Donald is a
commissioned Officer in the Army Air Force with the rank of 1st Lt. He and his
wife are now at Hanou, Germany. David is a graduate of B.H.S. He joined the
U.S. Navy after High School. In the navy he was sent to school for fifteen
months, then assigned to duty aboard the Saratoga, Aircraft carrier of the
sixth fleet. Here he did duty in the Mediterranean Area for three years. David
advanced to the rank of G.M.T. 2. He is now a sophomore at B.J.C., Bismarck,
No. Dak. Daphne is a graduate of B.H.S. of Beulah. She graduated from the two
year college course at B.J.C. in Bismarck. She is now at Fargo taking a part
time course at N.D.S.U. and works as a secretary. Dale the youngest is still at
home. He is in the eighth grade.

        Daphne          Dale

GOTTFRIED OST AND THE CHRIST AND ADAM OST FAMILIES

Gottfried Ost was born on Sept. 6, 1861 in Bishterick, So. Russia to Christian
and Karolina, nee Wolt, Ost. He married Wilhelmina Boeshans in 1888. In 1903
the Gottfried and John Ost families immigrated to America. They came as far as
New Salem, where they were met by Johannas and Joe Boeckel. They went with
wagon and horses to the Boeckel's homestead. The next day the Osts were taken
to the Adam Baish farm and to their father Christ Ost's (he had come over to
America in 1895) home north of Krem. Gottfried and his brother John rented a
house and land from Johannas Weigum. Each of them bought two horses and they
plowed the land. Gottfried seeded the wheat by hand. They made a drag from
trees which they had cut, and with this they dragged (harrowed) the land. In
June they homesteaded twelve miles northwest of Beulah. Here Gottfried built a
house of stone (which they gathered from the fields) and mud. This house was
well built and the Ost family lived in it for many years. Here the Ost children
grew to adulthood and attended the rural schools. Their nearest trading place
was a thriving country community known as Kasmer. After the R.R. came through
and the town of Beulah was formed they did most of their trading in Beulah.
Gottfried moved to town in 1930, he lived here until his death in 1944. Mrs.
Ost died in 1926.

The Gottfried Ost's had 10 children: Joseph, Katie (deceased) Carrie,
(deceased) Christian, Pauline (deceased) Philip, (deceased), Gust, (deceased)
were born in Russia; Adam, William (deceased) and Edward (deceased) were born
here on the homestead.

        Esther, Gilbert, Ruby
        Christ and Bertha Ost

Christian was born on April 2, 1895. He married Bertha Wiedrich on December 18,
1921 at the Kronthal Lutheran Church, Pastor Grill officiated. Bertha was born
on January 25, 1900, the daughter of John and Franciska, nee Briedenbiecher,
Wiedrich of Hazen. There were 10 children in the Wiedrich family. Seven are
still living; they are: Magadelna Sailer; Elizabeth Hoffman; Margaret Pfenning;
Bertha Ost; Conrad Wiedrich; Rudolph Wiedrich and Otto Wiedrich.

After their marriage Christ and Bertha moved to Christ's farm 12 miles
northwest of Beulah. On this farm their three children were born, raised and
educated in the nearby rural schools. Here they lived until 1949, when they
retired and moved into Beulah. They still live in Beulah where they are members
of the Zion Lutheran Church.

The Ost children are all married and live on farms.

Ruby, the oldest is married to Edward Boeshans, son of Mrs. Louisa Boeshans of
Beulah. They farm north of Golden Valley. Their children are: Donald, Darlene
(deceased), Sandra, Dennis, Mark, Stanley, and Rita. Esther married Herbert
Oberlander who is the son of the late Mr. and Mrs. Fred Oberlander of Hazen.
They live on a farm north of Stanton. They have two children: Sharon and Jerry.
Gilbert is living on the Christ Ost home farm. He is married to Violet Lorenz,
daughter of the late Mr. and Mrs. John Lorenz, of Zap. Their children are
Vicky, Cary Lee, Joette, and Linda Carol.

        Emma and Adam Ost

Adam Ost was born on the Gottfried Ost family homestead on March 1, 1906. Here
he grew to manhood. He married Emma Mittelsteadt on May 4, 1930, at the
Wittenburg Lutheran Church, Pastor Ludwig Reimler officiated.

Emma Mittelsteadt was born in Russia on April 7, 1908, the daughter of Fred and
Juliana, nee Renn, Mittelsteadt. She came to America with her parents and
family in 1905. They took up a homestead five miles north of Zap, in 1906. All
of the Mittelsteadt children, except Ida, Mrs. Carl Morast were born in Russia.
They are Sophia, Mrs. William Wiedrich, Driscoll, N. Dak.; Mrs. Rose Boeckel,
San Diego, Calif.; Emma, Mrs. Adam Ost; Adolph and Walter, both of Zap; Two
children are deceased. Their father died on December 25, 1963 and their mother
lives in the Joachim Memorial Home at Beulah.

Adam and Emma Ost lived on the farm north of Beulah from 1930-1947. The first
ten years that the Osts lived on their farm were about the worst years for any
young couple in this area to begin farming. Many other young couples tried to
"start out" in the thirties, but became discouraged and gave up, but not so
with the Ost's. They stayed with it and in fact they are still farming. They
have lived in Beulah since 1947, where Adam does all kinds of carpenter work
during the slack farming season. They are members of the Concordia Lutheran
Church.

The Adam Osts have two sons. Ronald Adam is married to Roberta Jean Eweing.
They have two children: Sara Lynette and David Ronald. They live in Casper,
Wyoming. Leonard Walter, their second son is now employed at Laramie, Wyoming.

        Ronald          Leonard

GEORGE AND KAROLINA KESSLER

George Kessler was born in Glueckstahl, South Russia on September 6, 1867 to
Johann George and Christina Kessler. Karolina Lippert Kessler was born on
January 22, 1868 in Neudorf, South Russia. The Kesslers were married in the
Gleuckstahl Lutheran Church on November 12, 1891, by Pastor Martin Frederick
Schrenk.

In 1905, the Kesslers and their five children, George Jr., Christine, Rosa,
Christian, and Jacob, immigrated to the United States. That first year they
lived at Akaska, South Dakota, but work was scarce and the pay was poor (George
Sr. many times picked corn for 75¢ a day), so they decided to move.

In 1906 the Kesslers made the trip from Akaska to Mercer County by horse and
wagon. On the way they did stop off at Elgin for awhile, and then proceeded on
to Mercer County. There were no bridges across the Knife River, but there was a
good river crossing about a mile east of what is now Beulah. When the Kessler's
arrived they saw that the Knife had flooded its banks, so they had to drive
their horses and wagon down to about 6 miles west of Stanton where there was a
bridge. Their Uncle Lorance Kessler met them here and showed them the way to
his homestead. They lived with their Uncle Lorance until they took up their
homestead, four miles north of Beulah, and until they had their one-room sod
house built. The Kesslers and their five children moved to their homestead and
life, in the first home they owned here in America, began. During this time
there was not a great variety of food on their  table. Most of the time all
there was to eat was dry corn cooked in water. Somehow they managed, none of
them starved and they all grew up to be strong and healthy.

In 1907 they built a two-room mud house; here the last of the Kessler children
were born; Fred, Otto and Lydia. On May 21, 1913, George and Karolina Kessler
and their 7 children became citizens of the United States. For many years the
Kesslers operated a coal mine on the family homestead.

In 1938 Mr. and Mrs. Kessler moved their house into Beulah, here they lived for
thirteen years, until the death of Mr. Kessler on August 23, 1945. In later
years Mrs. Kessler made her home at the Joachim Memorial Home, she passed away
on March 21, 1952. They were members of the Zion Lutheran Church at Beulah.
They were blessed with eight children; George Jr. (deceased) married Emma
Huether; Christine, Mrs. Fred Saeman; Rosa, died when she was eleven years old;
Christ married Alvina Renke; Jake married Ida Sailer; Fred is married to
Frances Hipfner; Otto married Phyllis Hipfner; and Lydia, Mrs. Pius Usselman.

MR. AND MRS. JOHN KRAUSZ

John Krausz was born on October 16, 1893 at Alt Fridenstahl, South Russia to
John and Susan, nee Moser, Krausz. There were 3 children in the family. John's
father died when he was four years old. After a time his mother remarried, a
widower, Jacob Schlecht, who had six children. In 1910 John and a step-sister
Luisa, (Mrs. Fred Reub Jr.) decided to go to America. They were both only
sixteen when they said goodby to parents and family and set out for this
strange new land. John wasn't old enough to take up a homestead, so he worked
for various people in the Mercer County area; Emil Klundt, J. Schlichenmeyer,
August Ziemann, Jake Heihn's Livery Stable and Karl Heihn. He had farmed for a
year when he married Katharina Reub.

Katharina was born in Hoffnungstahl, South Russia on June 10, 1895 to Frederick
and Charlotta, nee Schopp, Reub. In 1905 Katharina came to the United States
with her parents. The first winter they lived at Velva. In August of 1906
Katharina and her family homesteaded in Mercer County.

John and Katharina were married on December 1, 1914 at the Zion Lutheran Church
of Beulah by Pastor J. Mau. They were the first couple married in the new
wooden church. The Krausz's farmed on rented land for 11 years. In 1925 they
bought the "old Karlin Place", which they own to the present day. This union
was blessed with four children:

Emilie, Mrs. Charles Ferguson, lives in Richmond, California and has two boys,
Dehmar and Robert (Bobby).

Leonard married Hilda Winkler, they have 6 children: Shirley, (Mrs. Lawrence
Christman), Richard, Dianne, Judy, Donna Mae, and Vonnie. Leonard lives on the
Krausz farm. He served in the U.S. Army during World War II in the European
theatre.

Erma married Gordon Neuberger, who is a Lutheran Pastor at New Leipzig, North
Dakota. They have two boys Wayne and James.

Oscar married Rose Neuberger and they have three youngsters; Lorraine, Donald
and Kerry Dean. They make their home at Eugene, Oregon.

On January 1950 the Krausz's retired from active farming and they moved to
Beulah where they still reside. Mr. Krausz has served on the town board for 5
years. The Krausz's are looking forward to celebrating, God-willing, their
Golden Wedding Anniversary this year (1964).

THE POTRATZ FAMILY

Our dad, the Rev. Gotthard Potratz was a Lutheran Pastor of the Missouri Synod.
He served congregations at Hillsboro and Elm River in the Red River Valley when
Dakota was a territory. He taught German Confirmation School during the winter
months. Besides this, he was a travelling pastor in the new settlements. He
would hold services in the homes until such a time as the people of the new
community were able to call their own pastor. He travelled about by train,
team, horseback and on foot as far west as Coleharbor, N. D. as well as into
neighboring Minnesota. We saw very little of him because of his travels for the
church.

When he died in 1902, he left mother with seven children. I, Agnes, was the
oldest--12 1/2 years. Then there were John 11, Gerhard 9, Gunther 8, Berthold
4, Werner 2 and Erna 6 weeks.

My mother moved with us to Niagara, a small settlement in Nelson County. Here
we lived for two years. Then Mother decided to go to western N. D. and try
homesteading.

She had cared for Herman, the infant son of Wm. Rettke since the mother had
died in childbirth. Mr. Rettke went west to look for homesteads for my mother
and for himself. It was in 1903 when my mother went to Bismarck to file on the
claim chosen for her, and in October of that year, we moved west. We came by
train. All our possessions: cow, calf, dog, chickens, 12 sacks of potatoes and
our household goods were shipped to Glen Ullin. There Mr. Rettke met us with a
lumber wagon to take us to our homestead, 22 miles north of town. He had
started building a house on our claim and had built a combination barn and
"shack" on his claim for himself and his team of horses. He had also plowed
fire breaks around both buildings for these were a must in those prairie fire"
days.

Our friends at home said we would be eaten by mountain lions, scalped by
Indians or burned out by ranchers, if we homesteaded in western Dakota.

Mother had left Gerhard and Gunther with relatives in Niagara to finish
Comfirmation school, for she knew there were no churches or schools out here.

On our trip from Glen Ullin to the claim, the team tired; in order to lighten
their load the potatoes were cached under a haystack along the way, to be
hauled home later. However, a heavy frost got to them before we did, so there
were no potatoes that first winter. We lived on homemade bread, beef, tea and
barley coffee (mother roasted barley and ground it). We had nothing else--but
we were healthy.

Our closest neighbors were Tom Keogh, Chas. Herman and Bernie Evans. There were
only a few prairie trails in the long, waving prairie grass. There was grass as
far as you could see--with here and there a herd of grazing horses or cattle.
The Glen Ullin trail ran 2 miles east of us. Once when we hadn't been to Glen
Ullin for 3 months because of the heavy snow, the mail filled a 100# flour
sack. It took us a week to read it all.

I married Oscar Johnson who had a homestead 3 miles west of us. We had eight
children. Oscar passed away in 1959.

At present the children live: Irene with me; also Walter and Otto, when their
work permits; Erma--Mrs. Herb Fandrich in Los Angeles; Wilfred, married to
Ellyn Johnson, lives in Seattle, Wash.; Arlene--Mrs. Geo. Caswell; Ruth--Mrs.
Gordon Montgomery and Joanne--Mrs. Keith Fretty, all in Los Angeles.

I have seventeen grandchildren.

This page sponsored by H. R. Engbrecht.

MAX MARTELL

The Martells came to Beulah from Stanton in 1915. Max was needed here for he
was a skilled mason. Among the monuments to his skill are the Roxy Theater,
Greystone Hotel, Beulah Drug Store and Doctor's office.

Max Martell was a talented musician and served as Beulah's early band director.
He was an enthusiastic baseball fan. His truck equipped with benches and
covered with a tarp transported the local baseball team on its travels
throughout the season.

Mrs. Martell was the former Agnes McLaughlin of New Salem. Their children are:
Carol and Irene (both Beulah High School graduates) Lucille, Dorothy, Leo and
Max Jr. Martells moved to Albuquerque, N. Mexico in 1938 where he continued as a
mason until his retirement. The Martells now enjoy retirement in Helena, Mont.
where his special pleasure is trout fishing.

H. J. HALBEISEN

The yodelling dentist, who loved animals, gardening and music was our dentist
for the greatest number of years--1927-1938. H. James, better known as Jimmie,
was easy to know and has never been forgotten, nor forgotten us.

He was married to Esther Bauer, a school teacher, in Chicago in June of 1929.

MR. AND MRS. ADAM HERRMANN

Adam Herrmann was born at Krem, S. Russia on March 7, 1873, to Leonard and
Elizabeth, nee Schnaible, Herrmann. At the age of sixteen Adam came to the
United States with his aunt and uncle, Christian and Magdelena Herrmann.

Christina Walz was born in Freeman, South Dakota on April 12, 1875, to Johannes
and Dorthea, nee Huber, Walz.

Christina and Adam Herrmann were married on March 7, 1893. They went to Butte,
Nebraska where they took up a homestead. They lived here until 1902 when they
decided to move to Mercer County. In May of 1902 they began shipping their
cattle and other belongings to New Salem. From here it took eight days to haul
it by wagon and teams to Mannhaven. They drove the cattle with a pony. They
stayed with the John Ellweins Sr. and the George Tschaekofske families for
about a week. Adam had used his homestead right, so he bought a section of land
seven miles north of Zap. This is the present Ernest Herrmann farm. They built
their sod house and shed and put out their first crop, which was eleven acres
of corn. At this time there was no cultivated farm land north of Zap, only
rolling prairies. They had to travel to Hebron to sell their grain and buy
supplies. Later on they were able to take it to Ree and ship it by boat. In
1913 the town of Zap was established and the Herrmanns did not have to go so
far for supplies.

The Herrmanns told of many hardships and the scarcity of machinery, such as;
threshing machines were so rare that it was almost impossible to get around to
thresh all the crops before the snow came and many times they had to wait until
spring to finish the crops. The Herrmanns farmed until 1929, when they moved
into Zap. Their youngest son, Ernest, took over the family farm at this time.

Adam always took an active part in church and county affairs. The Herrmanns
were charter members of the St. John Lutheran Church north of Zap and later on
when they were living in Zap, they helped organize St. Luke's Lutheran Church
at Zap. (1938)

The Herrmann's had twelve children, three of whom died in infancy: the children
are: Theodore, Ernest, Mrs. John (Emma) Wiedrich, Mrs. Theophil (Elizabeth)
Reiner all of Zap. Mrs. Ernest (Dorothy) Fedderson, Mrs. Alex (Hertha) Weigum,
Mrs. Ernest (Ottilia) Hafner, and Mrs. Sam (Rufina) Miller all of Beulah, and
Mrs. Paul (Edna) Ding, Stockton, California, Christina Herrmann passed away on
October 14, 1938. Adam married Mrs. Eva Bohrer of Mannhaven in August of 1939.
Adam passed away on April 17, 1954. His second wife is still living and makes
her home at the Beulah Community Nursing Home.

SAM MILLER, SR.

Sam Miller Sr. of Golden Valley, was born on April 27, 1884, a son of Stephen
and Cathrine, nee Leithold, Miller in Crimea, Russia. Their main shopping
center was Simforobel. He received his education there and took up a four
year-apprenticeship as a blacksmith, working under Sam Stern.

He was married in 1907 to Maria Boeckel, daughter of George and Katherina, nee
Weigum, Boeckel. He operated a blacksmith shop in Russia for a few years.
During this time many of their friends and family had moved to the United
States and the Millers decided to go too. They arrived here in May of 1910 and
took up a homestead in Mercer County, 14 miles north of Golden Valley. In order
to help with the family finances, Sam went to work for other people.

From 1911 to 1912 he worked for Kasimer Mastel at Kasmer, as a blacksmith. When
he had enough money to buy his own tools he set up a shop on his homestead.
Here he operated the shop until the death of his wife (as the result of an
accident). At the time of her death there were four children: Sam Jr. now
living in Beulah; Ella, Mrs. Bert Onstott of Jardine, Montana; Jean, Mrs. Mike
Borssard of San Leandro, California; and Emil who passed away January 14, 1934,
when he was only seventeen.

In 1918 he rented a blacksmith shop at Dodge which he ran until 1922, when he
moved to Isabel, S. Dak. After operating a shop at Isabel for four years he
decided to move back to Mercer County and settled at Zap, N. Dak. Here he
married Louise Harsh, daughter of Gottlieb Harsh. To this union were born 4
children: Leo, in Jamestown, N. Dak., Hugo, of Albuquerque, New Mexico; Gilbert
of Golden Valley, and Melvin in the Armed Services stationed at Dickinson,
North Dakota.

In the winter months he worked at the Lucky Strike Coal Mine as a  Blacksmith.
In 1932 he built his own shop at Zap, which he sold in 1946, when he went to
work at the Garrison Dam (1946-1947). That same year he bought a shop at Golden
Valley, which he operated until it burned to the ground in 1949. At the loss of
this last shop, Sam Sr. retired and is now making his home in Golden Valley,
where the Millers are members of the St. James Lutheran Church.

He is 80 years old and 52 of these years were spent in the Blacksmithing
business, the trade he learned as a youth in Russia. He has always been proud
of his trade, which he in turn taught to his oldest son, Sam Jr.

SAM MILLER, JR.

        Kimberly, Myron, Carol, Rufina and Sam Miller
        David and Charles

Sam Miller Jr. was born October 27, 1908, to Sam and Maria, nee Boeckel, Miller
Sr. He came to Mercer County with his parents and here received his education
and grew into manhood. He attended mechanic and welding school at Fargo, N.
Dak. from 1927 to 1928. After he finished school he worked part time on the
farm and in the winter months at the Lucky Strike Coal Mine at Zap. In 1932 he
started working in his father's blacksmith shop, with his father teaching him
what he had learned through his many years of working as a blacksmith.

On October 21, 1933 Sam was united in marriage to Rufina Herrmann, daughter of
Mr. and Mrs. Adam Herrmann. They were married at the St. John's Lutheran Church
by Pastor T. H. Hilgendorf. They made their home at Zap until March of 1939
when they moved to Beulah.

When the Millers moved to Beulah, Sam went into business for himself. He bought
the "old Smith Shop" and started operating a Blacksmith and Welding Shop under
the name of The Beulah Blacksmith Shop. He has been in this business for the
last twenty-five years.

In 1948 he built the present structure on East Main Street. His first hired man
was a brother-in-law, Paul Ding, now of Stockton, Calif., who worked for him
for four years. For the last 13 years Joe Klein, formerly of Linton, N. Dak.,
has been assisting with the blacksmith and welding work.

In 1963 the. Millers celebrated their 30th Wedding Anniversary. The Millers
have three sons: Myron, who is married to Carol Hafner, daughter of the late
Leo and Katie Hafner. The Myron Miller's have one daughter Kimberly Ann (born
1961), and they live at Bismarck where Myron is a Student Nurse at the Bismarck
Hospital. David is a Senior at the Beulah High School. Charles is now a
freshman at the Beulah High School, and is active in Scout Work.

The Millers are members of Concordia Lutheran Church at Beulah. Sam has been a
member of the Beulah Fire Department for the past 18 years. He is serving his
second term as a director of the Chamber of Commerce and served on the City
Council for two terms from 1954-1962. He served for three terms on the board of
directors of the Joachim Memorial Home and is presently on the Board of
Directors of the Beulah Community Nursing Home.

MARGARET AND MARTIN SCHUTZ, SR.

Martin and Margaret, nee Yanke, Schutz Sr. were born in Bessarabia, South
Russia. In 1914 they immigrated to America with five of their children, first
settling in Delmont, South Dakota. Martin Schutz Jr. had come to America eight
years before his parents. He had married Emila Uttecht and had one child, and
had been making his homein Delmont. The Martin Schutz Srs. made their home with
the son until they moved to North Dakota in the fall of the same year. Here
they rented a farm and they all farmed together for one year. In 1915 Martin
Schutz Srs. moved to Beulah with the four children at were still at home:
Louise, Christine, Fred and Catherine. Here they built a new home and became
members of the Zion Lutheran Church. Mr. Schutz worked for the Power Elevator
and Lumber Co., and later on the Farmers Elevator and various other jobs. In
later years they sold their home and moved in with their daughter and
son-m-law, Mr. and Mrs. Nick Hertz.

Mr. Martin Schutz died on July 31, 1941 having reached the age of 79. Margaret
Schutz died April 21, 1949 at the age of 82.

The Martin Schutz's had six children: Martin, married Emilia Uttecht, they had
seven children. He died on August 12, 1953. Maria, Mrs. Ed Kraft, lives in
Billings, Montana and had seven children. Christine, Mrs. Jacob Wiebe, had
seven children and lives in Gladstone, Oregon. Fred married Margaret Lapp, they
live in Beulah and have four children. Catharine married Jake Strecker (now
deceased) has four children and she lives in Beulah. Louise, Mrs. Nick Hertz.
lives in Beulah and has one son.

MR. AND MRS. ED KRAFT

Ed. Kraft was born in the Odessa Area of S. Russia and came to America in 1913.
Maria, nee Schutz, Kraft was born in Bessarabia, Russia to Martin and Margaret,
nee Yanke, Schutz, and came to the United States with her parents in 1914. Both
settled in the New Salem area.

On January 12, 1915 Ed. and Maria were united in marriage at the Zion Lutheran
Church at Beulah, with the Rev. J. Mau officiating.

Mr. and Mrs. Ed. Kraft were among the early settlers of the Beulah area. They
farmed in the Beulah vicinity from 1915-1926. They farmed an average of
three-hundred acres of land, growing wheat, barley, oats, and rye. Average
yields were 15 to 20 bushels per acre. A small acreage of corn was grown for
feed. The Krafts kept a dairy herd, which at its maximum numbered twelve cows.

In 1923 the Krafts moved into Beulah, where Mr. Kraft was employed in the
Beulah Coal Mine for nine years. In 1935 they moved to St. Xavier, Montana.
Here they farmed until 1959 when they moved into Billings, Montana, where they
now reside.

The Krafts have three sons and three daughters living. They are: Martin of
Billings, Montana; Traugott of Ennis, Montana; Gottlieb of St. Xavier, Montana;
Katie, Mrs. Harold Muhlbeier; Margaret, Mrs. Albert Schmidt of Billings,
Montana; and Mary, Mrs. Edwin Kaiser of Lodge Grass, Montana. One son, John
died at Billings in 1940. The Kraft children all attended school at Beulah. The
Kraft's have 17 grandchildren and 4 great-grandchildren.

While the Kraft's resided at Beulah, they were members of the Zion Lutheran
Church; and are now members of the Pilgrim Congregational United Church of
Christ at Billings.

Mr. and Mrs. Ed. Kraft plan to attend the Fiftieth Anniversary festivities of
the Pioneer Home Town, Beulah, where they are looking forward to seeing many
friends and renewing old acquaintances.

        Nick Hertz         Theodore Hertz

NICK AND LOUISE HERTZ

Nick Hertz was born on April 11, 1899 in S. Russia to Christ and Rose Hertz. In
1901 he immigrated with his parents to America and located at Glen Ullin, N.
Dak. His parents died when he was only 10 years old. He then made his home with
his sister and brother-in-law, Mr. and Mrs. Kasper Barth. He lived with them
until he was 16, when he was old enough to go out on his own.

He worked for two years on the bridge gang between Mandan and Billings,
Montana. Nick served in the Armed Services in World War I, after which he came
to Beulah in 1919. He ran a threshing rig for Christ Moen, worked in Coles
garage and then started to work for the Knife River Coal Mining Co. as a pump
man in the deep shaft mine.

Louise Hertz was born on December 2, 1905 in S. Russia to Martin and Margaret,
nee Yanke, Schutz. She came to the United States with her parents in 1914,
first settling in Delmont, South Dakota and later on in the fall of 1914 they
moved to a farm south of Beulah. In 1915 Louise and her parents moved into
Beulah. Here she grew to young womanhood.

        Mrs. Nick Hertz

Nick and Louise were married on October 23, 1923 at the Peace Lutheran Church
of Hazen by Pastor Groszhans. They made their home at Beulah, where they joined
the Zion Lutheran Church, of which they still are members.

Nick worked for the Knife River Coal Mining Co. as a cutting machine operator
for 19 years. In 1949 they sold their house in town and moved on their farm 14
miles S. E. of Beulah. Here they made their home for the next 14 years. This
fall (1963) the Hertz's retired and sold their farm, built a new home in Beulah
where they now live.

The Hertz's have one son Ted, who was born on May 6, 1931, who grew to young
manhood in Beulah, graduating from the Beulah High School in 1949. He has
served in the U.S. Army Air Force since 1951. His present rating is S/Sgt. In
1954 he married Joyce Hagen. They have three children; two girls, Cindy Kim,
and Candice Dianne and one boy, Christopher Stanley. They are stationed at
Phoeniz, Arizona at the time of this writing.

JACOB WIEBE

        Standing--Left to right: Lourie, Elsie, Jacob Jr. Wilhelm, Esther
        and Marge. Sitting--Left to right: Theodor, Jacob and Christina Wiebe,
        Beverly.

I, Jacob Wiebe, was born in Cottenwood County in the State of Minnesota, on
June 18, 1893. I migrated to North Dakota with my parents in June of 1900. My
father John J. Wiebe homesteaded about fourteen miles south and five miles
east, of the present Beulah. Before Beulah came into existence our business had
to be transacted in Glen Ullin. I can remember how excited all of us were when
the north branch railroad was finally completed and the first elevator was
ready to take grain. At that time we were mainly grain farmers and having an
elevator several miles closer meant a great deal to us in time and money.

I started farming on my own, and in 1916 I bought a quarter section of land
from Dad. I married Christina Schutz, daughter of Martin and Margareta Schutz.
Eight children were born to our marriage, they are as follows: Elsie, Mrs.
Henry Boehler, lives in Missoula, Montana; Marge, Mrs. Roger Davidson, is
living in Minneapolis, Minnesota; Jacob died of pneumonia as an infant; Jacob
Jr. is married to Elsa Bartram of Center, they live in Savage, Montana; Esther,
Mrs. Donald Larson, lives at Wilton, North Dakota; Whilhelm E. married the
former Marcia Hilden, of Reeder, they live at Savage, Montana; Lourie, Mrs.
Edgar Strom, lives on a farm near Beulah, North Dakota; Theodor married Sandra
Milton, of Gladstone, Oregon, they live on the family farm; and Beverly, Mrs.
Glenn Dahl, whom we adopted as a baby lives at Badger, Minnesota.

We at present have 22 grand-children--all living. In 1960 my better-half,
Christina, and I decided to retire and take it easy. Our son Ted took over the
operation of the farm. In 1961 we moved to Gladstone, Oregon, acquired a little
five-room house and now watch the roses grow. I wish to add this: that North
Dakota is a wonderful state for a young man to start farming. I spent sixty
years in the Beulah territory and never got tired of the people nor the
climate, but finally had to make room for the younger generation. We are sure
that Beulah and it's surrounding area will always prosper and grow. Here many a
young man and woman will get a start in life and happiness.

THE MICHAEL PFENNIG FAMILY HISTORY

Michael Pfennig was born on July 25, 1893 in Leipzig, Bessarabien--South
Russia. He came to America with his parents Fred and Marie, nee Nikolaus,
Pfennig, in 1898. Fred Pfennig Sr. took up a homestead claim 15 miles north of
Beulah near Kronthal. Mike grew to manhood on his father's homestead. He
started farming on his own in 1914, when he received a 160 acres from his
father.

Emilea Koehler was born at Kronthal, Mercer County, on January 30, 1896, a
daughter of Johann and Louise, nee Breidenbucher, Koehler. She was the youngest
of six children.

Mike and Emilea were married on January 24, 1915 at the Kronthal Lutheran
Church, Pastor Buck officiated. After their marriage they went to live on
Mike's farm. It was on this farm that the four Pfennig children were born and
reared.

Mike had many setbacks in his farming operations. The worst of these was the
mysterious "drying-up" of all their wells in 1925. The Pfennigs had to haul all
of the water for their stock from the neighboring farmers. For years Mike dug
countless wells all over his land, trying to find water. The search was futile.
This situation existed until 1940 when they finally dug a successful well. It
was during these years that Mike began digging wells for the neighboring
farmers--this helped supplement the family income during these waterless
years.

The Mike Pfennigs gave up farming and moved to Beulah in 1944. He was employed
as custodian of the Beulah School for five years. After which he worked at
various jobs as a common laborer. In 1954 he became ill and was forced into
almost complete retirement. After an illness of four years he lost his battle
with cancer and passed away on February 14, 1958. Mrs. Pfennig is still
residing in her home in Beulah.

During the years they were living on the farm the Pfennigs were members of the
Kronthal Lutheran Church. After they moved to Beulah, they became members of
the Zion Lutheran Church. The four Pfennig children are: Martha, Mrs. Ted
Heihn, of Beulah; Hilda, was married to Ingelow Eid (deceased), now is Mrs. Joe
Schulz of Beulah; Pauline Vinson of The Dalles, Ore.; and Olga, Mrs. Gottlieb
Zeller of Beulah.

        Mr. and Mrs. George Miller, Louise and Twins

        Standing--Left to Right: Cleo and Florence. Sitting--Left to Right:
        Janet, August and Tillie, Mardell.

THE AUGUST MILLER FAMILY HISTORY

The following account contains the background, heritage, and contributions Mr.
and Mrs. August Miller have given to their community, county, and state.

We begin first of all with their background. August is the son of the late
George Miller and Dorothy (Kasper) Miller, who came to the United States from
South Russia in the year 1889. Upon their arrival they homesteaded north of
Beulah in the community known as Kronthal. They had fourteen children, ten of
which died in infancy. The four living are: Elizabeth (Louise), Mrs. John Roth
who lives at Missoula, Montana; Lydia, Mrs. John Wild, a widow and living in
Yakima, Washington; Brigetta (Betty), Mrs. Bert Dunker, who lives in Muscatine,
Iowa. George Miller lived on the homestead until 1928 when he and his wife
moved to Hazen, North Dakota. George passed away in March, 1931, and Dorothy
passed away in September, 1938. Both are buried at the Kronthal Cemetery.

Mrs. August Miller was the former Tillie Weil whose parents were Mr. and Mrs.
Jacob Weil. Jacob Weil and Katherine (Fischer) Weil first homesteaded near
Java, South Dakota. They came to the United States with their parents from
South Russia in the year 1891, and were married in the year 1899. In 1907 they
came to North Dakota and bought a farm in Mercer County. This farm was located
approximately 7 miles south of Zap. The Weils had seven children. They are:
Anna, Mrs. Sam Miller who lives at Hazen, North Dakota; Henry who married the
former Ida Spier, and now lives at Quincy, Massachusetts, Tillie, Mrs. August
Miller of Beulah; Ruth, Mrs. Theodore Kusler of rural Beulah; Alvina, Mrs.
Arthur Oster of rural Ellendale, North Dakota; Solomon who married Hulda
Webber, lives at Billings; and Ida, Mrs. Herbert Rueb of Beulah, North Dakota.

August Miller and Tillie Weil were married November 29, 1928 at Emaus
Congregational Church which was located near the Weil farm south of Zap. Rev.
Albert Held was the pastor of the Emaus Church at that time. Immediately after
their marriage, they bought the Miller farm, which they still own and operate.
The Millers are the parents of four daughters. They are: Cleo, Mrs. Fred Kulish
who lives at Dickinson, North Dakota. Cleo is a graduate of Hazen High School,
and a Degree graduate of Dickinson State College. She taught school in Mercer
County during the years of 1947-49. She also taught in rural schools in Adams,
Dunn, and Stark Counties, as well as at Killdeer Public, Killdeer, and at South
Heart, North Dakota. She is presently an instructor at Central Grade at
Dickinson, and is a Supervising Critic for college students who do their
Student Teaching in Grade Four at Central Grade. Florence is married to Robert
Kostelnak and makes her home at rural Killdeer. Florence is a Standard Graduate
of Dickinson State College and has taught in Dunn and McKenzie Counties.
Presently she is a teacher at Killdeer, North Dakota. Mardell, Mrs. Carlos
Tatum, makes her home at Colorado Springs, Colorado. She is a graduate of
Beulah High School, and a Standard Graduate of Dickinson State College. She,
too, taught school in Mercer, McKenzie, and McLean Counties, as well as at
Miles City, Montana. Before her marriage she served her country, as a WAC.
Through she has taken up the universal profession of all women, she is also
employed with Civil Service and does IBM Key Punching. Their youngest daughter
Janet is a graduate of Beulah High School and is presently employed at the ASC
