HISTORY OF ABBIE M. TYRRELL

Fifty years ago in the fall of 1914, a young blue-eyed brunette from Indiana
came to North Dakota to teach school. Abbie Tyrrell first taught a seven month
country school in McLean County. The following fall destiny brought her to
Mercer County, in whose schools she taught for 22 years.

On Sept. 2, 1915 Abbie began teaching the 8 month Endreson School in Knife
River District #10. The first year she lived with the Sovereign family. The
second year in October she moved to Effie Turner's--later--(Mrs. George
Endreson). Between these 2 years she attended summer school in Valley City.
After summer school and before school started the second year she was at home
on the Tom Keogh ranch (now Ralph Murray ranch) where they were building their
new modern home. On the school board were: Jim Keogh, Emmanuel Kusler and Sam
Murray--(John, Ted and Ace Kusler among the early pupils). The second school
year Warner Potratz tended the school fires for 10 cents per day. In January of
1916 A. D. Brown's moved from Stanton to Beulah. It was to their home that
Abbie's mother came on a cold March day in 1917--so cold she froze her nose
walking from the depot to Brown's (Clarence Schneider residence). Mrs. Tyrrell
opened a millinery shop in Beulah. The shop was on Beulah's main street on the
north side in the block across from the present Roxy theater. The millinery
occupied this location until, when Mrs. Tyrrell was on a visit to Indiana in
1918, Mr. Sovereign paid the landlord a years rent in advance for the location
forcing her to move. Abbie Tyrrell then purchased the Nagel house in 1919 for
$1700, and Mrs. Tyrrell set up shop in the home when she returned to Beulah
that year.

In the fall of 1918 Abbie had begun teaching in Beulah in the new school--the
present Legion Hall--a building erected in 18 days. There were 2 teachers in
this building. Her first grade pupils numbered 21. The second year in this
building she had a combination first and second grade (among pupils here were
Redmond Cox, Virginia and Maxine Brown). Then for the succeeding 5 years it was
straight first grade again. The 1921 class was the first one in the new brick
school house on the hill. At the close of summer school in 1922 Abbie had
earned her Second Grade Standard Certificate.

During the school year 1924-25 she taught in high school and served as high
school principal. She taught English, Latin and history. That year's graduates
were: Elwilda Murray Webber, Wilfred Herman, Eldon Shirley and Lonnie Vreeland.

After this for 6 years Abbie left herself be buffetted by the business
world--bookkeeping for Copeland Motor Co.

She returned to the Beulah school system in the fall of 1931 as teacher of a
combination first and second grade. From 1932 until 1943 when she moved to the
Bismarck school system she taught first grade.

Her summers were busy. She taught the first five summer kindergartens--years
1939-40-41-42-43-ever held in Beulah. That first kindergarten in 1939 was after
the summer session at Valley City where she received her B.A. degree in
elementary education.

Abbie taught in Bismarck the years 1943-46. She took a years leave of absence
to complete her M.A. degree. She did not return to North Dakota but accepted a
teaching position in Terre Haute, Indiana--her native state and taught there
for fifteen years until her retirement in 1961. Mrs. Nettie Tyrrell passed away
in 1960. Miss Tyrrell continues to reside in Terre Haute.

Abbie was a Congregational minister's daughter. She and her mother were very
active in the Beulah Congregational Church.

Her mother's table was as famous as her millinery. Some of the early years she
had boarders--among them some of our well known citizens today (L. F. Temme).
The table they set (for Abbie too learned to cook) fairly groaned with Indiana
specials. Their garden flourished and they preserved and pickled. Their dinner
parties with a variety of desserts were highlights in Abbie's fellow-teachers'
school year.

C. A. THELANDER FAMILY

"Carl" Thelander came to Beulah from New Salem in July of 1932 to open the
Beulah RCU Store in partnership with Wiegman Mercantile of New Salem and A. C.
Wolf. This general store was located on the corner across the street, west of
the German State Bank. In 1953 Thelander and Wolf purchased the store building
directly across the street south of their first location and moved in as the
RCU store--groceries and Federated Store--soft lines and ready-to-wear. They
became sole owners of the store in 1954. Following this they acquired a Ben
Franklin franchise under which name the store now operates.

Carl is a native of River Falls, Wisconsin; a graduate of River Falls State
College. In 1933 he married Ragnhild Jelstrup, a North Dakota school teacher; a
graduate of the University of North Dakota, whom he had met in New Salem. They
have two children: Nancy, born Dec. 5, 1941; a 1964 graduate in music from the
University of North Dakota, and Charles, born Sept. 7, 1943; a student at
Bismarck Junior College.

Their civic activities include for Carl: 8 years president of the town board;
continuous membership in the Lion's Club; County Club, Athletic Club, Rodeo
Association, Community Club and Chamber of Commerce, in all of which he has
taken his turn in office. Mrs. Thelander has served three terms on the Beulah
school board; is a member of Woman's Club, Mother's Club, Country Club and the
Congregational Ladies' Aid. Her chief interest lies in the City Library.

They enjoy the outdoors--canoeing, hunting, hiking, fishing, boating,
picnicking, gardening or just riding and looking at a North Dakota sunset.

        Marie, David, Hulda, Herman, James

Hulda and Herman Hildebrand have been a part of Beulah's Main Street since
1943. Previous to coming to Beulah they operated a Beauty and Barber Shop at
McClusky (1938-1943). Their first shop in Beulah was located in the old Beulah
Hotel building which they had purchased from C. E. Peterson. They built the
present structure in 1959 and moved into this modern shop on September 12,
1959. Herman is a graduate of the Moler Barber College at Fargo, North Dakota.
Hulda graduated from the Christie Beauty School of Bismarck.

        Hulda at work                      Herman and son, David

The Hildebrands have three children, James a graduate of the Beulah High School
(1958) and Concordia College (1962). Marie is a graduate of the Beulah High
School (1960) and Concordia College (1964). David was born in 1950 and is a
student of the Beulah Jr. High. He is very active in the local Boy Scouts.

Herman and Hulda grew up in Mercer County and have lived here most of their
lives (with the exception of the years they lived at McClusky). They are active
members of Zion Lutheran Church. They are proud of the part they have played in
Beulah's development in the past and hope to remain as active in the future.

JUZELER

Peter Juzeler and Adeline Anken--grandparents of David Juzeler, about whom this
history is written, came from Switzerland when they were about 12 or 14 years
old. They, and the group of immigrants with whom they came, made the trip in a
sailboat. The journey was a long one--12 weeks or more--long enough so the
immigrants supply of food and water ran very low. Their destination was New
York state. There they lived and raised a family of six children--John,
David, Charles, Lydia, Fred and Godfrey. They were cheese makers. Mr. Juzeler
passed away and Mrs. Juzeler, with her children (when David was 14 years old)
moved to Iowa. In 1887 David followed his uncle David Anken to Taylor, N. D. to
manage a cheese factory, located north of Taylor.

The following year David Juzeler began looking for a ranch site. In 1893 he
married Emma Paduch; then in 1896 he filed his claim for the land on Spring
Creek, which now comprises the Wilfred Herman ranch.

The land on which Mr. Juzeler filed his claim had originally been leased by
Lyons & Dunlap of Minneapolis. Juzeler's holdings later grew to include all the
land along Spring Creek from his home place to Beulah. In fact, one addition in
Beulah still bears his name, the "Juzeler addition."

The first years were rugged. The winters were severe. In the winter of 1897
many cattle, sheep and horses froze to death. From early December until the
last of March there had been no mail or communication of any kind. On March 24
he set out on foot to walk the 40 miles to Taylor for mail and supplies. His
half-way stop was at the Jacob Zimmerman place south of the Knife River--from
there they drove into Tavlor in a home made bob sled. Mr. Zimmerman loaned him
a horse for the return trip. Meanwhile a chinook wind melted the snow and his
horse had to swim the river where the Hanson Bros. now live.

Hebron was the nearest town--30 miles away. There were times when Juzelers
didn't go to town for 7 months. They visited Mrs. Juzeler's mother once a year,
if possible. Money being scarce, Mrs. Juzeler sometimes waited a long time for
the 2 cents necessary to send a letter to her mother. The first woman neighbor
was Mrs. Keller, wife of Sam Keller, who filed on a claim on the present site
of Dodge.

Church was conducted in the homes by traveling ministers or missionaries. The
general meeting place was the Ellen Maxie home. Early pastors were the Revs.
Mitchell and Hardy. The first church building followed the railroad.

Their daughter, Grace had 3 to 6 to 8 miles to travel to school. These too were
held in homes. Mrs. Pluma Harvey taught at Jason Peabody's sod house NW of
Golden Valley. It was a dark dismal place. School was held at the E. M. Walker
home for many years also. Here the pupils and teacher boarded and roomed during
the winter months. Imagine the work Mrs. Walker had with extra mouths and no
conveniences. The first schools built were cold and poorly equipped--
(sometimes 4 pupils to a text book). This included the school teachers, some of
whom had a 3rd grade education. Children coming to school on horseback of a
morning were asked to pick up kindling for the school stove.

For medical attention it was necessary to travel to Dickinson. When Grace broke
her arm at the age of three she was taken there by horse and wagon to have Dr.
Victor Stickney set her arm. Again, later--Nellie had her tonsils removed on
the dining room table by Dr. Plassman of Golden Valley and Dr. Larobee of
Mandan.

Life on the prairie was plain and rugged. At that time fruit trees were no
closer than the Missouri--It was a special treat to make the trek by wagon
there and to spend the night under the stars--with the wagon box for a bed. As
Grace Juzeler Smith says "Until one has one never will realize how beautiful a
night can be." It was a 2 day trip--Boilers, tubs and sacks were filled with
the plentiful fruit--plums, chokecherries, June berries and buffalo berries.

Mr. Juzeler was a Swiss and a cheesemaker. They cooked 10 to 20 pounds of
cheese in one day. Their cheese was made by first heating a boiler full of milk
to 110 degrees in the winter-115 degrees in summer--then adding a Rennett
tablet which had been dissolved in water. After it had clabbered, the whey was
removed--the cured cut fine with special cheese cutters--heated again, placed
in a form and pressed. The next morning it was removed from the press, salted,
wrapped and stored in their cheese caves. The salting process was repeated 3
times.

During high water, boats were needed to cross the streams. If the current was
strong or a man was not a boatsman, a post on either bank connected by a rope
with a pulley attached, sometimes with a swing seat hooked on, was used to make
the crossing.

One of the greatest dangers, and the most feared, to the settlers was the
prairie fire. The grass on the virgin prairie was lush and the fires numerous.
A good breeze or a strong wind would fan a fire and speed it  along. The wise
settlers plowed a fire guard around his buildings and around his hay. Later it
was a law to plow a fire guard around rural school houses. Mr. Juzeler was once
separated from his horse while fighting a fire, and had to cover his head and
run through the fire to safety. To fight fires ordinarily a team and wagon,
hauling barrels of water and wet rags was used, but when the fires were of
greater proportion, a critter might be split open, legs tied by a long rope or
wire which was wrapped around the saddle horn and then dragged by a horse over
the fire. Other firefighters followed, putting out what horse and critter
hadn't. All in all, it was a colorful life on the prairie.

THE LUDWIG BOECKEL FAMILY

        Standing--Left to Right: Albert, Herman, Ervin, Herbert, Art, David.
        Sitting--Left to Right: Evelyn, Esther, Mr. and Mrs. Ludwig Boeckel,
        Rose Martha.

Ludwig Boeckel was born in Kronthal, South Russia on August 27, 1880, the son
of Thomas and Elizabeth, nee Meyer, Boeckel. The father Thomas died in 1891.
Mrs. Boeckel and her four sons Joehanes, Joseph, Ludwig, and Peter, decided to
go to America. Their sister, Rosina, Mrs. Bernard Galster, had come over
earlier and they were anxious to see her again. In 1898 they set sail on "The
Kaiser Wilhelm" and arrived in the United States on August 20th. They came
directly to Mercer County, where they lived that first winter, with their
sister Rosina. Ludwig went to work for Jacob Hafner for about one year.

Dorthea Morast was born on January 13, 1883, in South Russia to John and
Elizabeth, nee Wiedrich, Morast. She came to the United States with her parents
in 1892, when she was 9 years old. They came to Mercer County and settled in
the Kronthal area. There were 6 brothers and 3 sisters in the Morast family.
George (deceased), John and Philip of Hazen, Ursus, Robert and Carl of Zap;
Katherine, Mrs. Jacob Wolff (deceased); Rosina, Mrs. Matt. Benz; and Dorthea,
Mrs. Ludwig Boeckel (deceased).

Ludwig and Dorthea were married on November 23, 1902, at the Kronthal Lutheran
Church north of Beulah. After their marriage they homesteaded 7 miles north of
Beulah. Here they made their home from 1902 until 1941. Grandma Boeckel lived
with them for 15 years, until her death.

The five years of "proving up" on their homestead were hard years, many
sacrifices had to be made, there were countless disappointments, but with the
help of God the Boeckels overcame these numerous obstacles and helped shape a
family estate of which their children could be proud. Their faith, devotion,
and courage, even in later years, will always be an inspiration to their
children.

The Boeckels retired from farming in 1941, when they moved into Beulah. The
Boeckels had joined the Immanuel Baptist Church of Beulah on August 7, 1927,
here they were members at the time of Ludwig's death, on September 22, 1952.
Several years later Mrs. Boeckel joined the Beulah Church of God, where most of
her children are members. Mrs. Boeckel lived in her home as long as she was
physically able. In 1958, when her health had completely failed her, she
entered the St. Luke's Nursing Home at Dickinson. She was there until 1962,
when the Beulah Nursing Home opened it's doors and the Boeckel children brought
their mother back to Beulah. She was a patient in the Nursing Home until her
death on August 26, 1963.

The union of Ludwig and Dorthea Boeckel had been blessed with thirteen
children. Three of these passed away as infants. The ten that are still living
are:

Albert married Rose Daffee (deceased), they had three children; Violet, Mrs.
John Newbauer; Lorretta, Mrs. Herbert Hart; Hilma, Mrs. Leo Curtiss. Albert's
second wife was, Bertha Oberlander, their four children are; Doris, Mrs. Paul
Beckman; Elaine, Mrs. Victor Walker; Bernice and Albert Jr. are still living at
home.

Herman, who lives on the Boeckel Homestead, married Elisabeth Wolff and they
have ten children: Irene, Mrs. Ray Fischer; Ruth, Mrs. Carl Hausauer; Leona,
Mrs. Eddie Rueb; Lenard married Ruby Horn; Jeanette, Mrs. Melvin Morast;
Marjean, Gaylen, Gary, Fern, and Faye are still living at home.

Martha, Mrs. Herbert Beuchler, of Zap, has three children: Calvin, married
Caroline Walz; Virginia, Mrs. Lester Schramm; and Larry, married Charleen Unruh.

Evelyn, Mrs. Theophil Miller, lives at Beach, N. Dak. and has four children:
Clifford, married to Rosy Weigum; Shirley, Mrs. Clayton Sandau; Dian and Debbie
at home.

Rose, Mrs. Andrew Wolff, lives in Beulah and has five children: Delaphine, Mrs.
Vernon Entze; Mildred, Mrs. Oscar Wetzel; Arliss, Mrs. Albert Entze; Pearl,
Mrs. Albert Gegelman; and Rick.

David married Sophia Wittmaier. They have eight children: Deloris, Mrs. Henry
Hess; Darlene, Mrs. Jack Harris; Melvin married Luella Bagely; Marliss, Mrs.
Robert Hess; Clayton married Dianne Coleman; Le Roy, Peggy and Leon.

Arthur married Pauline Pischel and they have two children: Rosella, Mrs.
Clarence Sayler; and Donald.

Ervin married Bertha Flemmer and they have two children: Dale and Donna.

Herbert married Sylvia Becker (now deceased). Their two children are; Betty and
Carol.

Esther, Mrs. Val Wieand, lives in Longview, Washington and has three children;
Valorie, Kim, and Kurt.

There are fifty-seven great-grandchildren. The Boeckel children will always be
grateful that they had such good Christian parents.

MR. AND MRS. PETER BOECKEL

        Back row--Left to right: Loraine, Harold, Lenora, Thomas, Loretta,
        Robert, Hilda, and Bernice. Front row--Left to right: Virginia,
        Rachel, Peter and Louise Boeckel, Sarah and Erna.

Peter Boeckel was born at Kronthal, South Russia, to Thomas and Elizabeth,
Meyer, Boeckel on March 1, 1883. In the year of 1898 Peter came to America with
his widowed mother and three brothers, John, Joe, and Ludwig. They sailed to
America on the ship, "The Kaiser Wilhelm". They arrived on August 20, 1898 and
came as far as Hebron, North Dakota by train, and the rest of the way to Mercer
County by wagon. The Boeckel's stayed with their sister and brother-in-law, the
Bernard Galsters, that first winter. The following Spring Peter's mother
homesteaded north of Beulah. She later sold the homestead to Jacob Weiss. The
older Boeckel brothers also took up homesteads near by. Peter was too young to
take up a homestead so he decided to go to college. He had plans of becoming a
minister. He went to work for $55. a year, and by earning his own money he
worked his way through college at Waverly, Iowa. He also attended Dakota
Business College at Fargo for one year in 1907.

On January S, 1908 he married Louisa Mohl, daughter of Frederick and Maria
Buchmiller Mohl. Louisa had also been born at Kronthal, S. Russia. Her
birthdate was July 1, 1889. She came to America with her parents in 1899, also
on the ship "The Kaiser Wilhelm". The Mohls homesteaded at Mannhaven. Louisa
had two brothers Jake and Peter, and three sisters; Helena Becker, Sophia Beck,
and Frieda Lang. Peter Mohl of Hazen is the only one of her immediate family
still living.

Peter Boeckel served as postmaster at Friedenthal from 1908 to 1912 receiving
wages of $6. a year. The Boeckels then homesteaded northwest of Beulah, this he
sold later on to his brother Ludwig. In 1916 he bought some land from Adam
Hintz with the understanding that Peter would serve as pastor for one year.
This land was located 5 miles northwest of Beulah. He, with the help of
neighbors, started to build. When it was time to move, the house was not
finished, so they had to live in a granary, that was attached to the barn,
until their house was ready. Here they settled permanently. In 1922, the
Boeckels joined the Baptist Church to which they belonged until Peter's death.

Peter served as State Representative in 1927 and 1928. He was a firm believer
in the Farmers Union and helped to get the Gas station in Beulah started. He
was secretary of the Farmers Elevator at Beulah and for the Taxpayers
Association for many years. During the years of the depression and drought he
went to work, in order to help out with family finances, at the State
Laboratory Commission in Bismarck. He worked here from 1934 until 1941, during
which time Mrs. Boeckel and the children stayed on the farm. It was not easy,
but somehow Mrs. Boeckel kept going, raising her large family and managing the
farm. Peter would come home as often as he could and in the spring of 1941 he
came home to stay. But this period of working together did not last long, for
on September 16, 1941, Peter passed away after a short illness. Again the
responsibility of farm and family rested on Louisa's shoulders. Mrs. Boeckel
stayed on the family farm until 1942, when she moved into Beulah. The Peter
Boeckels had 14 children, two of which died in infancy. The twelve remaining
are: Sarah, Mrs. Rudulf Weidrich, they farm at Washburn, and have 8 children;
Rachel, Mrs. Jake Fuhrman lives at Bismarck, he works as a custodian, they have
five children; Thomas married Viola Isaack, they have 9 children and live on
the home farm; Erna married Reinhold Reich (deceased) farms north of Beulah and
has 6 children; Loraine, Mrs. Otto Pfenning lives in Beulah, but they farm
north of Golden Valley, they have 4 children; Hilda, Mrs. Ervin Mohl, lives on
a farm west of Beulah and has 9 children; Harold married Junita Moore, is a
barber at Seattle, Washington and has 5 children; Lenora, Mrs. William Schulz
lives in Beulah where he is employed by the City of Beulah, they have 4
children; Lorretta, Mrs. Richard Teske lives in Beulah where Richard is
employed by the Farmers Union Oil Co., they have 2 children; Robert married
Ilene Teske. They live in Bismarck where he works for the U. S. Postal
Department, they have 2 children. Berniece, Mrs. Richard Pfenning lives on a
farm north of Golden Valley and has 2 children. Virginia is married to Master
Sargent Leon Outz of the U. S. Air Force, they have 2 boys and live in Orlando,
Florida. In 1942 Louisa moved into Beulah and resided here until her death on
February 14, 1958. During the time she lived in Beulah, she became a member of
the Church of God. She belonged to this church at the time of her death.

SAMUEL MILLER, SR.

There aren't many of Sam Miller's age who can trace their ancestors to their
great grandparents. Christ Miller, the great grandfather and John Miller, the
grandfather were both born in Germany. They moved to South Russia when John
Miller was nine years old, in 1811. There, Sam Miller's parents, the Fred
Millers were born.

In 1886 Mr. and Mrs. Fred Miller and their 6 children, 2 girls and 4 boys,
immigrated to the United States. They arrived at Hebron on April 27th in 1886.
There they lived until the following April when they moved to Mercer County. At
that time there were 6 families living between the Knife and Missouri rivers,
in that area. They were the Wm. Priebe, Dan Schimke, John Suess, Robert Lauf,
Solomon Isaak and Wm. Richter families.

They homesteaded before the government survey was completed. After the survey,
they found they were living on railroad land. However, arrangements were made
between the government and the railroad, permitting them to keep the land,
which is located 11 miles north of Hazen, near Krem.

Sam Miller, Sr. homesteaded for himself in 1898. He searched for and found a
place with a flowing spring, 6 miles north and 1 1/2 miles west of Hazen. His
choice of location was good, for this spring has continued to flow these 65
years. After "batching" for five years, in 1903 at the age of 27, he married
Maria Schuh. Maria Schuh had come to this country from South Russia in 1902.
They lived on their homestead for 46 years, after which they moved into Hazen,
where they lived for 10 years until Mrs. Miller's death. Since that time, he
has made his home with his children. They are Reinholdt F., Art W., Oscar E.,
Walter S., Pauline (Mrs. Christ Knoell), Hulda (Mrs. Albert Oster), Lydia (Mrs.
Herbert Wolf), Elsie (Mrs. A. Wolf-Urness), Martha (Mrs. Henry Heth), Regina
(Mrs. Albert Knoell) and Frieda (Mrs. Cecil Berg).

Talking with Mr. Miller, one can gain many interesting facts about pioneer
living. As a young boy he herded cattle for 3 years for Christ Sprecher. His
pay for this was $25.00 for 6 months. While herding he made for himself sun
clocks on the prairie. For these he used one long stick and 12 shorter ones,
arranging the short ones in a semi-circle. In 1881 when they came to Mercer Co.
he remembers that the woods animals were the deer, wildcats, cottontails and
squirrels. On the high prairie were ducks and prairie chickens. Five years
later they noticed the wild geese, cranes and crows. By 1900 the government
restocked prairie chickens 15 miles north of Hazen. It wasn't until 1913 that
rats came to Hazen in a carload of lumber from the east.

In the early years they traveled by oxen to New Salem for their supplies. Mr.
Miller remembers trading with Emil Ott, a storekeeper on the east side of New
Salem. Buffalo chips were a source of money for these hardy pioneers. They
gathered them on the prairie and sold them at New Salem or Hebron; these chips
sold for $8 to $12 per ton; and were shipped east to make fertilizer. The trip
to New Salem--45 miles--took 3 days and nights.

The government loaned the homesteaders seed wheat, which the homesteaders
repaid over a period of 3 years, bushel for bushel with no interest. There was
a mill at Krem where wheat was milled. For 60 lbs. of No. 1 wheat they received
38 lbs. of flour and 20 lbs. of bran. The cost for grinding was 21¢ per bu. 50
bu. of wheat produced enough flour and bran for a large family for a year. Mr.
Miller remembers when 15 acres of oats produced 400 bu. (price per bu.--40¢) 5
acres speltz--57 bu.; 35 acres wheat--160 bu. at 45¢ per bu. and 20 acres
flax--60 bu. at 60¢ a bu. Early prices had butter at 8¢ per lb.; eggs at 8¢ per
dz.; Arbuckle coffee (coffee bean) 7 lbs. for $1.00; workshoes, $2.00; Sunday
suit (coat, vest and 1 pr. trousers) $10.00, and a fur coat $25.00

Oscar Miller now runs and owns the farm.

FRANZ L. KASPER

Franz Kasper has a record of his ancestors, dating back to 1790. His
grandfather, Vincent Kasper and his father, Brakov with his family, migrated
from Austria to South Russia, to the village of Neusatz, in the Crimea. This
village is about 100 miles from the Black Sea.

Vincent Kasper was a shoe maker by trade. He continued in this business in
Russia for some time. Later, he purchased an interest in a flour mill and
operated this until he decided to go into farming. He purchased 200 acres of
land, about 100 miles from Neusatz and moved there. This is where Franz was
born July 26, 1896. Here they lived and farmed until 1904 when Brakov and his
family decided to leave the old world for the new. Upon arriving in the United
States they went to Muscatine, Iowa to the farm home of an uncle, Vincent
Kasper, spending some time with him, and particularly remembering they were
there on July 4.

They left Iowa by train for Underwood, N. D. where they had friends. At
Underwood they hired two teams and wagons to haul the family and personal
belongings to the ferry at Manhaven. Adam Sailer and Fred Bohrer, who operated
a General Store in Manhaven, met the Kasper family at the ferry landing and
arranged a place for them to stay until they could get word of their arrival to
George Miller and Jacob Hafner, who were already settled and farming in the
Kronthal community.

Mr. and Mrs. Brakov Kasper and their children, Louise and Franz, came to N. D.
at this time. Their daughter, Dorothy (Mrs. George Miller--mother of August
Miller, Beulah) was married and living in Mercer County at that time.

Brakov Kasper homesteaded about 2 miles north of the George Miller farm in Twp.
146--R. 87. Franz continued to live with his father on his homestead. In 1908
Brakov purchased 320 acres of land about four miles south of the original
homestead, on which Franz built some shelter and farmed these acres with horses
given him by his father. In the same year, 1908, Franz homesteaded on Sec.
4-146-87. He moved there after he had completed his sod buildings.

On Nov. 16, 1909 Franz married Nathalia Boeshans. She had one child  (Emilia
Mrs. Bernard Galster) by a previous marriage. The Kaspers had seven children,
and are: Theodore, Fairfield, Mont.; Toni, Mrs. John Huber, Missoula, Mont.;
Hilda, Mrs. Robert Roberts, Cutbank, Mont.; Freda, Mrs. George Hensleigh,
Jordon, Mont.; Otto, Beulah; Martha--died at age of 10; Walter, died in
infancy. Mrs. Kasper passed away Nov. 17, 1920.

Mr. Kasper continued to live on the farm. In December of 1929 he married Agneta
Kirchmeier from the Rhine community in Oliver County. They had no children by
this marriage, but his step-children include: Edna Roeser--Mrs. Robert
Benton--Seattle, Wash.; Ervin F. Roeser, Beulah; Leona Kirchmeier--Mrs. Walter
Hafner, Beulah; Ted Kirchmeier, Bismarck, N. D. and Herbert F. Roeser--
deceased.

In 1939 the Kaspers rented another farm in the same area. Here they lived until
1943 when they moved to Beulah. His son Otto now farms his land.

Franz Kasper is the only member of Brakov's family of four boys and three
girls, still living. Two of his sons and one daughter who never came to the
U.S. have died in So. Russia. August--lived north of Golden Valley; Dorothy,
Mrs. George Miller--and Louise--Mrs. Wm. Link--are all deceased.

Mr. and Mrs. Franz Kasper continue to make their home in Beulah. They are
members of the Concordia Lutheran Church.

        Kronthal School

HENRY RENKE FAMILY

Henry Renke, with his family of 8 children, Reinhold, Emil, Ida (Kauffman),
Molly (Wittenberg), Alvina (Kessler), Elsie (Hilz), Jake and Emma (Richardson),
left Charles Mix County, South Dakota in the spring of 1917 and came to Mercer
County to farm south of Zap. This is the present Ralph Johnson farm. In 1918
they moved to the C. F. Ewald farm, now known as the Mike Giese farm. They
farmed here until the fall of 1929 when they retired and moved to Zap. Here Mr.
Renke died in August of 1941. Mrs. Renke lived almost a year longer. She died
of a stroke in July of 1943.

REINHOLD RENKE FAMILY

Reinhold Renke, eldest son of Henry Renke, and his wife Nettie, came with his
parents to North Dakota in 1917. The Reinhold Renkes farmed with his parents
for a year and a half until the fall of 1918 when they set up farming on the
Pete Engbrecht place, now known as the Christ Bieber farm. While farming this
land he served as local school clerk.

In 1920 Reinhold Renke moved north of Beulah onto a farm known as the Philip
Schlecht farm. When his father retired in 1929 he and his family moved back to
the C. F. Ewald farm and farmed it for 4 years. In 1933 he moved onto his own
farm north of Beulah. Then in 1935 the Reinhold Renke's moved fo Dodge where he
was manager of the Peavey Elevator. Again he served on the school board for a
term.

In 1941 Reinhold was transferred to Beulah as Peavey manager--a job he held
until his retirement in 1961. During this time he was active in community
affairs. For eight years he served as street commissioner on the City Council.
During this period, the new city hall was built; 67 blocks in Beulah were
blacktopped and a modern water treatment plant was built. He is active in the
Congregational church--having been deacon, church board member and usher at
different times. for several years.

The Renke's have one daughter, Florence (Mrs. Art Knoell) of Hazen. Mrs. Renke
is a skillful needle woman, especially adept at quilting. Her shiny buns are a
favorite at church get-to-gethers. Reinhold Renke now has time to be the ardent
fisherman he always has been.

THE JOHN L. SCHMID FAMILY HISTORY

        Mr. and Mrs. John Schmid

John L. Schmid was born January 7, 1868 in Groswolde Kreis Leer Ost Freesland,
Germany. He was the third son of Mr. and Mrs. Ludwig J. Schmid. He grew to
manhood in the vicinity of his birthplace and served in the German Army from
1889 to 1892. At the of twenty-one, he came to the United States, where he
lived with his sister Mrs. Ben Zimmerman (Helen) and her husband, Ben, at
Freeport, Illinois.

During his stay with his sister, John learned to speak English by attending
classes in the public country school beginning with the primer classes on up.
He also attended school in Rochester, New York where he studied for the
ministry.

On July 15, 1903 he married Nellie Lydia Koglin. To this union were born four
children; Helen, Elsie, John Jr. and Lloyd. Mr. Schmid served as pastor of
churches in Holland, Minnesota and Hebron, North Dakota. In the year 1915 his
wife Nellie passed on.

After his wife's death, John moved with his family to the Golden Valley area.
Later he decided to go into farming, his farm was 2 1/2 miles west of Beulah.

In 1917 he married Mary Lindsteadt. This marriage was blessed with four
children; Irene, Berniece, Irvin and Verna Deane. He continued to farm on this
first farm until the spring of 1925. John moved his family to a new farm,
located four miles west of Beulah. This farm John farmed continuously from 1925
to 1944. He continued to serve area churches along with his farming operations.
The Schmid children were raised on the farm and educated in the Spring Creek
and Beulah School.

In 1944 the Schmids retired from farming and their son, Irvin took over the
family farm.

After retirement John and Mary moved to Los Angeles, California where they
resided until their deaths; John passing on in 1952 and his wife Mary in 1954.
At the time of their deaths they had twenty-five grandchildren.

The children of John L. Schmid are:

Helen, Mrs. Harvery Clemmens of Tacoma, Washington has four children. She
teaches at Handicapped Wing-City of Tacoma Seward School.

Elsie married Peter Ford (deceased), lives in Costa Mesa, California, she has
four children. John Jr. of Portland, Oregon, has two children. He is employed
as a landscaper, gardener and Minister. Lloyd is manager of a Chevrolet Garage
at Moses Lake, Wash. He has three children. Irene, Mrs. Don Spurrier of Jermyn,
Penn. has four children. Bernice, Mrs. Harold Burns of New Orleans, La. has
three children, Irvin farms near Beulah, and Verna Deane, Mrs. Walter Browning
of Redondo Beach, California has two children.

After his father retired Irvin took over the farming operations, Irvin was born
in 1921. He grew to manhood on his father's farm and attended local schools;
graduating from the Beulah High School in 1938. After which he worked in the
community and farmed with his father. He was married in 1944 to Esther Binder,
daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Albert Binder. Esther is a graduate of the Beulah High
School and has been employed as secretary by the Beulah schools for the past
four years. They have three children: Ivan born in 1945, Earl in 1950 and
Elmira in 1951. Ivan entered the University of North Dakota in Grand Forks as a
first year student in 1963. Earl and Elmira are attending public school in
Beulah.

BEN AND HULDA MUHLHAUSER

        Betsinda, Margareta and Hulda Muhlhnuser

Benjamin Muhlhauser was born on August 19, 1899 at Krem, S. Russia to John and
Barbara, nee Schmidt, Muhlhauser. In June 1900 he came to the United States
with his parents and settled in Hosmer, South Dakota. In 1908 they moved onto a
homestead in the Blue Grass area. When he was 18 he came to Beulah where he
worked in the mine and later, on the section as a section foreman at Zap, N.
Dak.

On November 7, 1920 he married Hulda Sandau at the Zion Lutheran Church with
Marcus Baudler officiating. Hulda was the foster-daughter of Jacob and
Magdelena Sandau. She was born on June 15, 1902 at Tripp, South Dakota. Before
her marriage Hulda worked at the Beulah Mine Cook house, Milligan Hotel and as
a house maid for Max Martells.

The Muhlhausers lived in Zap for about two years and then came back to Beulah
where Ben worked in the mine until the first big labor strike of 1922. They
moved to Gold Creek, Montana until 1925--when they moved back to Beulah. He
went to work for Geo. Slowey, the Farmall tractor dealer. Later J. B. Field
bought out Slowey and Ben kept working for Mr. Field until he went in business
for himself in 1940. He built Ben's Standard Service and sold Allis Chalmers
Machinery until his death on March 22, 1958.

The Muhlhausers were blessed with three children. Benjamin Jr. (born Sept.
1935) operates Ben's Standard Service and lives at home with his mother.
Betsinda (born Oct. 1937) is a 1957 graduate of the Beulah High School and
attended Jamestown College. She is a teacher in the Devils Lake Public School
System. Margareta (born May 1941) is a 1960 graduate of the Beulah High School.
At the present she is a student nurse at the Bismarck Hospital and will
graduate in July.

Mrs. Hulda Muhlhauser still makes her home in Beulah where she and her family
are members of the Zion Lutheran Church.

        Ben Jr. and Ben Sr.

LOUIS AND MARY THOMAS FAMILY HISTORY

        Back Row: Pauline, Cecelia (Orth), Amelia (Dietrich), Josephine
        (Walby), Jerome, Mary (Dietrich), Margaret (Hughes), Delaphine
        (Lynch). Front row: Francis (Henke), Louis and Mary Thomas, Linda.

Peter Thomas was born in Selz, Russia on August 15, 1872. On November 19, 1901
he married Margaret Klein. (Margaret was born Sept. 25, 1875) They immigrated
to America in 1906. After living in Aberdeen, So. Dak. for 2 years they took up
a homestead in Oliver County, in what is known as the St. Benedict Area. Peter
died in Bismarck on February 14, 1952 and Margaret died on Jan. 5, 1961 also at
Bismarck. There were seven children in the Thomas family. Those living are:
Louis, John Alich, Sylvester.

Louis Thomas was born in Selz, Russia on March 17, 1903. He was three years old
when his parents immigrated to America. He lived with his parents until he
married Mary Keller and then farmed on his own.

Mary E. Keller was born on August 17, 1910 in Oliver County. A daughter of John
and Amelia, nee Schutt Keller. Her father John Keller was born Oct. 3, 1882 in
Selz, Russia. He came to America in 1903 and took up a homestead in the St.
Benedict Area, South of Beulah. On November 5, 1907 he and Amelia Schutt were
married at the St. Clemens Catholic church at Haymarsh. This union was blessed
with 12 children. 9 of whom are still living: Frank, Mary, John, Martin, Rose,
Magdalena, Peter, Clementine and Dorothy. Joe, Frances and Adam are deceased).

Mary was born on the family homestead and here she grew to womanhood. Mary and
Louis Thomas were married on November 20, 1928 at the St. Benedicts Catholic
Church of rural Beulah. The Thomas's farmed until 1932. Louis went to work for
Lars Ostby of Bluegrass until 1935. They then moved to Hebron where he worked
in the Harnisch Coal Mine until 1943. They moved to Beulah where Louis worked
in the plant for a year. He then went to work for R. Grishkowsky until 1951. In
1952 he became employed by the Helm Bros. Coal Mine and in 1953 joined Knife
River Coal Mining Co., where he is still employed as an oiler.

The Louis Thomas's are active and faithful members of St. Joseph's Catholic
Church of Beulah. Louis is a member of the Knights of Columbus and Mary is a
member of St. Joseph's Altar Society and at one time was a Catholic Daughter
leader. She is also a member of the Royal Neighbors, Friendly Homemakers Club
and a 4-H Leader and Cub Scout Den Mother. She is also the local Tupperware
Dealer.

The marriage of Louis Thomas and Mary Keller was blessed with eleven children.
Ten of whom are still living and pictured above. The Thomas's have 32
grandchildren.

FRANK AND MIKE SKALSKY

        Mr. and Mrs. Mike Skalsky

Frank Skalsky Sr. was born in Poland on Dec. 3, 1845. He married a girl from
Selz, Russia, Marie Eberhardt. The Skalskys immigrated to America, arriving at
Hebron on May 16, 1892. They were met by an old acquaintance Henry Lanz whose
homestead was west of Hebron. The Skalsky's lived with the Lanz's until they
had found a suitable homestead. They filed claim on 160 acres south-east of
Glen Ullin, and built their first sod house and then their sod and stone barn.
Here the Frank Skalsky's raised their family of seven children. Grandfather
Skalsky was a blacksmith by trade, and many times this proved to be a valuable
asset for there were many of his own tools and neighbor's machinery which he
was able to repair.

When they retired they moved into Glen Ullin. Grandmother Skalsky died on
November 26, 1920 and Grandfather on Dec. 3, 1945.

Mike Skalsky was born in 1879 in Selz, Russia. At the age of 13 he came to
America with his parents. When he became of age he took up a homestead in the
St. Benedict Area in 1904. The winter before he had worked for Peter Weiand
herding sheep, so that he could have a little cash. He built a little sod house
and barn 28' x 40'. On January 23, 1905 Mike Skalsky and Barbara Zahn were
married in the Haymarsh Catholic Church. The following March he started
breaking the sod with a 4-horse team pulling the walking plow. The first year
he turned over 66 acres and he seeded it into 64 acres of flax and 2 acres of
wheat. The soil was so rich that the 2 acres of wheat burned up. The Skalskys
lived on the homestead until 1941 when they retired and moved into Glen Ullin.
They lived here 19 years until Mrs. Skalsky died July 1, 1960. In 1961 Mike
moved into the St. Benedict Old Folks Home at Dickinson. The Skalsky's have
been life long members of the Catholic Church. Mike was one of the original
organizers of St. Benedicts Catholic Church.

The Mike Skalsky children are: Frank, Wendelin, Joseph, Rose, (Mrs. Adam
Keller), Magdalena, (Mrs. Sebastian Hartze) (deceased), Ugena, (Mrs. Martin
Keller), Mary (Mrs. Pete Hipp), and Genevieve, (Mrs. Clarence Lynch).

        Eddie and Freda

I was born on October 26, 1915 on a farm 8 miles north of Stanton. My parents,
Fred and Mathilde Krieger were farming at the time. In 1927 we moved to Hazen,
where my sister, Tina, and I attended school. Here I grew to young manhood, I
have many fond memories of these years. It was in Hazen that my father and I
started our first Bar, on March 4, 1937. I can remember rolling out the first
keg of beer and putting it on Tap. In those days we had to ice it up before we
could use it. After we sold this Bar we moved to Zap and started Krieger's Bar,
on July 1, 1939. While living in Zap, I met my wife, Freda, the daughter of
George and Christine Unruh. We were married on May 9, 1941. When World War II
broke out, I was inducted into the Armed Services and sworn in on Feb. 6, 1942,
at Fort Snelling. I took my basic training at Fort Sill, Oklahoma, after which
I was sent to Camp Bowie, Texas. I knew I'd be there for sometime so I sent for
Freda, and we set up house-keeping in a one-room cottage at Brownwood, Texas,
which was near the camp. We lived here until I was shipped overseas 18 months
later. We shipped out on the Troop Ship "The Sturgis". The next 14 months were
spent in the Pacific Theatre of Operations. When the war was over I was shipped
back to the U.S.A. on the famous aircraft carrier, "The Saratoga". I received my
Honorable Discharge at Fort Lewis, Wash., on Jan. 14, 1946.

My parents moved the stock and fixtures from the bar in Zap to Beulah and went
into business with my sister, Tina, and her husband, Cecil Smith. This was the
beginning of The Mirror Bar. After the war I came back to Beulah and bought my
Dad's share in the Mirror Bar, and Cecil and I formed a partnership which
lasted until November, 1959, when I bought out Cecil's share. I now have the
responsibility of the management of the Mirror Bar. I employ one regular
bartender and the rest of the help is hired on a part time basis. I also help
manage the Stockman's Bar at Dickinson, which Harvey Unruh and I purchased on
January 12, 1963.

Freda and I were blessed with two wonderful children; Robert, born on  February
14, 1949, and Lo Ann born on April 27, 1952. I am a member of the V.F.W.,
American Legion, Elks and the Beulah Chamber of Commerce. Freda has always been
active in the V.F.W. Auxiliary. Our family is a member of the Zion Lutheran
Church.

We are proud of Beulah and the years we have lived here. It is our hope that
Beulah will keep prospering and growing in the future as it has in the past.

        Standing--Left to right: Marie, Fred, Edward, Dave, John, Rose.
        Sitting--Mr. and Mrs. Unruh.

MR. AND MRS. JOHN UNRUH, SR.

John, son of John and Katharma Stokk Unruh was born January 31, 1881 in South
Russia. Rosina, daughter of John and Rosina Roesch Gutjahr was also born in
South Russia, Jan. 11, 1881. Mr. and Mrs. Unruh were married there Feb. 7, 1906.

Mr. Unruh worked as a blacksmith in Russia. In 1911 they immigrated to the
United States. Their three oldest children, John Jr., Mary and Rose were born
in Russia. Their food and clothing was all they brought with them. The food
consisted mostly of "fronsollen brot," which was somewhat like a dried toasted
bread, and a good food for a voyage as it did not spoil readily.

They embarked at Bremen Bahnhofstrasse 30, Germany, bound for a new country--
on the ship "F. Missler"--so named in honor of it's Captain. Upon their
arrival in New York they boarded a train for Hosmer, South Dakota. Here they
spent their first winter at the home of Mrs. Unruh's brother, Christ Gutjahr.

In spring of 1912 they moved to North Dakota. Here they lived with Mr. Unruh's
uncle, Nicholus Unruh, who was established on his homestead 18 miles NE of Glen
Ullin. They spent most of the summer with them, while their own combination sod
house and barn was being built. In late summer of that year 1912, the John
Unruh's moved to their homestead about 10 miles south of present Zap.

Rev. Frank Loewen, an Evangelical minister, who traveled by horse and buggy,
would hold services at the Unruh home once a month. This was the extent of
their church attendance.

Mr. Unruh's farming equipment consisted of two horses and a walking plow;
consequently his farming the first years was limited to breaking but few acres.
The land was seeded by broadcasting the seed by hand. A reaper was used for
harvesting the crop.

The availability of water presented another problem, as their well was a half
mile north of their place. At first all the water used was carried in pails
this distance, but later they used a stone boat and team, and were able to haul
a 50 gallon wooden barrelful in one trip.

Mr. and Mrs. Unruh continued to improve their living conditions. First they
built a small frame house on the same location as their sod house, and later
built a much larger house and other farm buildings close to the well. Here they
lived until 1941, when they moved to Zap. Mrs. Unruh passed away Nov. 1, 1962
at the age of 81 years. Mr. Unruh passed away Feb. 24, 1963, and was 83 years
of age.

MR. AND MRS. MIKE KELLER

Mr. and Mrs. Adam Keller came to America from Selz, South Russia in 1901. They
made their home in Bismarck, where they resided for three years with their
seven children, six boys and one girl; Sylvester, John, Benedict, Christ,
Katherine, Mike and Carl. In 1904 the Keller family took to the trail in a
covered wagon to stake a claim in Oliver County, 15 miles southeast of Beulah.
Here the family was raised, living first in a sod house and later in a stone
house. Only three of the seven children are living, and are Katherine, (Mrs.
Frank Heier), Richardton, N. D.; Carl, Butte, Montana and Mike, Beulah, N. D.

Mike married Mary Ann Goldade at St. Mary's Catholic Church in Hague, N. D. in
1922. Mary is the daughter of the late Mr. and Mrs. Joe Goldade, who came to
America in the 1890's, living in Bismarck until 1903 when they homesteaded in
Emmons County. The Goldades were instrumental in getting the Kellers to America.

After their marriage, the Mike Kellers rented a farm in western Oliver County
and began their farming operations with a gang plow, rake, mower, drill,
harrow, binder and seven head of horses. In 1924 the first one hundred sixty
acres of land was purchased. In 1928 the Kellers erected a set of farm
buildings on this land. Here they lived until their retirement. From time to
time they purchased more land. By 1948 their farm's acreage totaled 2180 acres,
of which about 1000 acres were under cultivation, and the balance pastured
several hundred head of Hereford cattle. They built 27 miles of fence for these
pastures.

Their first tractor was purchased in 1940. In succeeding years, other modern
machinery was added until in 1962 when they decided to discontinue their
farming operations and retire, their farming equipment was modern and complete.
This equipment was disposed of at an auction sale. During the latter years the
Kellers farmed, they operated a feed lot which accommodated about 75 head of
cattle at a time.

All this could not have been accomplished without the help of a wife and
family. Mrs. Keller was a busy woman, as she helped with the farming and kept a
growing family nourished. As the children grew older they took over many of
their mother's farm tasks, and she was then able to spend her time in the home.

Nine children were born to Mr. and Mrs. Mike Keller--two girls and seven
boys--two of which are deceased, Victor and Pius. Both daughters, Mrs. Amos
Hoepfner, (Barbara) and Mrs. Carl Fischer, (Loretta) help on their own farms in
the St. Benedict area southeast of Beulah. Joe, married to the former Elaine
Schumacher, farms the original home farm, which he purchased in 1962.
Sylvester, married to the former Betty Neuberger, lives in Beulah, where he is
an Auctioneer and construction worker. Ralph, married to the former Lorraine
Wilhelm, lives in Bismarck, where he is engaged in general contracting of
commercial buildings. Wilhelm, (Bill) married the former Joyce Ziegler, and
lives in Beulah. Bill is Beulah's City Auditor and operates their motel.
Francis is a senior at Beulah High School and plans to further his education.

The Mike Kellers retired in 1962 and now make their home at 104 NW 4th St. in
Beulah. They are members of St. Joseph's Catholic Church. They have nine
grandchildren to enjoy--five girls and four boys. One grand-daughter is
deceased.

BEULAH LIVESTOCK AUCTION

Where Top Prices are always paid

        The Fastest growing Auction Market in the heart of the Tri-County area.

Late in the summer of 1963 the Beulah Businessmen and many of the area Farmers
and Ranchers heard the rumor that Beulah would be without a Sales Ring. They
became duly alarmed. Many of them felt that something had to be done. In August
the first meeting was held. At this meeting it was decided that some of the men
should investigate the cost of building, and the various types of construction,
of some of the newer Sales Rings in the State. After a considerable amount of
research was completed, the second meeting was held, and reports given on the
sales rings that had been visited. There were a large number of farmers,
ranchers and businessmen in attendance and they decided to form a corporation
and incorporate for $75,000.00, selling stock to individuals at $100.00 a
share, but each share holder having only one voting share. The men signing the
Articles of Incorporation were Herbert Hafner, John Kusler, and Fred K. Flemmer.

After $30,000.00 had been solicited and collected, construction on the Sales
Ring began. Many men donated labor in the contruction of the pens and grounds.
Some of them received a share of stock in the corporation in payment for their
work.

        The Board of Directors and the co-signers of the Articles of
        Incorporation. Standing--Ed. Unruh, Doug Dettmann, Herb Hafner, John
        Kusler. Sitting--John Maier and Fred K. Flemmer. Not pictured--Ed
        Schulz.

The first sale was held on November 22, 1963. The regular sale day is Saturday.
Manager of the yards and ring is Wilfred Winkler, Manager of the office staff
is Darold Benz and Mrs. Alex Voegele operates the Lunch Room. The Local
Veterinarian is Dr. Rydell, who is always on deck at every sale.

The Beulah Livestock Auction Market is proving itself to be a successful
venture and an asset to the economy of Beulah.

At the present time there are 142 individual stockholders.

The Board of Directors are: Edward Unruh, President; John W. Maier, Vice
President; D. H. Dettmann, Sec.-Treas; Directors; F. K. Flemmer and Edward
Schulz.

        The Sales Ring Arena

HENRY SPIER SR.

        Mr. and Mrs. Henry Spier Sr.

Henry Spier Sr. was born on January 30, 1871 in Rumania. There he met and
married Rosana Gunsch in 1897. They came to America in 1898 and lived in
Belmont, South Dakota. In 1903 they homesteaded six miles south of Zap, where
they lived until 1940. Mrs. Spier passed away in May 1958, Mr. Spier who is 93
years old is in good health and is one of the oldest residents of the Joachim
Memorial home in Beulah.

Seven children were born to the Spiers: Joe and Dan deceased, Fred of Hazen,
Henry Jr. of Beulah, Helmuth of Renton, Washington, Ida, Mrs. Henry Weil of
Quincy, Mass., and Rose. Mrs. Sam Weidrich of Lodi, Calif.

HENRY D. SPIER JR.

        Mr. and Mrs. Henry Spier Jr.

Henry Spier Jr. was born November 30, 1913, six miles south of Zap, on his
parent's homestead. He met Frieda Mohl of Golden Valley. They were married on
November 26, 1936 and went to live on his father's farm. They purchased the
farm from his father in 1938 and actively farmed it until 1948.

In 1949 he purchased a half interest in the. Field Implement Company of Beulah.
In 1952 he sold his interest in the Field Implement Company to J. B. Field, and
acquired the Ford agency from Flemmer Sales and Service. They have since added
several farm machinery lines. In 1959 he built Spier Bowling Lanes in Beulah.

The Spiers have three children, Orbadella Fetch, residing in California, Murel
of Beulah, and Diana, their adopted daughter, who lives at home. Two grandsons,
Rene and Howell Fetch.

Henry is proud of Beulah's Golden Jubilee since it is also his fiftieth
birthday. The Spier's are proud of their success in business in Beulah, and
wish to thank their many customers for making it possible.
