THE JACOB FLEMMER FAMILY

        Back Row--Left to right: Fred, John, Lydia, Louisa, Barbara, Rose,
        Christine. Front Row--Left to right: Carl, Jacob, (Ida on Father's
        lap) Fredericka, David.

Jacob Flemmer was born at Gleuckstahl, S. Russia, on April 7, 1863 a son of
Christian and Rosina Klein, Flemmer, Jacob was a blacksmith by trade. He had
worked as an apprentice under Jacob Huber. He served in the Russian Army as a
gunsmith. One thing that the Flemmer children always remembered about their
father was that on Good Friday he would not eat one morsel of food. This day of
fasting went back to his years in the Russian Army. It was a practice that at
Easter time they would draw lots and three soldiers from the company of men
were allowed to return home. Jacob was so filled with a longing to go home and
see his family again that the night before the drawing (Good Friday) he made a
vow that if his name was one of the three drawn, he would never let it be
forgotten. The Lord answered his prayers and until his death he commemorated
this occasion with a day of complete fasting every Good Friday.

Jacob married Fredericka Jakober on June 18, 1889, in Gleuckstahl. Fredericka
was born on May 6, 1866 a daughter of Karl and Laisa, nee Stock, Jakober. The
Flemmers decided to come to the United States and in the spring of 1906, Jacob,
his wife Fredericka and 9 children sailed on the Kaiser Wilhelm II. They landed
in New York and set out by train for Ashley where some of Mrs. Flemmer's family
had previously settled. They arrived at Ashley on May 6, 1906. What should have
been a happy family reunion turned out to be a sad and tragic one. Fredericka's
younger sister, Rosina-Mrs. Matt. Hettick, had passed away during childbirth
five days earlier and had been buried that very day.

In June the Flemmer family came to Glen Ullin where they were met by an old
friend from Russia, Michael Voegele Sr. Here they purchased their homesteading
outfit; a wagon, two horses, one cow and ten chickens. For several days the
family stayed at the Frederick Voegele family homestead. Conditions were rather
crowded so some of the youngsters had to sleep in an adjoining stock shed, but
they were not alone and they had the flea bites to prove it.

When the family moved over to their homestead (about 16 miles south of Beulah)
their wagon was to be their only home until the one-room sod house was
completed. That wagon wasn't large enough to accommodate the entire family so
the older children had to sleep under it. Their mother Fredericka did the
family baking in an outdoor oven, which Jacob had made out of stone and mud.
After the house had been built the next building to be built was a barn for the
livestock, for a good farmer would never think of leaving his stock out in the
cold. Later on when time allowed, another room was added to the house. By this
time it was harvest and since they had not time to plant a crop, Jacob Flemmer
went out harvesting at neighboring farms for thirty dollars a month.

There was no school available so the boys would hire out to herd cattle and the
girls as hired girls to whatever neighbors needed help. Finally in 1912 the
first school opened it's doors, and at least some of the younger children were
able to attend.

Two children were born in this country, David and Ida. The year 1911 was one
that the Flemmer family would never forget. For it was on that year they lost
their first born and Fredericka gave birth to their last child, Ida. Jacob (the
oldest of the Flemmer children) was gassed while working in the family well on
February 23, 1911, on his 21st birthday. This was not the only child they were
to lose during these first hard years, for their son, John, died while serving
in the U. S. Army on November 3, 1918 and was buried on November 11, 1918.

In 1920 the Flemmers moved into town for about three years, at which time Jacob
was employed as a janitor at the Beulah School. In 1923 they tired of living in
town and moved back to the farm. On January 6, 1924 Jacob succumbed of a heart
attack. Fredericka farmed with the two younger boys until 1930 when her
youngest daughter Ida and her husband, Christ D. Voegele took over the family
farm. Here she lived until Ida's untimely death on March 20, 1943. Ida was 32
years old when she passed away leaving behind her husband and nine small
children. Fredericka, who was now 77 years old, took over the responsibility
and the care of these small children. She did the cooking, baking (nine loaves
of bread at a time) and many other of the tasks that had to be done. This she
did until she was 80 years old when Christ remarried and she was relieved of
this responsibility.

Fredericka then moved her little house onto her daughter Christina's (Mrs.
Philip Hausauer) farm. There she made her home until she could no longer live
independently. She then moved mto the Joachim Memorial Home at Beulah where she
lived out her remaining years. She passed away on January 7, 1952. At the time
of her death she had eight children still living: Rose, Mrs. Henry Zimmerman of
Glen Ullin; Barbara, Mrs. Carl Tesky of Beulah, Fred of Beulah; Christina, Mrs.
Philip Hausauer, Beulah; Louisa, Mrs. Henry Rehling, Hebron; Lydia, Mrs. Fred
Young of Mandan; Karl of Hebron; and David of Everett, Wash.

She had fifty grandchildren and twenty-nine great-grandchildren at the time of
her death.

THE YEAR OF 1915

January--the first band was organized.
February--Beulah has it's first Doctor, Dr. Hugo Neukamp.
March--Our first baseball team was organized. H. H. Kenyon was mgr., Jesse
  Crow, captain and J. J. Walker--sec.-treas.
May--new school was dedicated. Beulah was the only small town in state with
  school house built according to State requirements.
September--1915 - South Farmers circulated petitions for rural mail delivery.
The price of coffee--3 lbs. for 89¢ and a pound can of salmon sold for 22¢.
  November 4, 1915 the following article was printed. "That our town is a good
  trading point is evidenced by the fact that we have four mercantile stores,
  all doing a flourishing business, and two more under construction, two
  excellent hardware establishments that can hardly handle the immense amount
  of business that comes here, three grain elevators, that are running full
  capacity, and in fact every line of business that is generally conducted in a
  town of this size is represented here.
All our citizens are true blue boosters and each is determined to put Beulah in
  a class with the best towns in the state.
November 4 Beulah's population was 300.
This fall there was an epidemic of Black Leg.

January, 1923
The people of Beulah are enjoying some very good radio concerts each evening
over the radiophone which has been installed in the basement of the
Congregational Church. Programs have been received very clearly from as far as
Los Angeles, Fort Worth and Altanta. It is certainly very interesting
entertainment.

HISTORY OF WILHELM KLUNDT, SR.

Wilhelm Klundt was born on May 11, 1874, in Rohrbach, South Russia, the son of
Henrey and Marie, nee Riedlinger, Klundt. At the age of twenty-one he was
called into the Russian Military Service. Here he served and trained for four
years. After his discharge he married Elizabeth Losing.

Elizabeth was born on April 2, 1884, a daughter of William and Margaret, nee
Wagner, Losing. Elizabeth and Wilhelm were married on May 11, 1903 at Rohrbach,
Russia. The same year this young married couple immigrated to America and took
up a homestead 7 miles north of Garrison, North Dakota. Together they broke the
sod and built their own sod house and barn. They were very proud of their first
home in America for it was a product of their own two hands working together.
In order to help out with family finances Mr. Klundt would haul hay at Ryder
for 20¢ a day.

During this time the area was not so thickly populated. Most of the surrounding
land was nothing but prairie. When it was dry, this presented quite a hazard
because every once in a while a big prairie fire would break out. The Fire
Dept. consisted of two fire fighters; Elizabeth and Wilhelm, who were armed
with buckets of water and wet grain sacks. If the fire was bad enough, friends
and neighbors would get together and help with the fire fighting. For the first
five years the Klundts had no well so they had to water their cattle and horses
at the neighbors. Flickertails were plentiful and they caught as many as fifty
a day. The bounty was 2¢ a piece. This does not seem like a great deal of money
today; but they welcomed every additional bit of money for cash was mighty
scarce.

In 1913 they sold the land and moved to Mercer County, where they purchased a
farm seven miles north of Beulah. Here the Klundt's eight children were raised
and educated in the nearby rural schools. They lived on the farm until 1940
when they retired and moved into Beulah. Their son-in-law Ed Schultz farms the
land at the present time. The Klundt's were active and faithful members of the
Zion Lutheran Church. Wilhelm passed away an January 11, 1953. After his death
Elizabeth sold their home and has been making her home with her children and a
sister, Margereth Steinert of Bismarck.

Wilhelm and Elizabeth were blessed with eight children: Louise, Mrs. August
Keller of Beulah; Sopbia, Mrs. Samuel Buehrer of Archbold, Ohio; Christene,
Mrs. Oswald Geist of Bismarck: Rose, Mrs. Edward Schultz of Beulah; Ella, Mrs.
John Maier of Beulah; Wilhelm Jr. of Hebron; Edward of Minot: August of
Moorhead, Minnesota.

FRED K. AND KATHERINA FLEMMER

Frederick was born September 24, 1898 at Gleuckstahl, S. Russia to Jacob and
Fredericka, Jakober, Flemmer. He was the fifth child in a family of 11
children. He lived in Russia until he was seven years old. He remembers the
trip over on the Kaiser Wilhelm II very well, for never did he see so many sick
people gathered together at one time.

His first few years in America were spent herding cattle either for his father
or for neighbors who would hire him by the month. When he was about 14, the
first school was opened near the Flemmer homestead, and Fred attended for a
total of 60 days. Other than what instruction he received at Confirmation
School and Church School this was the sum total of Fred's formal education. He
worked on his father's farm until he was twenty-one. He went to work in the
Lucky Strike Coal Mine near Zap for one year and then returned to the family
farm.

Katherina Voegele was born at Gleuckstahl, S. Russia on August 18, 1902. She
came to America with her parents, Michael and Fredericka Voegele when she was
three years old. When Katherina was twelve the first school was opened near
their farm. She attended only a short time, most of her formal education was
also gotten at German Bible School and Confirmation School. After her
confirmation at 13, Katie "worked out" at various places as a baby sitter or
hired girl (whenever her mother could spare her.) When the Voegele's moved to
Beulah in 1919 Katie found there was much work to be had in this small thriving
community. She worked at Ida Hamilton's Cafe and Boarding House for about a
year, she then went to work for the Ted Muhlhauser's in the Rex Hotel.
Katherina and Fred were married on December 4, 1921 at the Zion Lutheran Church
with Rev. Markus Baudler officiating. They lived on the farm until the fall of
1923. Here their older daughter Gertrude was born December 20, 1922.

The Flemmers moved into town on October 25, 1923. Fred had decided to go into
business for himself. They planned on starting a trucking and dray business,
for they were the proud owners of a 1921 Model T Truck. Six days after they had
moved to town was Halloween, and fearing that some prankster might damage their
precious truck, they stored it in Copeland's Garage. Tragedy struck! for that
nite the garage burned to the ground. It wasn't insured and neither was their
truck, and they hadn't even paid for the new tires. After this setback they had
to look for another means of livelihood. On December 4, 1923 they signed with
the Northern Produce Creamery. They were now in the cream buying business.
Fred's first month's cream commission check was $1.50. This could hardly be
called a thriving business, and they did have the bad habit of eating, so Katie
called up the Mine Office and left word that if any miners wanted their laundry
done she would be glad to oblige. Fred's cousin Dave Hettick was their star
boarder while he worked at the mine. Soon business at Fred's Cream Station
picked up and pick up it did, so much so that on the 4th of July, 1924 they had
gotten so much cream that the floor in their little cream station broke down.
Early in 1925 they built the main half of the present Jack and Jill Store. They
started a little country Grocery Store (cider in a barrel, bananas hanging from
the ceiling, a pickle barrel, "self-service" was unheard of). It was about this
time that they started to buy junk, hides and furs. They would also buy poultry
live and sell them dressed to Armour and Co. After store hours Fred and Katie
would dress as many as eighty in a night. Their second daughter, Ottilde
Katherine, was born July 22, 1926.

In 1927 they built on another addition--a five room apartment in the rear of
the store. They expanded their merchandise line to include; caps, sweaters,
socks, and Crosley Radios; which Ronnie Slupe always advertised as the best
radios in town cuz you could get 2 stations at the same time. In 1928, a 13
year old niece of Katie's, Bertha Voegele, came to live with the Flemmer's. She
was a combination hired girl and clerk for which she received $12.00 a month
plus board and room. This was considered good wages for a young girl.

In 1929 the Beulah Independent came out with an Ad that Fred Flemmer bought a
carload of apples and had a "special on." This was the first car-load sold to
one individual grocer on the North Branch. Much to everyone's surprise and the
family's relief (for there were apple boxes stored all over the place) they
were all sold.

The year 1930 saw another phase of business started. They bought two trucks and
started a daily cream route from Grassy Butte to Mandan (following the old MKM
trail). There was very little gravel on the road and many times they would
become bogged down in the mud. The drivers would have to drive in shifts. Jake
Herman would drive as far as Beulah with a truckload and Christ F. Voegele
would drive the rest of the route to Mandan. Jake would take the 2nd truck back
to Grassy Butte and start over.

In June 1932 they leased the East-end Service Station. This was run by Albert
Schwagler. In August of that same year Fred started the Standard Bulk Station
and hired a young man from Carson (Reine Blumhardt) to drive the gas truck and
do the bookkeeping. (He was the Flemmers bookkeeper for the next 19 years). The
bookkeeping was done in back of the cream station where they also candled eggs
and stored many miscellaneous items.

Fred's cream station was the setting for many a checker game. While the women
sat on a bench in front of the Wear-U-Well shoe department and did their weekly
visiting, the men were either playing or kibitzing a game of checkers. This
bench served many purposes, here the shoe customers would sit when they tried
on shoes, or if it was lunch time our rural customers would go to the kitchen
and get a glass for cider, buy a hunk of sausage and a box of crackers and the
family would "dine out". On Saturday nite the bench was its' busiest for
everyone came to town, the young people would be visiting friends out on Main
Street and the women and smaller children would be using the bench and all the
chairs the Flemmers owned. By the time everyone went home there would be
sunflower seed shells an inch thick on the floor. These were the davs when
stores would be open until eleven or twelve every nite.

In 1933 our hired help unionized; as Bertha and Reine became united in Holy
Wedlock. This was also the year that the Flemmers built a new Service Station
on the corner where the present Ford garage is located. This station was the
center of many a heated political discussion. Here again checkers was played
with fervor. This led to the County invitational Checker Tournament; some of
the local checker champs were: Lew and Don Voeltz, Bert H. Poffenberger and
Fred Flemmer.

        Service Station and First Garage

In 1933 Fred started selling cars. He had the Plymouth and Dodge franchise. He
sold the first car, a Plymouth, to Rev. S. Joachim. As Fred became more
involved in the automobile and gas business the Flemmers decided to lease their
store to Bill and Abbie Froelich. They moved out of the store in October of
1934. This was quite an adjustment for Katie for she missed all the sociability
of their little country store.

In 1935 they built the first garage. This is still being used by Spier Sales
and Service as a warehouse. Fred was still buying junk and this was the year
that he shipped 23 carloads of scrap iron.

In 1937 the Flemmers dropped the Plymouth and Dodge franchise, bought out Frank
Pitzer's Ford garage, added a showroom to the garage and sold Fords from there
on. Fred sold the first Ford to Albert Lauf. In 1938 he dropped Standard Oil
and switched to Phillips 66 Gas and Oil (Independent dealer).

In 1939 they built the present Post Office building. They tore down the old
Jim's Cafe building and put up the new building in less than two months (the
excavating of the basement was done by pick and shovel).

When World War II began, (December 7, 1941), business changed overnite. Cars
were pulled off the market for the duration. Gas was sold to those who had
stamps as were tires. All of a sudden Uncle Sam was looking over your shoulder
watching every move you made and making sure that only those with ration stamps
would be buying.

Edwin Jose who was operating the local Gamble Store was drafted and was forced
to sell his store--and being that the automobile business was almost
nonexistent, Flemmer Sales and Service branched out again. The Gamble Store
stock was bought and put in the now vacant garage building. In January of 1943
Gertie and her husband, Bud Buscher, took over the management of the hardware
store.

In April Bud enlisted in the Merchant Marines and was sent to Sheepshead Bay,
New York. The following June Gertie (like so many service men's wives) joined
her husband at New York. It was at this time that Economy Hardware was
purchased from Irv. J. Niedermeyer and combined with the Gamble Store. The two
combined stores were moved over to the old R.C.U. building and became known as
the Corner Hardware. When Bud was sent overseas Gertie came home. On October
29, 1944 the Flemmers first grandchild, Mary Kay, was born. Bud was out at sea
at the time and didn't receive notification of his daughter's birth until she
was 2 1/2 months old. This was wartime and now there were four generations
living under one roof at the Flemmer home. Grandfather Voegele, The Flemmers,
their 2 daughters and a granddaughter. They were fighting their own little
battles on the home front. Great grandfather was a spry old gent of 84 years
and proved to be quite a reliable playmate and sitter for Mary Kay.

HURRAY!: 1945 saw an end to World War II and Bud came home that fall and took
over the management of the Corner Hardware. Building had been at a standstill
during the wartime years so living quarters were at a premium. The Buschers had
to get a priority permit to build their house (1946). Now the Geo. Bentz
residence.

Business activity really began to pick up now and slowly everything was put
back on the market. The first Ford sold after the war by Flemmer Sales &
Service, was to a Veteran with a priority--Jess Thompson.

In 1947 the present Ford Garage was built and business went on at a steady
pace. Bud and Reine in the Parts Dept. and Nick Fetch at the Gas Station.
During the next years two members were added to the family circle: Gertie
presented the Flemmers with their second grandchild Linda Rae (born May 19,
1950) and Tillie presented them with a new son-in-law, Douglas Dettmann (1951).
In June 1951 Reine Blumhardt suffered a mild heart attack and had to resign
from Flemmer Sales and Service after having worked for the Flemmers for
nineteen years. He moved to Longview, Washington, where he and his family still
reside.

In April 1952 Henry Spier bought the Ford Garage. Buschers moved to
Thermopolis, Wyo. Doug taught Vet's Agr. and later managed the Corner Hardware.
On March 11, 1953 the Flemmer's first grandson, Michael Dettmann was born.

On May 15, 1955 the Dettmanns, Flemmers and Bob Keoghs bought the Bank of
Beulah. In July of the same year Flemmer Sales and Service Inc. was dissolved
when they sold the farm to Otto Reich and later the Corner Hardware to Helmuth
and Helen Hilz. This was the year the youngest grandchild was born, Kathleen
Ann Dettmann (July 22, 1955).

Doug and Fred still work together at the Bank. They leased some land west of
Beulah where they raise Angus cattle. Their right hand man at the ranch is Jake
Wittmaier, who has worked for Fred for 19 years.

Fred was City mayor for eight years and chairman of the Joachim Memorial Home
for thirteen years. He resigned from this in 1959. He also served on the
organizing board of the Community Nursing Home.

Katie has always been known to have the "green thumb", but the Garrison Dam has
changed all of this. Gardening is no longer her favorite pastime. She has now
become an enthusiastic fisherman and collects rocks for her rock collection.

Looking back over the past 41 years in business in Beulah one can also see some
changes and progress the town has made. The Flemmers are proud of the part they
have played in the early history of the community and prouder still to be a
part of it at the present time.

        Katie and Fred

GERTIE AND BUD BUSCHER

        Bud        Gert         Mary Kay       Linda Rae

Gertie and Bud were married on August 23, 1942 in the Zion Lutheran Church by
Rev. S. Joachim.

During the years they lived in Beulah, they were members of the Zion Lutheran
Church, where Gertie taught Sunday School and Bible School. She was also an
active member of the W.M.F. Bud served on the Church board for several years.
At the same time they also belonged to several Civic Organizations. Bud was a
member of the Beulah Fire Department, Beulah Athletic Club and Commerical Club.
Gertie was a member of Mothers Club.

After having lived in Beulah for 10 years, they moved to Thermopolis, Wyoming.
Here they bought the Rainbow Court. They took an active part in Church and
community affairs. Bud was a member of the Lion's Club, President of Chamber of
Commerce, Mayor of Thermopolis, President of the State Motel Assc. and a
Director of the Nat'l Motel Assc.

Due to Bud's severe heart attack in 1957 the Buscher's were forced to sell the
motel. They bought the motel on Tuesday, July 8, 1952 and sold it Tuesday, July
8, 1958. They moved to Billings and it was while living here that Bud on a
plane enroute to Billings from a business trip to Jackson, Wyo. succumbed to
his last heart attack. (Feb. 5, 1959).

Gertie and the two girls moved to Bismarck, where Gertie has a small Ceramic
Shop. The Steve Bjorklunds (Mary Kay) make their home in Bismarck at the
present time. Linda Rae is a student at Hughes Jr. High.

TILLIE AND DOUG DETTMANN

        Michael, Tillie, Kathleen, Doug

Doug Dettmann was born at Judson, N. Dak. March 22, 1920. The oldest child of
Hugo and Agnes Dettmann. He graduated from the Judson high school in 1937. He
attended Wahpeton State School of Science for two years. He was working in the
Judson Post Office when World War II began. He served in the U.S. Army Signal
Corps attached to the U.S. Air Force, serving in the European Theatre of
operations. After the end of the war Doug took advantage of the G.I. Education
program and went back to school (N.D.S.U.) majoring in Animal Husbandry. He
graduated in 1948.

He came to Beulah in 1948 where he was employed as a Veterans Ag Instructor by
the Beulah School until 1951.

On April 8, 1951 Doug and Tillie were married in the Zion Lutheran Church. Rev.
T. Kammerer officiated.

Tillie is a member of the Beulah Mother's Club and Beulah Womans Club. Doug is
a member of the Beulah Chamber of Commerce (director at present) and Beulah
V.F.W.

The Dettmann's have two children: Michael (age 11) and Kathleen Ann (age 8 1/2).

THE R. A. BLUMHARDT HlSTORY

        Reine and Bertha Blumhardt

Reinhold A. Blumhardt was born on November 23, 1912, the son of August and
Elizabeth, nee Stuckle, Blumhardt. He attended the Carson Public School
graduating in 1930. He attended Concordia College at Moorhead, Minnesota until
ill-health forced him to return home.

On October 3, 1933 Reine came to Beulah to work for F. K. Flemmer as a driver
of the Standard Oil Bulk truck and to be his part-time bookkeeper. Here is
where he met his future wife, Bertha, who was also employed by the Flemmers.

Bertha was the daughter of Michael and Bertha, nee Meske, Voegele. She was born
on February 13, 1914. Her father had passed away when she was but five years
old. She came to live with the Flemmers when she was thirteen years old. She
helped with the household duties but most of the time worked as a clerk in the
store.

Bertha and Reine were married at Carson on June 14, 1934. Reine continued
working for the Flemmers (with the exception of a year or two) for the next 19
years. Reine was stricken with a mild heart attack in 1952 and was advised to
change climates. He resigned and moved to Longview, Washington where they make
their home at the present time.

While at Beulah they were active and faithful members of the Zion Lutheran
Church. Bertha was a member of the German Ladies Aid and the Womens Mission
Circle. Reine taught Sunday School for many years, he was a member of the
Lutheran Brotherhood, and had various Church Offices.

Reine took an active part in the civic affairs of Beulah, he was clerk for the
village of Beulah for about ten years, the Beulah Lions Club 10 years,
Commercial Club 12 years, Athletic Club 4 years, and P.T.A. of which he was
president for a number of years.

The Blumhardts have two daughters. Edith Ann, who married Chuck Sonnenberg,
lives at Vancouver, Washington. They have five children, Michael, Robin, Danny,
Pamela, and Kathleen. Linda lives at home with her parents and is a student at
the Longview High School.

MICHAEL AND FREDERICKA VOEGELE

        Taken 1950--91 years old.        Taken 1918

Michael Voegele was born September 11, 1859 in Glueckstahl, S. Russia to
Michael and Kristina, nee Schanzenback, Voegele. In 1881 he married Elizabeth
Flemmer, daughter of Philip Flemmer. To this union were born two children;
Elizabeth and John. Elizabeth, Michael's first wife, died in 1885.

In 1886 Michael married Fredericka Goetz who was born May 10, 1864 to Karl and
Christina, nee Kolp, Goetz. They had nine children, three of whom died in
infancy. They were Michael, Christiana, Mrs. Jacob Flemmer (deceased) Daniel
(deceased), Christian, Katharina, Mrs. Fred Flemmer, and Jacob.

Michael Voegele was born a man of the soil and died a man of the soil. He had
learned the trade of shoemaking in Russia but he took great pride in his
vineyards and under his expert care they flourished. But he had a large family
and the small plot of land was just not big enough for him to make an adequate
living for him and his family. He could see that this was no future for his
growing boys. When he heard of the 160 acres of free farm land available, he
decided that this was where he should take his family. Gleuckstahl was a small
village which enjoyed a climate similar to that of California. Michael had
visions of acres of vineyards, fruit trees and enough work for all his sons to
make an adequate living. In the fall of 1905 with seedlings from his vineyard
and orchard carefully packed, the Voegele family set sail on the Kaiser Wilhelm
II for the "promised land". It was fall by the time they got here and too late
in the season to build their own sod house, so they had to move in with his
brother's family, the Daniel Voegeles. The Daniel Voegele family lived in one
room while the Michael Voegeles and their 8 children lived in the other. That
winter the eldest daughter Elizabeth decided to marry Henry Becker, Sr., so
that meant one less. Having an old-fashioned German Wedding under such crowded
conditions took quite a bit of maneuvering but somehow they managed to make
enough room and Elizabeth had her wedding with all the neighbors and relatives
participating.

In the spring of 1906 the Michael Voegeles moved to their homestead just a mile
north of Daniel's and life on the N. D. plains began. Michael soon learned that
weather was not like that of Russia (then the bread basket of the world), for
all the plants and seedlings that he so carefully brought over, and nursed
through the winter froze that very first spring. The growing season wasn't
nearly as long nor did it rain as often as one would have liked it to, so
dreams of vineyards and fruit trees were put aside and honest sweat and back
breaking toil began.

On this homestead Peace Lutheran Church actually had its beginning. Long before
a minister had been made available the people would gather here on Sunday and
have their worship services, with Michael reading out of "Das Predig Buch" and
Fredericka keeping an eye on the dinner she had perking on the range. Many
times "Ein Feste Burg Ist Unser Gott" was sung to the tantalizing aroma of
sauerkraut and ham, and when the weather turned bad, there would be more for
dinner than one had expected. People would come to church in whatever mode of
travel they had or could afford; horseback, hayrack, stone-boat, buggy, or even
afoot if they had to. As poor as most of them were, they never came emptyhanded
and they never left empty hearted.

For the first few years there was no school in the immediate area, so the
Voegele children were "hired out" as much as they could and as often as Mike
could spare them. The family was not so large now, Elizabeth was married, John
and Michael Jr. each had a homestead of their own. When they finally did start
a school it was a disappointing experience for both the youngsters and the
teachers, for the pupils couldn't talk English and the teachers couldn't talk
German. It was discouraging and many times both the teacher and the pupils
would sit and cry. The Voegeles (like so many of the other homesteaders) taught
their children to read and write the language that they knew, German. This
practice was kept up in this part of the country as late as in the 1940's.

        The Michael Voegele Family Homestead

Mike and his family moved into Beulah in 1919 for a couple of years. He opened
a Wear-U-Well shoe store. Living in town was not for him so in 1921 he moved
back to the farm. These years on the farm were among the hardest years that
Michael and Fredericka encountered. Death was no stranger here, three of their
youngsters had died in infancy. In 1916 the oldest daughter Elizabeth died of
pneumonia at the age of 31. She was survived by her husband Henry and four
children; Katie, Annie, John and Henry Becker. During the terrible Flu Epidemic
of 1919 their son Michael was taken from this world at the age of 29, leaving
behind his widow, the former Bertha Meske, and three small children; Bertha age
5, Lydia age 4, (deceased) Ann age 2. Then in 1924 another son Daniel was taken
from them by death at the age of 30. Surviving him was his young widow, the
former Louisa Fuchs, and three children Louise age 4, William age 2, and
Pauline age 7 montbs.

In the early thirties they started farming with their youngest son Jacob. They
built a new and smaller house here on the farm, and lived on their homestead
until the death of Fredericka on March 10, 1937. After the death of his wife
Michael made his home, for the greater share of the time, with his youngest
daughter Katherina, Mrs. Fred K. Flemmer. He died on August 18, 1955, one month
short of being 96 years of age. He had always been in good health and was spry
and alert until the last month, when he suffered a stroke. Even at 95 he walked
erect and sure-footed. He was strong in body and spirit. A deeply religious
man, he kept his strong religious convictions until the day he died. At the
time of his death five of his children were still living; John who died in
1958; Christiana, Mrs. Jacob Flemmer, died in 1960; Christ who lives in Beulah;
Katherina, Mrs. Fred K. Flemmer of Beulah; and Jacob who makes his home at
Beulah, and 35 grandchildren and 52 great grandchildren and 2 great great
grandchildren.

On August 22, 1955 Michael was laid to rest at the Peace Lutheran Church
Cemetery fifteen miles south of Beulah.

MR. AND MRS. EMANUEL HETH

        Mr. and Mrs. Heth--on their 50th Anniversary (1962)

Emanuel Heth was born on September 11, 1888 at Alt Arzis, South Russia, to
Michael and Luisa; nee Mackus; Heth. There were seven brothers in the Heth
family. In 1909 Emanuel left the "old Country" alone and ventured to America.
His father and brother Wm. came over later on. He worked in the Krem area for
awhile and then in 1910 took up an 80 acre homestead five miles northeast of
Beulah. Here he built a 10 x 20 frame house. That first winter he shared this
home with his Mare, who was with foal. Horses were a precious commodity and due
to the cold weather he was afraid of losing her and her foal. His house was the
only place where she would be protected from the elements.

Emilie Heine was born February 8, 1890, at Klostitz, South Russia to Jacob and
Wilhelmenia, nee Yanke, Heine. She came to the United States with her parents
and family in 1894. The Heine's and their 10 children homesteaded near Krem.

Emilie and Emanuel were married on January 12, 1912, by Pastor August Schult.
After their marriage they went to live on Emanuel's homestead until 1917, when
they bought a different farm eight miles north of Beulah. Here they raised
their children to adulthood, giving them a solid Christian foundation. The
Church was the center of the Heth family life. They were Charter Members of the
Zion Lutheran Church of which they are still active members. Mrs. Heth was very
active in the German Ladies Aid until it dissolved. Mr. Heth has served various
times as deacon, on the council and later as custodian of the Church for 9
years.

The Emanuel Heths were blessed with four children: Herbert married to Eva
Fetch, lives in Williston. They have three children; Shirley, Gary and Donald.
Otto died as an infant. Paul is married to Mildred Wiedenmeyer and lives in
Spokane, Washington. They have six children: Bruce, Bernita, Mary Ann, Kenneth,
Robert (Bobby), and Debora Kay. Olga, Mrs. George Weiss, lives in Williston.
They have five children: Kathleen, Karold, Tom, Douglas and Nancy.

The Heths retired from farming in 1945, when they moved into Beulah. They sold
their farm two years later. After moving to town, Mr. Heth kept on working; on
county roadwork, custodian for the church, and remodeling houses. Ill health
has forced him into complete retirement these last three years.

The Heths feel that they have been especially blessed because: they had no more
than the average amount of sickness in the family; their trials were no greater
than those of their neighbors and friends; and although their health has failed
them in these later years, their faith in their Heavenly Father has never been
stronger.

JOHN AND CHRISTINA VOEGELE

John Voegele was born in Gleuckstahl, South Russia on October 17, 1884, a son
of Michael and Elizabeth, nee Flemmer, Voegele. Two children were born to this
union, John and Elizabeth. Their mother died when John was two years old. Later
Michael married Fredericka Goetz. This union was blessed with six children,
which added four stepbrothers and two stepsisters to John's life.

As a young man of 20, John desired to join some of his friends in the "new
country", so he and his sister Elizabeth left their parental home to come to
America. They made the journey over with their Uncle Daniel and his family.
John found employment on a sheep ranch in the Otter Creek area and later on at
various farms in the Blue Grass vicinity.

In 1905 he took up a homestead 15 miles south of Beulah where he turned the sod
by following a horse-drawn hand plow. On December 31, 1908, he was married to
Christina Stauss at the Friedens Congregational Church at Blue Grass. He
brought his bride to the little sod house on the homestead where they lived
till 1915 when the sod shanty was replaced by a wooden structure.

Christina Stauss was born January 9, 1890, at Tolstoy, S. Dakota, a daughter of
Jacob and Christiana, nee Whittmaier, Stauss. Christina was 2 months old when
the family moved to the vicinity of Ashley, North Dakota. When she was nine
years old her folks made another move, this time to Blue Grass, N. Dak. Here
Christina grew to womanhood along with seven sisters and one brother.

John and Christina became parents of two sons and five daughters, namely:
Edward, Minneapolis, Minn.; Alex, Beulah; Emma, Mrs. Fred G. Kessler, Lincoln,
Nebraska; Bertha Voegele, Beulah; Ella, Mrs. Ralph Murray, Zap; Irene, Mrs.
Raymond Cherrey, Williston, N. Dak.; Elsie, Mrs. Walter Baier, Beaver Bay,
Minn. To date the family has increased to sixteen grandchildren and twelve
great-grandchildren.

John and Christina worked hard, weathering many storms. The drought of the 30's
brought many anxious moments; little feed for the few head of cattle they had
left, and even less water; nevertheless, their pioneer spirit kept them going.

They were members of the Neu Gleuckstahl Congregational Church south of Beulah.
Christina passed out of this earthly abode very suddenly of a heart attack on
September 9, 1940. After her death, John lived his remaining years on the
original homestead with his son, Alex, his wife Ida, and their three girls. His
daughter Bertha also made her home with them. John passed away March 9, 1958,
at the Hazen Hospital after a lingering illness.

JACOB AND EMILIEA VOEGELE

        Standing: Leonard and Lawrence
        Jake, Lugale, Emiliea

Jacob Voegele was born in Gleuckstahl, South Russia, the son of Michael and
Fredericka Voegele. When he was nine months old his parents immigrated to
America. They settled on a homestead fourteen miles south of Beulah. This was
to be Jake's home for the next 58 years. Jake recalls the years when all the
"gay young blades" of the South Beaver Creek Community would gather at the
Fuchs farm every Sunday afternoon and play baseball. All of the farm
communities would have their team and they would have regular games with one
another. There was quite a bit of competitive spirit amongst these communities
so they always had a large crowd in attendance. Jake played on the team for
quite a number of years. This love of baseball was passed onto his sons,
Leonard and Lawrence. They both played on the Beulah Baseball teams.

Emiliea Welk was born in Delmont, South Dakota, the daughter of Fred and
Caroline, nee Holtzwart, Welk. She is an identical twin (her twin is Mrs. Matt
Fischer).

The Fred Welk's Sr. came to North Dakota in 1916, settling in the South Beaver
Creek Area. Emiliea attended Ash School and remembers how their teacher, Orrin
Abbey, would ride to school on horseback. For his lunch he usually brought only
a bag of apples. Apples were rather a rarity in these parts, so the children
would trade their sandwiches, cookies or other homebaked goodies for an apple.
This worked out very well--Orrin, a bachelor, wouldn't have to fix his own
lunch and the kids all had apples.

Emiliea and Jake were married on September 28, 1930 at the Peace Lutheran
Church with Rev. Em. Fuchs officiating. They both became active members at
Peace--as they sang in the choir for fourteen years and Jake was the church
organist for the same amount of time. Jake also served in various offices of
the church.

The Voegeles rented Jake's father's farm until 1942, when they bought it. They
farmed until 1959 when they sold it and moved into Beulah. At the present time
Jake is employed as a janitor in the Beulah School System. The Voegeles have
three children: Leonard, Lawrence and Lugale, Mrs. Jim Backlin. They have five
grandchildren.

MR. AND MRS. DANIEL VOEGELE SR.

        Daniel and Rosina Voegele
        Christ, Rose, Daniel Jr.

Daniel Voegele was born on June 14, 1876 to Michael and Christina, nee
Schanzenbach, Voegele at Gleuckstahl, S. Russia. Rosina Bieber was born on Feh.
27, 1882 a daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Christian Bieber. Daniel and Rosina were
married at Gleuckstahl on Feb. 4, 1903.

They left Russia on April 25, 1905. They went to Bremer, Germany where they
boarded the ship, Wilhelm the Great. They arrived at Glen Ullin, N. Dak. on May
25, 1905 by train. Glen Ullin, at that time, was very small; there were only a
few houses and a couple of stores. John Heinle took them by buggy (this was the
only way of transportation) to Daniel Voegele's brother Frederick's homestead,
where they stayed for sometime until some more friends arrived from Russia so
to make room they stayed at Peter Kessler's for the next 2 weeks until their
first sod house was finished. This sod house had two rooms but in the fall they
added another large room and this was used as a barn. The Dan Voegeles
homestead was located on S. W. 1/2 of Sec. 2-141-88. The Voegeles had no
furniture, so as soon as their house was finished they went to Glen Ullin with
the wagon and bought a bed, table and three chairs, a range and a trunk.

They did not seed the first year as it was too late in the season, so Daniel
worked the first summer as a farm laborer for Jacob Bickel in the Blue Grass
area. This left Rosina home alone all week with one child. In the fall Daniel's
brother Michael and his family came from Russia and moved in with them. They
shared their two-room house with the Michael Voegele's that whole winter.

They seeded their first crop in 1906. Daniel plowed with a hand plow, while
Rosina cleared the rocks off the fields with a stone boat.

For their first crop they seeded 10 acres of oats, 15 acres of barley and about
50 acres of flax. They had a fair crop and the grain was all sacked and hauled
to Glen Ullin with a wagon. The Voegele's first livestock was three horses,
four cows, which supplied milk and butter for the table, (they also sold some
butter to buy other groceries). A friend gave them a cluck with some baby
chicks. They shared the sod house with the cluck and her chicks and finally the
cluck supplied them with an egg a day, which she laid in the middle of their
bed.

The first winter was very cold and lucky for them everyone was in good health
or they may not have survived. The long winter evenings were spent in driving
to the neighbors with a stoneboat, visiting about the "old country", and
singing their favorite German Hymns. Neighbors were not only neighbors but also
friends and whenever help was needed there was always someone ready to help,
and they expected nothing in payment.

The marriage of Daniel and Rosina Voegele was blessed with ten children, two of
whom died in infancy. The children still living are: Rosa married John W.
Flemmer (deceased) and lives in Beulah; Christ married Ida Flemmer (deceased)
and is now married to the former Katie Lesslie. He owns and operates the Beulah
Hide and Fur. Four of the Voegele brothers: Ferdinand, Ed, Fred and Daniel live
on farms within a five mile radius. Ferdinand is married to the former Pauline
Schmidt; Ed is married to the former Hilda Flemmer; Fred is married to the
former Annie Flemmer; Otto is married to the former Ella Schmidt, he lives in
Beulah and does construction work; Annie, Mrs. Adolph Mittelsteadt lives in
Glendive, Montana, where he is employed as a trucker.

        Standing--Left to Right: John, Bertha, Alvena, William, Martha, Anna.
        Seated--Left to Right: Helen, Mr. Unruh, Dale, Mrs. Unruh, Hilda.

MR. AND MRS. JOHN UNRUH

John, son of Mr. and Mrs. Nicholus Unruh, was born in South Russia and
immigrated to the United States with his parents, three brothers and two
sisters, in May of 1906.

Mrs. Unruh (Katherina Voegele) eldest daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Christ Voegele
also immigrated to the U.S. with her parents in the same year. Both the Unruh
and Voegele families homesteaded in Mercer County.

In 1912 John and Katherina were married. They farmed in southwest Mercer County
for some years.

John, with his brothers, Ludwig and Jake, owned and operated a threshing rig,
which they purchased in 1912. Each fall they would thresh their own crops,
before starting out to do threshing for others in the area. Some years they
didn't get their rig home until late November, when the last of their jobs were
done. Time was too--when snow had to be moved before they could get the machine
to the stacks for threshing. It took three men to operate the rig. Louie ran
the engine, John the separator and Jake was both waterboy and fireman. It took
two hours to get steam up before the days threshing operations could begin, so
it was "up early" each morning for John and Louie.

The John Unruhs moved to Beulah in April of 1920. They bought property and
built theur home. Mr. Unruh, however, continued to operate the threshing rig
for some time after moving to Beulah.

Mr. and Mrs. Unruh were the parents of 10 children: William, (Bill) married
Naomi David, Beulah; Bertha, (Mrs. Sam Schmidt) Medina, N. D.; Alvena (Mrs. Art
Rabial) Moses Lake, Wash.; Martha (Mrs. Tyler Mason) Minot, N. D.; Anna (Mrs.
Dave Hausauer) Moses Lake, Wash.; John--married Shirley Bloyt, Jamestown, N.
D.; Hilda, (Mrs. F. Herman) New Salem, N. D.; Helen (Mrs. Reuben Weiss) Arvida,
Colo. and Dale--married Maylo Schulz, Arvida, Colo.; Paul, deceased. They have
12 grandchildren and 3 great-grandchildren. Mrs. Unruh passed away in August,
1956. Since that time John has sold the family home and lives with his children.

The Unruh home saw much activity--what with 10 children of their own and
neighborhood friends. A vacant lot nearby was used for softball games in
summer, with 3 or 4 of the Unruh children participating. In winter, sled riding
on the Joos hill was the chief source of entertainment--whether on a sled or
piece of tin.

John amused his and the neighborhood children with stories about his early days
on the farm. His "perch" for storytelling was the oven door of the range--with
the eager children sitting cross-legged on the kitchen floor. This "tall tale"
was a favorite: "One hot day in August he started to Beulah with a load of
popcorn. It was so hot and the road so long, that before he reached town the
corn began to pop. As the heat became more intense, the corn popped faster.
Soon it started overflowing the wagon box, so the team, wagon, road, horses and
himself were engulfed in a sea of popcorn. When the horses saw all that
popcorn, they thought it was snow, and just laid down and froze to death!"

THE ALBERT WINKLER FAMILY HISTORY

        Standing--Left to right: Edwin, John, Paul, Martha (Mrs. Fulton
        Fischer), Reuben, Gust, Henry, and Herbert. Seated--Left to right:
        Albert Jr., Lydia, Albert Winkler Sr. and Eva Winkler, Hilda, Mrs.
        Leonard Krausz.

Albert Winkler Sr. was born March 18, 1883 in Chicago. He married Eva Nies on
April 3, 1906. Mrs. Winkler was born September 16, 1888 in Ashley, North
Dakota. Her parents Elizabeth and Friedrick had immigrated from Russia in 18?.

Albert and Eva homesteaded thirteen miles southwest of Beulah in 1907. They
lived on the original homestead (adding additional land, as times got better)
until they retired in 1948. They purchased a home in Beulah and moved into town.

Albert Winkler Sr. died March 2, 1956. Mrs. Winkler is still a resident of
Beulah. She is a member of the E.U.B. Church of Beulah.

Ten of the Winkler children are still living. Two of the daughters have passed
away. Emilia when she was three and Lvdia in 1952.

John Winkler, born June 5, 1911, married Pauline Miller December 13, 1932. To
this union were born five sons; Wilfred, Norman, Arnold, Leroy, and Howard.
John farms about two miles from the home place.

Martha Winkler, born August 11, 1913, married Fulton Fischer Sept. 6, 1936.
They have a daughter and son, Connie Mae and Gaylen. They farm about a mile
north of Beulah.

Henry Winkler, born March 11, 1917, married Laura Stuber May 17, 1945. One
daughter, Linda, died as an infant. They have one son, Lyle. They farm
southwest of Beulah.

Gust Winkler, born May 15, 1919 married Myrle Hannon July 15, 1944. They are
living at Stanton at the present time. He is a Millwright.

Paul Winkler, born October 22, 1921, married Elma Stuber June 27, 1948. They
have a daughter, Paulette, and a son, Perry. They live on the Winkler home farm.

Hilda Winkler, born January 12, 1924, married Leonard Krausz August 30, 1943.
Six children were born to this union; Shirley, Richard, Dianne, Judy, Donna Mae
and Yvonne. They farm three miles east of Beulah.

Reuben Winkler, born June 11, 1925, married Helen Luithle June 29, 1952. They
have three sons; Larry, Gary, and Rodney. They farm twenty-five miles south of
Hebron.

Edwin Winkler, was born November 24, 1926. He farms the home place with Paul.

Albert Winkler, Jr., was born July 14, 1930, married Florence Lennick, December
26, 1956. They have two daughters, Debbie and Carla, and two sons Russel and
Kenneth. He works at the mine and lives in Beulah.

Herbert Winkler, was born September 11, 1932. He lives with his mother in
Beulah and works at the mine.

        The Winkler Original Homestead

MR. AND MRS. JOHN RICHTER

Johm W. Richter, son of John Charles and Susan Richter was born on September
19, 1892. The family moved to North Dakota when he was a boy and settled at
Esmond. They later moved to Center, North Dakota where on Dec. 12, 1918 he
married Ida Wetzel, daughter of John H. and Ida (Miller) Wetzel. She was born
at Canton, S. Dak. on November 30, 1899.

After their marriage the Richters lived in the Center and Stanton area, until
1920 when they moved to Beulah. Mr. Richter worked in the Beulah Coal Mine for
a while and in 1923 he went to work at the Zap Strip Mine. Aside from mining he
also did some farming. He took great pride in his horses, many of which he
would work at the mine.

In 1948 they moved their house from the Strip Mine into Beulah. In 1951 Mr.
Richter retired from mining. He was the first miner to draw his retirement
pension from the Zap Mine.

Four daughters were born to the Richters: Francis, Mrs. Ed. Stuhr, Glendive,
Montana; Vernice, Mrs. Loren Fanks, Greenacres, Washington; Pearl, Mrs. Willie
Krecklau, Beulah; and Judith, Mrs. Bill Keller, Beulah.

Mr. Richter passed away on November S, 1953. On June 26, 1960 Mrs. Richter
married David Keller. They lived in Beulah until she passed away on October 31,
1962.

JOHN H. WETZEL FAMILY

John H. Wetzel, son of William and Katherine (Wolver) Wetzel was born June 3,
1869 in Penn. and moved with his family to Milford, Illinois.

On Sept. 6, 1893 he married Ida Miller at Milford. They lived in Illinois and
S. Dakota before coming to N. Dak. in 1906. The family spent the first winter
just north of Glen Ullin, while Mr. Wetzel was building the house at their
homestead, five miles northwest of Beulah.

Mr. Wetzel owned one of the first threshing machines in the area and traveled
great distances threshing for farmers each fall. At times he also served as
local undertaker, making many trips to Glen Ullin to purchase coffins. They
later moved into Beulah.

To this family were born fifteen children: William, Katerine (deceased), John,
Bismarck; Ida (deceased); Harry, Beulah; Lem, Lakeside, Calif.; Emma, Salem,
Ore.; Nelson (Lew) Bismarck; Ellen, Bismarck; Mary (deceased); Susie, Seattle,
Wash.; Frank (deceased); Charlie, San Diego, Calif.; Lillian (deceased) and
Helen at Portland, Oregon.

Mrs. Wetzel passed away on December 15, 1936 and Mr. Wetzel passed away on
October 5, 1948.

MR. AND MRS. HARRY WETZEL

Harry E. Wetzel, a son of John H. and Ida (Miller) Wetzel, was born on July 22,
1901 at Canton, South Dakota He came to North Dakota with his parents in 1906.
He spent his boyhood days on his fathers homestead west of Beulah.

As a young man he worked in the local coal mmes. In 1920 he worked in the
lower-level underground mine at Beulah. In 1923 he went to work at the Zap
Strip Mine and except for a brief period in the 1920's has been there ever
since. He now holds the record of being there longer than any other man on the
present payroll.

On July 31, 1925, he married Frances Werner. Frances was born at Elmore,
Minnesota on June 15, 1898. She is the daughter of Henry and Susan (Strong)
Werner, who were also some of our early settlers. They came to North Dakota in
1902, homesteading four miles east of Beulah. For many years Mr. Werner bought
cattle and horses in this area.

Henry Werner was born at Blue Earth, Minn. on March 21, 1869 and Susan, Strong,
Werner was born on July 31, 1872, also at Blue Earth. They were united in
marriage in 1893.

The Wetzels lived at the Dakota Colleries Mine Camp near Zap, until the
Camp-site was closed in 1950. They moved to Beulah in June, 1950, where they
are living at the present time, and are active members of the E. U. B. Church.
The Harry Wetzel's were blessed with four sons: Dale is a Postmaster at
Broadview, Montana. He married Thelma Dickey, their children are; Donna, Terry,
and Greg. Donald is the District Superintendent of M.D.U. at Beulah. He married
Lenora Abbey, they have four children; Kay, Barry, Kim, and Marsha. Robert is a
Results Engineer at the M.D.U. Plant at Mandan. He married Norma Herdebue, they
have one son, Kyle. James is a Lt. Commander of the U.S. Navy Air Force. He
married Janice Ecker and have a daughter, Jennifer.

As a hobby Mr. Wetzel collects antiques and old relics, he has accumulated
quite a collection through the years. The Wetzels are also avid fishermen, and
spend many pleasant hours at the lake trying to catch the "big ones".

        The John Wetzel Threshing Rig

CHARLES SEBASTIAN NIEDERMEYER

Charles S. Niedermeyer was born in Montfort, Wisconsin, August 8, 1887, a son
of Charles and Mary Niedermeyer. In his freshman year he quit school in Prairie
du Chein. He went to work for his folks helping on the farm and in the store
until 1915. He then went to work in a store in Minn. Later that year he came to
North Dakota and did farm work at Leeds, North Dakota.

In 1916 he started farming N. W. of Beulah. A year later he was joined by his
parents, Mr. and Mrs. Charles Niedermeyer Sr. He was inducted into the army in
1918 and was stationed at Camp Grant, Texas, where he received his basic
training in preparation of overseas duty. After the signing of the Armistice
Charles was honorably discharged on December 23, 1918.

On September 24, 1919 Charles Niedermeyer and Miss Katherine Barlow of Washburn
were united in Holy Matrimony. The marriage rites were performed by the Rev.
Father F. X. Braeunig, at the St. Paul's Church at Halliday.

The couple made the Niedermeyer farm their home until Charlie became too ill
(October, 1937) to carry on. He entered Hines Veteran's Hospital, near Maywood,
Illinois, where he spent the remainder of his days on this earth. He passed
away on November 1, 1938. The Rev. Father Schweitzer conducted both of the
religious services at the St. Joseph's Church and Cemetery on November 5, 1938.
The Argonne Post of the Beulah American Legion conducted the Military rites.

Charles was always interested in local and civic affairs, as well as politics
on a local and state level. Charles served on the school board, the election
board, as Democratic precinct man and for a time on the Welfare Board. He was
always a champion for good gov't. He belonged to the American Legion, the
Modern Woodmen of America Lodge, The Farmers Union and the Catholic Church. He
and the County Agent, Mr. Grover Poe combined forces to help modernize his
farming operations. This was hampered somewhat by Charles's limited budget, but
it enabled Charles to pay his way as he went and remain solvent, in spite of
the depression after World War I which was followed by the years of drought.

After Charles's death Katherine ran the farm for two years without much
success, therefore she sold it.

Previous to her marriage, she had taught school, (serving as Principal of the
grade school) in Beulah for 2 years. Katherine now went back to teaching. She
taught in the rural schools for six years. In order to advance her education
she would attend summer school. On August 2, 1946 she was graduated from the
Valley City State Teachers College with a degree of Bachelor of Arts in
Education. Hoping for more advancement, she took ten weeks of Post-graduate
work at the University of Colorado, at Boulder in the summer of 1947. She
taught for twelve years in the high school of Washington, Montana, and North
Dakota. She retired in 1957. She now owns and operates a trailer court and
apartment house in Beulah.

Katherine Niedermeyer is a member of the St. Joseph's Catholic Church of
Beulah, Confraternity of the Royal Neighbors, and in 1942 joined the Gamma
Theta Upsilon a National Professional Geography Fraternity, Kappa Chapter,
State Teachers College, Valley City.

In 1944 she was acting Mercer County Superintendent of Schools for about ten
months at Stanton, North Dakota. She labored in the teaching field for a total
of 28 years.

CHARLES AND MARY ALTMAIER NIEDERMEYER

        Mary, Charles Niedermeyer and sister Lizzie

The Charles Niedermeyer Sr.'s came to Mercer County from Wisconsin in 1917.
They lived on a farm north of Beulah until 1919, when they moved into Beulah.
They operated a shoe store and shoe repair shop until Charles Sr. passed away
on Dec. 24, 1926. Mary operated the store which later became a hardware store
until her retirement in 1930. She passed away on Sept. 24, 1934.

        Alma, John Jr., Leona
        John and Fredericka Wolff

JOHN AND FREDERICKA WOLFF

John Wolff was born June 14, 1891, in Neufridenstahl, So. Russia to Christian
and Margarita, nee Schnabel, Wolff. His mother died when he was 2 years old.
His father married a second time to Elizabeth Rau.

The Christian Wolffs immigrated to America and arrived at Winnipeg, Canada.
They came to Mercer County in June, 1911 and took up a homestead 10 miles south
of Golden Valley. As funds were low and no income was forthcoming, John went to
work on various farms and ranches in order to help with the family finances.

Fredericka Retzer was born July 3, 1894 in Gleuckstahl, South Russia to John
and Christina, nee Henger, Retzer. She came from a family of 11 children, one
of whom is still living.

John and Fredericka were married on March 4, 1916 at Golden Valley, by Pastor
Bertsch. This was not as they had planned. They were supposed to have gotten
married in the Peace Lutheran Church, south of Beulah; but the Knife River had
flooded its banks and the pastor couldn't come out to perform the marriage
rites. A large wedding feast had been prepared. All the guest and relatives
arrived so they had the "wedding" anyway. When it was over, they went to Golden
Valley and were married. To this union were born 5 children--Edward who died
when he was 9 years old, Alma, Mrs. Albert Kusler, Johnny married to Olga
Hintz, they have 1 child, Vonnie, Christ who died as an infant and Leona, Mrs.
Christ Zuren, they have a daughter, Janice.

The Wolffs rented farmland until 1918 when they bought a farm 9 miles south of
Beulah and this is still the John Wolff farm. There were good years on the farm
but the hardships of dry years and poor prices of the 1930's will not be easily
forgotten. They moved to Beulah in 1946. Fredericka had been in ill health for
27 years, during which time she was in and out of the hospital 42 times. She
never complained. Because of her firm belief in the Lord she remained His
cheerful and faithful servant to the very end. Her last words were spoken in
prayer as she asked to be relieved of this world and be taken Home and as she
whispered "Amen" she passed from this life into the next on Sept. 13, 1954.
After her death John went to live with his son Johnny until 1956.

On November 28, 1956 John married Mrs. Rozalia Isaak. They now reside in Beulah
where they are members of the Zion Lutheran Church.

        Raymond, Alvin, and Lorna
        Adolf, Marlyn, Rosalia

MR. AND MRS. ADOLF KRUCKENBERG (MRS. JOHN WOLFF)

Adolf Kruckenberg was born in Alt Arzis, Russia on October 30, 1898, the son of
Mr. and Mrs. Emanuel Kruckenberg. In 1910 he came to America with his parents
and the rest of the Kruckenberg family. His mother passed away just two months
after their arrival in this country. About 1912 his father and step-mother took
up a homestead in Mercer County. On January 13, 1924 Adolf married Rosalia
Klaus.

Rosalia Klaus was born February 24, 1902 at Hebron, North Dakota, to John and
Marie, nee Bunk, Klaus. She was one of 3 children. On January 13, 1924, she was
married to Adolf at the St. Paul's Lutheran church by Pastor E. H. Vetter.

The Kruckenbergs farmed east of Halliday for 21 years. Four years before
Adolf's death they purchased his father's farm north of Dodge. They were living
here at the time of his death on May 24, 1950. This union that lasted
twenty-five years was blessed with four children.

Lorna, Mrs. Wm. F. Lindeman, Grants, New Mexico, graduated from Halliday High
School in 1943. She attended D.S.T.C. and taught rural school for nine years.
The Lindeman's have one daughter, Anita.

Alvin, of St. Maries, Idaho, married Ramona Lang. He served in the U.S. Army
from 1945-1946, spending a year in Italy. He is employed as foreman on a
hereford ranch in Idaho. They have two children: Myron and Shirley. Raymond of
Encinitas, California married Faye Mooney and they have one son Ronald. He
served in the U.S. Marine Corps in 1952-1953. He is in business for himself,
doing landscaping, finish grading etc.

Marlyn graduated from the Beulah High School in 1959, where he was very active
in Science. Two years he won a trip to the State Science Fair and one year he
represented N.D. at the National Science Fair at Hartford, Conn. He attended
Wahpeton School of Science, taking a two-year course in electronics. He is now
in the U.S. Navy.

In 1951 Rosalia married Gotthilf Isaak of Golden Valley they lived on a farm
north of Golden Valley until 1953 when they moved into town. Here they lived
until Mr. Isaak's death in 1954.

On November 28, 1956 Rosalia was united in marriage to John Wolff of Beulah.
She and her young son Marlyn then moved to Beulah, where Marlyn finished his
high school education. Mrs. Wollf is active in church work. She is a member of
the Lutheran Women of the Church Society of the Zion Lutheran Church.

THE YEAR OF 1917

The first three men to enlist from Mercer County were Peter W. Reese, Beulah;
  Jacob Silver, Beulah; and Arthur Loy, Stanton.
May 25--Registration for draft began.
Summer--First tennis court was built--on main street east of the German State
  Bank.
August--Mercer County Red Cross was established. Beulah was represented by P.
  S. Chaffee and A. D. Brown.
Fall--Beulah has its first compulsory vaccination of school children.
November 2--the Beulah Coal Mine ships its first load of coal by railroad.
During the year of 1917 approximately $250,000.00 worth of livestock had been
  shipped from Beulah, and $50,000.00 worth of cream was shipped

THE YEAR OF 1926
Mercer County Retail Merchants organized--all stores were to close at six every
  night except Saturday. Mine now averages 1200 tons of coal.
June--Beulah's first Market Day--huge success. Pig calling contest won by
  Ernie Stetson in the men's division and Mrs. C. B. Murray won the womens.
October--Prof. Geo. Kadlec organized the first Juvenile Band.
December--first H. S. Girls B. B. team organized.
Main street consisted of: 11 grocery stores, 5 general stores, 1 drug store, 1
  jewelry store, 1 mens and shoe store, and three confectionary stores.
January--P. S. Chaffee bought a centrifugal pump which was propelled by a gas
  tractor to fill the Skating Pond he built.

C. B. HEINEMEYER

We owe much to the late C. B. Heinemeyer for gathering and compiling much of
our early historical information into booklet form in 1932. We referred to it
many times. He was a great promoter for this area. He also had a tremendous
amount of faith in the potentialities of the lignite coal industries in Mercer
County.

He passed away on June 9, 1940. He had spent 58 years in Mercer County, during
which time he was a school teacher, served on State Legislature, and worked for
the State Land Department. He had two sons; Bertram (deceased) and Raymond of
Bismarck, North Dakota.

City Cafe

This cafe is operated by Leo and DeLoras Fischer and their two children Russell
and Cynthia. Included in this picture is Mrs. Clara Elders who has been working
in this cafe for 16 years. Both Mr. and Mrs. Fischer were born and raised in
North Dakota after spending 13 years in California they bought into the cafe in
July of 1962 and have been working hard to give you the best of service. Thank
you folks of in and around Beulah and surrounding towns.

THE STORY OF THE LITTLE WHITE SCHOOL THAT - - -
THE FIRST BEULAH SCHOOL

This little frame school house was dedicated in 1915. It was the pride and joy
of the community for it was built according to the latest state requirements.
At the time it was built it was part of the Spring Creek School District. In
July 14, 1917 the Beulah School District #27 was organized. The new district
received this building as its share of the Spring Creek School District
property. This building was used by the local district until 1930.

The principal at the time that the district was organized was Steven Faust who
received a yearly salary of $1,000.00.

THE SECOND SCHOOL BUILDING

        THE LEGION HALL

In 1918 the Beulah School was in dire need of additional classrooms, so the
above building was built as more or less a temporary school-house. This was
built at a cost $1695.00. The principal of the school at this time was
Katherine Barlow. (now Mrs. Katherine Niedermeyer). A year later Lawrence
Williams became principal. Abbie Tyrell started teaching primary grades in the
above schoolhouse. She continued to teach in the Beulah School System for a
total of twenty-two years.

THE FIRST BRICK SCHOOL HOUSE

The officials of the school district realized that they needed more room for
their future expansion program. They purchased the entire Block #16 for their
new brick school house. They started building in 1919 (this was the first brick
building in Beulah), and finished in 1920. At the time it was considered quite
elaborate, for it had four class rooms, a small library and an assembly room
(that could be partitioned into two class rooms). Now Beulah School could offer
a four year high school curriculum. In 1923 the Beulah High School graduated
it's first class; Helen Moore (Tuskind) and Lee Perkins. By 1925 this building
was filled to overflowing, and they had to use the old frame buildings again.

FIRST ADDITION

The above addition was added at a cost of $38,000.00 in 1930. Leonhard was the
Architect and J.C. Beattie the Contractor. This new addition added seven new
classrooms, a large assembly room and a large gym which for many years was
considered to be one of the finest in the entire area. This addition proved to
be more than adequate for many years, and the school district was able to go on
without a building program for about nineteen years.

SECOND ADDITION

Following World War II the field of education saw many changes in teaching
methods and in curriculum. Students were taking a greater interest in Science,
more of the rural youngsters were going to high school and the Beulah School
kept pace with the current trends. In 1949 the second addition was built, this
housed the Home E. Dept, the Vocationl Ag. Dept, and a two room Science Dept.
This was built by Chase Construction at the cost of $45,809.00.

THE NEW GYMNASIUM AND AUDITORIUM

The new gym was built in 1953 with a seating capacity of 1200. The gym also
serves as an auditorium with the stage located on the south end of the gym. New
locker rooms and dressing rooms were built in this part of the building. One
half of the old gym was converted into a Music and Band room and the other half
into the Hot Lunch Room.

THE THIRD AND FINAL ADDITION THE PRESENT SCHOOL

The third floor on the west wing and the gym was built in 1953 at a cost to the
taxpayers of $166,287.00. Besides the new gym this added four additional
classrooms for Jr. High and High School use. Ten years went by and the district
was completely out of any indebtedness (1963), but not for long because the
school again was bursting at it's seams so this same year the voters of the
School District voted to build again.

        Victor Burchill, Superintendent

        Present School Board D. Stickney, J. Muhlhauser, F. Galloway and W.
        Schulz, clerk

THE NEW BEULAH HIGH SCHOOL

This new high school is being built completely separate of the old plant. It is
located three blocks north of the present building, and should be ready for use
by the fall of 1964. Arlo C. Beattie, son of the contractor who built the first
addition, is the architect and Lunn Construction, the contractor. The estimated
cost of the new school is $420,000.00, and there will be a $30,000.00
remodeling project in the near future on the present school.

When the new High School opens this fall, the Beulah School System will be run
on the 8-4 program. The first 8 grades will be in the present building and the
four high school grades in the new building. There will be an additional three
teachers hired to the present faculty of 26.

The Music Dept will be divided--one teacher as Band Instructor and one teacher
in charge of the Choral groups.

The present school budget is $212,000.00 and the budget for the coming fiscal
year should run close to $232,000.00.

of 1933 when they began using the name of Beulah Miners. They did retain the
name of "cubs" for their smaller teams.

THE BEULAH BAND

        The First Band in Uniform--1916

Beulah has always been a "town with a band". It was barely a year old when they
organized the first band. The Beulah Booster Band was comprised of local
townsmen who had the desire to provide a little musical culture into the lives
of the area residents. It was organized many times and had many different
directors. The best known was the Booster Band of the late twenties and early
thirties when townspeople and students combined their muscial talents into what
proved to be one of the more successful musical aggregations in Beulah's
History.

This band was under the direction of Geo. Kadlec, who had started the first
juvenile band in 1926. This band would hold a concert every Saturday night in
the "ole band-stand". Cars would be parked all around the stand and the
youngsters would crowd on the steps hoping to be asked to hold the music for
one of the players. As the years went by more and more students and less and
less adults were playing in the band. Soon it was entirely made up of High
School Students.

1940 HIGH SCHOOL MARCHING BAND

        Top Row: Geo. Easton, Summer Director, I. Helvik, Morman Missionary,
        O. Huber, L. Held, K. Christenson, Director. Third Row: A. Huber, J.
        Unruh, D. Grishkowsky, D. Zimmerman, K. Schulz, K. Hoffert. Second
        Row: B. Uhler, L. Foster, A. Grishkowsky, A. Uhler, V. Fisher, L.
        Huber. First Row: G. Flemmer, G. Easton, A. Hertz, S. Renden, V. Nei,
        E. Huber, T. Flemmer.

The band director generally thought of as having directed the band during the
late thirties and early forties is George Easton. He took over the duties as
band director every summer. This was primarily a marching band, which performed
every Saturday nite.

GLEE CLUBS AND CHORUSES

Beulah has not had choruses and other singing groups as consistently as they
have had band. The first group that received any publicity at all was the
Beulah Jr. High A cappella Chorus which had the distinction of being invited to
sing over K. F. Y. R. radio on May 28, 1936. The paper called them a HIT! This
chorus was under the direction of Sam F. Kerbs.

The Beulah High School Band has always been of the highest caliber.  They
always receive some of the highest ratings at the District Music Festivals.
Some of the School Band Directors have been:  Geo. Kadlec, Henry Hertz, Steve
Giovannini, Geo. Easton, Kenneth Christianson, H. Lang, Martha Bieber, Earle
Lovestrom, Vincent Raba, Bert Skakoon, Don Larson, Wm. Gleason, L. Thomason and
Don Barta (the present band director).
